                        Our Namesake

        Mr. John H. Wishek, Sr.

In loving memory to our town's gallant namesake. His courage, industry and
conviction has been and always will be an inspiration to all loyal Wishekites.

                        Our Mayor

        Mr. Fred Thurn

In grateful recognition of invaluable services rendered to the city of Wishek
before he was elected to office and during his term as mayor.

                WISHEK GOLDEN JUBILEE 1898-1948

                    July 2nd and 3rd, 1948

                        Foreword

                 By Earle J. Fortmann
        One of Wishek's Many Out-of-State Friends

This is a book with a heart. It contains all of the love, devotion, loyalty and
sorrow comprising life. As you turn the pages dissolve yourself into the mood
of the book and live again the early days of Wishek when her heart beat slowly
and weak. Follow the pages and experience the change in the mood as her life
blood becomes stronger and faster until you come to the final page ...out of
breath...with a strong upsurge of joy as you realize completely for the first
time that you are one of God's chosen children to be living in this wonderful
town of Wishek. There are a thousand stories contained in-its pages ...each
would take a book in itself to relate. But perhaps as you gaze upon the
countenances of the old pioneers you will close your eyes and see in your
mind's eye a small log cabin, a rude clearing and a surge of excitement will
fill your being for here is man, the early Wishek pioneer, battling the known
and the unknown...laying the foundation for things to come...hewing his niche
in the tree of life. A life that was to bring into being others who would carry
on his courageous and noble work, unselfish in their devotion to the land,
unswerving in their honesty of purpose, and irresistible as they forge ahead.
Turning the pages illustrates "of such stuff are these men made"... and a
reflection on Wishek brings forcefully to mind "of such towns are these United
States made." Through the unstinting efforts of these pioneers Wishek was
founded. Pioneers who forged and carried high the banners of liberty, justice
and fraternity through battlefields stenched with the blood of their brothers,
through honesty of purpose, courage of conviction, and imbued with the
knowledge that the land would care for her own...these ideals have sparked
succeeding generations to carry on. These children have earned their place in
this book by their willingness to make the supreme sacrifice to perpetuate the
ideals of their forebearers. Gaze long and lovingly upon the pictures of Wishek
as you know it today...a tribute to progressive citizens. As I close the cover
on this wonderful book I am reminded of those famous lines in the poem
"Distinction" written by Mark Antony de Wolfe Howe in 1864--

        "The village sleeps, a name unknown, 'till men
        With life-blood stain its soil, and pay the due
        That lifts it to eternal fame--for then
        'Tis grown a Gettysburg or Waterloo."

                        Dedication

To Mr. H. E. Timm...For years of unselfish devotion and untiring service to the
city of Wishek; we gratefully dedicate the History of Wishek section of this
Wishek Golden Jubilee Anniversary Book.

History of Wishek

Wishek, the Golden Jubilee City of 1948, is located on the Missouri River
Division of the Soo Line railroad in south central North Dakota. It is located
341 rail miles west of Minneapolis and 70 miles south east of Bismarck, at an
elevation of 2010 feet. A branch line of the Soo to Pollock, South Dakota,
serves towns to the south and southwest. Wishek is served by two state
highways--No. 3 and No. 13.

Founded fifty years ago with the coming of the railroad, its townsite includes
the S 1/2 of Section 10 and the N 1/2 of Section 15 in Township 132, Range 71.
It is situated in the center of the rich farming and ranching area of McIntosh
and Logan counties. It was named in honor of John H. Wishek, Sr., an early
pioneer and builder of the Dakota Territory who helped organize McIntosh County
and founded Ashley, the county seat. He was instrumental in bringing the Soo
Line into this section of North Dakota and personally procured all of the
right-of-way in McIntosh County on which the railroad was built. Although Mr.
Wishek always maintained his home in Ashley, he displayed a constant personal
interest in the growth and development of the town which bears his name, from
the time of its founding until the time of his passing--in 1932.

Wishek's Firsts

Rails were laid into the present site of Wishek by the Soo Line in September of
1898. There were two trains scheduled for each week--one on Thursdays and one
on Sundays. The first scheduled train arrived early in October, 1898. The
Wishek depot was built in sections in Minneapolis, shipped out on flat cars,
and set up on the station site. Frank Spurzem was credited as being the first
Soo Line agent at Wishek.

Gottlieb Nickisch, the only original pioneer of this community who still lives
in Wishek, arrived here from Eureka, So. Dak., on September 8, 1898. There was
no building to be seen at the time of his arrival but the very next day M. E.
Pratt, who had been a pioneer homesteader in this township, moved the first
building onto the town site from Kulm, No. Dak.

A few days later S. L. Lowe and Company pitched a large tent and started the
first grocery and drygoods store. The tent was used for about two months until
a frame building was completed. The building was located on the present site of
the old Sheridan House. It was destroyed by fire about one year later. About
the 22nd of September Mr. Paul Pudwill, Sr., started the erection of a frame
building for use as a restaurant. This building still stands on Wishek's main
street, is owned by J. W. Hofer, and is now occupied by the Farmers Hardware
Company.

About this same time the Werre brothers, Fred, Joe and John, came from Eureka,
built a frame building and started a grocery store. With them came Andrew
Wallman who built a frame building for use as a liquor store.

On or about October 1st, 1898, R. J. Bailey, Sr., together with his son, Wm.
G., arrived from Eureka and built the first Livery Barn in Wishek. They also
did house moving and in January, 1899, with the help of Reinhold Nickisch, they
moved a house from Eureka to Wishek for Steve Fischer. This house still stands
on Lot 14 of Block 6, Wishek, and is at present occupied by Henry Foerderer.
The elder Bailey--familiarly known as Dick--was prominent in the early annals
of the town. He was the first school director elected in Wishek and served for
many years. He also served as a village trustee and was captain of the first
organized fire department. He continued to live in Wishek until the time of his
death in 1931.

With the arrival of trains, the St. Croix Lumber Company shipped in a lumber
stock and had their lumber piles and yard on the railroad right-of-way. C. H.
Suess was the manager of their yard.

On October 20, 1898, Gottlieb Nickisch built the first blacksmith shop which he
located near the railroad right-of-way, but later moved the building up on to
the hill when town lots were available. In September and early October the
surveyor of the Tri-State Land Company laid out and platted the original
townsite of Wishek, consisting of Blocks 1 to 6 inclusive. Lots were made
available to buyers about the middle of the month of October.

On or about November 1st, 1898, John J. Doyle, one of the early pioneers of the
Wishek locality who had been farming and ranching in partnership with his
father, Patrick Doyle, since 1884, came into Wishek and built the first up-town
flathouse for the handling of grain. This building was located on the southwest
corner of Block 2. About this time a man named F. Baumgartner built a flathouse
on the railroad right-of-way and the Woodworth Elevator Company erected the
elevator now owned and operated by the Union Elevator Company. Work was also
started by Osborn-McMillan Company on the elevator building now owned and
operated by the Doyle Grain Company.

On or about the 15th of October, 1898, Mrs. Jennie Hill came from Eureka, So.
Dak. She had construction started at once on a hotel which for years was known
as the Hill House. While she waited for the completion of the hotel, she rented
the flathouse from Mr. Doyle and served meals in it during the fall of 1898.
When the hotel was completed--about the first of the year--she took over its
operation and continued to operate the Hill House as Wishek's leading hotel for
many years. She continued to live in Wishek until the time of her death. During
the fall of 1898 all the men of the new community were sleeping in the hayloft
of the Bailey Livery Barn awaiting the completion of the new hotel.

An interesting note from the memory of Gottlieb Nickisch is that just before
Christmas of 1898 all the men of the community were busy building a grade
across the creek in the bottom west of the town so that farmers could get into
Wishek with their grain.

On or about November 1st, 1898, the first Drugstore was opened in Wishek by
Tony Bjornson of Ashley. He rented the Paul Pudwill building for this purpose
and his brother, Nordi Biomson, was manager of the Wishek store.

The Charter of the First State Bank of Wishek, which was the first bank in
McIntosh County and in the town of Wishek, was issued under date of October 24,
1898. It was chartered by John H. Wishek, Sr., Nina Wishek, and Fred Helss. A
temporary bank building was erected on Lot 13 of Block 2 in the fall and when
the building was completed the bank opened for business on December 2, 1898. Mr.
A. P. Guy was its first cashier.

Mr. Guy arrived on the first scheduled train that came into Wishek. As a young
lawyer he immediately formed a partnership with John H. Wishek, Sr., known as
Wishek & Guy and opened a Law, Land, Loans and Collections office in the First
State Bank building on December 2, 1898. Mr. Guy was able, energetic and
versatile and much of the progress made by the community of Wishek during the
early years must be credited to him. He was instrumental in establishing the
first school and the first church organization. He was the first clerk of the
school board and first clerk of the organized village and was active as the
village attorney. In 1901 he established and published the first newspaper (The
Wishek News and Wisheker Nachrichten). In 1910 he moved with his family to
Oakes, North Dakota, where he still resides.

J. E. (Bert) Monroe was Wishek's first barber. He had taught prairie schools
before the beginning of the Wishek community and after the town was started
began cutting hair and shaving during vacations and Saturdays in any building
space that was available to him. In the winter of 1899-1900 he built the first
barber shop building. He continued in his barber business for many years. When
he retired he moved to Port Townsend, Washington. His death came about three
years after he moved from Wishek.

M. E. Pratt was the first Wishek postmaster. For a number of years he had
conducted an inland postoffice (known as Youngstown) in his home on his
homestead which was located two miles east and one mile north of Wishek.
However, during the fall and winter of 1898-1899 he built a postoffice building
which contained living quarters for his family, on the lot north of the First
State Bank building. The Youngstown postoffice was moved into Wishek and the
name changed to Wishek postoffice. The records show that the postoffice of
Youngstown was discontinued January 24, 1899. Youngstown postoffice, when in
function, was served by a weekly overland mail route from Steele to Napoleon
and from Napoleon to Ashley.  Mr. O. F. Bryant, an early pioneer in Logan
County and for many years publisher of the Napoleon Homestead, was the driver
of this overland mail route for a number of years prior to the arrival of the
railroad.

Reinhold Nickisch, a cousin of Gottlieb Nickisch, who arrived in Wishek with
him on September 8 1898, was Wishek's first drayman.

Youngstown School District No. 19, which embraces all of Township 132, Range 71
in which Wishek is located, was organized in 1896. This was prior to the
founding of the town and school had been held at the Pratt homestead for two
years before Wishek started. The first school conducted in Wishek was held in
an empty frame building known as the Palm Building, situated two doors north of
the First State Bank. R. C. Miles was the first teacher. He taught from April
18 to June 12, 1899--a two months' term--for the sum of $35.00 (teachers were
underpaid even in those early days). The first schoolhouse, a one-room frame
building, was built during the summer of 1900 on the site of our present school
grounds. Mr. R. C. Miles was the first teacher of this school. It appears from
the records that R. J. Bailey, Sr., was the first school director, A. P. Guy
the first school clerk, and M. E. Pratt the first school treasurer.

During the early years of Wishek's existence, community church services were
held in the Palm Building in which the first school was also held. They were
non-denominational but were conducted by such pastors at large of any
denomination as might come to Wishek. However, on January 10, 1904, primarily
through the efforts of Mr. and Mrs. A. P. Guy, an organization known as the
Grace Presbyterian Church of Wishek was effected by Rev. A. W. Wright of
Bismarck and plans were made for the building of a church in the summer of
1905. Contract was let to the Linton Construction Company for a cement block
building and in September and October of 1905 the Grace Presbyterian church was
erected as the first church in Wishek. It was dedicated on January 28, 1906, by
Rev. W. H. Hunter.

The first child born in Wishek was Wishek Pratt, son of Mr. and Mrs. M. E.
Pratt. He was named after the founder of the town and was presented with a free
lot in the town. He came to his death during his boyhood years by a fatal
accident.

The first death in the community of Wishek was that of Andrew Pfeifle who was
accidentally killed in the railroad yards.

The first Justice of the Peace in Wishek was E. H. (Uncle Ed) Lunn who
homesteaded that portion of the Wishek townsite on which Pfeifle's Addition is
now located.

Wishek's first creamery, built in 1902 by John H. Wishek, Sr., was located one
block west of our present lumber yards. R. H. Gillman was its manager. The
creamery was destroyed by fire in 1910.

In the year of 1907 the first flour mill was built, located on the north half
of Block 14. The owners were Gottlieb Schock, Fred Eisenbeis and Joe Tschetter.
This mill was destroyed by fire in 1913.

Wishek's first jeweler was Gustave Oulie who opened a shop in the early years
of 1900 and continued in business until about 1914 when he sold out his
business and returned to his native Norway. He still resides in the capitol
city of Oslo.

Wishek's first fraternal organization was the Independent Order of Odd Fellows.
Wishek Lodge No. 99 was chartered on March 15, 1906, and its first meeting held
on April 4th of the same year.

In 1905 the First State Bank building was remodeled and a second story added,
giving Wishek its first public hall and community meeting place. In this hall
the community dances and basket socials of early Wishek were held. The
community's first local dance orchestra was composed of Lou West, fiddle; Mrs.
Lou West, organ; and Warren (Teed) West, cornet. Those were the days of the
waltz and two-step and the old fashioned square dances. Almost every adult in
the town attended each of these occasions and a good time was had by all.

The first telephone line into Wishek was a two-phone affair between Wishek and
Ashley with wires strung on poles set along the right-of-way of the proposed
Pollock branch of the Soo Line. This was before any rails were laid on that
branch. One phone was in the First State Bank building in Wishek, the other in
the office of J. H. Wishek, Sr., in Ashley--giving the two communities a means
of communication. However, the first franchise for a local telephone exchange
was granted to E. H. Cook by the Village Board of Trustees on December 13,
1907. Mr. Cook did not exercise his franchise but sold his rights and interests
to O. F. Bryant of Napoleon who built the first telephone exchange in Wishek in
1909. This he operated until 1925 when he sold it to the Dakota Central
Telephone Company.

The first movie theater in Wishek was operated by H. L. Bohlman in the building
occupied by the Weisser Body Shop. This he started back in the 'teens' at a
time when silent films showed serial thrillers such as 'The Perils of Pauline'
and when Jack Bunny and Fatty Arbuckle were screen stars.

The first marriage of real Wishekites was that of Daniel G. Nickisch and Grace
Sheridan. This occurred in 1905. Both had come to Wishek at the time of its
founding. Mr. Nickisch had worked for his brother, Gottlieb, as a blacksmith.
After their marriage they bought the Sheridan Hotel from Mrs. Nickisch's mother
and operated it until 1916. Later he operated a blacksmith shop. They now make
their home in Minneapolis.

The first Federal Census of Wishek as an organized community was taken by Mr.
H. E. Timm in April, 1910, at which time the count showed a population of 432.

        Wishek's First Fourth of July Celebration

Wishek as a Municipality

During the first eight years after Wishek's founding, it existed as an
unorganized community without the benefit of municipal ordinances and local
supervision. The male voters of the community (for this was before the advent
of woman suffrage) participated in county and state elections as voters of a
country precinct. An early poll book of that period lists the following names
of male voters in the community: A. P. Guy, F. D. McCartney, F. J. Zahn, Jacob
Herr, Jr., Patrick Doyle, Jacob Balliet, John Dewald, C. H. Suess, Frank
Spurzem, Peter Ackerman, W. T. Best, Gottlieb Nickisch, James Welsh, F. N.
Gillis, Leopold Hunkele, J. E. Monroe, D. G. Nickisch, Wm. V. Curtis, Jacob
Wolf, R. H. Gillman, M. E. Pratt, E. H. Lunn, and B. F. Henry.

However, on April 22, 1907, the male voters--39 in number--voted unanimously to
incorporate the community into an organized village. Corporate limits were
established and the town government organized. The first Village Board of three
trustees was elected. They were John J. Doyle, Gottlieb Nickisch and Jacob
Herr, Jr., with Mr. Doyle serving as chairman of the Board. A. P. Guy was
elected as clerk and also acted as village attorney. The first village
ordinances were passed and approved June 10, 1907, with an emergency clause
attached and Wishek and its citizens came under municipal law. The streets and
alleys were regulated as thoroughfares; lightposts with kerosene lamps were
placed at the main corners in the business section of the town; and a village
jail was built on the site of the present city hall, where ordinance violators
could be jailed. In 1916 the first issue of village bonds in the amount of
$5,900.00 was voted and with the proceeds of said bonds, the present concrete
city hall was erected. Cross-walks were also laid according to a street and
sidewalk grade plat which was at that time established. In the same year of
1916 the village Board granted a franchise to the Krein Electric Company for
the use of streets and alleys for light and power lines. Street lights were
also provided for at that time.

In the '20's an effort was made to change the form of government from village
to city. However, this was defeated by the voters. The citizens of the town who
served as chairmen of the Board of Trustees during the years from 1907 until
1930 included the following: John J. Doyle, Gottlieb Nickisch, Jacob Herr, Jr.,
James Welsh, John Kuiper, F. N. Gillis, F. J. Sayler, Karl K. Wacker, M. H.
Miller, John Dockter, and John Weber, Sr. Clerks of the village during the same
period were: A. P. Guy, James Welsh, J. W. Hofer, H. E. Timm, C. L. Smith,
Robert Greiser, John W. Ackerman, H. H. Nolte, and William Huber.

In 1930 the electors voted a change in municipal government and Wishek was
incorporated as a city. H. E. Timm, who had served as an official of the
village for 18 consecutive years, was elected as the city's first mayor. The
ones who have served Wishek in the office of mayor are:

H. E. Timm      1930 - 1932
J. W. Hofer     1932 - 1934
John J. Doyle   1934 - 1938
Jacob C Krein   1938 - 1942
E. H. Cook      1942 - 1946
Fred Thurn      1946 - present incumbent

City auditors who have served during the 18 years of city government are:

William Huber   1930 - 1938
Fred Thurn      1938 - 1946
R. W. Pfau      1946 - present incumbent

During the early years of the town's existence, voluntary bucket brigades did
the fire fighting. As the town grew, fire became its greatest hazard. At a mass
meeting held on December 16, 1913, the Wishek Voluntary Fire Department was
organized and has actively functioned since its organization as an
instrumentality of the municipal government. Its first officers were: H. E.
Timm, chief; August W. Herr, assistant chief; R. J. Bailey, captain; Ed
Mattison, assistant captain; and Fred Herr, secretary. It has served well and
has proven its worth on many occasions. One such case was the conflagration of
1916, the most disastrous fire in Wishek's history. Six frame buildings on the
east side of the town's main street were completely destroyed. The fire proved
a blessing in disguise, however, for the frame row was soon replaced with
concrete structures which are a credit to the city.

Another adjunct to the municipality is the Wishek Association of Commerce.
Purely civic in its nature, the Association was organized by Wishek business
men in the year 1923 under a unique plan which permits all public-spirited
citizens to hold membership and give financial aid and active support. The
activities of the Wishek Association of Commerce are guided and directed by a
board of ten Directors, elected annually by the membership. Its projects have
been varied and successful. By close cooperation with the municipal and school
authorities the Association has proven itself an asset of inestimable value in
the growth and progress of our city.

Through all the years of Wishek's history its citizens have been conscious of
the beauty of trees and shrubbery, and have endeavored to landscape and
beautify their private homes and yards. During the early years, Green Lake and
its shores were used as Wishek's recreational spot. However, in the '20's
agitation started for a town park for use by children and adults alike. At this
time a group of citizens selected an unplatted block which is now the east half
of the area included in our present park. They had the same fenced and by
private contribution and personal labor, planted sixty Chinese Elm. Apparently
this species of tree is not adapted to North Dakota climate and in a few years
all of the trees had winter-killed. However, the sponsors did not quit but
continued the work of replanting with other species of trees.

After the organization of the Association of Commerce in 1923, that
organization assumed park development as one of its projects. In 1934 it
sponsored a park enlargement program, selected the unplatted block adjacent on
the west, vacated a 60-foot street and thus procured a tract 300 by 660 feet
which is now incorporated in our present city park. John H. Wishek, Jr., and
Max A. Wishek of the Wishek Investment Company donated one of the blocks to the
City of Wishek for park purposes. The other block was purchased by the City.

During the six years following, the Association sponsored a successful park
improvement program. This was done in cooperation with the municipal
authorities. Work was done by WPA labor while materials were paid for by
special donation and the small annual grant from the city treasury. The year
1935 saw the following park development: 500 trees furnished by the government
were planted; a well-equipped children's playground and three clay-surfaced
tennis courts were provided, as well as a well-lighted skating rink; and a
well, equipped with adequate pumping facilities to water the trees, was
installed. In 1937 a shelter-house and outside fire places were built with
brick donated by the Soo Line railroad.

In August of 1938 the Association urged the construction of a swimming pool in
the park. The city council placed the proposal of a $6,000 bond issue for
materials before the voters in the general election in the fall of 1938 and the
same was approved. The year 1939 saw the building of the pool by WPA labor. The
bathhouse was completed early in 1940 and dedication was held on June 26th of
that year. Two of the tennis courts were also cement surfaced in 1940. Wishek
now has a park to which its citizens can point with pride. As a prairie park
and recreational grounds, it can neither be equaled nor surpassed by any city
of like size in the state.

In 1940 the Association again started an agitation for an adequate public hall
or place of meeting to accommodate Wishek's increasing population. The city
council, encouraged by the success of the park development project, laid plans
for a city auditorium. Working in close cooperation with the Board of Education
of Youngstown School District No. 19, a project was worked out under the Public
Works Administration for the building of a city auditorium and school
gymnasium. The city furnished $12,000 by a bond issue for a portion of the
material, the federal government providing for the labor and part of the
material required. The structure was erected on the school grounds in Block 7
so as to be heated and janitored as a part of the public school system. During
the years of 1941 and 1942 the building took shape--its outer wall facing of
prairie boulders set in beautiful design. Completed in the spring of 1943, the
building was dedicated in June of that year. Lt. Governor Henry Holt gave the
dedicatory address. For beauty and utility the building has no equal in any
city of like size in North Dakota.

Late in the year of 1945 the Association of Commerce again sponsored a drive
for a municipal athletic field. The following year a tract of land adjacent to
the city park on the south was acquired by purchase. The Association of
Commerce and the city each paid half of the purchase price. The city election
of 1948 will bring to the voters a proposal to establish the park and the
athletic field as a Park District. With this becoming an accomplished fact,
further development and improvement of both projects might be expected.

An adequate supply of good water is essential to the growth and development of
any town or city. Many towns and cities in our state lack this essential.
Happily, Wishek was located fifty years ago above a subterranean lake which has
furnished its residents with the best water in the state from wells from 25 to
40 feet in depth. With good water so easily available, little need was found
for a city water system. However, in 1944 agitation was started for a city
water plant for civilian use and for fire protection --and what is even more
important, an adequate system for disposal of the city's sewerage. In the year
1945 plans for the system were drawn and the voters approved the necessary bond
issue at a special election. During 1946 the W. H. Noel Company of Jamestown,
North Dakota, completed the installation of a city water system and a city
sewerage disposal plant. This improvement is proving itself a real asset to
Wishek's modernization and growth. Already, the system has more patrons than
had been anticipated in the original plans.

The most recent development in the city's modern growth and expansion is the
airport. Realizing the necessity for a landing field in this age of modern air
travel, the Association of Commerce sponsored a drive for a local landing
field. Funds were raised by popular subscription. In March, 1947, the SW 1/4 of
Section 14-132-71, which corners with the townsite to the southeast, was
purchased. The land was deeded to the City of Wishek. Two earthen landing
strips have already been laid out. The new port boasts two hangars with the
capacity for housing six planes. One is owned by Elmo Nickisch--the other by
John M. Doyle and Walter J. Doyle. Already, five locally owned planes are
stationed at the port. It has recently been approved by C.A.A. as an accredited
G-I Training Field auxiliary to the Jamestown airport. A flying school is being
conducted at the field by a licensed instructor.

It is the desire of the Historical Committee to here record a tribute to the
vision and untiring efforts of city officials in office during these late years
of municipal development. Special palms are due to William Huber, Fred Thurn
and R. W. Pfau as city auditors during the development years, who worked
unceasingly on the projects which are now realities.

Other Interesting Notes from Wishek's Business

In January, 1899, John J. Doyle, Steve Fischer and Joe Senger formed a
partnership, enlarged the flathouse which Mr. Doyle had previously built and
opened a general merchandise store known as The Wishek Bazaar. A frame
structure located on the southwest corner of Block 2, it stood as a landmark of
Wishek's early years until 1946 when it was torn down and replaced with a new
and modem implement building by J. J. Kramer. In 1905 The Wishek Bazaar was
sold to Dewald and Herr, and in 1908 it came into the possession of W. T. Best
who had previously operated a store in the Palm Building.

In the year 1905 Peter Ackerman moved into town from his farm located 1-1/2
miles west of Wishek. In 1906 he bought the Bailey Livery & Feed Barn, selling
it in the fall of that year to Jake and Mike Hilscher. In 1907 he, together
with his father, Johannes Ackerman, who was one of the real pioneers of the
area, acquired a general store located on the southwest corner of Block 1.
After the disastrous fire of February, 1909, which destroyed the Herr Brothers
& Co. building, the Palm building, and the postoffice and home of M. E. Pratt,
the Ackermans sold their store to Herr Brothers & Co. Later that year the
Ackermans and Charles H. Julius bought out W. T. Best. In 1911 the Ackermans
bought the Julius interests and until 1920 operated under the firm name of
Ackerman & Sons. Peter Ackerman was assisted in the operation of the store at
various periods by his brother Fred E. Ackerman and by his son John W.
Ackerman. In 1920 he bought his father's interest and continued to operate the
business as Ackerman's Store until 1936 when he retired. He sold the stock to
Julius Wetsch and the building and location to T. T. Kramer.

Mrs. Carrie Sheridan, a widow, with her two children, Grace and Clarence, came
from Eureka S. Dak., in company with Mrs. Jennie Hill in the fall of 1898. She
helped Mrs. Hill in her restaurant and later in the hotel which Mrs. Hill
operated. In the fall of 1899 she started a restaurant of her own. Patrick
Doyle built a building for her restaurant which building still stands as the
old addition on the west side of the Sheridan House, now occupied by the Brandt
Electric and Jewelry Shop. In 1901 Mrs. Sheridan built the front portion of the
building, named it the Sheridan House and operated it for several years. In
1905 D. G. Nickisch, her son-in-law, then became the proprietor. He continued
in the hotel until 1916 when the place was sold. Mrs. Sheridan died in Wishek
in 1938 at the ripe old age of 88 years.

In the fall of 1899 Mike Klein and Raymond Volk started a general merchandise
store which was the fore-runner of the present department store of the Herr
Mercantile Co., Inc. In 1903 Klein and Volk sold out to Jacob Herr, Sr., Jacob
Herr, Jr., and August W. Herr, who formed a partnership known as Herr Brothers
& Co. The new company enlarged their building in 1906 to a two-story iron-clad
structure and successfully dealt in all types of merchandise from a needle to a
threshing machine. A disastrous fire in February, 1909, destroyed the Herr
Brothers & Co. store, together with the adjacent Palm building and the Wishek
postoffice which was also the home of M. E. Pratt, the postmaster. The Herrs
started immediate construction on the concrete and brick structure which is now
the home of Herr Mercantile & Co., Inc. They were back in the new quarters on
the old location in the fall of 1909. In 1916 the store was incorporated as the
Herr Mercantile Company. At the time of the incorporation three other brothers
of the Herr family--Christian J. Herr, Gottlieb Herr, and Edward Herr--became
interested in the Company and came into active management. Through all the
years of its operation the Herr Store has maintained its position as the
leading department store in this section of the state and has proved a real
asset to the city. For 45 years it has been and still is owned and operated by
the Herr family.

In 1901 E. H. Cook came to Wishek from Minnesota. He purchased the business of
Ferdinand Senger and for many years operated a Pool & Billiard hall on the site
now occupied by the Pfeifle Garage. Mr. Cook built three concrete business
buildings in Block 1, one of which is the Lyric Theater building. He operated
the theater for a number of years until it was leased and later sold to Walter
R. Sayler. Mr. Cook has disposed of most of his real estate interests and is
retired, but still makes Wishek his home.

J. W. Hofer also arrived in Wishek in the year 1901. He taught school and
worked at various jobs until 1905 when he formed a partnership with Dr. George
Grant, which was known as The Wishek Drug Company and together, they bought the
drug store from Tony Biornson. In 1910 they erected the two-story brick and
concrete building which now houses the Wishek Drug Company. Later, Mr. Hofer
bought the interest of Dr. Grant and still operates the drug store with the
assistance of his son, Grant. He has resided in Wishek for 47 years.

About 1899 or 1900 Sullivan Brothers permanently located a lumber yard on the
west half of Block 3. It was known as the North Star Lumber Company, with C. H.
Suess as manager. In 1905 F. J. Sayler came to Wishek from Artas, S. Dak., as
assistant to Mr. Suess. In 1908 the yard was sold to Wells-Thompson and Mr.
Sayler took over the management. The Hollinsworth-Hart firm also started a yard
on the northeast corner of Block 3 in the early years of 1900 with Fred Weber
as manager, but same was later sold to the Merrick Lumber Company. The
Wells-Thompson yard was later sold to Thompson Yards, Inc., and Mr. Sayler
continued as its manager. In 1928 Mr. Sayler purchased the stock of the Merrick
yard and started in business for himself. In 1931 he bought the stock and plant
of Thompson Yards, Inc., organized a company known as the Wishek Lumber Company
and continued in the lumber business until 1946. He changed the corporate name
from the Wishek Lumber Company to the Sayler Lumber Company about the year
1940. In 1946 he retired after 40 years in the lumber business. The Sayler
Lumber Company is now operated by his sons. Wishek is still his home.

William G. Bailey came to Wishek with his father, R. J. Bailey, in the fall of
1898, when the town started. Together they built and operated the first Livery
and Feed Barn under the name of R. J. Bailey & Son. They sold the barn to Peter
Ackerman in 1906. Mr. Bailey then filed on a homestead located 2-1/2 miles
south of Wishek. About that time he married Naomi Petrehn of New Albin, Iowa.
They made their home on the farm until 1912, then moved back into Wishek. He
repurchased the Livery & Feed Barn which he had previously owned. In 1916 he
sold the barn to Newton Pericle and for many years he was employed as a grain
buyer. He also dealt in livestock. In 1944 he moved to Fargo where he is now
employed by the Central Selling Agency at West Fargo.

In 1916 August W. Herr built the two-story concrete building adjacent to the
Herr Mercantile Co. on the south. He opened a meat market in a portion of the
building. His brother, Fred Herr, together with Martin H. Miller, occupied a
part of the same building operating a hardware store. Late in the same year the
Hoff Brothers--Fred L. and August F.--came from Eureka, S. Dak., bought out the
hardware store of Miller & Herr and opened a hardware and furniture store in
this building. They operated as a partnership for years but later dissolved
their company. August F. Hoff continued in the business while his brother, Fred
L., retired and later moved to Lodi, Calif. In 1945 August F. Hoff erected a
modern furniture and hardware building in Block 6. In 1947 he retired and moved
to Texas, turning the store over to his son, Delmar E. Hoff.

Wishek's Banks

The First State Bank of Wishek, which was the first bank organized in McIntosh
County, was chartered in October, 1898, with John H. Wishek, Sr., as its
founder and president. He continued in the office of president until the time
of his death in 1932. The cashiers of the bank, in order of succession, were:
A. P. Guy, F. D. McCartney, F. N. Gillis, George A. Barnum, F. N. Gillis, H. E.
Timm, and Wm. Huber. Other personnel employed in the bank over the years were:
W. L. Johnson, H. E. Kusler, J. C. Nippolt, John W. Meidinger, E. P. Pfeifle,
Henry T. Holtz, W. H. Leiser, John H. Wishek, Jr., Harold Nolte, and Gertrude
Stading. This bank ceased to exist in 1940.

The Security State Bank was organized by John J. Doyle and was chartered in
1909. Mr. Doyle served as president from the time of its organization until his
death in 1946. Cashiers of said bank in order of succession were: F. N. Gillis,
John Kuiper, and Henry Kusler. Mr. Kusler, after many years of service, retired
in 1947 and moved to Spirit Lake Idaho. Other personnel who served in the bank
over the years were: Frank Koehler, Ben Bode, and W. H. Dickson. This bank has
functioned without interruption since its organization and is still being
operated by Mrs. John J. Doyle and her two sons, John M. Doyle and Walter J.
Doyle, with W. H. Dickson in charge.

Wishek's Newspapers

For the first three years of the community's existence, Wishek had no
newspaper. A Wishek section was published in the McIntosh County Republican at
Ashley. However, in 1901, A. P. Guy and F. D. McCartney founded the Wishek News
and Wisheker Nachrichten with Leopold Grueninger as editor. In 1908, Mr.
Grueninger, in partnership with E. H. Cook and Charles Roehm, bought out the
founders of the paper and continued to edit and publish it until 1915 when it
was sold to a group of local men who organized the German-American Printing
Company. The company published the News for about a year when it was disposed
of to C. C. Lowe. In 1919 Robert Greiser, who had worked on the paper for Mr.
Lowe, bought him .out and became editor and publisher. Mr. Greiser continued
the publication of The Wishek News for 25 years, except for a short period of
six or eight months, during which time it was published by Esther Billigmeier,
until about 1944, when he sold the subscription list to the Ashley Tribune and
disposed of the plant and equipment. Wishek was without a newspaper for about
two years. The Association of Commerce, realizing that a newspaper was
necessary to the growth and progress of Wishek, induced Edwin T. Brost to
locate in Wishek and establish a paper known as The Wishek Star. Brost started
publication of the Star in October, 1945, operated for a year and then sold to
M. H. Amundson. Amundson operated for a year and in the fall of 1947 sold to
Jay A. Bryant, publisher of the Napoleon Homestead, who now operates both
papers. The Napoleon Homestead has a record of 62 years of continuous
publication by the Bryant family.

Wishek--Electrically

For 18 years after its founding, Wishek's inhabitants had to rely on kerosene
lamps and gasoline pressure systems for light. Its streets were unlighted.
However, in 1916 three Krein brothers--Christian F. Krein, Jacob C. Krein and
Fred Krein--sons of Mr. and Mrs. Christian Krein, who were early pioneers of
our farming community, moved into Wishek from their farms, formed a company
known as Krein Electric Company and built our first electric power plant.

Dwellings and commercial buildings were wired, poles were set, wires strung,
street lights hung and in the fall of 1916 the first current was turned on.
This was an epoch in Wishek's history for it ushered in the era of motors, wash
machines, irons and other electrical appliances. Candles and lamps were still a
necessity, however, for the power was turned off at midnight. Many a party was
broken up by the warning "blink" at 11:45 p.m. The Krein Electric Company
operated until 1924 when it was sold out to the Northern Power & Light Company.
The purchasers operated the local plant for two years until power lines could
be built from Mobridge, S. Dak. Since 1926 Wishek has been serviced by high-line
power which is now furnished by the Montana-Dakota Utilities Company.

Wishek's Automotive History

W. T. Best, J. J. Doyle, and August W. Herr owned the first cars operated in
Wishek. Best had a two-cylinder Cadillac while Doyle and Herr owned early model
Buicks. This was in 1910 or thereabouts. Later, J. E. Monroe, the barber,
bought a Brush car and Dr. George Grant owned a Maxwell.

With the advent of the automobile and the exit of horses for road
transportation, the livery barns of Wm. G. Bailey and Jim Ruzicka became
outmoded and public garages were built. In 1916 Edward Wolff erected a concrete
garage in Block 12, across main street from the Security State Bank. In the
year 1915, Ackerman and Sons became the first authorized Ford dealers in
Wishek. A large number of model-T Fords were sold by this dealership during the
next four years. John W. Ackerman was the leading car salesman of the
organization. The Ackerman Sales Company organized in 1919, then bought the
garage in Block 12 and opened the first sales and service garage in Wishek with
Fred E. Ackerman in charge. In the year 1923 Fred E. Ackerman received an
appointment as postmaster of Wishek, selling the garage to John W. Ackerman who
in turn operated same until 1929. In that year, John W. Ackerman was appointed
as postmaster of Wishek and discontinued his garage business. The building was
then sold to John J. Pudwill, dealer in Overland cars and farm implements. This
building was later gutted by fire but has recently been rehabilitated and is now
the home of the Doyle Implement and Supply Company.

In the year 1918, E. P. Pfeifle was designated an authorized dealer for GMC
cars and trucks, and started the first Chevrolet and Buick dealership. He later
built the concrete building in Block 2 which still houses the Pfeifle Chevrolet
Garage. He added the Pure Oil bulk station later and has been in business
continuously for the past 30 years.

The year 1920 saw the erection of a tile and brick garage building on the
southwest corner of Block 6 by Edward Wolff. In 1927 Preszler Brothers took
over--procured the Ford dealership and operated until 1933 when this garage and
the Ford dealership was taken over by Dan and Ben Schlenker. This garage is
still being operated as an authorized Ford garage by Dan Schlenker under the
firm name of Schlenker Garage.

Another large garage was built by E. J. Ensslen, located in Block 6 and was
later operated by Fred Baumann and W. J. Fiechtner. This building was destroyed
by fire in 1936 or 1937.

Oil and gas are closely related to the automotive industry. In the early years
of 1900 the John D. Rockefeller interests established the first bulk station
oil tanks in Wishek. This first storage plant burned and exploded in 1907. It
was quite an exciting fire for the residents of the community. No one dared
venture near the fire. Men of the town owning high-powered rifles kept at a
distance and shot holes into the tanks to keep them from exploding. However,
several tanks did explode, throwing flaming oil high into the air and throwing
the tops of the tanks a distance of two or three blocks away. Soon after the
fire, the newly organized Standard Oil Company rebuilt the bulk station plant.
They have continued the distribution of oil products continuously since that
time. Agents for the Standard Oil Company in the early years of operation were:
F. F. Zimmerman, W. F. West, Walter Forsythe, F. R. Wolff, J. G. Ritter, C. M.
Ritter, and J. C. Krein. In 1924 Fred Thurn took over the agency and has
operated it continuously since that time. He has a record of 24 years of
service with the company.

Also in 1924 Mr. John Doerr of Lehr established a bulk station known as The
Wishek Oil Company, handling Texaco Products. In 1926 Socony Vacuum Oil Company
bought the plant and installed Christ Sayler as agent. He continued as manager
until 1941 when Adolph Gall took over and is still the agent for Mobiloil
products in Wishek.

The Pure Oil bulk plant was built by E. P. Pfeifle in 1932 as an independent
station. He has operated it continuously since that year in connection with his
Chevrolet Garage. He still distributes Pure Oil products.

In the early years of the town, petroleum products were handled by the
merchants and gasoline was dispensed through curb pumps owned by the stores.
However, in 1925 Gottlieb G. Wolff built the first modern auto service station
on the northwest corner of Block 6. After his death it was operated by his
sons, Aaron and Gideon. It is now owned and operated by Eckman Brothers under
the firm name of Eckman's Standard Service Station.

About 1930 John Dockter built a station on Block 40 in west Wishek. This
station has served through the years under various managements and is now
operated by Ferdinand and Arthur Vossler.

In the year 1932 Adolph Gall took over the Dockter station in west Wishek,
handling Mobiloil products, but in 1935 he moved into the present station
located on the southeast comer of Block 3. When he assumed the management of
the Socony Vacuum bulk plant in 1941 he sold the service station to his brother
Theophil Gall. It is now being operated as the Gail & Aipperspach Service
Station.

Wishek Hotels

As stated earlier in this history, Wishek's first three-story frame hotel--The
Hill House--was built in 1898 and operated by Mrs. Jennie Hill. For many years
it was the town's leading hotel. The Sheridan House, built by Mrs. Carrie
Sheridan in 1900 also cared for much of the transient trade. Later, H. L.
Bohlman built the Soo Hotel which was utilized mostly as a rooming house.

In the 20's it was evident that Wishek's greatest need was a modern hotel and
cafe. Shortly after World War I Gus Sholley, who had been a troop transport
cook during the war, arrived in Wishek. He started a cafe known as The Auto Inn
and operated it for a number of years. In 1925 he bought lots in Block 13 and
built our present two-story concrete hotel, operating a cafe in connection with
it. He continued in business until 1944 when he sold out his business and moved
to Tucson, Ariz., where he now resides. H. P. Mensing took over the hotel in
March, 1945, and continues to operate same as a modern hotel.

The Hill House was torn down and removed some years ago while The Sheridan
House and Soo Hotel have been put to other commercial uses.

It is the wish of the Historical Committee to pay special tribute to Wishek's
two pioneers and the businesses which they started fifty years ago. These
businesses have operated throughout the full fifty years of Wishek's existence.

Starting as a blacksmith with the founding of the town in 1898, Gottlieb
Nickisch added a machinery line to his business in 1904. Later he sold his
blacksmith shop to his brother, Dan G. Nickisch, and then added the furniture
line to his implement business. In 1931, his son, Elmo, was licensed as an
embalmer. The next year, Mr. Nickisch formed a partnership with his son, Elmo
and his daughter, Gladys, known as Gottlieb Nickisch & Company. A complete and
modern mortician service was added to the business. The partnership continued
until 1946, when it was dissolved. Mr. Nickisch retired from business but
continues to make Wishek his home. Elmo Nickisch bought out the assets of the
firm and continues to operate under the firm name of G. Nickisch Co. In 1947,
Elmo Nickisch, who is a licensed pilot, added an air taxi and ambulance service
to the business.

John J. Doyle built his first grain flathouse in 1898 at the founding of the
town and through all the years, in connection with his other interests, he
continued his grain business. Since his death in 1946, his sons, John M. and
Walter J., have continued to operate the business started by their father fifty
years ago under the present firm name of The Doyle Grain Company. The buyers
working for Mr. Doyle during the greater portion of the fifty years were: Peter
Schnor, M. E. Pratt, and John Dockter.

As was the case with all frontier towns, Wishek, during the early years of its
existence, had its share of liquor bars and gambling joints commonly known as
'blind pigs.' With North Dakota a dry state and with prohibition the law of our
land, these places operated outside of the law and were unlicensed. For this
reason and for the reason that little or no information is available on them,
this history is not recording a chronology of them. It is just as well that
this particular page in Wishek's history--unsavory as it was--be discarded and
forgotten.

Other legitimate business concerns that operated during different periods of
Wishek's fifty years of history but are no longer in existence are listed
below. Dates and other information in connection with them are not available.

Gackle & Ziegenhagel, Implements
George & Hilscher, Implements
W. T. Best, General Merchandise
R. J. Bailey, Meat Market
Dockter & Schmitt, Furniture & Implements
Herr & Pudwill, Implements
Charles Roehm, Meat Market
Gottlieb Roeszler, Meat Market
H. E. Timm, Little Cozy Restaurant
Auerbach's Store, General Merchandise
Mackoff Brothers, General Merchandise
George Scharf, General Merchandise
Wolff & Pfeifle, General Merchandise
Jacob Wolff, General Merchandise
Mrs. E. W. Davis, Ladies Wear
Frank Rigler, Wishek Cash Store
John Gaschk, Jeweler
I. Dorfman, Meat Market
John Stoller, Meat Market
Jacob Will, Meat Market
Wishek Bottling Works
Wishek Cheese Factory
Wilhelm Mittelstadt, Harness Shop
John H. Cater, Barber
John J. Pudwill, Implements
Karl K. Wacker, Harness Shop
Gottlieb Bader, Barber

Miscellany

Wishek's Doctors

During its early years, Wishek had no resident doctor and Dr. Fred Maercklein
had to be called from Ashley when a physician's services were needed. In 1903
Dr. George Grant procured his license to practice in North Dakota and spent his
summers in Wishek until 1905, in which year he located here permanently. He
formed a partnership with J. W. Hofer known as The Wishek Drug Company. He also
served as Vice President of the Security State :Bank from the time of its
organization until the time of his death. He also acquired considerable land
and was interested in other enterprises. He continued his practice as an M.D.
until his death in 1939. In the '20's Dr. Ivan Maercklein, who had been a
medical officer in World War I, located in Wishek and practiced for a number of
years. In 1934 he moved to Oregon. In 1936, Dr. A. A. Whittemore, a former
Secretary of the State Board of Health, came to Wishek and started practice. He
passed away in 1940. Following his death, Dr. J. H. Barrette, formerly of Lehr,
moved his offices to Wishek and is still in practice here.

Dr. Julius Theurer was Wishek's first chiropractic doctor. He now practices in
Fergus Falls, Minnesota. He was followed by Dr. H. H. Werre, who now conducts a
chiropractic clinic in Fargo. After Dr. Werre left, Dr. H. K. Walth, son of
Fredrick Walth--one of Wishek's farmer pioneers, located in Wishek in the year
1932 and is still in practice, conducting the Wishek Chiropractic Clinic.

Wishek's Dentists

Wishek's first dentist was an unlicensed practitioner--F. H. Newton by name. In
1919 Dr. T. A. Alexander moved to Wishek from Carrington and continued in
general office practice until his death. By a strange coincidence, death came
to both Dr. George Grant and Dr. T. A. Alexander on the same day and date in
1939. Dr. W. H. Mehlhaff located here in 1927, coming from Eureka, South
Dakota. He is still in general practice. He is now in the process of building a
modern five-chair dental clinic which should prove itself a real asset to our
city. Dr. Chester A. Faul and Dr. Ted C. Ritter are associated with Dr.
Mehlhaff in his dental clinic.

Wishek's Postmasters

Wishek's postmasters, in order of their succession during the fifty years of
the community's existence, are:

  M. E. Pratt ........... 1898 - 1914
  J. C. Nippolt ......... 1914 - 1923

  James Welsh (acting) .. 1928 - 1929
  J. W. Ackerman ........ 1929 - 1933
  J. H. Case ............ 1933 - 1944
  F. W. Roehm (acting) .. 1944 - present

  A. P. Guy

  Oscar G. Abern

Tri-County Fair

In 1926, sponsored by the Association of Commerce, a group of Wishek business
men and a group of leading farmers of the area, realizing the need for better
breeds of livestock and better strains of grain organized the Tri-County
Exhibitor's Association. It was also an effort to bring closer cooperation
between the farmers of our area and the people of Wishek. H E. Timm served as
its president for many years and Robert Greiser was its secretary. The first
Fair was held in September, 1927, with tents being used to house the exhibits.
Increased interest by town and country folk alike assured the directors of the
Fair's success. In 1933 an 80-acre fair grounds was purchased from the State
Land Department located on the hill one mile west of Wishek. Three permanent
fair buildings were then constructed. Successful fairs were held annually and
without interruption from 1927 until 1941. Fair showings were discontinued
during the critical war years of 1942 to 1946, inclusive, but 1947 saw the
return of the fair as an annual fall festival in the community.

After the Soo Line had built the Pollock branch south out of Wishek in 1901
Wishek became a freight division point and important railroad center. For years
the Soo Line maintained an eight-stall roundhouse and extensive car repair
yards in Wishek. As many as twelve train crews worked out of the town at one
time. The W. Lasley Company of Minneapolis constructed a cement brick building
on the station site for use as a 'beanery' and rooming house for trainmen. With
the laying of heavier steel, the use of larger engines and the pulling of
longer trains, use of the roundhouse was discontinued and the car repair yards
were moved to Enderlin, North Dakota. The Lasley building has since been
converted into our present depot and the roundhouse building is now occupied by
the Wishek cement block and brick factory.

A record for length of time in railroad service and services to the Wishek
community was established by E. W. Davis. After the untimely death in 1907 of
Frank Spurzem, our first Soo Line agent, one agent followed another in rapid
succession until 1909 when Mr. Davis was checked in as local agent. He
continued in this capacity without interruption for 28 years and in 1937 was
retired from service by the company. He now resides in Minneapolis.

An item that may prove interesting to posterity is the change in the methods of
handling certain kinds of farm produce during Wishek's fifty years of
existence. Wheat is and always has been king of the small grains produced in
this area. Wishek, all through the years, has been a large primary wheat
market. In the early years wheat was marketed in sacks and could be handled
through flathouses without the aid of pit and elevator. Dump scales were not in
common use and the day of the trick and bulk delivery had not arrived. On any
fall day during those early days it was not uncommon to see hundreds of
horse-drawn wagons loaded with sacked wheat lined up two and three abreast, the
lines extending from the elevator and flathouse driveway back and up Wishek's
main street for two or three blocks. The drivers were waiting their turn to
unload.

Butterfat is another item. The production of butterfat by the farmers of
Wishek's trade territory during the past fifty years has been phenomenal.
However, it was not always marketed in the form of cream. Before the general
use of the farm cream separator, this commodity came to market in great round
rolls of butter and was known as "packing stock." Taken in trade by the
merchants, it was packed in barrels and shipped to processors in the Twin
Cities. With the introduction of the Babcock system of cream testing, methods
of marketing were changed. Milk was separated on the farms and the cream
marketed in cans, as now. Creameries started bidding for cream and a cash
market was established. Times and methods changed and Wishek changed with them.
Independent cream stations were established. Philip Thurn and Philip Mueller,
Sr., opened one of these early stations. Other cream buyers in Wishek's history
were: Joseph Stabler; Dave Silver; John H. Kundert; The Farmers' Cream
Association, a cooperative; J. C. Krein; C. F. Krein; Christian Unruh; Ferdinand
Ketterling, and Joseph Swerdlow.

During the early years, the inhabitants of the community were rabid baseball
fans and early Wishek boasted baseball clubs that were champions of this
section of the state. It is regrettable that information and data as to the
personnel of those early teams has been lost or discarded. From such
information as we have, however, we find that with Fred Gillis on the mound,
Jim Ruzicka behind the plate, and Ed Cook on first base, the team was virtually
unbeatable. In those early years, the baseball diamond was located directly
west of the present site of the Security State Bank.

A complete history of the Wishek Public Schools will be found in a later
section of this book. This is also true of Wishek's civic and fraternal
organizations and its churches. For this reason further mention of them is not
made in this general history. However, as an historical fact, it should be noted
here that 25 years ago Wishek had about a dozen Jewish families among its
residents. A Jewish synagogue was built in 1917 located on Block 30 in
southeast Wishek. It was dedicated by Rabbi Hess of Ashley in the fall of that
year and served as a temple of worship for a number of years. Later, with the
exodus of our Jewish families to other places of residence, the synagogue was
discontinued, the building sold and moved away. However, while these Jewish
folks lived among us they contributed their part to the city's developments.
The following family names are therefore listed:

Abe Auerbach
Nath Auerbach
Frank Rigler
Isadore Dorfman
Dave Silver
Nathan Silver
O. G. Abern
Joseph Swerdlow
Mayer Mackoff
Harry Mackoff
Sam Rapaport
I. Pomerance
N. Finkel
Harry Rosen

An unusual occasion in the days of early Wishek should be recorded here. It
concerned Mr. Anton Helmer, the man who hauled the mail from Ashley to Wishek
during the years from 1898 when Wishek was founded until the day the first
train went south on the Pollock branch. When that day arrived and Mr. Helmer
had no more mail to haul, the men of Wishek bought him a complete outfit of new
clothes and gave him a shave and a haircut at Bert Monroe's barber shop, then
put him on the train with his mail bags. He delivered the mail to Ashley by rail
and his team and buggy were returned to Ashley by another driver.

Personalities

Honor Residents

The Historical Committee honors nine residents as pioneers of our city. They
are not natives of North Dakota but came from other states as young men in the
early years of Wishek's history. Each has lived in Wishek continuously for
forty years or more and still makes Wishek his home.

Gottlieb Nickisch--to Wishek from South Dakota in 1898.
E. H. Cook--to Wishek from Minnesota in 1901.
J. W. Hofer--to Wishek from South Dakota in 1901.

Honor Where Honor Is Due

It is a well established fact that the growth and development of any town or
city located in an agricultural area depends wholly on the prosperity of the
farmers in the adjacent territory. Such a community rises or falls with its
farmers. The Historical Committee therefore proposes to give honor where honor
is due--paying homage and tribute to those sturdy sons and daughters of the
soil who made possible the city of Wishek as we know it today. Without them
there would have been no Wishek and this history would not now be written.

Up until the decade of history in which North Dakota became a sovereign state,
this section of our state now comprising the counties of McIntosh, Logan and
Emmons, was for the most part virgin, treeless, sparsely settled prairie. In
the early '80's a few adventurous souls moved into the area which is now
McIntosh County. They settled on the shore of Lake Hoskins. Our namesake, John
H. Wishek, Sr., was among them. He arrived in 1884 and assisted in the U. S.
Government survey of the area. In order to speed up settlement, the Federal
Government designated 14 sections in each township as homestead land. An influx
of settlers began in earnest about 1885 and continued for twenty years or until
all free lands had been taken. Some of our settlers came from our eastern
states, some direct from western Europe, but the vast majority--approximately
95%--came from the Russian Ukraine. These of the majority are often referred to
as Russians, but this is a misnomer. They were Russian nationals because of
their birth in Russia. However, they were of genuine German descent and
lineage. They were descendants of those carefully selected German families
transplanted from Germany into the Russian Ukraine by Queen Catherine of Russia
in her colonization effort during her reign from 1763 to 1796. During their
century of sojourn in Russia these Germans maintained their racial identity.
They retained their language, their customs and their religion and such of their
descendants as came to our shores and to our community brought these marks of
identification with them.

All of our farmer pioneers, of whatever origin, were a hardy folk. They built
their houses and barns from the native sod of the prairie. They gathered
buffalo chips and twisted hay for fuel. They collected buffalo bones and hauled
them over the long miles to Ellendale or Eureka where they exchanged them for
groceries and provisions. They were true lovers of the soil. They knew the
meaning of work and sweat. They carried on through any and all circumstances.
They laughed at hardships--they thrived on adversity. They were frugal and
thrifty.

        Grasshoppers Take Over

They were honest and honorable. They were neighborly and helpful. They lived
through the scourges of drought and heat, rust and blight, frost and hail,
prairie fires and blizzards, gophers and grasshoppers --but nothing could deter
them from their determination to reach their goal--A HOME ON LAND OFTHEIR OWN
IN A COUNTRY THAT GUARANTEED THEM FREEDOM AND EQUALITY.

In tribute to the hardy farm pioneers of Wishek's community we list the family
names of those whom we would honor. Any member of our younger generation
bearing one of these names should bear it with pride and cherish it as an
heritage from attributes possessed only by the true pioneer.

Conclusion

In closing this review of fifty years in Wishek's history, the Historical
Committee fully appreciates the inadequacy of the task which it has endeavored
to perform. Early records have been found to be fragmentary and much early data
unavailable. Undoubtedly, much has been omitted which should have been
included. However, many interesting events and incidents are to be found in the
individual biographies which follow in this book. The Committee acknowledges
with thanks the information furnished by Gottlieb Nickisch, A. P. Guy, F. D.
McCartney, J. C. Nippolt, Wm. G. Bailey, Sr., and Mrs. D. G. Nickisch. Grateful
thanks are also due to Mrs. Nina Farley Wishek, the widow of our founder and
namesake, who graciously made available to us such pertinent data as is
contained in her interesting and informative history of McIntosh County--a
volume entitled "Along the Trails of Yesterday."

Wishek in Its Early Days

                        Church History

HISTORY OF ST. PATRICK'S CATHOLIC CHURCH

St. Patrick's Catholic Church's present structure was Wishek's first school
house. The late John J. Doyle is credited with the purchase of this building.
Through his efforts the building was remodeled and given to the congregation
for a church. Credit is also given to Mr. John H. Wishek, Sr., who kindly
donated two lots for this purpose. The church was dedicated in 1925.

Previous to this, services were held in the school house, city hall or in the
private homes of the members.

Donations were made and collections obtained for the purpose of purchasing the
pews, altar, linens and books needed for the services.

Some of the earlier members of this mission were Mr. and Mrs. John J. Doyle and
family, Mr. and Mrs. Matt Steil, Mrs. Derrick, Mr. and Mrs. Edward Scheller and
family, Mrs. H. E. Kusler and Children, Mrs. J. W. Ackerman, Frank Koehler, the
Griffin Family and Mr. and Mrs. J. C. Nippolt.

The mission's first priest was Rev. Father George Fritz of Napoleon, and the
present priest is Rev. Father Vincent Wiederholt of St. Anthony's Parish,
Napoleon, No. Dak.

The present membership numbers 65.

HISTORY OF ST. LUKE'S LUTHERAN CHURCH
Wishek, N. Dak.

With the coming of the railroad to Wishek, many new families arrived to seek a
new home. Among them were several families of the Lutheran faith. Because the
number was small at first, these pioneers had to be content to practice their
faith by themselves. This they did by diligent use of the Bible, their old
German Hymnals, and occasionally a book of printed sermons which they had
brought from their old homes in South Russia. Later several families began
gathering in private homes on Sundays for a "Lese Gottesdienst," in other words
a service, conducted by one of the men, in which hymns were sung, prayers
spoken, and a sermon read from a prized sermon book.

It wasn't long until itinerant Lutheran pastors, sent out by the Iowa Synod to
look up such small isolated groups, found these people and began to give them
an occasional service. In 1904 we find that Pastor A. Wenninger came from
Ashley to conduct several services in private homes near Wishek.

The early history of the congregation takes place northeast of Wishek. Services
were held, alternately, in the school near the John S. Wolff farm and the
school near the Jacob M. Frey farm. On January 1, 1905, St. Luke's congregation
was organized. Pastor E. Moeckel, who had been serving the Zeeland parish,
approximately 20 miles southwest of Wishek, was called as the first pastor. It
is interesting to note, that his salary was set at $125 per year. Because the
parish was small, and because he had a family to support, we find that he took
up a homestead north of Wishek and also taught one of the country schools.

The first members were: Peter Pfeifle, John S. Wolff, George Wolff, Andreas
Pfeifle and Philip P. Mueller.

More families moved into the region, and hence the congregation assembled on
September 1, 1907, to discuss the building of a church. The committee, which
was to investigate possibilities consisted of John S. Wolff, chairman, George
Ziegenhagel, Jakob Helm and Jakob Perman. They were instructed to find a
central location for the church building, as well as determine the costs and
plans for such an undertaking.

On May 15, 1908, the group reached the conclusion that services should be held
in Frey's school house, and somewhere in the town of Wishek. Two months later
eight families who lived in the vicinity of Frey's school house decided that it
would be too far to come to Wishek for services, so the group divided into two
congregations. The one group formed the Jakobus Church, and the other group
concentrated their efforts on Wishek. From then on St. Luke's carried on its
work in Wishek. On January 3, 1909, Pastor Moeckel presented his resignation to
the congregation, which was accepted.

For several months the congregation had the services of Pastor W. Schroeder. He
was succeeded by Pastor John Mayer. Under his guidance, the congregation
continued its building plans, for they voted to build a church at a cost that
was not to exceed $2,000.00. At this meeting, we find that 7 individuals
pledged a sum of $475.00 toward the building of the church. In September, the
congregation was incorporated under the laws of the State of North Dakota.
Pastor Mayer remained until 1911.

Pastor John Willms was then called from Wartburg Seminary in Dubuque, Iowa, to
be the next pastor. During his time the first parsonage was acquired. By 1913
thirty-five families belonged to the congregation. The following summer, the
group had grown to such an extent, that a new addition was added to the
original church building. The original building was moved 6 feet south and 10
feet east before the addition was begun. The new addition measured 32 feet by
26 feet and cost the group $3,081.00. The first electric lights were installed
in 1917. During this same year, we find the group who had established a
congregation near the Frey school disbanded and returned to the Wishek group.

Pastor Willms, too, was the first pastor of the group to conduct an English
service in St. Luke's church. This occurred on March 10, 1918. When Pastor
Willms accepted a call to Monticello, Iowa, in the fall of 1918 the
congregation had grown to 60 families.

The longest period of ministry by any one Pastor begins in January of 1919 when
Pastor A. F. Freymann was installed as pastor. Under his guidance, many of the
old difficulties were ironed out. In 1926, it was found necessary to add still
another addition to the church, this time a wing on the south side. This was
done after original plans to erect a Sunday School building had been revoked by
the congregation. In this same year, too, the parish made plans to build a new
parsonage. This was done during the absence of Rev. Freymann, who was spending
some time visiting his relatives in Germany. This house still serves as the
parsonage today. On the building committee for the parsonage we find such names
as: Peter Pfeifle, John S. Wolff, Gottlieb Nickisch, Jacob Frank, Adam Kuebler,
Daniel Schnabel and Jacob Eichhorn.

1926 seems to have been a very active year for the group. On October 27 of this
year the first organization had its beginning. It was the Ladies Aid.

After 17 years of service, Pastor Freymann left the congregation to return to
the Zeeland Parish, which he had served before coming to Wishek. This occurred
on January 1, 1936. The congregation now had a membership of 100 families.

Pastor Freymann was succeeded by Pastor G. C. Landgrebe in the spring of 1936.
During his stay regular English services were begun, and the Sunday School,
too, introduced the English language into its program. An organization for
young people, bearing the name "Luther League" was organized. When Pastor
Landgrebe left the congregation in 1938 to become the full-time President of
the Dakota District of the American Lutheran Church, the group had grown to 131
families.

Several months later, Pastor L. R. Schulz arrived from Belle Fourche, S. Dak.,
to become the new shepherd of St. Luke's Congregation. Under his guidance we
find the group growing to the number of 165 families. When he accepted the call
to Regent, N. Dak., the congregation experienced the shortage of pastors then
existent. One year passed before another Pastor was secured.

During this year of vacancy, credit must be given to the congregation and its
church council for the manner in which they continued to work together.

On June 11, 1944, Pastor T. F. Doyen of Wartburg Seminary was installed as the
new Pastor. Since his coming regular Sunday Services have been conducted in
both languages. The English Service is conducted at 9:00 a.m. and the German
Service begins at 11:00 a.m.

In 1947 two new organizations were begun, namely the Women's Missionary Society
and a Brotherhood.

At this writing the congregation has a membership of 835 baptized members, of
which 600 are communicant members. There are 238 family units in the group.

Present members of the Church Council are: D. D. Aipperspach, Henry A. Mueller,
Philip Bender, Jacob Just, Fred Frank, M. G. Frank, Ferdinand Ketterling, Edwin
Wolff, and Fred Thurn.

A new building program for the church has begun. A planning committee,
consisting of George Wolff, Fred Thurn, August Eckman, C. P. Ritter, Philip
Bender, and the pastor, has been working on a building plan to present to the
congregation. As soon as conditions warrant, work will be begun on this project.

St. Luke's congregation has enjoyed a steady growth until now. May the good
Lord, who has guided her in the past, continue to bless her that she may bring
forth much fruit.

Organizations of St. Luke's Lutheran Church

LADIES AID--On Oct. 27, 1926, nine women of the church gathered to form St.
Luke's Ladies Aid. The first president was Mrs. Fred Wolff, and the first
secretary was Mrs. John Rau. Since its organization, this group meets every
second Wednesday of the month. During the first years of its existence, the
women met in private homes, but soon the membership increased to that extent,
that it was found necessary to meet in the church basement. Except for a
temporary decline in membership during the depression years, the organization
has grown steadily. Today 60 women belong to the group. The purpose of the
organization is to further the work of the church, both at home and abroad, and
to provide fellowship among the women of the church. Present officers are: Mrs.
Wm. Huber, president; Mrs. Fred Thurn, vice-president; Mrs. Jacob Schmitt and
Mrs. Amos Brinkman, secretaries; and Mrs. Wm. Fiechtner, treasurer.

LUTHER LEAGUE--In September, 1936, the young people of the church gathered in
the church basement and organized a young people's organization, to be known as
the Luther League. The first officers were: Tillie Wolff, president; Irene
Perman, vice-president; Norman Ritter, secretary; and Lourine Bender,
treasurer. In August, 1939, this group was privileged to be the hosts for the
first annual Convention of the Dakota District Luther League. The group has
always taken a lively interest in Federation Rallies, Conventions, and Bible
Camp. A fine representation has always made its appearance at these outside
activities. The Luther League, in past years, has sponsored Watch-Night
services on New Year's Eve and the Sunrise service on Easter Sunday. Present
officers are: Stella Eissinger, president; Marilyn Brinkman, vice-president;
Florence Brown, secretary; and John Pfau, treasurer.

WOMEN'S MISSIONARY SOCIETY--This group was organized in the fall of 1947 to
promote a greater interest in the mission activities of the church and to
provide another means of fellowship among the women of our church. Officers
are: Mrs. F. W. Roehm, president; Mrs. Elmo Nickisch, vice-president; Mrs. A.
W. Pfeifle, secretary; and Mrs. Helmond Wiest, treasurer.

BROTHERHOOD--Because the men of the church have seldom been organized into
groups, a Brotherhood was deemed necessary in 1947. This group of twenty-four
members meets every second Monday evening of the month. The officers are: V. D.
Rice, president; F. W. Roehm, vice-president; A. W. Pfeifle. secretary; and
Helmond Wiest, treasurer.

SUNDAY SCHOOL--Last, but by far not least, comes the Sunday School. This is the
oldest organization within the congregation. In early years several men were
elected by the congregation to teach the youngsters. Only two or three men
were needed at first. Today there are 250 enrolled in our Sunday School in
thirteen classes. The teachers are: Mrs. Arnold Brown, Irene Frank, Mrs. Wm.
Huber Mrs. T. F. Doyen, Jacob Schmitt, Mrs. Otto Walth, Marvin Ketterling, Mrs.
H. H. Schmitt, Marilyn Weber, Luella Vossler, Dr. T. C. Ritter, Mrs. F. W. Roehm,
Mrs. Roy Mindt, and Pastor Doyen.

CHOIRS--St. Luke's at present has two choirs, the Ladies Aid Choir of
approximately 16 members, and a Mixed Choir of 35 members.

HISTORY OF ST. JOHN'S CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH

The St. John's Congregational Church was organized February 27, 1912, under
the leadership of Rev. Herman Seil, Pastor-at-Large, and Rev. Louis Ebertz, who
acted as secretary at the meeting. Thirteen members met and organized the
church. They were: Mr. and Mrs. Philip G. Mueller, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Hilscher,
Miss Mathilda Hilscher, Mr. and Mrs. F. J. Sayler, Mr. and Mrs. Adolph
Hochhalter, Mr. and Mrs. John Junkert, Sr. Of these charter members three
remain on the active membership list and honor roll, namely, Mr. and Mrs. F. J.
Sayler and Mrs. Jacob Hochhalter, Jr.

The church was served from the Fredonia Parish, except from 1925 to 1931 when
Rev. F. J. Wacker resided here. From 1932 to 1936 our church was again served
from the Fredonia Parish, Rev. H. Hoersch being the pastor. Ministers who
served this church are as follows: Rev. L. Ebertz, Rev. Zissler, Rev. T. H.
Pfenning, Rev. P. Hedinger, Rev. John Meer, Rev. F. J. Wacker, Rev. H. Hoersch,
Rev. H. E. Wilske, Rev. Edward Schmidt, and Rev. John Hoersch. Since the fall
of 1937 the church has had its own pastor.

During these years ?0O children were christened, 219 received religious
instruction and were confirmed, 38 weddings and 54 deaths were recorded. The
church membership grew from 13 members to 169 members.

Two ministers, Rev. Edward Sayler and Rev. Elmer Bettenhausen call this their
home church, because they received their inspiration to enter the high calling
here. These are some of the visible records, the invisible service cannot be
calculated by mortal man.

At the present time there are 157 members enrolled in Sunday School, a Ladies
Aid of 23 members, 36 Christian Endeavor members, and an active choir.

The church building which was erected in 1912,is still standing and serving its
purpose after 36 years of service.

Sunday School

The congregation would not really fulfill its duties without a Sunday School.
Ever since it was organized, in 1912, it has seen to it that its children
received proper religious education. At present there are 157 members enrolled.
There are six classes and 5 teachers. First class--Mrs. Arthur Sayler; Second
class--Mrs. Edward Klein; Third class--Mrs. Herbert Enzi; Fourth class--Mr.
Edward Klein; Fifth and Sixth classes, which are taught in the German
language--Mr. Henry Sayler.

The Sunday School officers are as follows:

Henry Sayler .................. Treasurer
Mrs. Hulda Klein .............. Pianist
Margaretha Gall ............... Assistant

Congregational Ladies Aid

In the spring, April of 1936, a few of the ladies of the Congregational church
met for the purpose of organizing a Ladies Aid. The following officers were
elected: Mrs. Barbara Gaier, president; Mrs. Henry Gall, Sr., vice-president;
Mrs. Henry Sayler, secretary, and Mrs. Adolph Gall, treasurer.

The purpose of this organization is spiritual enrichment for us and those
around us, to spread Christianity and to help out in the church and Sunday
school whenever we are needed.

The books of the Bible are studied and different themes worked out.

Different ways of raising money were by holding chicken suppers, serving at

Our oldest member, who is also a charter member, is Mrs. Magdaline Sprenger.

There are 23 active members of which nine were charter members. They are: Mrs.
