St. John's Congregational Christian Endeavor

The young people of the St. John's Congregational Church of Wishek, North
Dakota, met and organized a Christian Endeavor on October 17, 1937. The meeting
was opened by Rev. Wilske. The officers elected were as follows:

    President ......... Mrs. Lillian Sayler
    Vice-President .... Mr. Emil Junkert
    Secretary ......... Christina Klein
    Treasurer ......... Ellen Bettenhausen

As the years went on the membership increased.

The different cards used are Get Well, Sympathy. Congratulations, and
Graduation. The total number of cards sent to people since it was organized are
163 cards.

The Christian Endeavor puts on special programs for all the holidays. Sometimes
socials are held in the church basement.

The Christian Endeavor at times sends donations to such organizations as the
Red Cross, Annuity Fund, Crippled Home at Jamestown, Orphanage Home at Fargo
and Students Aid Fund at Yankton.

The Christian Endeavor also has a library and have some very interesting books.

The officers now presiding in the year of 1948 are as follows:

    President ......... Edward Klein
    Vice-President .... Elmer Brosy
    Treasurer ......... Mrs. Edward Klein
    Secretary ......... Laura Eissinger

HISTORY OF WISHEK BAPTIST CHURCH

In the year 1885 John Brokofsky left Russia with his family and he landed in
McIntosh County on the 7th day of September of the same year. Other families
arrived the following year and settled in this same territory. In October,
1886, with 15 charter members, the church, which is now the Wishek Baptist
Church, was organized. At this meeting Mr. Johann Brokofsky was elected
chairman and Mr. Wm. Koth deacon. The church was built four miles west of
Danzig, N. D., and was then known as the Danzig German Baptist Church.

This group conducted their own worship services until 1888 when on the l9th day
of July the Rev. E. Matzke, of Spring Creek, S. D., paid them a visit. He was
extended a call to become their pastor which he accepted. He became the
minister of the congregation on the 28th day of July and served until the 30th
of May, 1892. During his ministry the church grew rapidly in membership. Many
were brought to the saving knowledge of Christ and were baptized into the
fellowship of the Church. This group then reached out into the regions round
about and soon had a mission station and a membership of 195 baptized members.
Not always was the going so smooth. Through moving away as well as through the
interpretation of Scripture, disruptions shook the very foundations of the
group until a membership of 80 was left. Undaunted, with missionary zeal, they
worked on and soon had a membership again of 133 souls. There was an ever
increasing effort put forth until 6 stations were connected with this same
organization.

Some families moved into Wishek and in the year 1906 they began gathering in
homes and in the school for worship. Then at a meeting in the Danzig church,
held on March 20, 1908, it was decided to accept Wishek as a new Mission field.
This same meeting authorized a building committee to construct a frame
structure, 24x 32 ft., with steeple entrance and a choir loft addition, at an
approximate cost of $2,000.00. The land for this church was donated by John H.
Wishek, Sr. The building committee composed of: Jacob Herr, Jr., Konrad Kogler,
Jacob Roeszler, and William Koth, took steps immediately for the erection of
this building so that it was possible to hold the dedication service on October
18, 1908. The speakers for this occasion were Rev. H. G. Bens and Rev. George
Burgdorf. The charter members of the church were: Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Roeszler;
Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Herr, Jr.; Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Herr, Sr.; Mr. and Mrs. John
Herr; Mr. and Mrs. August W. Herr, and Konrad Kogler.

This membership, along with friends and followers, raised the sum of $890.00.
The mother church at Danzig gave a grant of $305.00; an offering at Eureka, S.
D., netted $40.00 and a dedicatory offering at the dedication service netted
another sum of $265.00. A small loan was obtained from Denominational
Headquarters. This loan and the balance due were paid in full a short time
later.

In 1912 the church of Danzig undertook a division of its stations. Their
property at Ashley was assigned to the present Ashley church; their property
near Venturia, namely the Kassel church to the present Venturia church. The
stations Wishek and Beaver Creek were retained. At this division the Rev. August
Herringer accepted the extended call to become the minister with residence in
Wishek. That same year the present parsonage was built.

At a regular business meeting on May 20th, 1930, the name of the church was
changed from the Danzig German Baptist church to German Baptist Church of
Wishek. The name has again been changed to Wishek Baptist Church.

As the attendance and membership grew, it was on several occasions proposed to
erect a new building. The motion finally carried in 1935. Notwithstanding the
drouth and depression of the time, the membership strongly felt the need of a
new building so that in 1934 a building committee was named and they laid plans
for a new building. The committee, composed of Rev. B. W. Krentz, manager and
chairman; Ernest W. Herr, treasurer; Edward Herr, secretary; Wm. Stading, E. W.
Bonnet, W. H. Mehlhaff and Christian J. Herr, laid plans for the present
basement. This structure measures 44 ft. x 64 ft. with an addition for a
furnace room, and was erected at a cost of $6,500.00. This building was
dedicated in October, 1935, with the Rev. August Herringer and Rev. Willie
Luebeck as the guest speakers. Plans are getting well under way for the
building of the church proper on the present basement.

The following pastors served the Wishek church from the time of its
organization at Danzig: Rev. E. Matzke from 1888 until 1892; Rev. Marks,
1892-1894; Rev. Hermann, 1897-1899; Rev. Edward Wolf, 1900-1901; Rev. Christian
Bischof, 1903-1909; Rev. August Herringer, 1910-1927; Rev. C. M. Knapp,
1928-1932; Rev. B. W. Krentz, 1932-1935; Rev. Albert Itterman, 1936-1941; Rev.
J. C. Gunst, 1942-1944; Rev. Arthur R. Weisser, 1944-.

This Church today has a membership of 229 baptized members. In 1941 the station
at Napoleon was set aside as a church by itself. The present leaders of the
Wishek Baptist Church are: J. J. Meidinger and J. J. Kramer, deacons; Mrs.
Edward Herr and Mrs. J. J. Meidinger, deaconesses; Edward Herr, treasurer;
William Stading, secretary; and Arthur R. Weisser, minister.

In the sixty-two years of existence this Church has contributed $44,547.38 to
Missions and $111,999.46 for local work and expenses; a grand total of
$156,546.84. Besides this the Ladies Mission Society has contributed liberally,
the figures of which are not available at this time.

A report of this kind could not be complete without paying tribute to Rev.
August Herringer, who ministered to this church for 17 years. Much credit is
due him for the prospering of this Church for he labored untiringly and under
his leadership the church grew. He was a man with a vision for Christ and His
cause, and upon this field he spent the best years of his life. In 1912 when
the division of the Danzig field came, he accepted the call to serve the new
field at Wishek. It was during this time that the present parsonage was built.
Through his ardent zeal a mission work was begun in Burnstad, N. D. and also in
Napoleon, N. D. On March 25, 1941, Napoleon became a self-supporting church.

During the 15th year of his services here, a special occasion was celebrated at
the church. It was the 15th year of his pastorate, the 25th year of his
services as a pastor and the 40th year of the existence of the Wishek Baptist
Church.

Rev. Herringer went to his eternal home in 1942. He was laid to rest in Salem,
Oregon. He was loved and honored as a good pastor and a true man of God by all
who knew him.

A word must be added in behalf of several organizations within the church. The
first of these will be our Wishek Women's Mission Society.

On July 22, 1914, seven women gathered in the Baptist parsonage to organize
into a Mission Society. The following were present: Mrs. John Schmierer, Mrs.
C. J. Herr, Mrs. August Herr, Mrs. Gottlieb Herr, Mrs. John Herr, Mrs. Jacob
Schmierer and Mrs. August Herringer. Mrs. John Schmierer was elected president,
Mrs. August Herringer, vice-president; Mrs. C. J. Herr, secretary; and Mrs.
Jacob Schmierer, treasurer.

The purpose and aim of the society is five-fold, namely: To acquaint itself
with Missions the world over; to foster the spirit of Christian Fellowship and
unity; to support and promote the work of the Church; to acquire Bible
knowledge; to visit the sick and help the poor.

In the 34 years of its existence the Society has had 100 names on their
membership list. Death claimed 14 of these, 37 moved away and 11 dropped out.
At present there are 38 active members. Meetings are held on the last Wednesday
of each month. No meetings have been missed since organization and
consequently, 408 consecutive meetings have been held. A total of 5,039 sick
calls have been made. This organization does itself proud by having contributed
the first $1,200.00 towards the building of the present church and are well
under way with a fund for the finishing of the church which we plan to build
soon.

This group has also always shown interest in worthwhile enterprises. Minutes of
a meeting in 1918 records the sale of fancy work and food as well as a public
supper. Half of the proceeds were given to the local chapter of the Red Cross.
In the past two years over 200 food and clothing packages have been sent to
Europe. Besides this there was 800 lbs. of food and clothing and $89.00 in
cash, to cover packaging and mailing of same, sent to our Denominational
Headquarters. A tidy sum was also contributed towards a Nurse's Training School
for our Cameroon mission field in Africa.

There are but two of the charter members left of this Society: Mrs. Gottlieb
Herr and Mrs. August Herr. The present officers are: Mrs. Edward Herr
president; Mrs. Emmanuel Herr, vice-president; Mrs. W. H. Mehlhaff, treasurer;
and Mrs. Emil Herr, secretary.

The Dorcas Society

A younger group, called The Dorcas Society, organized under the name of the
"Sunshine Club" in February, 1924. The first meeting was held at the home of
Eleanor Herringer Haas. There were five charter members: Eleanor Herringer
(Mrs. Abe Haas); Eleanor Herr (Mrs. Gerald Bailey); Margaret Herr (Mrs. Mel
Dirks); Priscilla Herr (Mrs. John Bartholomew); and Viola Stading (Mrs. Edward
Kautz).

        Baptist Ladies Aid Top Row, left to right: Mesdames C. J. Herr, Otto
        Woehl, Ed G. Herr, August W. Herr, A. R. Weisser, Vernon Herr,
        Gottfried Herr, Emil Herr, W. H. Mehlhaff, Henry Ebel. Second Row, left
        to right: Mesdames John Sieler, J. J. Kramer, Aaron Sukut, Fred Bender,

        Miller, Bill Stading.

On March 14, 1930, the Sunshine Club became an organization within the Baptist
Church. And on October 1, 1930, the name was changed to the Dorcas Society.
This Society now has a membership of 21 members with 45 honorary members.

The purpose of this Society is for spiritual and social upbuilding of its
members as well as for charitable and mission purposes. Its meetings are held
on the second Tuesday of each month.

This group maintains a varied program such as study of Mission Fields, Lives of
great Christian leaders, Bible, Hymns and their authors, religion in
Institutions, customs of various countries, and the study of Bible characters.

Contributions of money and clothing have been made to national charitable
institutions and relief organizations. Shut-ins are remembered as well as the
church.

During the war, bonds were purchased, and our service men remembered with gifts
and letters.

Newly elected officers are: Margaret Baum, president; Harriet Halverson,
vice-president; Irene Pfeifle, secretary; Nita Paul, treasurer; and Esther Herr
and Connie Herr. social secretaries.

The Sunday School

Our church maintains a well organized and well equipped Sunday School. There
are classes for all ages and in both the German and English languages. A vital
interest is shown in this branch of the church. Teachers meet monthly for
conference and study so that they might be more efficient to teach, instruct
and guide these lives entrusted to their care. Mr. Ernest W. Herr is the
efficient superintendent of the school and has been for the past 14 years.
Much credit is due him for the efficient school that we have.

HISTORY OF WISHEK REFORMED CHURCH

For many years the Reformed Wishek Parish consisted of three congregations,
Neudorf, Johannesthal and Wishek. The Nedorf congregation, 12 miles southwest
of Wishek, was organized in 1890. Most members came from the Kassel
congregation near Zeeland where a rural parsonage was located until 1908. The
Johannesthal congregation, 6 miles northwest of Wishek, was organized in 1905.
The congregations first accepted a Constitution and started to build a church.
On November 5th, 1916, the Wishek congregation was organized with seven
families: Christian Krein, Sr., Christian F. Krein Jacob C. Krein, John
Ackermann, Henry Becker, Fred Sprenger, and Karl Wacker.  In 1917 the
Wishek Church was built and dedicated on October 21 of that year. Until 1920 the
three congregations were served from Eureka, Ashley, Kassel and Artas. The
following pastors fulfilled organizing work and clerical dut1es and preached
the Gospel from 1890 until 1920: Frank Grether, U Reue, W. Feige, H. W.
Stienecker, W. H. Knierim, H. Niehaus, W. Landsiedel, Peter Bauer, Peter
DeBuhr, F. Aigner and M. Nuss. The first resident pastor of the Wishek Parish
was Rev. John Klundt, 1920-25.

In 1940 began the merger proceedings of the two rural congregations
Johannesthal and Neudorf with the Wishek congregation. These were completed in
1944. In Wishek the Ladies Aid Society was organized March 25, 1940. A church
basement was built and improvements made in the parsonage. The Wishek
congregation now has 138 confirmed and 35 unconfirmed members in 41 families,
60 children in the Sunday School. A Youth Fellowship Society has also been
organized.

When Rev. John Klundt terminated his pastoral services in 1925, Rev. A. A.
Depping became his successor from 1926 until 1929. From 1930-39 the parish was
served by Rev. Erich Kaempchen from Fullerton, N. D. Rev. John Bodenman began
his services on October 1, 1939, and is still serving the congregation at this
time.
                           --J. B.

HISTORY OF GRACE PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH

The organization of Grace Presbyterian Church was effected on Jan. 10, 1904, by
Rev. A. W. Wright, Pastor-at-Large for the Bismarck Presbytery. Its organization
was largely due to the efforts of Mr. and Mrs. A. P. Guy.  Mrs. Guy had
organized and conducted a Sunday School previous to the above date and it was
later incorporated as an adjunct of the church.  The first members of the
congregation were Mrs. E. F. McCargar, Mrs. A. P. Guy, Mrs. M. E. Pratt, Henry
P. Baker, Alexander Thompson, Mrs. Emma Thompson, Christina Weixel and Charles
St. John, with Mr. St. John as ruling Elder and Mrs. E. F. McCargar, H. P. Baker
and Alex. Thompson as Trustees.

In March, 1905, Mr. S. C. Benson came to the congregation as a student Pastor,
and served until about September 1st of that year. During his period of
service, plans were made and funds were raised for the construction of a church
building. On August 4, 1905, the contract was let to the Linton Construction
Co. for the erection of a concrete block structure and the building was erected
in September and October of 1905. Dedication services were conducted by Rev. W.
H. Hunter on January 28th, 1906.

The church has functioned continuously since its organization. Its activities
have been sporadic at times, due to the scarcity of ministers. The following is
a list of pastors who have served the local church at various periods during
its history: Rev. S. C. Benson, Rev. MacEchern, Rev. Palmer, Rev. N. Wood, Rev.
Joseph Gaston, Rev. F. G. Westfall, Rev. Robert Johnson, Rev. C. E. Curtis,
Rev. L. M. F. Jordan, Rev. H. C. Knock, Rev. J. W. Van Dyke, Rev. M. R.
DeForest and Rev. J. H. K. Moffett. Rev. J. Way Huey, Pastor-at-Large, also
called frequently and did much to keep the church functioning. A tribute is due
to Mr. James T. Lowers who was elected as ruling Elder in 1910. He continued as
such for thirty years until he moved to Illinois in 1940. The church is now
being served by student Pastors from the Jamestown College.

        Wishek Reformed Church Ladies Aid First Row, left to right: Mesdames
        Lydia Raile, Emanuel Kramer, Alvin Hochhalter, William Hoff, Leopold
        Hochhalter, John Link, Ben Eissinger, Ted Rueb. Second Row, left to
        right: Mesdames Henry Hochhalter, John Bodenman, Ted Boschee, Emil
        Boschee, Ed Wiest, Ferdinand Krein, Christ Kinzle.  Third Row, left to
        right: Mesdames August Vilhauer, Christ Dockter, John Sprenger, Fred
        Preszler, Christ F. Krein, John Stroh.

The Sunday School of this Church, organized by Mrs. A. P. Guy several years
before the erection of the church building in 1905, was discontinued in 1943.
In that year the congregation lost many of its families, who moved to other
places and went into defense factories for war work.

The Presbyterian Ladies Aid of the Grace Presbyterian Church was organized soon
after the church was organized. The only one charter member living today is
Mrs. Clara Bell Grant.

It has continued through the years as a helpful auxiliary of the church and is
still functioning, with a membership of nine.

HISTORY OF THE EVANGELICAL UNITED BRETHREN CHURCH

The early settlers were, as a rule, people who sought religious services, but
due to the lack of churches in that era many homes were opened to the people of
the various communities for religious services. Among the many homes was the
Martin Miller home where the first services of the Evangelical church were
held. During those days traveling ministers came to these homes and rendered
their services to those people who had no regular pastor. Many of these people
did not have a church of their own or did not as yet belong to a church body at
that time.

        Grace Presbyterian Church Ladies Aid Top Row, left to right: Mesdames
        Elizabeth Osborn, Eleanor Erbele, Ruth Meyer, Myrtice Scott, president
        of Aid. Lower Row, left to right: Mesdames Krien, Althea McClelland,
        Bernice Sayler. Members not pictured: Barbara Hockburger and Clarabell
        Grant.

The Evangelical church of Wishek had its birthplace in the Miller home in 1915,
under the capable leadership of the visiting pastor of Lehr, Rev. E. C. Oeder,
and Rev. William Suchow. Services were held in these premises for two years,
with nine potential families attending, and finally organized as a church in
February, 1916, under the leadership of Rev. C. A. Bremer.

There were many questions and plans in the minds of the people where to build
and how to meet the cost, but these problems soon vanished when the present
location was chosen and donated by Wishek and Co. The financial problems
vanished when the conference extension board gave a $1,000.00 loan and another
party a loan of $3,000.00.

The first minister to be assigned to this church was Rev. W. L. Martin in 1918,
who remained here until after the dedication of the church in May of that year.
The charter members of the church were the following: John Becker, Jacob Gable,
Christ Goll, Regina Gust, Jacob Holwagner, Bernard Kranzler, John Kurle, Martin
Miller, and John Stoller. Due to many difficulties and hardships Rev. Martin
left Wishek and this church was without a minister until the fall of 1919 when
Rev. Alex Gehring came here to be their pastor. During his pastorate the local
parsonage was the first building project to be undertaken with considerable
difficulty.

The membership of the church in 1919 was 45, and 1921 it was 84, and by 1931
the membership of the charge had grown to 163, that, however, included
Napoleon. A decline in membership came when the annual conference partitioned
Wishek and Napoleon as two stations.

The pastors that have served this church in the past 33 years are the
following: Rev. W. L. Martin, 1918-1919; Rev. A. Gehring, 1919-1923; Rev. R.
Bloedau, 1923-1924; Rev. William Storkman, 1924-1927; Rev. C. E. Bach,
1927-1929; Rev. E. K. Heimer, 1929-1933; Rev. Karl Hirning, 1933-1938; Rev. A.
H. Ermel, 1938-1947, and Rev. G. Eberhart, 1947 to date.

Organizations

The local Sunday School was organized in 1915 in the home of Martin Miller with
the attending families as the charter members. The present Sunday School roll
stands at 128 members.

The Evangelical League of Christian Endeavor was organized on September 29,
1933, under the supervision of Rev. Hirning, the following being the charter
members: Rev. and Mrs. Hirning, Anna Mueller, Rachel and Clarence Hirning,
Emanuel Fercho, Esther Fercho, Frieda Herr, Allene Herr, Ruth Fercho, Frieda
Miller Ben Schlenker, and Jake Schlenker. On August 19, 1945, the above named
organization changed its name, and adopted the present denominational name of
"The Evangelical Youth Fellowship," with a membership of twenty-six.

        Evangelical Ladies Aid First Row, left to right: Mesdames Fred Kautz,
        John V. Boschee, Edward Scharf, Fred Kallenberger, Gottlieb Fercho.
        Second Row, left to right: Mesdames Gottlieb Speidel, Gottfried Woehl,
        Edmond Ulmer, John Ottmar, John Mueller, B. B. Kaseman. Third Row, left
        to right: Mesdames Emanuel Fercho, John Kogler, John Bechtle, Gideon
        Eberhart, Reinhold Schaffer, Ben Hass, Henry Seefried. Member not
        pictured: Madam Fred Meier.

The Evangelical Ladies Aid was organized in 1938 under the supervision of Rev.
and Mrs. A. H. Ermel, with the following as the charter members: Mrs. A. H.
Ermel, Mrs. G. Fercho, Mrs. E. Ketterling, Mrs. J. Kogler and Mrs. M. Miller.
The present membership of the Ladies Aid stands at 19. On April 14, 1948, the
Ladies Aid organized into the Women's Society of World Service, with the
entire group as charter members.

The Christian Service Guild was organized on Dec. 12, 1947, under the
leadership of Mrs. G. Eberhart. The charter members being the following: Mrs.
Arthur Erbele, president; Mrs. Elmer Kaseman, vice-president; Mrs. Reuben
Kautz, secretary; Miss Theresa Mueller, treasurer; Miss Ruth Fercho,
corresponding secretary; Mrs. Gideon Eberhart.

On November 16, 1946, at a union session of the Evangelical and United Brethren
denominations, these two church bodies consummated this union into the
"Evangelical United Brethren Church."

                Scout History

HISTORY OF THE BOY SCOUTS

Wishek Troop No. 81, Boy Scouts of America, was first organized and received
its charter on May 31, 1926. The Presbyterian Church was its original sponsor
and Rev. C. E. Curtis was the first Scoutmaster, with T. Johansen as Assistant.
H. E. Timm, James Welsh and D. G. Nickisch, officials of the Church, were
commissioned as the first Troop Committee. Twelve boys were listed on the first
official registration.

        Lower Row, left to right: Marvin Raile, McCloyd Rudolf, Douglas Herr,
        Rodger Herr, Clifford Kramer. Center Row, left to right: Gerry Just,
        Farrell Rudolf, Delmer Kemmeth, Roland Sayler, Raymond Raile. Upper
        Row, left to right: Donald Pfau, Harold Mensing, Dean Dickson, Arden
        Doering. Left at Troop Flag: Nayle Gall. Right at Troop Flag: Harold
        Boschee. Color Bearer: Delane Pfiele. Scoutmaster, second top row: Vern
        C. Scott.

Rev. Curtis left in 1927 and T. Johansen was appointed as Scoutmaster. The
Troop was very active under his leadership from 1927 till 1933. Several members
of the Troop attended Summer Scout Camp at Chan Owapi during these years. May
22 to 24, 1931, found the entire troop in Camp at the Area Jamboree held in
Bismarck. The boys returned honors with a number of individual and Troop prizes,
won in competition with other Troops of the Area. During this period of
activity, outstanding Scouts attaining high ranks in Scouting, were:

   Allen Hoff   ....... Eagle Scout
   Lloyd Rigler ....... Eagle Scout
   Elbert Timm ........ Eagle Scout
   Donald Rigler ...... Life Scout
   George Grant ....... Star Scout

In 1934, Eagle Scout Elbert Timm represented the Area Council at Regional Eagle
Scout Camp in Itasca State Park in Minnesota.

With the departure of Mr. Johansen from Wishek, the Scouting activity waned.

However in 1942, the Troop again became active under the leadership of Rev. J.
H. K. Moffett. The Wishek Association of Commerce became sponsors for the
reactivated Troop. When Rev. Moffett was transferred to a new Pastorate, Vern
C. Scott was appointed Scoutmaster. The troop is at present actively
functioning under his leadership.

High ranking Scouts of the present troop are:

   Arden Doering ....... Star Scout
   Harold Mensing ...... Star Scout

The Troop has attended summer Scout Camp at Chan Owapi during the recent years.

The Troop has attended Camp-O-Rees and Rallies from which the troop has brought
back contest awards and helpful ideas.

THE WISHEK GIRL SCOUT TROOPS

The first Girl Scout troop was organized in 1929 under the leadership of Miss
Alice Kittelson (Mrs. Arthur Weber, rural Lidgerwood), upper grade teacher,
assisted by Mrs. J. W. Ackerman, Mrs. H. E. Timm and Mrs. E. W. Davis as
committee women.

The schoolroom was used as the meeting place of the original troop consisting
of the following girls: Eleanor Pudwill, Blanche Bailey, Ann Elizabeth
Alexander, Irene Krein, Bessie Pudwill and Harriet Rott.

Since 1933 the Girl Scout Troops have been under the sponsorship of the
American Legion Auxiliary of the Fred Kelley Post No. 87.

Fundamentals in the program are: Girl Scouting begins with the individual girl
and her development through group experience. From the age of seven through
high school, Girl Scouting aims to show what she has already learned and how to
use this in a practical way, to encourage her to develop abilities and
appreciations as yet untrained, and most of all to help her get along with
others, to develop a strong character, a healthy attitude toward life, and an
enduring set of values by working in ten different fields and earning various
badges.

On February 1, 1945, Miss Bonnie Bovee, Special Field Advisor for the Hiawatha
Girl Scout Portage with headquarters in St. Paul, reorganized and expanded our
Scouting program, adding two more troops: Intermediate Scouts and Brownies. The
council now consisted of sixteen women.

To date our Scouting organization consists of the following troops and leaders:

Troop One under the leadership of Mrs. Walter Doyle and Mrs. August Eckman
includes fifteen girls between the ages of twelve and sixteen. Officers for the
troop are Florence Rau, president; Loretta Kramer, secretary; Erna Ketterling,
treasurer; Marlys Rae Eckman, reporter.

Troop Two, whose leaders include Mrs. Ben Meidinger and Mrs. Wm. Hoff, has
twenty-two girls between the ages of ten and twelve. Officers for the troop are
Loretta Schmitt, president; Florence Ketterling, secretary; and Donna Just,
treasurer.

The original Brownie Troop was under the leadership of Mrs. T. F. Doyen and
Mrs. H. E. Timm and included the following sixteen girls: Phyllis Gall, Sylvia
Jean Gohl, Idella Hiller, Darlene Ketterling, Florence Ketterling, Yvonne
Miller, Marilyn Moeckel, Eileen Pfahl, Volverine Rudolf, Ramona Schaeffer,
Lorretta Schmitt, Darlene Wanner, Luetta Wanner, Janice Wanner, Jonna Wishek
and Eileen Grace Woehl.

At the present time the Brownie Troop 1 consists of sixteen girls between the
ages of eight and ten under the leadership of Mrs. John H. Wishek and Mrs.
Herbert Ottmar. Troop officers are: Nina Wishek, president; Marilyn Schaeffer,
secretary; and Marvelene Hochhalter, treasurer.

Brownie Troop Two was organized in February, 1948, and includes six girls
between the ages of seven and eight. The leaders are Mrs. Ivar Knapp and Mrs.
Emil Herr.

HISTORY OF THE WISHER CIVIC LEAGUE

On January 16, 1920, a group of ladies met at the home of Mrs. J. Theurer and
organized Wishek's first women's club, under the presidency of Mrs. J. J.
Doyle. The following year with nine charter members and Mrs. E. P. Walker as
president the Wishek Civic League was federated with the National Federation of
Women's Clubs and has continued that affiliation to the present time. In
choosing the name "Wishek Civic League" the early organizers had in view a
group of united women who would use their time and talents in making Wishek a
pleasant, interesting, and desirable community center. The history of the
Club's activities are replete with community service.

        First Row, left to right: Lourine Rice, Sarah Hofer, Phyllis Doyle,
        Irene Pfeifle. Second Row, left to right: Grace Krein, Mildred
        Barrette, Elsie Pfeifle, Doris Weisser, Frieda Nickisch, Juanita Faul.
        Third Row, left to right: Luella Roehm, Esther Doyen, Wilma Gall, Lydia
        Boettcher, Ansie Knapp, Kathryn Herr, Esther Herr. Not pictured: Ethyl
        Enzi, Anne Hoff, and Mrs. A. A. Whittemore.

The Wishek Civic League was the establisher and for many years the maintainer
of the City Library. The Club raised funds for and purchased a Grand piano,
placing it in the city auditorium for the school children and the general
public.

The records of the years show contributions to all the worthy charities who
have asked for financial help. Club members have sewed for the Red Cross and
for the Florence Crittenden Home. Donations of books and pictures have been
made to the school rooms of the public school.

In their aim to create a friendly spirit in the school relationships, the Club
members annually sponsored a party evening for the faculty members. Members'
husbands, and children's groups were entertained on several occasions.

The Club history reveals an active interest and participation in projects for
community betterment. Trees have been planted. Needy families have been aided.
The Club has stood foremost in the city in local health education through the
sponsoring of clinics.

In addition to all the above mentioned activities which have occupied the
Club's time, the members have maintained, since organization, a high quality
literary program at each meeting. Music, poetry, good books, subjects of
historical interest and geographical information have high-lighted many
inspirational evenings.

History is the recording of the past, and out of that past come the source
springs which launch our activities for the present and for the future. Pioneer
spirits live on in the full blown flowers which have sprouted from the infinite
seeds of first ideals.

                American Legion

FRED KELLE POST. NO. 87, OF THE AMERICAN LEGION

After World War I Veterans in towns and cities throughout the United States
started organizing the American Legion. The Local Organization was named after
Fred Kelle, who lost his life in that War. The Post carried on with sometimes
only enough members to hold their charter. Men of the World War II have
strengthened the membership and will carry on the high ideals of the Legion.
From 1919 to 1948, inclusive, the following men were elected as Post Commanders
and served in the order named:

H. T. Holz              John Weber, Jr.
John W. Ackerman        Christ J. Schock
F. R. Duerenburger      G. S. Boettcher
F. E. Ackermann         Christ C. Huber
H. H. Nolte             John H. Wishek, Jr.
T. Johansen             H. E. Lemke
J. C. Larson            Edwin T. Brost
E. P. Treick            John M. Doyle
Andrew Schlenker        Grant J. Hofer

HISTORY OF THE AMERICAN LEGION AUXILIARY UNIT TO FRED KELLE POST #87,
DEPT. OF N. D.

On September 28, 1927, the wives, sisters and daughters of the members of the
Fred Kelle Post met at the home of Mrs. E. P. Walker to organize an Auxiliary.
Mrs. A. G. Porter of Edgley, N. D., District Committeewoman, very ably assisted
us. Mrs. J. W. Ackerman was elected President and Mrs. E. P. Walker, Secretary.
The remaining officers being left open until the next meeting. It was decided
to meet at the home of Mrs. Ackerman on October 7, 1927, to complete the
organization. At this meeting the election of officers was completed. Mrs. T.
Johansen was elected vice-president, Mrs. E. P. Treick, treasurer; Mrs. Emil
Herr, chaplain; and Mrs. Nolte, historian. Besides the officers there were six
other members whose names are on the charter, namely: Mrs. John H. Wishek, Jr.,
Mrs. Fred Roehm, Mrs. Ivan Maerklein, Mrs. John C. Larson, Mrs. F. R.
Duernberger and Miss Adeline Herr (now Mrs. Art Nickisch).

        Back Row, left to right: Mesdames Christ Ruff, Otto Brandt, J. G.
        Ritter, Henry Vossler, John Fandrich, E. P. Pfeifle, Raymond Pfeifle,
        Armand Pfeifle, John Weber, John Wishek. Third Row, left to right:
        Mesdames Frank Roehm, Christ Ritter, Philip Burlack, John Ackerman, Gus.
        Boettcher, Emil Herr, John Rott, C. C. Huber, George Meyer. Third Row,
        left to right: Mesdames Lydia Raile, J. J. Kramer, J. W. Hofer, Ferd.
        Krein, Rubin Rueb, C. F. Krein, Wilbur Ammon. Front Row, left to right:
        Misses Jonna Wishek, Joan Herr, Virginia Wishek, Roberta Wishek, Loretta
        Kramer, Nina Wishek. Members not pictured: Mesdames Ida Alexander, Theo.
        Brandt, John M. Doyle, T. Johnansen, George Scharf, C. J. Schock, A. A.
        Whittemore, Misses Lillian Wishek, Joan Weber, Arlene Kramer.

The aims and purposes of the organization: "To uphold and defend the
Constitution of the United States of America; to maintain law and order; to
foster and perpetuate a one hundred percent Americanism; to contribute to the
accomplishment of the aims of the American Legion; to consecrate our
association by our devotion to mutual helpfulness."

Rehabilitation, Child Welfare, Community Service and Youth Conservation have
always been closest to our hearts. In 1929 we adopted Lloyed Thorson, a
six-year-old boy of New Rockfort, N. D., the son of a veteran who left his
family. The mother not being well, several Auxiliary units assisted her to
supply school clothing for her four children, of which Lloyed, the youngest,
was our care. We continued to help him until 1942 when he became
self-supporting. Our biggest efforts at present are devoted to the sponsoring
of the Wishek Girl Scout Troops.

We've had three Gold Star Mothers of World War I as members Mrs. Jakob Kelle,
after whose son, Fred Kelle, the local post is named; Mrs. August Sukut and
Mrs. Frederick Meidinger. Mrs. J. J. Kramer is a Gold Star Mother of World War
II.

We lost three members through death, six dropped out, twelve moved away and
forty-two are on our membership list today. Four of our twelve charter members
are still members, Mrs. J. W. Ackerman, Mrs. J. H. Wishek, Jr., Mrs. Emil Herr
and Mrs. T. Johansen of Enderlin, N. D. The latter always kept her membership
here.

The officers serving the unit at this time are: Mrs. Ruben Rueb, president;
Mrs. Emil Herr, vice-president; Mrs. Gust Boettcher, secretary; Mrs. J. W.
Hofer, treasurer; and Mrs. C. P. Ritter, chaplain-historian.

                Lodges

INDEPENDENT ORDER OF ODD FELLOWS

Wishek Lodge No. 99, I.O.O.F., was chartered by the Grand Lodge of North Dakota
on March 15, 1906. The charter members and first officers were John T. Moore,
R. C. Miles, LaMotte Miles, W. C. Kennedy, Wm. C. Soper, and R. H. Gilman.

The first members initiated by degree teams from Lodges at Oakes and Edgeley at
the first meeting held on April 4th, 1906, were as follows: M. E. Pratt, H. E.
Wolf, Gottlieb Nickisch, R. J. Bailey, George Grant, J. Miles, J. H. Krien,
Fred N. Gillis, S. P. Obershaw, John Lundberg, Fay Harding, Peter Schnor, D. G.
Nickisch, W. T. Best, J. E. Monroe, H. D. Piper, P. T. Kretschmar, M. A.
Godfrey, W. J. Forrest, W. S. Fulmer and J. W. Hofer.

Since its organization the Lodge has functioned continuously and been very
active. Meetings are held every Thursday evening. During the forty-two years of
its existence, many of the men holding membership have passed away, while
others have withdrawn. Those now holding membership who may be classed as
Veterans are D. G. Nickisch, H. E. Timm, Nath Auerbach, Wm. H. Bettenhausen, O.
C. Bettenhausen, J. W. Ackerman, F. W. Roehm and R. O. Greiser.

During the years, one member of Wishek Lodge No. 99 has advanced to positions
of prominence in Odd Fellowship in the State of North Dakota. He is H. E. Timm,
the oldest member of the Lodge still residing in Wishek. In 1923, he was
appointed to the seven-man Board of Trustees, which operates and supervises the
Odd Fellows Home of North Dakota, where aged and indigent Odd Fellows and
Rebekahs may spend their sunset years. Mr. Timm has been elected to said Board
continuously since his appointment and is now completing his twenty-fifth year
of service as a Trustee and is the senior member. In 1937, he was elected to
the position of Grand Master, the highest office in Odd Fellowship in the State
and served his year as such. In 1940, he was elected to the office of Grand
Secretary of the Order in North Dakota, with offices at Devils Lake. This
position he filled on a part-time basis for six and one-half years until
January 1st, 1947, when he was obliged to resign, on the advice of his doctor
and because of the illness of Mrs. Timm. Wishek Lodge is indeed highly honored
in having one of its members rise to such prominence in the Order.

From 1906 till 1929 the Lodge held its meetings in the hall of the First State
Bank building. In 1928 a movement was started for a Lodge home with ample room
for the increased membership. Committees were appointed to work out plans. A
suitable location on the northeast corner of Block 6 was purchased from the
Wishek Investment Company. The Building Committee consisted of the following
members: Fred Thurn, M. N. DeVoix, George Roehm, A. F. Hoff, John Bronner and E.
P. Treick. The Finance Committee were J. T. Moore and Toralf Johansen. Various
methods were used to raise funds. Members bought Building Certificates. Much of
the work was done by Lodge members. During the summer of 1929 a one-story
concrete building, size 24 x 60, with full basement, was constructed. It has
recently been modernized with a club room for members, in the basement.

Wishek Lodge and its building are real community assets and all those who have
labored to make them realities, can look back on their efforts with justifiable
pride.

The Past Grands of Wishek Lodge No. 99 now in membership are D. G. Nickisch, H.
E. Timm, Wm. H. Bettenhausen, R. O. Greiser, O. W. Bettenhausen, F. W. Roehm,
J. W. Ackerman, Fred Thurn, Gideon P. Herr, W. H. Mehlhaff, John H. Wishek, A.
R. Krein, Adolph Gall, R. W. Pfau, Elmo Nickisch, Walter J. Doyle, Ferdinand
Ketterling, and H. M. Larson.

Wishek Lodge has a present membership of 96 with a goal of 100 members during
1948. Meetings are held regularly every Thursday evening. Present officers are
Ivar Knapp, Noble Grand; Enoch Stroh, Vice Grand; Vern C. Scott, secretary; J.
H. Bellon, treasurer.

GOLDENROD REBEKAH LODGE, NO. 90, I.O.O.F.

The Goldenrod Rebekah Lodge, No. 90, was organized March 28, 1914, and was
instituted April 29, 1914. Charter members were Anna Carolyn Timm, Nellie
Bitner Gillis, Clara Belle Grant, Grace B. Nickisch, Emma Anna West, Lois
Nancy Jennings, Faith Mary Mattesen, Genevieva May Nippolt, Rachel Bronner and
Jessie Moore. The first presiding officer, the Noble Grand, was Mrs. Anna
Carolyn Timm. The Rebekah Lodge was the first Women's organization in Wishek.
Its functions locally have been purely of a social and fraternal nature. The
present officers are Barbara Stockburger, Noble Grand; Laura Bailey, Vice
Grand; Eleanor Erbele, secretary; and Bertha Ottmar, treasurer.

                National Guard

The history of the State of North Dakota had to be lived before it could be
written. Among those who have lived it most nobly are thousands of its sons who
through many generations served the military traditions by which our honor as a
State and as a nation have been kept alive and shining.

Service Battery 188th FA Bn North Dakota National Guard was organized in
Wishek, North Dakota, January 24th, 1947. This unit now has 31 enlisted men and
5 officers. Full strength required for this Unit is 55 enlisted men and 5
officers.

The new National Guard is vital to all of us as a force to be trained, equipped
and ever ready for instant mobilization to supplement the Regular Army in time
of threat. It commends itself for many other reasons.

Here at home, the individual may train while pursuing his regular occupation
and living a normal civilian life. He draws a full day's Regular Army pay for
two hours of spare-time training and for each of 15 days of field training. He
may learn new skills, or improve old ones, and acquire valuable experience in
organization and leadership.

An active National Guard unit is a community asset. It is a spark to real
community service. It is citizenship at work. The community and local merchants
benefit too, from Federal funds. A single company of 50 enlisted men receives
$10,000 annually.

The National Guard is a State and Federal organization. It functions at the
call of the Governor or Unit Commander as a protector of life and property or
at the call of the President in national emergencies. It has an outstanding
record of service in times of flood or other disasters, as well as in defense
of the nation. But of all its functions, the most important is:

Your National Guard Helps Guard the Peace.

                Biography

Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Walth

Friedrich Walth was born in Glueckstal, South Russia, on January 7, 1862, the
son of Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Walth (Schmidt). He was married to Magdelina
Retzer, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Retzer, (Magdelina Albright) in 1885.

After spending a few years in Russia, they decided to migrate to America with
their two children. They set sail and arrived here in 1889. They spent the
first winter with the Jacob Vilhauer family. The next spring they built a house
of sod, which still stands but is not in use. It still serves as a land mark
and many memories of the old past are thereby revived.

Many and varied were the hardships endured by the family, the undertaking of
building a home and farm of sod was a difficult task, yet so freely practiced
by all pioneers. The breaking of the soil with hand plow and oxen and the
seeding by hand were indeed a slow process, but with the coming of modern
machinery all those ways were replaced, no more horse-power for threshing,
being replaced with the steam engine. Sickness too, was a terrifying condition
to invade the homes.

Mr. and Mrs. Walth had 11 children. Ten boys and 1 girl. Five of the children
died. Those remaining are John H. Jacob, Gottlieb, Christ, Fred R. and Henry.
At one time, three of the Walth children died of diphtheria within twenty-four
hours.

The enjoyable experiences of the Walths were the newer and better things that
America offered and gave them, such as a good place to rear their family and to
secure enough land so that everyone of their children would have his own farm,
which they achieved. They had the desire of enjoying the last few remaining
years in retirement but that was interrupted by the sudden accidental death of
Friedrich Walth on July 5, 1924. Mrs. Walth, however, moved to the city of
Wishek in 1928 and spent her remaining years here. She especially enjoyed the
electric lights and the other modern modalities that were in use the last few
years. Mrs. Friedrich Walth passed away on January 13th, 1943. The Walths have
been lifelong members of the Lutheran Church.

August Boschee

An early pioneer of Wishek was August Boschee, born at Kassel, South Russia, on
January 25, 1866. He was the son of John and Rose (Huepel) Boschee.

Mr. Boschee arrived at Menno, South Dakota, in the fall of 1884. After tiding
himself over the winter, he came to Wishek in the spring of 1885. He settled on
a homestead here. The first people he knew here were John Wishek, Sr., and John
Doyle, Sr.

In 1890, he married Rose Sprenger, daughter of Jacob and Margaret (Gehring)
Sprenger. To this union were born 3 children, of whom one died. In 1893, Mrs.
Boschee followed the child in death.

Mr. Boschee was married to Katharina Allerding in 1894. To this second union
were born 10 children, of whom 5 died. In 1911, his second wife died.

Again he remarried. This time to Dorothy Kies. Three children were born, all of
whom died.

In 1918 he was married a fourth time to Elizabeth Schnabel.

Mr. Boschee's remaining children are Pauline Hass, Miles City, Montana; Lydia
Perman, Racine, Wisconsin; Emma Waldbauer, Fairfield, Montana; Herman Boschee,
Bertha Breitbarth and Gustav Boschee of Wishek.

Mr. Boschee died on February 14, 1934, in his home at Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Gehring

Gottlieb Gehring, son of Johann Gehring and Katherina, nee Krams, came to this
country with his parents on March 11, 1898, and settled in the vicinity of
Ashley, N. Dak. That same year in the month of June, the 4th day, there arrived
Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb and Barbara, (Rath) Scharf with their daughter Maria, who
settled in the vicinity of Kulm, N. Dak. These two young people, Gottlieb
Gehring and Maria Scharf met and were married at Fredonia, N. Dak., on February
20, 1902. Maria Scharf, before her marriage, filed on a homestead 16 miles
north of Kulm, N. Dak., and lived the allotted time to gain rights to the land
in her 8' by 14' shack. After her marriage she moved with her husband to Ashley,
N. Dak., where they filed another homestead, that is still in their possession.

In 1907, they moved to Wishek, N. Dak., which was still rather in its infancy.
The Gehrings recall many of the early buildings and places of business and they
watched Wishek grow to its present proportions. Mr. Gehring commenced working
on the Soo Line Railroad shortly after his marriage. He also worked for John
Rath, who had a Dray and Transfer business at that time. Mrs. Gehring was
employed at the Sheridan House for some time.

Having no children of their own, Mr. and Mrs. Gehring adopted a baby girl from
Rosena Krenzler, to whom twins were born. Alma Marie was baptized on April 3,
1910, in the St. Luke's Lutheran Church of Wishek, N. Dak. Alma died on March
23, 1937.

Mr. and Mrs. Henry Woehl

One of the men to see Wishek in the early days was Henry Woehl, a pioneer
farmer of Wishek.

Mr. Woehl was born on June 17, 1878, in Rohrback, Russia, to Jacob and Margaret
Breniese Woehl. He came to Eureka, S. D., alone in 1889. He made several trips
to Wishek during the following 10 years, seeing it first in 1898. He worked at
various occupations until 1903 when he decided to settle at Wishek.

On December 4, 1904, he was married to Justina Boschee, daughter of Valentine
and Filiphine, nee Maier, Boschee. To this union were born 12 children, 2 of
whom died. Those living at present are Gottfried Woehl, Otto, Reinhold, Otto,
Henry, Albert, Paul, and Mrs. August (Esther) Huber of Wishek. Adolph of
Zeeland, Mrs. John (Lea) Lindeman of Linton, and Mrs. Julius Vossler of
Billings, Montana.

Mr. Woehl became a successful farmer, in spite of great hardships. He was a
supporter of better education and was always very interested in projects to
improve the community, economically and socially.

In 1944, Mr. Woehl retired from farming, buying a home in Wishek.

On June 20, 1947, Mrs. Woehl died and on February 28, 1948, Mr. Woehl married
again. This time to Mrs. Elizabeth (Miller) Meidinger. They reside happily in
Wishek, enjoying their last days in the peace for which they worked so hard for
many Years.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob M. Frey

Jacob M. Frey was born in Newdorf, South Russia, on August 9, 1879. At the age
of fourteen years, he came to the United States with his parents, Martin Frey
and Elizabeth Scheffely.

On April 2, 1905, Jacob Frey married Katherine Meidinger of Richwell, N. Dak.
Katherina was the daughter of Peter Meidinger and Christine Krum and was born
in Kassel, South Russia, on August 27, 1883.

In 1905, they came to this community and became acquainted with Jacob Kautz,
Jacob Sifferman, and Adam Becker.

The couple took a homestead nine miles northeast of Wishek where they lived for
thirty-six years. They did their shopping in Wishek and bought their groceries
from Best Store, then located on the Wm. Hoff Hardware premises. Mr. and Mrs.
Frey suffered many hardships. A severe blizzard struck and the animals had to
be moved into the house while the family lived in one room. A hole was dug
under the house where the calves were placed. Hay was twisted by hand and used
as fuel to keep from freezing during the storm.

To this union two sons were born, namely: Jacob Martin of Wishek and Julius
Edward of Lehr, N. Dak.

In 1941, Mr. and Mrs. Frey purchased a home in Wishek and retired from farm
life. Mr. Frey passed away in December, 1943, at this home in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Stockburger

Gottlieb Stockburger was born on December 2, 1865, at Gildendorf, South Russia.
In 1891, Mr. Stockburger married Maria Scheaffer at Gildendorf, South Russia.
Maria was born on March 29, 1874. To this union four children were born,
namely: Gottlieb, Jr., of Gardina, N. Dak., Mrs. Edward (Pauline) Gall John and
Andreas of Wishek.

Mrs. Maria Schaeffer Stockburger died in 1904 in South Russia.

In 1905, Mr. Stockburger married Mrs. Elizabetha Wetzel (nee Rembold). A family
of nine Wetzel children were brought into the Stockburger home. They are,
namely: Mrs. Elizabetha Barth (now deceased); John Wetzel of Omemee, N. Dak.;
Mrs. Gott. (Margaretha) Stockburger, Jr., of Gardina, N. Dak.; Edward,
Gottlieb, and Christina Wetzel of South Russia; and Mrs. Otto (Regina) Brandt
of Wishek.

Two children were born to Gottlieb and Elizabeth Stockburger. One child died in
infancy. The son, Theodore Stockburger, resides in Minneapolis, Minn.

In 1909, the family came to America and located eight miles north of Wishek.
Later Mr. Stockburger added additional land to his one-fourth section of
homesteaded land. The first people they met were Jacob and Claus Hoirup and
Andrew Weizel, Sr., and Jr. They came to Wishek on March 15, 1910, and in 1925
moved into Wishek for a well earned rest.

Mr. Stockburger died on July 22, 1939, and Mrs. Elizabeth Wetzel Stockburger
died on March 12, 1945. They were lifetime members of the Lutheran Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Dockter

Christian Dockter of Wishek, N. D., is the son of Gottlieb Dockter and his
wife, Magdalena, nee Bender. He was born on August 12, 1877, in Nuedorf, South
Russia.

Mr. Dockter came to this country in the spring of 1885 and lived at Menno, S.
D., for 4 years and then moved to McIntosh County.

His wife, Barbara (Rieger) Dockter, was born in South Russia on March 28, 1883,
and she was married to Mr. Dockter on November 19, 1891, at Venturia, N. D. One
child was reared in their home, Wilma Barbara, who was born September 7, 1920.

Mr. Dockter at one time served McIntosh County as Sheriff. Mr. Dockter is
dealing in real estate and is a practical bone setter.

Mr. and Mrs. Peter Frank

Peter Frank was born Feb. 2, 1843, at Worms, South Russia. His father was Peter
Frank, Sr., and his mother was Jacobina Lenz. Peter, Jr., was married to
Elizabeth Frank on Aug. 17, 1877, at Worms, South Russia. Mrs. Frank was born
Jan. 1, 1847, the daughter of George Frank and Elizabeth Moos.

The Franks entered this country April 19, 1902, and arrived at this community
May 20 of the same year. They located 10 miles northwest of Wishek.

Their newly built barn served as quarters until their house was completed.
Having brought some money from the Old Country, he bought about 40 head of
cattle at Eureka and made a successful profit after one year. Then after nine
years of farming Mr. Frank retired and purchased a house in Wishek. Mr. and
Mrs. Frank were members of the Lutheran Church in Wishek.

The Franks had 9 children, 6 of whom died. Those still living are Jacob and
Michael at Wishek and Mrs. Elizabeth Zoller of Los Angeles, California.

Mr. Frank died January 28, 1916. Mrs. Frank followed him in death on January
27, 1929. Both died at their home in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Eisenbeis

Jacob Eisenbeis, born on March 27, 1874, in South Russia, married Kathrina
Werre on November 14, 1897, in South Russia. His parents were David Eisenbeis
and Kathrina Eisenbeis, nee Werre. His wife was born on May 27, 1877. Her
parents were Gottfried Werre and Kathrina Werre, nee Landenberger.

To this marriage, twelve children were born. Eleven of the children are still
living. They are: Jacob, George, and Arthur, and Lydia Hochhalter of Wishek,
Mary Kapp, Christina Brosy, Edwin of Fredonia, Edward and Kathrina Fischer of
Aberdeen, S. Dak., Anna Reommich of Driscoll, and Rose Clark of Stockton, Calif.

Mr. Eisenbeis entered America on November 11, 1898, coming to McIntosh County
on December 14, 1898. When Mr. Eisenbeis first saw Wishek on May 15, 1899, the
only building to be seen was a shed near the railroad track. The first people
he knew here were Jacob Hochalter, Sr., Valentine Boschee, Sr., Joseph Werre,
Sr., and John Brantner.

He carried on his first business transactions at a general store run by Joseph
Werre and at a small cafe operated by Mrs. Hills. Mr. Eisenbeis worked on a
cattle ranch for a time, helped build the Pollock Railroad Line, and also the
Round House here in Wishek. He also worked on the Section. Mrs. Eisenbeis died
on February 12, 1946, in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Werre

Jacob Werre, born March 2, 1880, in Gluecksdahl, South Russia, married
Christina Gunst on December 27, 1904, in Napoleon, N. Dak. His parents were
Gottfried Werre and Katharina Werre, nee Landenberger. Mrs. Werre was born
September 14, 1885. Her parents were Wilhelm Gunst and Eva Gunst nee Rieger.

Thirteen children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Werre. Four of the children died.
The remaining nine are Edward and George Werre of Wishek; Edwin, Hulda Lang,
and Emilie Gunst of Jamestown; Reinhold and Maria Gunst of Kulm, Gottfried of
Ashley, and Jacob of Spokane, Wash.

In 1900, Mr. Werre came to this country, going first to Eureka, S. Dak. In 1905
he filed on a 1/4 Sec. of land located 10 miles west and 1-1/2 miles north of
Wishek.

In 1900, Mr. Werre first saw Wishek when he helped his second cousin, Mr.
Gottfried Werre, move his Merc. Store goods from Wishek to Eureka, S. Dak.

When Mr. Werre arrived in this community he had no funds of any kind, so he
hired out for work on farms. At one time Mr. Werre worked for one farmer for 12
months for $145. For 4 years he worked on one farm after another until he had
saved enough money to start farming on his own. After filing on a 1/4 Sec. of
land, he bought 8 head of cattle and 4 horses. Not being able to pay the full
price on the horses, he had to leave the remaining $150 as a debt, on which he
had to pay an interest rate of 12%.

There were many hardships for the Werre family, years when the crops were poor
or no crops at all.

Mr. Werre took great interest in school matters and community affairs. He
served for a number of years on the school board. Mr. Werre retired from
farming in 1940 and moved to Wishek. Mrs. Werre died October 23, 1940, at a
Bismarck hospital.

Mr. and Mrs. George Schilling

George Schilling, Sr., was born January 18, 1880, in Gluekstahl, South Russia.
His parents were Wm. Schilling and Carolina Schilling, nee Boschee. He married
Kathrina Woehl at Ashley on November 22, 1903. Mrs. Schilling was born March 4,
1888. Her parents were Henry Woehl and Kathrina Woehl, nee Brenneise. Nine
children were born to them. One died. The eight remaining children are George,
Theodore, and Adam Schilling, Ida Meidinger of Venturia, Emma Kasemen, Martha
Benkendorff, Rose Wolf, and Christine Ottmer of Wishek.

Mr. Schilling entered this country on June 1, 1901, coming to this community in
1903. He settled 14 miles northwest of Venturia. He first saw Wishek on March
20, 1903. His first business transaction was to buy land from Karl Kessels, and
a grass mower from John Doyle.

Due to the fact that the Schillings were not of the very first pioneers, they
were fortunate in not having to endure any of the severe hardships of pioneer
life. The family lost only one child. Financially, they were also fortunate.
Whenever possible they added to their land by purchasing a tract or more until
they had a total of 8 quarters with two sets of buildings and other personal
property to a fair extent.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian C. Bauer

Christian C. Bauer, whose parents were Christian Bauer and Kathrina Bauer, nee
Himmerich, was born on January 24, 1885, in South Russia. On November 10, 1907,
he married Magdelina Rueb in McIntosh County. Magdelina was born on January 7,
1883. Her parents were Philip Rueb and Carolina Rueb, nee Springer.

Mr. and Mrs. Bauer had nine children, six of whom are deceased. The three
remaining children are Christina B. Bauer of Danzig, Rosina Herr and Helmuth of
Wishek.

Mr. Bauer entered this country in 1885, going to Tyndale, S. Dak. In 1886 he
and his parents came to this community and settled 16 miles southwest of
Wishek. At this time they received citizenship papers and documents for
Homestead. Mr. Bauer first saw Wishek about 1900 when there were just a few
buildings here. They came here with their grain and other products.

The Bauers shared with their neighbors the first reaper, which did not tie the
bundles.

Because of crop failures, the Bauers suffered many hardships. Due to the
shortage of food, they ate ground cereal and the few other farm products they
had. To add to the other hardships, so much snow fell, that the banks were
level with the housetop, only the chimney was to be seen. On one occasion, when
the snow covered the house, a cow walked over the top of the house, without
breaking through the snow or roof.

Due to the shortage of churches and pastors at the time, people came together
in homes for worship, often at Christian Gruebeles. Later the Ebenezer
Evangelical Church became their church.

Mr. Bauer recalls that at one time, Mrs. Bauer's father, Mr. Rueb, walked 75
miles to Ipswich, S. Dak., to get some spike teeth to make a spring-tooth
one-section harrow.

Mr. and Mrs. John Sayler

John Sayler was born November 15, 1875, in Lichteenfeld, South Russia. He was
the son of Jacob and Christina Sayler, nee Kautz. He was married on November
28, 1897, to Magdalena Bier in Wurms, South Russia. Magdalene Bier was born
January 1, 1878, and the daughter of George and Anna Maria Bier, nee Munsch. To
this union were born sixteen children. The following survive: Edward, Wishek,
North Dakota; Reinhold, Wishek, North Dakota; Adolf, Sacramento, California;
Lydia (Knoll), Venturia, North Dakota; Paulina (Ketterling), Zeeland, North
Dakota; Bertha (Knoll), Venturia, North Dakota; Martha (Ketterling), Wishek,
North Dakota; Frieda (Deyle), Billings, Montana; Kathrina (Golz), Venturia,
North Dakota; and Edwin, Wishek, North Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Sayler came to this country in 1899 and located twelve miles
southwest of Wishek, North Dakota, in June, 1901.

Mr. Sayler's first business transactions in Wishek were with C. H. Suess, John
Doyle and Steve Fischer.

Mr. Sayler was very active in their school affairs. He was a member of Salem
School District for several years. Mr. Sayler and his family have always been
faithful members of the Lutheran Church.

Mr. Sayler and his family have endured severe hardships, during the early
years. With constant conservation and hard work, Mr. Sayler has accumulated
considerable property.

In 1940 Mr. Sayler retired and at present is enjoying his retirement in Wishek,
North Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob B. Siffermann

Mr. Jacob B. Siffermann was born November 15, 1885, in Kosharicka, South
Russia. He was the son of Jacob Siffermann and Christina Scheuffely and came to
America in 1893, settling at Eureka, S. Dak. On November 11, 1905, he was
married to Katherine Whittmyier at Lehr, N. Dak. Katherine was born in
Gluksdahl [Glueckstal], Russia, on December 21, 1882. She was the daughter of
Simon and Christina (nee Unruh) Whittmyier. In the fall of 1903, Mr. Siffermann
located on a farm three miles northwest of Lehr, N. Dak.

The first people the Siffermann family knew were the merchants of Wishek. Some
of these were Mr. H. E. Timm, who worked in Herr's Store; Charley Juluis, owner
of a store; Philip Thurn, who had a feed and coal store; and Ed Bond, farmer.

To this family eight children were born. They are: Christine (Mrs. Albert
Wolff) of Wishek; Jacob, Jr., of Acompo, Calif.; Philip of Lodi, Calif.; Martha
(Mrs. Leopold Hochhalter) of Lehr, N. Dak.; Katherine (Mrs. Fred Frank) of
Wishek; Edward of Lodi, Calif.; Helena (Mrs. Edwin Seibel) of Lodi, Calif.;
Bertha of Lodi, Calif.

Both Mr. and Mrs. Siffermann are happily living together at their home at 412
Eden St., Lodi, Calif.

Mr. and Mrs. Philip Thurn

In 1893, during the ebb tide of the great immigration from Russia, Philip Thurn
came to America with his parents and the rest of the family. He was born July
1, 1876, in Neudorf, Russia, the son of Friedrich Karl and Margaret Obenauer
Thurn. His sister Magdalena Mrs. Christoph Hochhalter and brothers, Friedrich
and John, had preceded them to the new land of unlimited possibilities. The
Thurns homesteaded near Eureka and there Mr. Thurn grew to manhood.

He was married to Christina Merkel September 19, 1897, in Eureka, South Dakota.
She was born February 14, 1878, in Marienberg, Russia, the daughter of
Friedrich and Margaret Bender Merkel.

The following spring they filed on a claim nine miles northwest of the newly
founded site of Wishek. The soil was rich and potatoes grew in abundance, often
the menu was potatoes and potatoes only, three times a day.

In those days there were no churches, a Reformed Minister came to the Johann
Ackerman or Fred Lang home occasionally and held services there. Later a
Lutheran Missionary Minister, Rev. E. Moeckel, came to minister unto the
settlers. There was little money to pay him a salary but the settlers got
together and moved a small house on to some land north of Wishek which Rev.
Moeckel homesteaded. A request was made to their synod for aid but none came,
however Rev. Moeckel saw the settler's plight and agreed to stay and minister
to this group for whatever they could give, which was mostly in the form of
meat, potatoes and feed. Services were held at the Thurn home until 1905.

In 1908 the family moved to Wishek where Mr. Thurn engaged in the cream, flour,
feed and coal business.

Mr. Thurn was a charter member of the St. Lucas Lutheran Church and served as
deacon and Sunday school teacher for many years.

Mr. Thurn died during the influenza epidemic; February 24, 1919, leaving his
wife and nine children. To Mrs. Thurn's lot fell the task of rearing the
family, with the help of her son, Fred, who was then but fifteen years old. She
took over her husband's business. Fred continued to manage this for her until
1923 when they disposed of it and Fred took over the Standard Oil Agency which
business he continues today.

Fred Thurn was married to Magdalene Bietz June 10, 1928. They have one
daughter, Ardyn. Mr. Thurn takes an active part in the progress of this
community. He has served six years on the local school board, sixteen years on
the Park Board, two years as City Treasurer, eight years as City Auditor,
twenty years as director and four years as President of the Commercial Club and
is at present serving as Mayor of Wishek. Fred is a member of the St. Lucas
Lutheran Church. There he has served twelve years as a Sunday School teacher,
two years as treasurer, and is at present serving as Trustee.

Mr. and Mrs. Philip Thurn were the parents of the following children: Emma
(Mrs. John Weber), Wishek, North Dakota; Martha (Mrs. Gottlieb Merkel), Steele,
North Dakota; Mathilde (Mrs. Dave Rieker), Bismarck, North Dakota; Fred Thurn,
Wishek, North Dakota; Margaret (Mrs. Otto Durr), Marysville, California; Arthur
Thurn, Hamilton, Ohio; Edward, who died in infancy; Irene, who died at the age
of seventeen, in 1931; and Lea (Mrs. Donald Miller), Clio, California; and
Magdaline (Mrs. Henry Zwanck), California.

Despite the stormy, lonely years Mrs. Philip Thurn's outlook on life remained
bright. She died April 30, 1940.

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Kuebler

In the year 1817 Adam Kuebler, a great-grandfather of Johan Adam Kuebler, left
his home in Schwartzwald, Germany, with his wife and 2 children. On the way to
Russia, tragedy struck the little family, for father and mother died, leaving
the two children orphaned. The daughter and the son, Christian, were taken
along by their uncle, Johannes Wolgemuth, into the colony Hoffnungstahl,
Russia. The uncle received his share of land from the Czar, and there the
grandfather, Christian Kuebler, was reared.

Grandfather Kuebler was born in Schwartzwald, Germany, in 1811. He was married
to Heinericka Weller of Bersola, South Russia. They made their home in
Hoffnungstahl and were blessed with 4 sons: Frederick, Adam, Christian, and
Johannes. Sometime in the 70's Grandfather Christian Kuebler bought land in the
village Neu-Glueckstahl and moved there.

The youngest son, Johannes, didn't want to be a soldier, so, at the age of 20
he left for America, settling in Menno, Dakota Territory. Adam Kuebler, his
wife and 4 children followed in 1885, first coming to Merina, S. Dak. In the
same year, they filed a homestead in McPherson County, 4 miles southwest of
where Eureka now stands. In 1888, the grandfather, wife, and 2 daughters, son,
Christian, wife and 3 children left for America, arriving in Eureka on August
28th. There they stayed with Adam Kuebler for sometime. Christian Kuebler, Jr.,
filed a homestead 5 miles southwest of Eureka and the grandfather lived with
them until his passing in 1891.

Johan Adam Kuebler was born on November 25, 1886, in New Glueckstahl, South
Russia, and was the son of Christian Kuebler and Margaretha, nee Wanner. On
June 29, 1909, he was united in marriage to Katharina Heiser, daughter of
Johannes and Christina (Schick) Heiser of Campbell County. The wedding
ceremony was performed by Rev. Adam Haag in the Hoffnungstahl Church, west of
Eureka, S. Dak. In the fall of 1909 they moved to a farm of Fred L. Klein, 7
miles north of Eureka and remained there for one year. In 1910 Mr. Kuebler made
a short trip to Canada but finding nothing suitable, he returned to Eureka.
Later he bought 160 acres from John P. Petterson, 2 miles south of Burnstad, N.
Dak. After threshing, Mr. Kuebler built a house and they moved in on October
15, 1910. In the Spring of 1914, he bought 320 acres of land from Mrs. John G.
Herr, 10 miles northwest of Wishek, making his home there until 1945 when he
bought and moved his family into a house in Wishek.

While on the farm, Mr. Kuebler was on the School Board for 31 years serving as
Director for 3 years, Clerk for 6 years, Treasurer for 16 years and then
Director again for 6 years. At the present time, being in good health, he
enjoys carpentry as a pastime.

Mr. Kuebler was a member of the Salem Lutheran Church since 1911, but now is a
member of St. Luke's Lutheran Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Kuebler had 13 children born to them, 7 daughters and 6 sons, of
whom one daughter and three sons died in infancy. A son, August, died on
September 7, 1928, at the age of 12 when he was kicked by a horse. Another son,
Johan Adam, Jr., died on September 26, 1936, at the age of 14. The remaining
children are: Martha (Mrs. Christ Ottenbacher) of Herried, S. Dak.; Paulina
(Mrs. Arthur Ottenbacher) of Kalispell, Mont.; Adolph, on the home place; Viola
(Mrs. Christian Eiseman) of Burnstad, N. Dak.; Bertha (Mrs. Theodore Schock),
on the home place; Ottilia and Esther, at home with their parents.

Mr. and Mrs. Peter Ackermann

Peter Ackermann was born in Rohrbach, South Russia, the son of Johannes and
Christina (Hildenbrandt) Ackermann, December 15, 1875. His early boyhood was
spent here. He attended school studying in both the German and in the Russian
languages. When he was fourteen years old he accompanied his parents to
America and settled with them on their homestead.

When the work on the farm was finished he worked out for neighbors to increase
the family income. Food was scarce at that time, especially meat. In the winter
time fish were speared out of Red Lake and rabbits were snared. In the summer
wild ducks could be shot for food.

On March 21, 1896, Peter Ackermann was married to Katherina Krein, the daughter
of Christian and Magdalina (Schauer) Krein. They began farming for themselves.
Their nearest market was Ashley, N. D. In 1898 the railroad was extended from
Kulm and entered what is now Wishek. Wishek's first business was carried on in
a tent at that time. Mr. Ackermann rode on the first excursion train over the
line to Bismarck.

In 1905 the family moved to Wishek. Mr. Ackermann going into machine business.
Later he operated the livery barn. This he sold and purchased with Mr. Henry
Wolfe a general merchandise store. This was the beginning of his years in
Wishek as a merchant. With various partners he remained in the general
merchandise business until 1936 when he retired and moved to Lodi, California,
where he and Mrs. Ackermann still reside.

They are the parents of six children, four of which reached adulthood. They are
John, Wishek; Mrs. Lydia Ritter, Stockton, California; Mrs. Martha Anderson,
Lodi, California, and Edwin, San Francisco California.

Mr. and Mrs. Johannes Ackermann

Early pioneer history cannot be complete without recording the names of some of
the true early settlers who are now recalled in Wishek in memory only. Such
pioneers were Johannes Ackermann and his wife Christina Hildenbrandt. Their
forefathers had migrated from southwestern Germany near the Rhine River to
Rohrbach, South Russia. Here Johannes was born in 1854, the son of Frederich
Ackermann.

He was married in 1873 and they established their home in Russia. There nine of
their children were born and there also three of them were buried. In 1889 the
couple with their six remaining children emigrated from Russia to America. They
were in search of national freedom and of land. They homesteaded a few miles
north of Wishek but at the time of their settlement, the nearest town was
Eureka, S. D., forty miles away. The homestead was located in Logan County,
section 22, township 133, range 71 west.

A first necessity was shelter for the family. A house was built of sod and
smeared with clay inside and out. This sufficed for several years till a stone
house was erected. The first sorrow in the new country was the death of their
two youngest children. Three more children were born in this prairie home, one
of which died while still a small child.

Their nearest neighbors were the Christ Krein family, five miles away. Judging
from today their life was one of dire hardships. Food was scarce and money very
scarce. Marketing of buffalo bones was the only cash income until the first
crops could be harvested. Long hazardous trips were with oxen. There was a
constant fear of Indians. When a hail storm completely destroyed one year's
grain crop, Mr. Ackermann decided to raise more livestock. From that time he
always had a large herd of cattle and many horses ranging on the vast prairie.

Early pioneers longed for a railroad through the territory. Mr. Ackermann, in
his great desire to see the railroad, donated the rail right-of-way through the
entire length of two quarters of land.

An ambitious, hard worker, Johannes Ackermann carved a home out of the prairie
and established each of his children. Those growing to adulthood were: Mrs.
Catherine Lang, deceased; Peter Ackermann, Lodi, California; Mrs. Christina
Hildenbrandt, Annamose, N. D.; Mrs. Mary Krein, Wishek; Mrs. Lydia Krein,
deceased; and Fred Ackermann, Fargo, N. D.

In 1910 Mr. and Mrs. Ackermann left their farm and moved into their new home in
Wishek. Mr. Ackermann operated a general store with his son, Peter, until 1920
when he retired from active business. In 1923 his wife died and ten years later
he passed away. His death marked the passing of one of Wishek's pioneer
patriarchs, a man respected and revered by the community, a man who had seen
Wishek grow from a grass covered prairie to a city, and who had loved every
part of that growth.

Mr. and Mrs. George Wolff

The town of Wishek had been established but four years when George Wolff moved
here from Eureka. Mr. Wolff was born in South Russia on March 29, 1882, the son
of Johannes Wolff and Fredericka, nee Bender. At the age of three his parents
moved to the United States and settled near Eureka.

In May, 1901, he married Julianna Kusler at Eureka, South Dakota. She was the
daughter of Nick Kusler and Carolina, nee Mutchuelknaus. The young couple then
came to Wishek to establish their home on a farm, six miles east of Wishek.
Five children were born to this union, Albert, Edwin, Emil and Arthur, all
living near Wishek, and one daughter, Ella (Mrs. Christ Witmayer), who lives at
Aberdeen, S. Dak. The home was temporarily broken up, when Mrs. Wolff was taken
by death on February 29, 1908.

When Mr. Wolff came to Wishek, he became acquainted with the Pratts, Jacob
Roessler, Philip Mueller, Baileys, Doyles, Kleins and all the early business
people. The old Thistle ranch was still in existence when he arrived. This
place, with its 1000 to 1500 head of cattle always interested him.

On July 7th, 1908, Mr. Wolff remarried, this time with Mrs. Maria Pfeifle, nee
Weixel. Her three children, Ida (now Mrs. John R. Wanner), Andrew and Arthur,
were thus brought into the Wolff family. This second marriage was blessed with
5 children, of whom these four are still living, Frieda (Mrs. Ray Clear),
Ellensburg, Washington; Anna (Mrs. Alfred Breitbarth), Alpha, Minnesota; Tillie
and Rubin of Wishek.

Mr. Wolff says of earlier days: "I used to come to my brother's place (John S.
Wolff), who moved to this territory in 1892. I remember when the grade for the
railroad was built. I helped build the railroad during the summer months.
Because the summer was dry, and no crops were raised, the people were only too
happy to assist in the work offered by the railroad company."

For several years Mr. Wolff assisted his brothers and parents in farm work, but
in 1902 established a home, by building a house with lumber purchased from Mr.
Suess. In 1919 he built a large modern home on his farm, a home that was and
still is the envy of many a city dweller.

Mr. Wolff served as county commissioner from the second district of McIntosh
County for several years. He was school director for 14 years and treasurer for
12 years.

The Wolffs have always taken an active part in the affairs of St. Luke's
Lutheran Church of Wishek. Mr. Wolff has served his congregation as deacon for
a period of nine years.

Although Mr. and Mrs. Wolff have been living in Wishek for several years, Mr.
Wolff still takes an active interest in farming and the raising of cattle.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Unruh

Christian Unruh conducted a business establishment in Wishek for a good many
years. He was born on July 2, 1871, in Glueckstal, South Russia. His parents
were George J. Unruh and Rosina, nee Dudt. He grew up in Glueckstal. His wife,
whom he married on May 23, 1893, is Katherina, the daughter of Kasper Krein and
Christina, nee Flemmer. She was born March 24, 1871.

Thirteen years after their marriage, the young couple moved to the United
States, arriving at Eureka on May 21, 1906. Their stay at Eureka lasted for one
year and in 1907 they moved to a farm, three miles northwest of Lehr. They had
visited Wishek already, during the time when they lived at Eureka. On May 22,
1914, they decided to give up farming and move to Wishek. Among the first
people they became acquainted with were Henry Schmitt, Philip Thurn, Daniel
Nickisch, Sr., and Fred Sayler.

Their first business transaction completed by the Unruhs in Wishek was the
purchase of Karl Sayler's shanty, which they used for a cream station. To the
business of buying cream, Mr. Unruh later added the sale of flour, feeds, and
coal. This business was continued until Mr. Unruh's death June 18, 1941.

Mr. and Mrs. Unruh were the parents of fourteen children. Only three are living
today. They are: Hahnah (Mrs. David J. Lassle), Salem, Oregon; Rev. George,
Arthur, N. Dak.; and Christ, at Wishek. Mrs. Unruh is proud of the fact that
she is the grandmother of 21 grandchildren.

Mrs. Unruh now makes her home at the Lutheran Home at Eureka, South Dakota. Of
early days she says, that although they experienced much illness, cattle
losses, and drought, they never experienced any dire need.

The Unruh family has always been affiliated with the Lutheran Church. Mr. Unruh
served his church for many years, as one of its deacons.

Mr. and Mrs. John K. Wanner

When writing the name John Wanner, it is of utmost importance to include the
middle initial, for there are at least four John Wanner families living in and
around Wishek at the present time.

John K. Wanner was born June 21, 1884, in Kassel, South Russia, as the son of
Karl Wanner and Karolina, nee Bender. He came to the United States in December,
1905, and after a few days went on to Wishek, arriving here at the close of
1905. In September, 1906, he took up a homestead 12 miles southwest of Wishek.
His first business transaction in 1906 was the purchase of lumber from the Suess
Lumber Yard, with which to build a small shanty for living quarters and a barn.
After six years, he moved to a new farm only five miles southwest of Wishek.

He was married to Magdalena Sprenger on January 12, 1909. She is the daughter
of Johann Sprenger and Rosina, nee Mehlhaff. She was born on May 8, 1889. They
are the parents of seven children, two of whom are no longer living. Their
children are: Reinhold, Wishek; Lydia (Mrs. J. L. Raile), Wishek; Adam, Wishek;
Karolina, at home with her parents; and Ida (Mrs. Agon H. Nehlich), Aberdeen,
South Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Wanner are members of the St. Luke's Lutheran Church.

In 1940 they purchased a home in Wishek and have been living in town ever since.

Mr. and Mrs. John S. Wolff

Among those who came to this community several years before the town was
established is the couple, Mr. and Mrs. John S. Wolff. They had come from South
Russia several years before and had lived near Eureka until June, 1893, when
they decided to move on to the north. They settled on a farm approximately
three miles from Wishek.

Mr. Wolff was born on March 11, 1868, in Marienberg, South Russia. His parents
were John Wolff and Fredericka, nee Bender. Mrs. Bender was the former
Friedericka Fischer, born at Bergdorf, South Russia, on June 11, 1873. She was
the daughter of Karl Fischer and Elizabeth, nee Weile. The date of their
marriage is December 29, 1891. The marriage was performed at Eureka. Eight
children were born to this marriage. Of these, the following children are still
living: John, Jr., Rosina (Mrs. Philip Bender), Lydia (Mrs. E. P. Pfeifle),
Elizabeth (Mrs. Wm. G. Fiechtner), Emelia (Mrs. Wm. Huber), all of Wishek; and
Edward of Stockton, California.

When Mr. and Mrs. Wolff came to this locality, no town was to be found. Elmer
Pratt was the Postmaster at that time, and the mail was brought from Hoskins
Lake on foot during the winter.

Mr. Wolff ofttimes told of the coming of the railroad in 1898. Mrs. Wolff
