recalls that in addition to her family work, she often cooked meals for twelve
or fifteen men, who were working on the railroad. Before the coming of the
railroad, the Wolffs carried on their trade at Eureka or Kulm. Many a trip was
made by wagon to get the necessary food and equipment.

During the early years the family home was kept warm by feeding straw and hay
into the stove. Family members took turns during the night tending the fire.

While Mr. Wolff was on a shopping trip, which lasted for several days, Mrs.
Wolff had the experience of having an Indian stop at the farm with some
antelope meat for the family. This was especially welcome for meat was a scarce
item at that time.

Mr. and Mrs. Wolff lived on their farm until 1916, when they retired and moved
to Wishek. Their son, John, Jr., took over the management of the farm from that
time on. Mr. Wolff continued to occupy his time by selling insurance and
adjusting losses until his death on July 9, 1947.

In 1941 the Wolffs were privileged to observe their Golden Wedding Anniversary.
Their children prepared a celebration for them on Christmas Day at the home of
their youngest daughter, Mrs. Wm. Huber. All the children and immediate
relatives gathered to honor the couple.

The Wolffs are among the charter members of St. Luke's Lutheran Church. Mr.
Wolff served his church as deacon for several years.

For twelve years Mr. Wolff held the public office of county commissioner.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Frank

Jacob Frank was born in Worms, South Russia, on October 19, 1880, as the son of
Peter and Elizabeth Frank. His wife, Eva, whom he married on January 28, 1901,
was the daughter of Michael and Katherina (Suess) Roemmich. She was born on May
6, 1879.

One year after they had married, the young couple decided to come to the United
States. They left their old home in February, 1902, and arrived at Eureka, S.
Dak., in April of the same year. They stayed there for a month until they found
a homestead. 10 miles northwest of Wishek. Here their first work was the
building of a house and a barn out of stone and sod.

On his first trip to Wishek for some lumber, he met Mr. C. H. Suess, the
manager of the lumber yard, who, to his surprise, was a second cousin of Mrs.
Frank and had come to this country some years earlier. On the way home with
this lumber, Mr. Frank experienced his first hail storm.

Mr. Frank has taken an active part in community and church affairs. He served
Salem Lutheran Church, 10 miles northwest of Wishek, for 25 years as deacon.
For twenty years he was a school director, for ten years he served as assessor
in the Second Commissioner's District of Logan County. From 1926 to 1938 he
served as County Commissioner.

After 33 years of farming Mr. and Mrs. Frank moved to Napoleon in 1935. Here
they lived for 10 years and then moved to Wishek, where they live at present.

The Franks were the parents of 9 children, of whom the following are still
living: Peter J., Napoleon, N. Dak.; Jacob W., Columbus, N. Dak.; Franz,
Burnstad, N. Dak.; Sophia (Mrs. C. P. Ritter); and Fred, both of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Daniel Aipperspach

Daniel Aipperspach was the son of Friederich and Dorothea Aipperspach, and was
born on January 4, 1863, in Bergdorf, South Russia. Mrs. Aipperspach was born as
Margaretha Ketterling on November 21, 1865, as the daughter of George and Anna
Maria (Merkel) Ketterling. Married in 1885, they were the parents of nine
children, two of whom died in infancy. Their children are: Daniel D., Wishek;
Friederich D., Wishek; John D., Enderlin; Jakob, Venturia; Albert, deceased
1940; Maria (Mrs. Jacob Speidel), Zeeland; and Gustav, Omaha, Nebraska.

Their first stop, after arriving in this country from Russia was Menno, South
Dakota. In April, 1886, they purchased two oxen, one cow, a wagon and a plow
for breaking sod, and shipped these by rail from Menno to Ipswich, which was
the end of the line at that time. From there they journeyed northwestward in
their wagon, drawn by the pair of oxen. The cow was tied alongside. On July 1,
of the same year, they filed their Homestead and tree-planting claim 22 miles
southwest of Wishek. At Ipswich they had purchased a few planks to be used in
the construction of a temporary shelter. Their first crop was flax, but the
yield was so poor, that the entire crop had to be put away for seed for the
coming spring.

This farm was their home until 1924, when Mr. and Mrs. Aipperspach retired in
Wishek. When they retired they were the owners of 2,480 acres of land in
McIntosh County.

The Aipperspach family was always active in the affairs of the Lutheran Church,
first as members of the St. Andrews Church near Zeeland, and later, after their
retirement of St. Luke's congregation in Wishek.

Mr. Aipperspach was privileged to spend 15 years of retirement in Wishek. His
death came on December 23, 1939. Mrs. Aipperspach lived until January 26, 1945.

Mr. and Mrs. Daniel D. Aipperspach

Among the names of the earliest children born in this wild and free country
around Wishek, we find the name of Daniel D. Aipperspach, more commonly known
by most people as D. D. Aipperspach. Mr. Aipperspach was born August 9, 1887,
on the homestead of his parents, Daniel and Margaretha (Ketterling)
Aipperspach. His parents had arrived at this, their new home, in the year
before.

The first time that Mr. Aipperspach came to Wishek, was in 1899, when his
father purchased lumber from the Suess lumber yard for a barn. It took 12 trips
to haul all the lumber for the barn. Although only twelve years old, Daniel had
to drive the team. On the trip to town the wagon was loaded with wheat, and on
the way home, the wagon was filled with lumber. The trip usually began at 3 in
the morning and ended at 10 p.m.

Mr. Aipperspach tells that his father and nearby neighbors purchased a 32-inch
J. I. Case threshing machine, one of the first in the community. This machine
was driven by six teams of horses. In 1906 these people purchased a steam
threshing rig. Mr. Aipperspach himself spent some of his spare winter time in
those years studying the principles of steam engineering. In 1908 he became a
qualified threshing steam engineer. In 1910 he began farming for himself. It
was on January 10, 1910, when he established his own home, marrying Anna Maria
Schnabel, the daughter of Adam and Katherina Schnabel.

Mr. Aipperspach has been active in school affairs. He served Frieda District #6
for 12 years as school director.

The Aipperspachs were brought up in the Lutheran faith and have been active
members of the Lutheran Church. Mr. Aipperspach has served St. Luke's
congregation as deacon for several years, and holds that office today.

In 1940 Mr. and Mrs. Aipperspach retired from farming and established their
home in Wishek.

Four children were born to them. Of these three are living today. They are:
Paul of Linton, North Dakota; Jakob D. and Rubin of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Johann Schmierer

Johann Schmierer was born on February 14, 1878, in Klein-Neudorf, South Russia,
the son of Gottlieb Schmierer and Rosina, nee Hertel. Mrs. Schmierer was born
January 30, 1877, as Rosina Thurn, the daughter of George Peter and Elizabeth
(Schneidt) Thurn. They were married on January 17, 1899.

On the 19th of February, 1903, the Schmierers, accompanied by his mother, and
one child left Russia. The first stop on their journey to their new home was in
Germany, from whence their ancestors had originally gone to Russia. Here they
were inspected by health authorities, and, given a clean bill of health,
continued their journey on the Crown Prince Wilhelm. They arrived at Tripp, S.
Dak., in March. Planning to spend only 8 days with their relatives here, their
stay was extended to six weeks, because of the severe illness of their child.
After a longer period of illness, their child died. Downcast and homesick, they
finally continued their journey and arrived at Zeeland in the month of May,
1903. Here they met with relatives and friends, who had preceded them to this
new land.

        Mom and Dad

As soon as they had recovered from their journey, they began looking for some
farm land. This they found and homesteaded.

The first person Mr. Schmierer met in Wishek was John Wishek, Sr., who helped
him file his land claim. Doyle and Fischer owned a store and machine agency.

Mr. and Mrs. Schmierer continued to farm until the fall of 1947, when they
purchased a home in Wishek.

Their children are: John, Peter, Gottlieb of Wishek; Elizabeth (Mrs. Edwin
Dockter), Venturia, N. Dak., and Fred of Bismarck. N. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. John Thurn

Born in Kassel, South Russia, on September 19, 1864, John Thurn came to this
country in October, 1884, with his brother Friedrich and brother-in-law
Friedrich Meidinger. He was the son of George Peter Thurn and Elizabeth, nee
Schneidt.

Spending the winter at Menno, South Dakota, he met and married Barbara
Schnabel, the daughter of George and Barbara (Schwindt) Schnabel. The wedding
took place on March 8, 1885. Her birthday was September 16, 1864. Mrs. Schnabel
had come to the United States in 1874 with her father and brothers and had
settled in South Dakota.

Two months after their marriage, the Thurns left for the north, going to
Ipswich by train, and thence by wagon and team to Eureka and Zeeland. After
spending a few days looking for a suitable location, they finally chose some
land, lying along the Beaver Creek, southwest of Wishek.

Children born to this union were the following: John J., Wishek; Rosina (Mrs.
Gottlieb Dockter), Venturia; Elizabeth (Mrs. Henry P. Wiest), Wishek; Fred,
deceased, Wishek; Christof, Ashley; Jacob, deceased Albert, Zeeland; Katherina
(Mrs. Edward Just), Wishek; and Andreas of Zeeland.

December, 1926, Mr. and Mrs. Thurn retired and moved to Zeeland. Mr. Thurn
passed away at Zeeland on October 15, 1942, and was buried in St. Andreas
Cemetery near Zeeland. The Thurns had been members of this congregation since
its founding. After her husband's death, Mrs. Thurn made her home with her
daughter, Mrs. Henry P. Wiest, until last year. She now lives with another
daughter, Mrs. Edward Just, in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. John J. Thurn

Born in McIntosh County but still a pioneer, is John J. Thurn, the son of John
Thurn, Sr., and Barbara, nee Schnabel. He was born at the farm home of his
parents on March 28, 1886. Because he was the oldest child in the family, much
work was expected of him, so that his education was very much limited.

He married Katherina Wanner, daughter of Jacob and Christina Martel Wanner, who
had been born on October 6, 1891. The date of the marriage was November 30,
1909. In the year before their marriage, Mr. Thurn had laid claim to a
homestead in Perkins County, South Dakota, near the present town of Faith. The
newly-married couple spent the winter with their parents, but in May, 1910,
returned to his land across the Missouri River. But crops were in minus
quantity, so the young family returned to their home community in 1912. Here
they began their farming operations anew. Their last farm lies 12 miles west of
Wishek, along Highway 13.

Mr. and Mrs. Thurn retired in 1946 and moved to Wishek. Their farm was turned
over to their son, Jacob.

The Thurns are members of the Lutheran Church. Mr. Thurn served the Jacobus
congregation west of Wishek as deacon and Sunday School teacher. He was also a
Director of Friedens School District #26.

Mr. and Mrs. Thurn are the parents of seven children: Ida Marie (Mrs. Theo.
Woehl); Otto Paul, a Veteran of World War II, killed in action in France, July
27, 1944; Esther (Mrs. Simon Rueb); Adam Jacob, Alvin Gottlieb, Lydia (Mrs.
Martin Hagel), and Wilma at home.

Mr. and Mrs. George Thurn

George Thurn, the son of Friederich Karl and Margaret Obenauer Thurn, was born
April 20, 1879, at Neudorf, Russia. When he was thirteen years old he came to
America with the rest of the family. His parents homesteaded near Eureka.

In 1900 Mr. Thurn was married to Friedericka Nehlich, daughter of Solomon and
Rosina Schumacher Nehlich. She was born March 21, 1879. They filed a claim ten
miles northwest of Wishek where they lived for seven years.

When this newly married couple settled here they had no money and had nothing
to trade, Mr. Thurn went to Wishek hoping to get a sack of flour on credit, he
came home empty handed. A few days later a new store opened, this man knew Mr.
Thurn and gave him the much needed flour, for which they could not pay until
they had harvested their first crop.

A prairie fire swept the plains that fall, lack of fuel became the settlers'
greatest problem. The chips, reeds and the slough hay which was twisted and
used for fuel were all destroyed. Mr. Thurn and his brother Philip drove to Red
Lake where they picked chips on a cattle ranch, there they also got a load of
dead trees and brush. This fuel was used so sparingly that frost covered the
bedding when their first child was born.

There was no market for produce, there were no schools or churches. The nearest
doctor was at Ashley. When the diphtheria epidemic broke out he came and
inoculated a few children. The Thurns couldn't have their child inoculated
because they didn't have the money. Luckily she escaped the dreaded disease but
many children died. There were no cemeteries so they were buried in a comer of
the garden. Disappointments were many but like the other staunch hearted
pioneers they bore their burdens without complaining.

In 1907 the Thurns moved seven miles east of Eureka. They had eight children:
Lydia, Paulina Schumacher, Aberdeen, South Dakota; Rosa Hoffman, Long Lake,
South Dakota; George, Amelia Hoffman, Long Lake, South Dakota; Bertha Wolff,
Eureka, South Dakota; Rudolph G., Eureka, South Dakota; Frieda Ottmar,
Greenway, South Dakota.

In 1936 Mr. and Mrs. Thurn retired and moved to Eureka. Mr. Thurn died May 13,
1947. His widow resides in Eureka.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Krein

Jacob Krein was born December 18, 1858, at Neudorf, South Russia. His parents
were Micheal Krein and Barbara Krein, nee Gehring. He married Margaret Fischer,
who was born January 8, 1859, at Russia. Her father's name was David Fischer.
Ten children were born to them. One of them, Carolina Rudolf, died. The
remaining children are John, Ferdinand, Kathrina Springer, and Christina
Vossler of Wishek, Henry and Gustave of Arlgan, Eva Springer of Bismarck, and
Christ of American Falls, Idaho.

In 1885, Mr. Krein came to this country and settled 12 miles southwest of
Wishek. Mr. Krein died December 1, 1924, and Mrs. Krein died March 10, 1941.

Mr. Krein was a member of the Reformed Church, where he was a deacon for many
years. When Mr. Krein began farming here, he had to break sod with oxen, beat
the grain, followed with a horsepowered thresher, then the steamer.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred Ritter

Fred Ritter was born June 27, 1863, in Glucksdahl, South Russia. He was the son
of George and Kathrina Ritter. On November 30, 1884, he was married to
Fredricka Kolle in South Russia, she was the daughter of John and Katharina
Kolle, nee Kessel. To this union were born twelve children, they are: Kathrina
(Bellon), Lehr, North Dakota; Fred, deceased; Jacob, Wishek, North Dakota;
John, Minneapolis, Minnesota; Christ, Wishek, North Dakota; Fredricka
(Burlack), Burnstad, North Dakota; Carl, Stockton, California; William, Wishek,
North Dakota; and Edwin, Sacramento, California.

Mr. and Mrs. Ritter came to Eureka, South Dakota, in 1898. In August, 1902,
they located on a homestead twelve miles northwest of Wishek, North Dakota. On
this homestead they erected a sod house. They purchased the lumber for the roof
of the sod house from C. H. Suess, Manager of North Star Lumber Co. Their
groceries were purchased from Doyle and Fischer, the first store in Wishek.

In the winter of 1903 the snow was deep, about four feet on the level. Ofttimes
the house and barn were covered with snow. They had to dig a tunnel to the barn
and well. Like all the early settlers, the Ritter family endured severe
hardships, having only three horses and two oxen for power to farm with.

Mrs. Ritter passed away in Wishek, North Dakota, in March, 1943. Mr. Ritter now
lives with his son-in-law and daughter, Mr. and Mrs. John Bellon, at Lehr,
North Dakota.

Friedrich Lang

Friedrich Lang was born on July 27, 1869, in Neustadt, South Russia, a son of
Jacob and Christina (Sayler) Lang. In 1889 he came to America, to Menno, South
Dakota, and from there to Wishek, N. Dak. He worked for his uncle, Friedrich
Sayler, who lived 6 miles northwest of Wishek, until 1892.

That year, 1892, Mr. Lang was united in marriage with Katherina Ackerman and
they moved to a homestead. They were blessed with 13 children, ten of whom are
living: Katharina Kienzle, Lydia Eissinger, Emma Hildenbrand, Theodor Lang,
Martha Rath, Christina Eissinger, Albert Lang, Helene Rueb, Edwin Lang, all of
Wishek; and Bertha Ruff of Fredonia, N. Dak.

On the homestead Mr. and Mrs. Lang had many trials but through much
perseverance, hard work and careful saving, were able to accumulate 1,160 acres
of land. Many and varied were the hardships, especially the long, cold winters
and the long trips to Eureka to market the grain and to buy the necessities of
life. The first time Mr. Lang saw Wishek was in the summer of 1898, when he
drove into town on a Sunday afternoon. The first people he knew in Wishek were
Mr. Doyle, Mr. Fischer, Mr. Klein, and Mr. Volk. In 1931 Mother Lang was laid
to rest, having lived a full and useful life.

In 1932 Mr. Lang re-married, this time to Karolina Buechler, nee Boschee, of
Goodrich, N. Dak. In 1942, Mr. and Mrs. Lang retired from farming and moved
into Wishek where they now live. Mr. Lang is a member of the Reformed Church of
Wishek.

        Road Building Scene with Horses Near Wishek

Mr. and Mrs. Fredrich F. Eckman

Fredrich F. Eckman was born in South Russia on March 10, 1879. His parents were
Fredrich Eckman and Louise Eckman, nee Kerhec. Mr. Eckman married Christiana
Weisser in November of 1901. She was born on March 10, 1881, in South Russia.
Her parents were Mathies Weisser and Katherina Weisser, nee Heib.

Mr. Eickman and his wife came to America and settled in McIntosh County in
1903. Seven children were born to this marriage, namely: Ottiala Just, August,
Edward, and Theodore Eckman, Lydia Just, Paulina Meidinger, and Magdlina Brown.
One of the children died.

Mr. Eckman first saw Wishek in April of 1903. The first people he knew here
were Mr. Fischer, John Doyle, and Mr. Pratt. His first business transaction in
America was to acquire land to begin farming. The Eckmans experienced hardships
in the years of 1911, 1933, 1934, 1935, 1936, during which time there were no
crops.

Mr. and Mrs. Henry Schmitt

Henry Schmitt was born November 19, 1880, in Bergdorf, South Russia. He came to
America in October, 1889, with his parents, Heinrich and Magdalena Schmitt, nee
Deyle.

Mr. Schmitt married Christiana Bender, February 3, 1903, at Leola, S. Dak. In
1903, Mr. and Mrs. Schmitt came to Wishek and established their home 7 miles
west of Wishek. Their first home was built of sod, and later they built one of
lumber. They sold their farm machinery and stock in 1910, and then started a
butcher shop with Dan Nickisch. Later he sold his share to Nickisch, and
started a furniture store and bought cream as a sideline. They added an
implement shop to this venture. The furniture store burned in 1916. Later he
traded the Implement Shop to A. W. Herr for land and farmed on this until 1942.
Mr. and Mrs. Schmitt then retired and moved to Wishek. In 1945 they moved to
Lodi, Calif., where they are now making their home.

Eleven children were born to this union. They are: Henry, Wishek, N. Dak.;
Jacob, Wishek, N. Dak.; Gottlieb, Wishek, N. Dak.; Aaron, Lodi, Calif.;
Magdaline (Mrs. Oster), Jamestown, N. Dak.; Ester (Mrs. Albrecht), Bismarck, N.
Dak.; Lydia (Mrs. Albrecht), Bismarck, N. Dak.; Wilma (Mrs. Stock), Wishek, N.
Dak.; Martha (Mrs. Kramer), Wishek, N. Dak.; Anna, Fargo, N. Dak.; Irene (Mrs.
Vette), Moorehead, Minn.

Mr. Schmitt first saw Wishek in 1900. His worst misfortune was in 1943, when a
tornado blew away four of his buildings on the farm. The dry years were another
hardship, but the poor years were surpassed by the rich blessings of the many
good years.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred Eckman, Sr.

The careers of Fred Eckman and his wife, Louise Kercher Eckman, came to a close
within a year of one another.

Due to injuries in a car accident, the old pioneer passed away in November,
1934, at the home of his grandson, August Eckman. His faithful helpmate
preceded him in August, 1933.

Both were natives of the village of Kassel, South Russia. He was born in
August, 1856, and his wife in October of 1858.

In 1903 Mr. and Mrs. Eckman and four children embarked on the long journey to
the new world with Zeeland, N. Dak., as their destination. In 1905 they moved
on a homestead fourteen miles southwest of Wishek. This was their home for a
long period of years after which time they retired and moved to Wishek.

Eight children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Eckman of whom four died in the old
country and one, Louise, Mrs. Christ Roth, who died in Wishek, 1947. Surviving
the aged pioneers are Katherina, Mrs. Weible of Bismarck, N. Dak.; Carolina,
Mrs. Jacob Ketterling, of Rupert, Idaho; and Fred, of Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. Eckman were lifelong members of the Lutheran church.

Mr. and Mrs. Philip G. Mueller

Philip G. Mueller was born in 1852 to George and Carolina Mueller, nee Leuzar,
at Groslieblensthal, South Russia. In 1876 he was united in marriage to Rosina
Goebel, a daughter of Philip and Rosina Goebel, nee Dehnen. Mrs. Mueller was
born in 1851.

This pioneer couple suffered many hardships in this country, but found
satisfaction in the democratic ways of life.

On May 16, 1889, they migrated to America. For 19 years they lived at Eureka,
S. Dak. On their homestead they had the trials and tribulations that many young
married couples had during their first years in a strange and new country.

After their 19 years at Eureka, they moved to Wishek. They first stopped at the
home of Daniel Nickisch Sr. The only store at that time was owned by Doyle and
Fischer and was located on the Kramer Implement corner.

In 1910 Mr. Mueller went in partnership with Philip Thurn selling flour and
buying cream. Later he bought the cream station now owned by Christ F. Krein.

To this union 6 children were born; one child died in infancy: Philip G.,
deceased; John F. of Wishek; Martin H. of Wishek, Elizebeth, Mrs. John Sieler,
of Wishek; Rose, Mrs. Fred Herr, of Moorehead, Minn.

On March 23, 1933, Mr. Mueller died in the hospital at Bismarck, N. Dak. Mrs.
Mueller passed away on May 27 of the same year.

Charles Roehm

Charles Roehm was born November 1, 1852, in Zetel, Oldenburg, Germany. He was
married to Rebecca Stegeman in 1882 at Parkers Prairie, Minnesota. To this
union were born ten children who are: John G. (deceased); Anna L. (deceased);
Wm. A., Enderlin, N. Dak.; Ida M. (Bastien), Easton, Ill.; Charles D., Buffalo,
Wyoming; George J., Richmond, Calif.; Frank W., Wishek, N. Dak.; Fred G., Great
Falls, Mont.; Lucille M., Eugene, Oregon; Richard R., Anaheim, Calif.

Charles Roehm came to this country in 1880, locating at Parkers Prairie, Minn.,
where he and his brother George operated a blacksmith shop. In April, 1886, Mr.
and Mrs. Roehm and children, John and Anna, came to McIntosh County, making the
trip with oxen in a covered wagon. They located on the west shore of Green Lake.

Mr. Roehm saw the town of Wishek at its founding in the fall of 1898. He was
operating a ranch and blacksmith shop at his home on the banks of Green Lake.
He operated the first blacksmith shop for homesteaders and ranchers, serving
homesteaders and ranchers within a radius of twenty miles. In the late 80's he
also gathered buffalo bones and hauled them to Ellendale, his nearest trading
post. In the winter of 1898, during the diphtheria epidemic, he walked to
Hoskins through deep snow in subzero weather for medicine for his sick
children. One daughter, Anna, died during the epidemic.

John H. Wishek, Sr., Patrick and John Doyle were some of the first men Mr.
Roehm had business transactions with in Wishek, having been acquainted with
these men before the founding of Wishek. Later there were A. P. Guy, attorney;
Frank Spurzem, first depot agent; "Uncle" Ed. Lunn, R. J. Bailey, Mrs. Jennie
Hill, M. E. Pratt, first postmaster, and the Nickisch family.

In 1902 Mr. Roehm moved his ranch to the east side of Lake May, the site of the
present home of his son, Frank W. This location is the historical site of the
first Post Office, named Youngstown, which served the sparsely settled
community until Wishek was started.

Charles Roehm was not a grain farmer, but was a stockman. He acted on his
belief that cattle and sheep were a safer and more profitable crop and with
free range and unrestricted his herds often numbered between four and five
hundred head.

On February 18, 1908, Mrs. Charles Roehm passed away on their ranch home near
Wishek, N. D. In 1910 Mr. Roehm established a meat market in Wishek which he
operated for several years but later returned to the ranch where he lived until
the time of his death on March 26, 1915.

Mr. and Mrs. Heinrich Bader

Heinrich Bader, son of Christian and Johanna Bader, was born November 13, 1876,
at South Russia. He accompanied his parents to America in 1884, locating near
Scotland, South Dakota. In June, 1885, Mr. Bader came to McIntosh County,
locating fifteen miles southwest of Wishek, where he was married to Kathrina
Klein on November 9, 1899. Kathrina Klein was the daughter of Jacob and
Christina Klein, nee Wittmaier, born June 20, 1880, at South Russia.

Mr. and Mrs. Bader are the parents of ten children, the following survive:
Christian, Lehr, North Dakota; Heinrich, Venturia, North Dakota; Anton, Wishek,
North Dakota; William, Wishek, North Dakota; Christina (Bauer), Wishek, North
Dakota; Kathrina (Bier), Minneapolis, Minnesota; Martha (Bier), Minneapolis,
Minnesota; and Frieda, Stockton, California.

During the early years when Wishek was started, Mr. Bader remembers Stephen
Fischer, John Doyle, R. J. Bailey and Gottlieb Nickisch. Mr. Bader hauled grain
to Doyle's warehouse.

Mr. Bader experienced severe hardships during the early years due to drought,
low prices and severe winters. With a firm and steadfast conviction, he became
a successful farmer.

Mr. Bader has always been an active and faithful member of the Lutheran Church.
He also was a member of the School Board in his district for several years. In
1943 Mr. and Mrs. Bader retired from farming and moved to Wishek, North Dakota.

Mr. and Mrs. Frederick Meidinger

One of the early pioneers of McIntosh County was Frederick Meidinger. He was
born in Kassel, South Russia, on May 18 1859. He was a son of Jacob and
Katherina Meidinger, nee Reich. On February 1, 1883, he was united in marriage
to Katherina Thurn, daughter of George Peter and Elizabetha Thurn, nee
Schnaidt. Twelve children were born; three died in infancy. The other nine are:
Andreas Meidinger of Wishek (deceased); Jacob Meidinger of Heil, N. Dak.;
Ferdinand Meidinger of Wishek; Maria (Mrs. August Rudolf) of Wishek; Katherina
(Mrs. Henry Dockter) of Zeeland, N. Dak.; Albert Meidinger of Wishek; Otto
Meidinger of Wishek; Paul Meidinger, Hawaiian Islands; Rosina (Mrs. Edward
Herr) of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Meidinger left the old country on October 21, 1884. They spent the
first winter at Tripp, S. Dak., where a colony of their own people had already
settled. They did any kind of work that was available to make a living.
Desiring a permanent home, they purchased a team of oxen, a wagon, and a cow,
and in the spring moved northward, arriving in McIntosh County on May 5, 1885.
They homesteaded on land about 20 miles southwest of Wishek. The first three
years in this community were not very successful because crops failed to mature.
For this reason, they were compelled to gather buffalo bones to sell in order
to make a living. The year of 1888 brought fields of waving grain.

This pioneer family encountered countless hardships and discomforts. Prairie
fires were not uncommon and proved to be a real hazard because there was little
protection against fires. Unfriendly Indians still roamed the prairies. The
nearest town was Ipswich, S. Dak., 75 miles away. A round trip to town and back
took nearly a week with the oxen.

Religious services were held in the farm homes because there were, as yet, no
churches. The Meidingers were brought up in, and held fast to, the Lutheran
faith. They were one of the organizers of the St. Andrew's Lutheran Church in
their community.

Mr. Meidinger was a prominent man in his vicinity. He served on the school
board for many years, and was also county commissioner of his district from
1910-1914.

After 37 years of strenuous farm life on the homestead, Mr. and Mrs. Meidinger
moved to Wishek in the fall of 1922 where they lived until they were called by
death.

Mr. Meidinger passed away at his home in Wishek on March 28, 1936. He was laid
to rest in the burial grounds of his own community church, 20 miles southwest
of Wishek. Mrs. Meidinger passed away on November 2, 1947. She was laid to rest
beside her husband.

Mr. and Mrs. Nick Humann

Nick Humann, the son of Nickolaus Humann and his wife, Johanna Ziegler, was
born May 11, 1882, in South Russia. On November 20, 1903, he married Paulina,
the daughter of Matteis Breitling of Napoleon, N. D. To this marriage there
were 14 children born, 8 boys and 6 girls: Emil, Berwyn, Ill.; Arthur, Miami,
Florida; William, Cumberland, Wash.; Johanna, Mrs. Foerderer, Wishek, N. D.;
Edwin, Wishek, N. D.; Ida, Mrs. Scheirmeister, Hazelton, N. D.; Henry,
Burnstad, N. D.; Pauline, Wishek, N. D.; Reinhold, Napoleon, N. D.; Viola,
Winnetka, Ill.; Alma, Mrs. Ritter, Wishek, N. D.

Wishek was first seen by Mr. Humann in 1901, when he located in this community,
Logan County, 9 miles northwest of Wishek, on a farm. Death came to his wife on
January 2, 1927. He re-married on October 25, 1927, taking for his wife
Katherina Herr, daughter of Jacob Herr, Sr., and his wife Margaretha Krein. To
them was born one daughter, Izathe M. Humann.

After his second marriage they remained on the farm for 8 more years and then
moved to Wishek where they lived for 10 years. They now reside in Salem, Oregon.

Before filing on his homestead Mr. Humann worked for John G. Herr. His first
acquaintances were John G. Herr, Fred Thurn, Christ Krein, Sr., and family,
Jacob Krein, Jacob Schauer, Sr., Jacob Herr, and A. W. Herr.

Mr. Humann saw his share of trials and hardship but fought his way bravely
through. In 1906 a windstorm demolished his barn. In 1916 lightning killed 8
head of cattle for him. He was hailed out at several different times. A tornado
destroyed all his farm buildings, except the house, even that had most of the
windows broken; in 1927 his house burned down with all furnishings in it. In
this same year his wife died and left him with a large family of children to be
cared for. Then in 1933 another tragedy left its mark for his son, Theodore,
was accidentally shot while away on a visit. Mr. and Mrs. Humann have chosen for
themselves a home on the West Coast. May their remaining years be bright and
happy

Mr. and Mrs. Emanuel Michaelsohn

Emanuel Michaelson was born in Israelofki, South Russia, on April 20, 1868. At
the age of eighteen years, Emanuel came to America with his parents, Andreas
Michaelsohn and Maria Koth.

In 1891, he was united in marriage to Elizabeth Moos of Greenway, S. Dak.
Elizabeth's birthday was on October 9th, 1873. To this union were born eleven
children, six of whom are living. The family lived ten miles southwest of
Wishek.

As many farmers, Mr. Michaelson made moderate progress in farming. Many times
there were no crops, and many hardships had to be met. He made three trips to
Ellendale. The first trip he walked to the city, to ask for a job. With the
money earned he purchased food. The second trip was made with oxen, and he
picked buffalo bones which were traded for food. The third trip was made with a
model T Ford to take his son to school. Mr. Michaelsohn came to this community
in 1899 and did much of his trading in Wishek.

On July 5, 1927, Mrs. Michaelson died at their farm home.

Mr. Michaelsohn moved into Wishek and retired from the farm. He was called to
rest on November 27, 1927, at his home in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Michaelsohn were ardent church workers and were members of the
Baptist Church of rural Wishek.

The family includes Mrs. Alvina Berendt, Avon, S. Dak.; William E., of Fargo,
N. Dak.; Herbert E., of Carlton, Ore.; Esther Hauck of Tyndall, S. Dak., and
Fred and Edwin of rural Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. David Foerderer

David Foerderer was born June 17, 1884, in South Russia. His parents were
Johann and Magdalena Foerderer, nee Diegel. He came to this country with his
parents and brothers, Frank, John, Jacob and his sister, Rosina, in May, 1889.
The parents homesteaded in this area.

Mr. Foerderer recalls the Indian scare November 20, 1890. It was a lovely day
when the terrifying rumor came that the Indians were coming to massacre all
white men. There were no means of communication, but the news spread fast. Mr.
Foerderer judges the rumor covered an area of about 150 square miles in three
or four hours. The settlers were badly frightened. Their only thought was to
escape. Some of the pioneers had no oxen or horses, so they set out on foot.
Some were barefoot, but all hurried away. Mr. Foerderer's parents had oxen, so
they loaded everything they could and started for Eureka, S. Dak. After they
had gone about four miles they found an abandoned farm. Next they came to the
John Meidinger farm. The men had just returned from the Missouri River with 3
wagon loads of cottonwood posts. It was about eight or nine o'clock and a clear
night, so the group decided to spend the night at the farm. Having no weapons
they dragged forks, hatchets and hammers into the sod house and waited
fearfully all night. Morning came at last, with no signs of the dreaded
Indians, so the settlers returned to their homes.

On June 26, 1892, Mr. Foerderer made a trip to Eureka with his parents, to
attend the dedication of the first Lutheran Church. This trip was made by wagon
and oxen.

There were no schools but later they sometime had school a month or two in the
year.

On March 24, 1905, Mr. Foerderer was married to Christana Sayler at Wishek. She
was the daughter of Jacob and Christina Kautz Sayler.

They were the parents of eleven children: Christian Foerderer, Elk Grove,
Calif.; Helen Foerderer, Yakima, Wash.; Henry Foerderer, Wishek, N. Dak.;
Frieda, Mrs. Fred Schauer, Elk Grove, Calif.; Christine, Mrs. John W. Rath,
Burnstad, N. Dak.; Alma, Mrs. Paul Schatz, Elk Grove, Calif.; Pauline, Mrs.
Gottlieb Schauer, Elk Grove, Calif.; Edward Foerderer, Wishek, N. Dak. Deceased
are: Alvina (Mrs. John Rath), Magdalene (Mrs. Jacob Rath), and David Foerderer.

In 1908, the Foerderer's located southwest of Wishek, Sec. 34, T. 132, R. 73.

They were members of the Friedens Lutheran Church of which Mr. Foerderer took
an active part. He also served as assessor of the third Commissioner's District
for two years and acted as school clerk and treasurer for twelve years in
Friedensthal School District. In 1937, he served as page in the Legislature of
the House of Representatives in North Dakota.

The Foerderers have retired from the farm and now reside in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Christ L. Vossler

Christ L. Vossler was born to Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Vossler on July 29, 1888,
in McIntosh County. He lived in this county until 1912, from where he moved to
Miles City, Montana. He settled on a homestead, located about 57 miles north of
Miles City. While they were moving back from Montana they traveled with a
covered wagon and also rode on horseback much of the way. Some of the colts got
sore feet from walking and had to be taken into the wagon part of the way. When
darkness came they would spend the evenings around a campfire with the
delicious smell of coffee and bacon surrounding them.

He was united in marriage to Maria Schwindt, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Simon
Schwindt, in December, 1914. They settled on his father's farm, 15 miles north
of Zeeland in 1919. Three children were born to this union. They are: Laura,
Mrs. Edwin Barette, Herried, S. Dak.; Irene, Mrs. Elmer Raile, Billings,
Montana; Ione, Los Angeles, Calif.

Mr. and Mrs. Vossler now reside in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried Stevahn, Sr.

Gottfried Stevahn was born in Kassel, South Russia, December 2, 1881. His
parents were Andrew Stevahn and Christine (Meidinger) Stevahn.

On February 22, 1905, he married Christine Herr, also of Kassel, South Russia.
Christine Herr was born October 14, 1882. Her parents were George Herr and
Elizabeth (Lehr) Herr.

Mr. and Mrs. Stevahn entered this country in 1905 and settled in this community
the same year. They settled 14 miles west of Wishek, and remained there for 14
years. They then moved to a farm 4 miles east of Wishek.

To this union 7 children were born, one of which died. Those living are: Otto
S., John, Emil, Adam, Lydia (Mrs. Ferdinand Thurn), and Gottfried, Wishek, N.
D., and Adam Ray, N. D.

The first time Mr. Stevahn saw Wishek was in 1906. He recalls his first
business transaction was with John Herr and that was to purchase furniture.

Their first summer living quarters were in a barn.

Early acquaintances of Mr. Stevahn's were: Adam Schnable, Adam Meidinger and
Andrew Stevahn.

Mr. Stevahn died in 1941. Mrs. Stevahn resides in Wishek.

Y. Adam Meidinger

Y. Adam Meidinger was born October 14, 1882, in Kassel, South Russia. He was
the son of Adam and Magdalena, nee Krause Meidinger.

In 1884 he came to this country with his parents. Their first stop was at
Tripp, S. Dak. The southern part of Dakota Territory was pretty well settled and
the available land was all taken, so the immigrants began to go north where
land was still open to homestead, pre-emption and tree claim entry. The
Meidingers did likewise and came to this community in 1885 and settled on Sec.
6, T. 130, R. 72. This is about sixteen miles from the site which later became
Zeeland.

In 1906 Mr. Meidinger married Dorothea Maier. She was born August, 1885, the
daughter of Christ Maier and Dorothea Aipperspach Maier.

They are the parents of the following children: Ida Emma Meidinger, Wishek, N.
Dak.; Paul Meidinger, Venturia, N. Dak.; Mrs. Ted Schilling, Venturia, N. Dak.,
Gottfried, Venturia, N. Dak.; Walter Elmer Meidinger, Venturia, N. Dak.

Mr. Meidinger farmed on his father's farm for sixty-three years. They retired
and are now living in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Simon Schwindt

Simon Schwindt was born May 2, 1858, in Gluecksthal, South Russia. He was the
son of Simon and Eva Schwindt, nee Schneider. In 1878 he was married to
Christina Wittmeier at Klein-Bergdorf, South Russia.

To this union were born twelve children. The following survive: Christina
(Mittelstad), Fallen, Mont.; Louisa (Dockter), Venturia, N. Dak.; Rosina (Opp),
Wishek, N. Dak., Magdalina (Just), Wishek, N. Dak.; Emma (Meidinger), Zeeland,
N. Dak.; Maria (Vossler), Wishek, N. Dak.; Simon, Wishek;' Martha (Meidinger).
Wishek; Carolina (Riech), Sacramento, Calif.;

Henry, Zeeland, N. Dak.; and Ida (Rattei), Minneapolis, Minn.

Mr. and Mrs. Schwindt came to this country in 1886, locating on a homestead
twelve miles north of Zeeland, N. Dak.

On June 14, 1914, Mrs. Schwindt passed away. Later Mr. Schwindt was married to
widow Katharina Brandner, nee Wittmeier. She passed away April 1, 1922.

In 1923 he moved to Wishek, where he married widow Jakobina Holzwarth, nee
Bauer. She passed away in 1938.

From that time on he lived with his son-in-law, Christ Vossler.

Mr. Schwindt was one of the founders of the Reformed New Kassel congregation.

He passed away July 5, 1947, in Wishek, N. Dak.

Jacob Woehl

Jacob Woehl was born October 8, 1849, at Rohrbac, South Russia, son of Peter
and Katharine Woehl Breneise, who was born in 1855 at South Russia, a daughter
of George and Elizabetha Breneise.

To this happy union were born 10 children. They are: Margaretha, Mrs. Adam
Bollinges, of Medina, N. Dak.; Jacob Woehl, deceased; Henry Woehl of Wishek;
Christina, Mrs. Andreas Hoff, of Aberdeen, S. Dak., George Woehl, of Lodi,
Calif.; Sofina, Mrs. Adam Haas, of Ashley, N. Dak.; John, of Lodi, Calif.;
Fred, of Wishek.

Mrs. Woehl passed away at her home on the farm on May 22, 1891, and Mr. Woehl
on November 20, 1933. Two of their children preceded them in death.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Vossler

Jacob Vossler was born January 26, 1884, in South Russia, Bessarabia. Son of
Fredrick and Freidrecka Vossler. In 1885 he came to this country with his
parents and settled at Scotland, S. Dak. A year later they moved 17 miles
southwest of Wishek. July 5, 1927, he married Eva Becker at Lehr. To this union
were born two children: Milton J., of Wishek; Herbert A., of Wishek.

He first saw Wishek in 1898 when he was working for the railroad leveling the
land. Quitting this job he became the manager of the Farmers Union Elevator in
Zeeland. He was also on the Town Board for 4 years.

Ill health forced Mr. Vossler to quit his work at Zeeland, and the family moved
to Wishek. His wife passed away January 13, 1944.

Mr. Vossler and his two sons are still residing here in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob J. Brosy

Jacob J. Brosy was born on March 30, 1883, in Kassel, South Russia, the son of
Ludwig and Eva (Bentz) Brosy. Mr. Brosy came to America with his parents in
1887. On November 24, 1904, he was married to Katherina Bauer, daughter of Mr.
and Mrs. Christian Bauer, early settlers of the community. In the year 1906, he
settled on a homestead ten miles west of Wishek, where he built a sod house for
his family. In 1927, a large frame house was constructed to replace the sod
house originally built. Some of the first people he came to know in this
community were John Sayler, Christ Bier, Sr., and Gottfried Werre.

Mr. Brosy was a progressive farmer and a leader in his community. He served as
a director of his school district for a number of years and was County
Commissioner from 1933 to 1936. He was an enthusiastic member of the board of
directors of the Tri County Fair for many years. His greatest pride were his
horses, his cattle, and his well kept farm.

The Brosy's had ten children, five boys and five girls, two having died in
infancy. The living children are Jacob, Jr., Mrs. Katherine Hochhalter, Mrs. Eva
Heyne, Mrs. Martha Heyne, and Martin of Wishek Otto, Fredonia, Ludwig,
Billings, Montana, and Mrs. Clara Ketterling, Dubuque, Iowa.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Herr, Sr.

In 1887 came a man to McIntosh County who was destined, through his honest
dealings as a successful farmer, cattle trader, and merchant, to become known
and respected throughout the entire county. That man was Jacob Herr, Sr.

Born in Kassel, South Russia, on July 25, 1851, as the son of Mr. and Mrs.
George Herr, nee Hagele, his boyhood years were spent attending the Village
School and assisting his father on the farm.

When he reached manhood, he was united in marriage with Margaretha Krein. Being
dissatisfied with his small tract of land, and having the desire to farm on a
larger scale, he decided to leave his native country and seek his fortune in
America.

In 1876, accompanied by his wife and small son, Jacob, he set forth on the new
venture, and after a successful voyage they arrived at Hutchinson County, S.
Dak. They filed on a homestead four miles east of Tripp, S. Dak. At the time,
their total possessions consisted of bedding, clothing, and about $200.00 in
cash. After settling here for a time, they heard of land further north in the
Dakota Territory which could be bought cheaply. With this in mind, Mr. Herr
sold his three quarters near Tripp, for the sum of $4,800. Early in the spring
of 1887, accompanied by his wife and 6 children, they traveled by train to
Bowdle, S. Dak. There, loading the property on wagons, followed by the
livestock on foot, they set forth in a northern direction to a place about 7
miles southwest of the present site of Wishek. Here they set up their necessary
buildings for their home and farming operations.

In this new location Mr. Herr bought and sold livestock and farmed until the
year 1890, when, with his brothers Fred and Conrad, he became interested in the
Mercantile Business in Eureka, S. D., and purchased the merchandise stock of
Ell, Wiesz & Sprenger--only to learn they had been tricked into purchasing a
bankrupt stock. In a very short time, the creditors took over, and losing
everything, Mr. Herr went back to the farm west of Wishek which he still owned.
With his keen foresight and constant laboring he increased his herd of
livestock. This success brought the problem of pasture and hay.

In his search for this type of land, he soon found what he wanted, a section of
land nine miles south of Wishek which he purchased from John Doyle in 1894 and
1897. And in the fall of 1898 he moved there. Now he had what he always termed
"the best hay meadow in McIntosh County." This new home filled him with greater
ambition, and in the same year he built the two-story frame house, which still
stands, 2 large frame barns, a frame granary, chicken and hog houses, and a
blacksmith shop. Shortly thereafter he increased his holdings to about 800
acres, using half for farm land and half for dairying and beef production. He
soon owned the most modern machinery of that time, including a threshing
machine and large steam engine, which replaced the old horsepower. He also
supplemented his earnings by acting as postmaster on this farm in 1901.

The losses sustained in Eureka were soon recovered and forgotten, and again in
1904 he decided to enter the general merchandise business, this time in Wishek.
Entering into a partnership with his sons Jacob, Jr. and August W., they
operated under the firm name "Herr Bros. & Co." They purchased the Volk and
Klein property consisting of a stock of merchandise and a store building
located on lot 17 of block 2. In 1906 lot 16 was added and the old building
remodeled into a larger two-story frame, iron-clad building.

At this time furniture and caskets were added, and the following year farm
machinery and the Buick agency was also added. In the early part of the year
1909, this building was totally destroyed by fire. By the fall of the same year
it was replaced by the present brick structure. Although this business had its
ups and downs, it prospered. In 1916 Mr. Herr purchased the interests of the
two sons and the business was re-organized under the name of "Herr Mercantile
Co." Although Mr. Herr was a partner in the store business in 1904 with his
sons, he remained on the farm until the fall of 1907.

Going back to early pioneer days in McIntosh County, the Herr family suffered
the same hardships as did other pioneers--blizzards, prairie fires and Indian
scares.

Mr. and Mrs. Herr were members of the Baptist Church and brought up their
children in this faith. They were the parents of 17 children, seven of whom
died in infancy. The others are as follows: Jacob, Jr., whereabouts unknown,
John, deceased; August W. deceased; Christian J., of Wishek; Edward, of Wishek;
Gottlieb, of Wishek; Christina, Mrs. John W. Koth, of Los Angeles, Cal.;
Magdalena, Mrs. Wm. Pudwill, deceased; Katharina, Mrs. Nick Humann, of Salem,
Ore.; Fred, deceased.

Mrs. Herr passed to the Great Beyond in September, 1912 followed by Mr. Herr in
November, 1916.

Mr. and Mrs. John J. Doyle

Patrick Doyle and his wife-to-be, Elizabeth Conway, were born, reared and
married in Ireland. The newly married couple decided that America offered more
than the old world and so crossed the Atlantic to locate in Minnesota.

The farm life appealed to Patrick, then a young man twenty-two years of age,
and he settled near Medelia, Minn., in Scott County in 1864. Here their two
sons, Thomas and John, were born, John on March 26, 1868. As a child, John
recalled having been placed on a tall tree stump with a rifle in his lap to
scout for Indians while his mother tended to their cabin and his father worked
the field with yoked oxen.

As the boys approached school age the family moved to Redwood Falls, Minn., and
Patrick took employment with the Wells-Fargo Express Company. Communication
facilities were being pushed westward and as advance man Patrick built the log
stockades for the care of personnel and horses over the route from Bismarck to
Deadwood. It was during this period that the "Custer Massacre" (June 25, 1876)
occurred at the Little Big Hom; but in spite of the Indian menace, the lure of
gold in the Black Hills continued to draw a steady stream of adventurers over
the Wells-Fargo Express route.

In the spring of 1880 the Northern Pacific started construction on a roadbed to
extend the route into Montana to a junction with the remainder of the route
being brought eastward from the west coast. The drama of the Iron Horse
spanning the continent from east to west was very much a live issue in the lives
of every person inhabiting the Dakota Territory. Patrick contracted to complete
the surfacing of this roadbed which started from a point approximately forty
miles east of Bismarck.

John, then twelve, was taken to Bozeman, Montana, to continue his schooling and
to work as a printer's devil on the Bozeman newspaper. While so employed it
fell to John the newsboy's lot to deliver the edition of the paper
commemorating the ceremony of "Driving The Golden Spike" to guests of the
railroad, among whom was General Grant. The east and west had been joined.

        JOHN J. DOYLE

Patrick, now forty-three, and son John, seventeen, moved to McIntosh County
where in 1885 he pre-empted a quarter section of land and also took up a tree
claim on a similar tract, locating about ten miles south of, what is now,
Wishek. Here father and son farmed summers while in the winter John attended
Business College in St. Paul, Minn. While attending school John made
acquaintances among the commission houses doing grain business in the cities and
was authorized by one of them to buy grain in Eureka, S. Dak.

In 1889, when John became legally of age he preempted a quarter section of land
and took up a tree claim near his father's. The two then joined in a cattle
business and Patrick moved into Wishek. This arrangement endured for six years
until in 1895 they sold the ranch and cattle. John then built in Wishek what
was called a Flathouse to engage in the grain business. Somewhat later he
joined with Mr. Steven Fisher and they opened a general store on the site now
occupied by the J. J. Kramer Implement Co. Their place of business was known as
the Wishek Bazaar.

Many can yet, no doubt, recall the days when in the winter time the store
served as a place for a night's lodging. The customers traveling their many
miles by bob-sled and finding, after their day's trading, it either too late or
too stormy to return home, would bundle up in their buffalo robes and spend the
night there. A short while later the Hill House was built to accommodate
travelers.

In the Bazaar John's duties were those of bookkeeper. His main interests were
cattle, farm implements and grain. Two or three times a week he would load some
implements on a wagon and travel about the country buying cattle and selling
implements.

In 1903, John sold his interest in the Wishek Bazaar and his implement
business. He went to Richardton and opened the First State Bank and in July of
the same year opened a second bank, the First State Bank of Glen Ullen, and
built a grain elevator and lumber yard at the latter location. In 1906, he sold
these businesses and returned to Wishek where he opened a lumber yard. He
disposed of this business in May of the same year, embarked in the land
business which is in existence today.

In August, 1909, John J. Doyle and Katherine E. Scanlan were married at Fargo,
N. Dak. To this union were born three sons of which two survive, namely: John
M. and Walter J. Doyle.

Katherine, the daughter of Michael and Margaret Hays Scanlan, was born on a farm
near Madison, Wisconsin, the only daughter in a family of four children. The
family moved from Madison to a more promising part of the state and settled on
a farm near Prairie du Chien, the site of an old Army Fort and trading post.
The area was known to be the battleground of the Fox and Kickapoo Indians and
these early settlers had many occasions to be wary of their foraging parties.

Katherine attended school in Wisconsin, Minnesota and North Dakota, receiving
her degree in education from the Valley City State Teachers College. For a time
she taught school but did not feel herself suited to the profession, and upon
the advice of her brother, Dr. Will Scanlan of Fargo, N. Dak., decided to enter
the profession of Pharmacy. Upon graduation from the school of pharmacy she
worked as a pharmacist at various places, finally settling at Volga, S. Dak.
The two brothers, deciding to leave for Oregon, offered their holdings for
sale, but the untimely death of Dr. Will Scanlan acted to postpone the venture.
In the meantime the store at Volga had been sold and Katherine accepted
employment with the firm of Grant and Hofer, which is now known as the Wishek
Drug Co. She arrived in Wishek, August, 1908; the year John J. Doyle was
elected to serve as Representative from McIntosh County in the North Dakota
Legislature.

John Doyle served in the legislature for the terms of 1908, 1910 and 1912.
During his terms of office he advocated the initiative and referendum, exemption
of taxes on farm improvement, and measures designed to promote education and
improve school administration.

In September of 1909 the Security State Bank of Wishek was opened, with John
Doyle serving as President. This organization is continuing with Mrs. K. E.
Doyle as President. At the present time the bank is undergoing extensive
repairs and alterations to continue its uninterrupted service to the community.
The reorganization contemplates improved efficiency and a wider variety of
service to its many patrons.

Herr Brothers Store, then doing business on their present location, were
dealers in Buick automobiles and in 1909 delivered the second automobile sold by
them to John Doyle. He registered and licensed the vehicle in 1910. It is being
maintained today as a family souvenir.

In March, 1914, in conjunction with others, John Doyle organized the Wishek
Lumber Company, which was later purchased by the Sayler Lumber Company. It
occupied the site of the present theater, postoffice and other buildings.

Various elevators were at one time or another included in the Doyle Grain
Company, which at present operates elevators in Wishek, Peters, and Streeter,
N. Dak.

John J. Doyle came to his reward December 31, 1945, and is survived by his wife
and sons.

George Rudolf

When only 8 years old, George Rudolf came with his parents to the United
States. This was in the year 1884. Mr. Rudolf had been born July 16, 1876, the
son of Ludwig and Katherina (Schnabel) Rudolf in Kassel, South Russia. He lived
with his parents on their farm about 10 miles southwest of Wishek. Mr. Rudolf
recalls helping his father dig a well and build several farm buildings on the
home place. Later he went to work on the construction crew, laying the railroad
track, as it left Wishek.

On January 16, 1900, he was united in marriage with Karolina Krein, the
daughter of Jacob and Margaretha (Fischer) Krein. They are the parents of 14
children of whom the following 9 are still living: Karolina (Mrs. George
Olson), Fort Pierre, S. Dak.; Henry American Falls, Idaho; Anna (Mrs. L. A.
Rogers), Vancouver, Washington; Andrew; Martha (Mrs. Christ Ruff); Eva (Mrs.
Elmer Sayler), Samuel; Leopold L.; and Paul E., all of Wishek. Mrs. Rudolf died
on May 3, 1935.

Mr. Rudolf married a second time on November 7, 1939, to Rosina Kemmet. His
second wife passed away on February 16, 1940.

Mr. Rudolf has always been active in school matters. He served as School
Director in his district for nearly 20 years. He and his family have been
members of the Lutheran Church until the present time.

Adam Miller

One of the natural born Americans whose personality impressed itself vividly
upon the Wishek community was Adam Miller, who was a skillful carpenter by
trade, working in the upbuilding of the community during his entire lifetime.
Mr. Miller constructed such places as the Pfeifle garage, Auto Inn Cook's
theatre, Delmer's Hardware and the Red Owl Building. He became ill and died
before the completion of the last two buildings. He died April 19, 1946.

Mr. Miller was born December 11, 1888, in McPherson county in South Dakota. His
parents were Martin Miller and Freida Phfitzer. He was married to Hattie
Kusler, who was born on November 24, 1892. Her parents were John Kusler and
Magdaline Werre. One son, Leo, was born to this union. He now lives here in
Wishek with his mother.

Gottlieb Nickisch Family

Of the men who played an actual part in the founding of Wishek in 1898 only one
man remains, who has spent his entire time since then, here in Wishek. He is
Gottlieb Nickisch. Mr. Nickisch came to Wishek on September 8, 1898, before the
town had even been established.

Born on September 12, 1877, in Sachsen, by Karlsruhe Oberschlesien, Germany,
the son of Daniel Nickisch and Augusta, nee Wieczorek. Mr. Nickisch had been
brought to this country in 1881 by his parents. Most of his childhood years
were spent in South Dakota.

Mr. Nickisch tells that he and his cousin, R. Nickisch, came to Wishek from
Mound City, S. Dak., on September 8, 1898, with two dray teams. Gottlieb had
$15.00 in his pockets. There were no buildings, but the track of the Soo Line
was being laid. One month after he came to Wishek, he put up a small blacksmith
shop. When he was not busy in the shop, he assisted his cousin on the dray
line. When he worked on the dray line, he received $1.25 per day. For hauling
coal he was paid 10c per ton, if the coal was placed in the sheds near the
tracks, or 17 cents if he hauled it up into the town. In the fall of 1899 he
loaded about fifty cars of wheat at 50c per car.

Mr. Nickisch went through some hard and trying times. Buying his tools for his
shop and erecting his building had depleted him financially. Mrs. Hill, owner
of the Hill House, carried his board bill until spring, when he began paying
his bill, a dollar at a time.

In 1901 business was a little better, so he moved his small building to the
back of the lot and erected a larger shop. His brother, Dan, who had now joined
him, and he lived in the small building. In 1904 he sold pumps and windmills,
in addition to running the blacksmith shop. In 1905 he added a full line of farm
machinery.

In December, 1905, he was united in marriage with Katherine Hellmuth. Mrs.
Nickisch was born on February 17, 1886. Her parents were John and Marie
(Selzer) Hellmuth. Mr. and Mrs. Nickisch are the parents of 4 children: Gladys
(Mrs. Dan Krumes), Berwyn, Illinois; Elmo, who has succeeded his father in
business; Wilma (Mrs. Adolph Gall), Wishek, and Florence (Mrs. Al. Fraijo),
Fresno, California.

What is known as the G. Nickisch Co. building was erected in 1916. Furniture,
Undertaking supplies and farm machinery were sold. The blacksmith shop had been
sold to Dan Nickisch and Christ Gohl.

In 1932 Mr. Nickisch's daughter Gladys and son Elmo took out shares in the
business. The partnership was dissolved on January 1, 1946, when son Elmo took
over the entire business.

Mr. Nickisch has retired from active business life, but still spends most of
his time in Wishek. The Nickisch Family has belonged to the Lutheran Church for
many Years.

Jacob Schauer

The month of May, 1886, marks the time when Jacob Schauer first entered this
country and on May 12, 1886, he came to the Wishek community and settled seven
miles west and three miles south of where Wishek now stands.

Mr. Schauer was born on July 30, 1866, and is the son of Martin Schauer and
Carolina, nee Goering. He married Friedericka Kessel, daughter of Johann Kessel
and Magdalena, nee Otterstaedt. To them 10 children were born, 8 boys and 2
girls: Carolina, Wishek, N. D.; John, Wishek, N. D.; Jacob, Egloo, S. D.;
Martin, Elgrove, Calif.; Magdelena, Sacramento, Calif.; Reinhold, Christ, Fred,
and Gottlieb, all of Elk Grove, Calif.

Mr. and Mrs. Christ. F. Krein

As a small boy of 5, Christ. F. Krein came to America from South Russia with
his parents, Christian Krein and Magdalena, nee Schauer. He was born in
Neidorf, South Russia, on January 18, 1881.

On a farm, 8 miles west of Wishek, Christ., his 2 brothers and 2 sisters were
reared. Life was very lonely on the plains until 2 years later Mr. Daniel Meek
became a neighbor, farming 2 miles from the Krein home.

Mr. Krein remembers his first childhood home as a sod house with walls 2 feet
wide, much warmer than the frame houses of today. Stoves being at a premium,
the parents built a heater and cook stove with flat sand stones and mud.
However, after the stoves were made, there was no fuel for the heating, so hay,
wild bushes, and dried manure served the purpose.

For the first 2 years the Krein family had no meat to eat except for the
occasional wild rabbit bagged by a lucky shot with a stone. One winter they ran
out of flour and there were no neighbors from whom they might borrow. The
father made a hand sled and walked 13 miles to get 100 pounds of flour.

On November 22, 1904, wedding bells rang for Mr. Krein and Mary Ackerman. They
moved to a farm 10 miles northwest of Wishek and there farmed for 12 years. In
1916 they moved to Wishek. His first business was a partnership with 2 of his
brothers, Jacob C. Krein and Fred, in an electric light and power plant. In
1924 they sold the business to the Northern Power and Light Company. From
1924-26 Mr. Krein worked for this company.

In 1926 he went into the Flour, Cream, and Coal Business which he is operating
to this date.

Mr. and Mrs. Krein have 5 children: Arthur Krein, Edwin Krein, Miss Alvina
Krein, Mrs. Amelia Herr, and Mrs. Frieda Kleppe.

Johann Foerderer

Johann Foerderer was born March 7, 1847, in South Russia of parents who had
just arrived there from Germany. He was one of the early immigrants into this
country, moving here with his family in 1889. They settled on a homestead on
the western edge of McIntosh County. Like all early pioneers, they endured the
hardships of the new unbroken country. The nearest trading center was Eureka, S.
D., until Wishek was begun in 1898. In 1919 Mr. and Mrs. Foerderer moved to
Wishek where they lived until their deaths. They were the parents of five
children who grew to adulthood.: Franz, Wishek; John, Jacob, Linton, N. D.;
Mrs. Rosina Just, Mercer, N. D.; David, Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. John Dockter

Johann Dockter, a native of Neudorf, South Russia, was born August 17, 1879. He
is the son of Jacob Dockter and his wife, Barbara Meyer. On September 2, 1886,
there was born to Jacob Dockter and Margaretha, nee Krein, a daughter by the
name of Karolina and on December 1, 1904, she became the wife of Johann Dockter
and to them 16 children were born of whom only 4 are still living: Frieda,
Chicago, Ill; Ida, Mrs. Emil Human, Chicago, Ill.; Edwin Dockter, Harvey, N.
D.; Lydia, Chicago, Ill.

In the spring of 1900 Mr. Dockter came to this country and made his first stop
at Eureka, S. D., and then in June of the same year he came to this community
to a farm 16 miles southwest of Wishek. Mrs. Dockter died on January 11, 1933.
He then married Magdalena Schrible, nee Schmidt, in 1934, and two days less 10
years she too was laid to her eternal rest.

Mr. Dockter made his living at various trades. First working as a farm-hand for
Fred Vossler for 4 years. Out of his earnings he bought himself 4 horses, a
wagon, 2 plows and harness. Then he filed on a homestead 1-1/2 miles northeast
of Wishek. In 1908 Mr. Dockter and John Herr began a furniture business which
was of short duration, due to the death of John Herr the following year. Then
Mr. H. Schmidt became his partner in business which also was of short duration,
fire destroying the business. Mr. Schmidt went farming and Mr. Dockter operated
the Doyle elevator in Wishek for 16 years.

Mr. and Mrs. Johann Sprenger, Sr.

Johann Sprenger, Sr., of Wishek, was born in Russia almost a century ago. His
father was Heinrich Jacob Sprenger, and his mother's family name was Ahl.
Johann Sprenger who was married to Katharina Mehlhaff, also of South Russia.
Her parents were Mr. and Mrs. Konrad Mehlhaff. In 1884 the young family
emigrated to Tyndal, S. D., and in 1885 they filed claim on 320 acres of land
about 12 miles southwest of Wishek. Their first home and barn built by Mr.
Sprenger was made of prairie sod. In a few years the prairie fires, which were
the terror of the country, came, burning their house, barn and entire crop. The
hardships of early pioneer life were many. Mr. Sprenger immediately began
working on another home and barn of sod. Through hard work and courage he was
able to provide a fine farm, home and a happy life for his family. Mrs.
Sprenger passed away at her home on the farm in 1898. Mr. Sprenger continued to
live on his farm until 1908 when he died.

The children born of this union are as follows: Katharina (Mrs. Christof
Schmidt), of McLaughlin, S. D.; John Sprenger (deceased); Christina (Mrs. Adam
Junkert), deceased; Magdalina (Mrs. John K. Wanner), Paulina (Mrs. Adolf
Stabner), both of Wishek; Barbara (Mrs. Christ Wanner), of Medina, N. D.;
Elizabeth (Mrs. Fred Sifferman), deceased; and Eva (Mrs. Emanuel Redman), of
Oregon City, Oregon.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob George Ritter

Jacob George Ritter was born in Glusckdahl [Glueckstal], South Russia, on
November 30, 1890, the son of Fredrick and Fredricka (Kelly) Ritter, Sr. He was
married to Rosina Schock of Wishek. She was the daughter of Gottlieb and
Fredricka (Eisenbeis) Schock, Sr. of Ashley. The young couple were engaged in
farming for 1 year and then Mr. Ritter operated a Standard Oil Bulk Station for
4 years. Following this he became manager of the Home Milling Co. and later
secretary-treasurer of Krein Electric. In 1921, Mr. Ritter went into the
electric business for himself. That is still his present occupation.

Mr. and Mrs. Ritter are the parents of 10 children, eight of whom are living:
Mrs. Edna Draheim, Minneapolis, Minn.; Esther, Fargo, N. D.; Ervin, Stockton,
California; Eleanor, Fargo, N. D.; Mrs. Leone Junkert, Wishek; Mrs. Adeline
Duryee, Redding, California; Deloris, Bismarck, N. D.; and Ramona, at Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Martin H. Miller

Martin H. Miller was born September 2, 1883, in Berlin, Germany. He was the son
of Philip G. and Rosina Mueller (nee Goebel). Mr. Miller was married December
1, 1904, to Margaretha Hoff in Eureka, South Dakota. To this union three
children were born, who are Ella (Gruebele), Emma (Gruebele), Frieda
(Schlenker), all at Wishek, North Dakota.

Mr. Miller came to this country on May 16, 1889. He filed on a homestead
fourteen miles northeast of Wishek in Logan County. The first people Mr. Miller
met in Wishek were Dan Nickisch, Sr., John Doyle, Gottlieb and Dan Nickisch,
and M. E. Pratt.

The first years on the homestead were spent in a sod house; it had two windows
and a clay floor. Mr. Miller owned the first two-bottom plow in that community.
The price of the plow was $65.

In 1910 Mr. Miller moved to Wishek. In 1914 he purchased the Wishek Hardware
store, selling same in 1917.

Mr. Miller is now a contractor and builder and also does plumbing.

Mr. and Mrs. John Hellmuth

On July 18, 1862, in Germany, John Hellmuth was born. He, like so many others
of his time, decided to come to America, the land of plenty. Here in America he
met and married Mary Selzer in Brooklyn in the year of 1883. Mary Selzer was
the daughter of Jacob Selzer. To this happy union five children were born, two
boys and three girls. Two of whom died. Those living are Elizabeth Osborn,
Katherine Nickisch, of Wishek, N. D.; George Hellmuth of Burnstad, N. D., and
John and Louise, who are deceased.

This family came to this community in 1902 and located north of Wishek. In the
prime of her life, Mrs. Hellmuth passed away on October 28, 1923.

In 1926, Mr. Hellmuth remarried to Mrs. Schock, who passed away in 1934.

One of the first people they knew after arriving here was Peter Ackerman, now
of Lodi. One of the trials that they suffered was the dreaded prairie fire. It
caught them the third day after they had arrived. Their home which they were
constructing was completely demolished and they had to start from the bottom
again. They moved what they had managed to salvage from the fire into the barn,
which had a plain roof. This made things a little damp when it rained and so
they covered the roof with canvas, which helped somewhat. While they were living
here, they were working on their sod house; however it collapsed before it was
finished and had to be rebuilt.

Mr. Hellmuth in his later days, moved to Wishek where he passed away in 1943 on
February 13.

Mr. and Mrs. John H. Walth

At the age of 3, John H. Walth came with his parents to the Wishek vicinity in
1889. He had been born in South Russia, February 26, 1886. His parents were
Frederich Walth and Magdalena, nee Retzer. Mrs. Walth, Christina, is the
daughter of John Rieger and his wife Barbara, nee Boschee, born on August 25,
1891. The couple was married on November 15, 1910.

In 1911 they built a home 13 miles southwest of Wishek. Here 8 children were
born to them. Five of them are still living. The children are: Arthur, Kathryn
(Mrs. Galen Argast), Moffit, N. Dak.; Julius, Lamoore, California; Emanuel and
Lea (Mrs. August Brandner), Wishek, N. Dak.

In January, 1945, Mr. and Mrs. Walth retired and moved to the city of Wishek.
They have always been members of the Lutheran Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Salzer

Gottlieb Salzer was born on October 29, 1878, in South Russia, the son of Fred
Salzer and his wife, Carolina, nee Weizenberger. His wife, whom he married in
1897, was Christina Weizenberger, the daughter of Gottfried Weizenberger and
Anna Salzer Weizenberger.

They are the parents of 7 children, five boys and two girls. Of these 4 sons
are still living, John and Jacob of Ashley, and Fred and William of Danzig.

The Salzers came to this country in 1908 and settled in the Danzig vicinity.
They first saw Wishek on December 11, 1908. Their first business transaction
was with the Nickisch Implement Co.

In 1936 Mr. Salzer remarried. This time with Mrs. Regina Munsch. Since 1942
they have lived in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Schnabel

Adam Schnabel is one of the retired farmers living in Wishek. He was born in
Kassel, South Russia, on October 3, 1865. His parents were Konrad Schnabel and
Anna Maria, nee Stoebner. Mrs. Schnabel was Katherina, daughter of Jacob
Meidinger and Katherina, nee Reich. They were married on January 23, 1390, in
Kassel. Two children were born to them, Anna Marie (Mrs. D. D. Aipperspach) and
Adam A., who are both living in Wishek.

The Schnabel family came to the United States in 1901, and settled on a farm 21
miles southwest of Wishek, on April 28 of that year.

Of early days, Mr. Schnabel says that the first one he met at Wishek was Fred
Pfeifle. His first business transaction was the purchase of a wagon and a plow,
as well as some groceries in 1901.

When they first arrived in McIntosh County, the Schnabels spent a few days with
the Adam Meidinger family, until a sod house could be built for them on their
own land, which they had homesteaded. Mr. and Mrs. Schnabel remained on their
farm until 1923, when they retired and moved to Wishek. The farm was turned
over to their son, Adam A.

Mrs. Schnabel passed away on April 5, 1940. For five years Mr. Schnabel lived
alone, but since 1945 he has lived with his daughter, Mrs. Aipperspach and
family.

The Schnabels have been active members of the Lutheran Church.

Mr. Aipperspach is 82 years old, but still enjoys the best of health.

Mr. and Mrs. Karl Just

Karl Just was born in 1885 in the Wishek vicinity, the son of Christof and
Elizabeth (Wanner) Just. He was married in June, 1907, to Kathrina Meidinger,
who was born December 12, 1887, the daughter of Adam and Magdalina (Grursie)
Meidinger. They settled on a farm 16 miles southwest of Wishek where they lived
for several years. Later they moved to a larger farm in the same neighborhood.

They were the parents of nine children, six of which grew to man and womanhood.
They are Aldina, Ashley; Adam, Zeeland; Reinhold, Ephrim, and Julius of Berlin,
N. D.; and Mrs. Marie Moos, Zeeland.

Mr. Just passed away in 1923. In 1924 Mrs. Just married Mr. Christof Thurn. In
1925 she passed away leaving an infant son, Edwin Thurn.

Mr. and Mrs. Johannas Diegel

Among the Pioneer families of the Wishek community mention should also be made
of the John Diegel Family. Mr. Diegel was born on May 17, 1881, in
Hoffnungstal, South Russia. He was the son of Johannes and Barbara (Beck)
Diegel. His wife Elizabeth was born in the same community on September 9, 1882.
Her parents were Gottlieb and Christine (Bauer) Holzwarth.

On November 9, 1904, these two were united in marriage in South Russia. They
were blessed with 14 children, of whom the following are still living:
Christine (Mrs. Jakob Eckman), Burnstad, N. Dak.; Rosina (Mrs. Theodor Lang);
Gustav; Amalia (Mrs. Jacob Eiseman); Christian; Emma; Reinhold, all of Wishek;
Hulda (Mrs. Martin Molto), Gary, Minnesota; Waldemar; and Esther (Mrs. Bernard
Fandrich), both of Dawson.

Mr. and Mrs. Diegel arrived at Eureka in December, 1905. Two years later, they
moved to their farm, 10 miles northwest of Wishek. Here they remained until Mr.
Diegel passed away, after a long illness, on June 2, 1943. Because two of the
sons were in the army at that time, Mrs. Diegel continued to live on the farm
until January, 1946. Then she moved to Wishek, where she lives at present with
her daughter Emma, who is a semi-invalid.

The Diegels have always been members of the Lutheran Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob F. Hochhalter

In 1884 Fredrich and Mararita (nee Rueb) Hochhalter came to this country
bringing with them their young son, Jacob F., who was born in South Russia on
the 24th day of July in 1872. After entering this country in 1885 they located
in McIntosh County. On March 7, 1892, Jacob was married to Karolina Vilhauer.
Karolina is the daughter of Jacob and Magdelina (Groshanz) Vilhauer.

This young family secured land, and began farming about 16 miles southwest of
Wishek.

To this union sixteen children were born, four of whom died early in life. The
remaining children are: Mrs. Henry H. Wiest (Eva) of Sunnyside, Washington;
Henry J., Emanuel, Mrs. Adam Ketterling (Magdalina), Alvin, Mrs. John D. Wanner
(Lea); Mrs. Fred Wanner (Katherina), all of Wishek. Mrs. Albert Wiest
(Margaret), and Mrs. Emil Dockter (Albina), both of Venturia; Mrs. August
Hochhalter (Mary), of Hardin, Montana; Leopold of Lehr, N. D.; and Calvin of
Ashley.

After the eventful years on the farm, the family moved to the city of Wishek in
1936. Their mother had passed away in 1933. Mr. Hochhalter was again married to
Maria Wanner in 1934. After five years of happy marriage Mr. Hochhalter died
here in Wishek on June 28, 1939.

Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Sprenger

Friedrich Sprenger was born in Kassel, South Russia, on December 26, 1851. He
was the son of Mr. Jacob Heinrich Sprenger and Mrs. (nee Ahl) Sprenger. Mr.
Sprenger met and married Magdalina Boschee in Kassel, South Russia. She was the
daughter of Johann and Margaretha (nee Heubel) Boschee and was born May 1,
1855. In that same year Mr. Sprenger migrated to Tyndal, S. D. In the spring of
1886 they came to this community without any funds and he had to go into debt
to establish himself in this new country. In 1886 he filed on a 3/4 section of
land 11 miles southwest of Wishek. His early life was typical of our first
struggling pioneers. Poor crops, low prices, storms, prairie fires and such were
problems to be met and conquered. Mr. Sprenger never lost faith in God or the
community. He served on the district school board for a number of years. It was
with the united effort of him and his neighbors that a church was organized and
a church building erected. The years of labor, careful management and patience
gave their reward to him and his family, and he became a well-to-do farmer. In
1912 Mr. Sprenger retired from active farming and bought a home in the city of
Wishek.

Mr. Sprenger passed away at his home in Wishek on February 28, 1934. Mr. and
Mrs. Friedrich Sprenger were the happy parents of eight children. They are:
Friedrick of Dupree, S. D.; Karolina (Mrs. John Rudolf), of Wishek; John, of
Wishek; Jacob, of San Francisco, Calif.; Christina (Mrs. John Krein), of
Wishek; Magdalina (Mrs. Wilhelm Kinsle), deceased, formerly of Durlodge,
Montana, and August of Ion, Calif.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Ketterling

Back in 1884 when many families decided to leave the "old country," John and
Katherina (nee Heupal) Ketterling decided to bring their family to America.
Their son, Gottlieb, was born in Russia on November 21, in 1866.

On December 17, 1895, Gottlieb Ketterling, was united in marriage with Margaret
Woehl, who was born October 21, 1877. The young couple located on a farm ten
miles north of Zeeland, N. D., in 1886. Fifteen children were born to this
union. Five of whom died. The ten surviving are: Hulda (Mrs. Philip Moench),
Katherine (Mrs. August Ketterling), Albina (Mrs. August Just), Alga (Mrs. Henry
Diale), Ernestine (Mrs. Samuel Rudolf), Emma, Frieda (Mrs. Leopold Rudolph),
all of Wishek. Gottlieb, Edwin and Henry reside in Zeeland.

Mr. Gott. Ketterling died in Zeeland on December 2, 1940.

Mr. and Mrs. Philip Rueb, Jr.

The Philip Rueb, Sr., family consisting of father, mother and four children
arrived in Scotland, South Dakota, in the spring of 1885 from Kassel, South
Russia. Philip was born on November 16, 1878. The family remained in Scotland,
S. D., with relatives for about one year before coming to McIntosh County in
1886. After they arrived here they settled on a farm. The nearest town was
Ellendale, a distance of about 70 miles. These trips were made by oxen to get
the sugar, flour and other necessities that were needed.

Philip Rueb, Jr., was married to Kathrina Schwindt on January 13, 1902, in the
New Kassel Reformed church. Twelve children were born to this union of which
nine survive. They are David, Simon, and Freida (Mrs. Calvin Meidinger), of
Wishek. Edna (Mrs. Edward Hildenbrand), Lansing, Michigan; Adolph and Calvin of
Colfax, California, Hulda (Mrs. Julius Werth), Lehr, N. D., and Benjamin who is
in the United States Navy.

The first threshing Mr. Rueb did was with a horsepower machine driven by 14
horses. Later threshing was done with a steam engine fired with straw. After
that a gas engine was used.

In 1902 the family moved to a farm 3-1/2 miles west of Wishek where they lived
for 41 years. Mrs. Rueb died on October 13, 1943. She was blind five years
before her death. Mr. Rueb resides in Wishek at the present time.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred Perman

Mrs. Elizabeth Perman was born January 9, 1883, in Niedorf [Neudorf], South
Russia. Her parents were Mr. and Mrs. Martin Frey (nee Elizabeth Scheuffle).
She was united in marriage with Fred Perman, whose parents were Fredrich Perman
and Kathrine Weintz, on April 11, 1904, at Gackle, N. D. To this union were
born 8 children, 4 boys and 4 girls. Two of the children passed away in
infancy. Those surviving are: Elizabeth (Mrs. John Klinger), of Stockton,
