California; Edwin, of San Francisco, California; Arthur, of San Francisco;
Irene (Mrs. Don Bailey), Aberdeen, S. D.; Gedion, of Wishek; and Dolly, of
Lodi, California.

Mrs. Fred Perman came to America with her parents as a small child in 1885 and
lived at Gackle, N. D., until she came to this community in 1914 with her
husband. After they were married they lived in a sod house about twelve miles
east of Wishek. They felt fortunate in having two horses and two cows. They
also owned one claim of land. When harvesting time came relatives always helped
each other.

In 1914 they moved to Wishek and bought a small home from John Dormair in which
they lived for 2-1/2 years. Later they moved into a house built by Mr. Perman
and the Roehm brothers. This house remained the family residence until 1944
when they left Wishek to make their home in Lodi, California. On June 15, 1945,
the family was broken when Mr. Perman suddenly passed away. Mrs. Perman still
resides in Lodi.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Kemmet

Mr. Jacob Kemmet was born at Gluksthal [Glueckstal], South Russia, on September
18, 1871. His wife, Rosina Schilling, also of Gluksthal, South Russia, was born
on September 2, 1877. Rosina was the daughter of William and Sophia (nee Inje)
Schilling. The young people grew up in Russia and in 1896 were married there.
As many other young couples had done, they came to America to establish their
new home, and finally, in 1898 located on a farm 13 miles south of Wishek. They
began their hard and pioneering farm life with four horses and two cows. Among
the first new friends that they acquired in Wishek were Gottlieb Ketterling and
Mathiaus Maier.

To this pioneer family of Jacob Kemmet nine children were born, namely: Rosina
(Mrs. Fred W. Thurn), Jacob, who died in 1900; John, who died in 1946, Maria
(Mrs. Gottlieb Walth), Jacob II, Christian, who died in 1915; Edward, Emma
(Mrs. Andrew Rudolf), who died in 1947, and William.

Both Mr. and Mrs. Kemmett continued to live at their farm home until Mr.
Kemmett died in 1933. Mrs. Kemmett died in 1940.

Mr. and Mrs. John D. Stroh

John D. Stroh, son of Jacob Stroh and Madgelina Stocker, was born December 2,
1888, in South Russia. In 1893, at the age of five years, he came to America
with his mother, one brother, and two sisters.

On August 2, 1906, John was united in marriage to Eva Dockter, daughter of
Jacob Dockter and Magdelina Krein. The couple settled two and one-half miles
west of Wishek and lived on this farm until 1928. The family moved to Wishek.
In 1933, John accepted the position as butcher in the Herr Merc. meat market.

There were ten children born to Mr. and Mrs. Stroh and are, namely: August, of
Mobridge, S. Dak.; Mrs. Martha Boschee, of Linton; Art and Walter, of Milan,
Mich. Mrs. Lydia Just, Mrs. Altanta Larson, and Enoch, of Wishek; Edwin and
Gedion, both of Wishek. One child died in infancy.

The people that Mr. Stroh first met in Wishek were Jacob and John Herr J. J.
Doyle, Nickisch's, Bailey's and Charles Roehm.

Mr. and Mrs. Stroh are active members of the Reform church of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Rudolf, Jr.

Ludwig Rudolf, Jr., was born on June 11, 1865, in Kassel, South Russia, as the
son of Ludwig Rudolf, Sr., and his wife, Katherina, nee Schnabel. At the age of
19 years he came to this country with his parents, and settled on a farm 17
miles southwest of Wishek.

When he had grown to young manhood, he met and married Eva Aipperspach, the
daughter of Friederich and Dorthea (Lechner) Aipperspach. She had been born on
May 10, 1870. The date of their wedding is December 10, 1890. Twelve children
were born to this couple. Five of the children are no longer among the living.

The children who are still living are: John, of Ipswich, S. Dak.; Katherina
(Mrs. Calvin Vilhauer), of Jamestown); Eva (Mrs. John K. Wiest), Herried, S.
Dak.; Christina (Mrs. Christ Walth), Wishek, N. D.; Daniel and Leopold, of
Wishek, and Frederick, of Medina, N. Dak.

Of pioneer times, Mr. Rudolf tells, that he had to build a sod house for
himself. The lumber that he needed was hauled by oxen from Ellendale, a
distance of 65 miles from home. The trip took nearly three and one-half days.

To make a living, he ofttimes journeyed from 12 to 15 miles north of his
homestead to gather buffalo bones. When a wagon had been filled, he would haul
them to Ellendale or Ipswich, S. Dak. Here they were sold for a price of $12 to
$15 per ton. With the money obtained from the sale of bones, he would then
purchase flour, sugar, coffee and other necessities.

He also tells that in the early years, families of the Lutheran faith gathered
at one of the neighbor's homes for services. After doing this for about six
years, they began building their church. It took them three years to complete
this project. Nine members donated 31 days of free labor to help in the
building. Mr. Rudolf served as deacon of St. John's Lutheran Church for nearly
30 years.

At the time of his retirement in 1933, Mr. Rudolf owned 1,720 acres of land. In
February, 1934, Mr. and Mrs. Rudolf moved to Wishek to make their home. Mrs.
Rudolf passed away on November 18, 1942.

George Ketterling

George Ketterling, son of George Ketterling and Anna Maria Merkel, was born
August 25, 1881, at Kassel, South Russia.

He came to America in 1885, coming to this community shortly after with his
parents. They homesteaded a farm 28 miles southwest of Wishek. He helped his
parents on their farm, experiencing all the hardships common to other pioneers.

In 1903, he homesteaded a quarter section of land, shortly after marrying
Katharina Wolff. To this union were born 15 children, 5 of whom died. Those
living are: Mrs. Hermann Boschee, and Calvin now residing in Wishek, Mrs. Fred
(Bertha) Brandner, of Kulm; Gideon Ketterling of Mercer, N. D.; Albert
Ketterling of Zeeland; Daniel of Mercer, N. D.; Theophil, also of Mercer, Mrs.
Anna Stevahn of Ray, N. D.; Mrs. Erna Balzer of Ray, N. D.; and Ed. G. of
Napoleon, N. D.

As time went on, Mr. Ketterling developed his property until he had a large,
well-equipped farm. Unfortunately, his health began to fail him, and hoping to
regain it, he moved to Wishek. He had no success in his attempts to restore his
health.

Mr. Ketterling died April 5, 1941, and was laid to rest in the Lutheran St.
Andreas cemetery.

Mr. and Mrs. Valentine Boschee

Valentine Boschee was a native of the colony of Kassel, South Russia, being
born there June 25, 1846. He was united in marriage to Philipa Meyer, born
January 28, 1856, in the same community. Not wanting his children to become
drafted into service in the Russian army, he brought his family to America. Mr.
and Mrs. Boschee and their nine children settled on a homestead near Wishek. He
became one of the community's best farmers.

He was not only a good farmer, but he was an indispensable man in the
community. He was the carpenter, wagoner, sled maker, and cabinet maker for the
region. Many of the early homes were furnished with necessary articles made by
him. He was the blacksmith for the entire community.

Mr. Boschee passed away October 17, 1920.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian G. Herr

Christian G. Herr, son of Mr. and Mrs. George and Katherina Herr, was born in
Kassel, South Russia, on December 25, 1872. Mr. Herr and his brother George and
sister Katherina left Russia with their mother and step-father, John Boschee,
arriving in Menno, South Dakota, in October, 1884.

He stayed with relatives around Menno, South Dakota, during the winter of 1884
and 1885. In the spring of 1885 he came to McIntosh County, with his half
brother, Conrad Herr, who had come to this country earlier. He worked for his
brother as a farm laborer for the next seven or eight years.

In 1894 he filed claim on a 160-acre homestead in Rosenfield District, fourteen
miles southwest of Wishek.

On October 28, 1894, he was married to Katherina Meidinger, daughter of Jacob
and Rosina Meidinger, nee Mueller, of Antelope Valley. She was also born in
Kassel, Russia, on April 25, 1876. She came to Antelope Valley with her parents
in 1886.

In the spring of 1895 they proceeded to construct their new home. First they
built a sod barn in which they lived temporarily until they finished the house
which was built with mud blocks. These buildings were in time replaced by
wooden structures and are now owned and occupied by their youngest son,
Benjamin.

Fourteen children were born to them, nine boys and five girls, two boys died in
infancy. They were: Emil, Wishek, N. D.; Theodore, Wishek; Benjamin, Wishek;
Gustave, Aberdeen, S. D.; Henry, Bismarck; Christian, Jamestown; Samuel,
Billings, Montana; Mrs. Katie Hoff, Venturia; Mrs. Henry Bertsch, Ashley; Mrs.
Rose Grandbois, Minneapolis, Minn.; Mrs. Adeline Nickisch, Venice, California;
and Mrs. Esther Zimmermann, Lehr, N. D. Mr. Herr died February 7, 1924.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Goebel

Mr. Jacob Goebel was born April 20, 1879, in Glucksthal, South Russia. He was
married in 1903 to Katharina Kaul in Russia. Six children were born to this
union, two died in infancy and one at the age of twenty-one years. The
following children survive, Lydia (Gruebele), Wishek, N. Dak; Martha (Brennice),
Streeter, N. Dak.; Esther (Kosanke), Lodi, California.

In 1905 Mr. and Mrs. Goebel came to this country, located eleven miles north of
Wishek on a homestead. In 1914 Mr. Goebel sold his farm to A. W. Herr and moved
to Wishek. Here he operated a cream station. He also purchased land near Wishek
and farmed it for several years.

Mr. Goebel was a member of the Evangelical Church; he was instrumental in the
erection of the present Evangelical Church in Wishek. He served as Class leader
and Superintendent of the Sunday School for many years.

Mrs. Goebel died while living in Wishek. Mr. Goebel was remarried to Martha
Ketterling, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Ketterling of Wishek. To this union
was born one child: Alleen (Hunter), Snohomish, Washington.

Mr. Goebel served on the town board and was marshal for several years.

In 1936 Mr. and Mrs. Goebel and daughter Alleen moved to Lodi, California,
where they now reside.

Nathaniel D. Furrer

Nathaniel D. Furrer, Wishek, N. Dak., born June 10, 1867, at Easton, Illinois,
in Mason County, the son of Andrew and Mary Ann Darl Furrer. Mr. Furrer was
married December 28, 1887, to Jennie Ingersoll, at Easton, Ill., who was born
December 4, 1866, the daughter of James L. and Mary E. Ingersoll. To this
family four children were born, three boys and girl. They are Chas. D. Furrer,
Bismarck, N. Dak.; Frank E., Sacramento, Calif.; Ruth Ann Herman, Pollock, S.
Dak., Howard F., Benton Harbor, Mich. Mr. Furrer came to this community in the
fall of 1908 and located five miles south of Wishek. The first time they saw
Wishek was in the fall of 1907. Some of the first people they met were the
Bettenhausen brothers, Harry Lowers, Lew West, Best, the Merchant, Herr
Brothers, H. E. Timm, J. W. Hofer, Dr. Geo. Grant, Mr. and Mrs. M. E. Pratt,
the postmaster, Mr. and Mrs. J. J. Doyle, John Cater, the barber.

In 1913 Mr. Furrer bought some lots in Wishek on which he built a home in
March. They moved into this home in July where they lived until they passed
away. Mr. Furrer was engaged in farming, and had one of the first tractors. He
used to help his neighbors with their plowing. Later he owned a threshing
machine; with it he helped his neighbors harvest. He continued with threshing
for others after they moved in to town with the help of his youngest son. Mr.
Furrer was always willing to give a helping hand and was generally liked by his
neighbors.

Mr. Furrer died December 7, 1917. Mrs. Furrer was very active in Red Cross work
during the first World War. She died October 9, 1937.

Mr. and Mrs. John Ottmar

John Ottmar, son of John Ottmar, Sr., and Elizabeth Goetz, was born at Neuburg,
South Russia, on December 28, 1886. As a small child, John came to America with
his parents. On January 7, 1912, John was united in marriage to Kathrine
Ackerman of Eureka, S. Dak. Kathrine was born on June 15, 1890, at Greenway, S.
Dak., to Jacob Ackerman and Caroline Hinger. The young couple moved to a farm
six and one-half miles southwest of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Ottmar viewed the small town of Wishek in the spring of 1912. They
liked the German people and felt right at home. They bought their lumber from
F. J. Sayler; their groceries from Peter Ackerman's store and their machinery
from Gottlieb Nickisch. They also met Dr. Geo. Grant. In 1913, they purchased
furniture from Dockter and Schmidt. In 1944, they retired from the farm and
moved into Wishek. Here the family has many friends and attends church.

Three children were born to this union, all of whom are living, namely: Inez
Link, Herbert and Clinton of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Will

Jacob Will was born on March 18, 1885, at Johannas Dahl [Johannestal], Russia,
to Michael Will and Reginia Diede. On October 15, 1900, Jacob and his parents
came to Scotland, S. Dak., and later moved to Eureka, S. Dak. The following
year they homesteaded northeast of Wishek, near Doyles Lake. In 1907, Jacob
took a homestead two miles south of Lehr; and the following year, on January
21, he was united in marriage to Christina Gable, a daughter of Jacob Gable and
Kathrine Dies. Christina was born on October 15, 1887.

The young couple farmed until 1919 when they moved into Wishek. For several
years Mr. Will worked as school janitor and then was employed on the railroad.
Several years later Mr. Will purchased and operated a meat market. After
disposing of this business, he became an employee in the meat market at the
Herr Merc. Co. for six and one-half years.

In 1941 Mr. and Mrs. Will moved to Lodi, Calif., where they reside at 528
Harold St.

To this union was born five children, all of whom are living, namely: Rubin and
Marvin of Lodi; Arthur, of Sheboygan, Wis.; Mrs. Martha Herr of Lodi, and Mrs.
Freda Vennard of Red Lodge, Montana.

Mr. and Mrs. Andreas Grabau

Andreas Grabau was born May 10, 1878, at Marienberg, South Russia, the son of
Johann and Anna (Jusa) Grabau. He was married in August, 1899, to Margaretha
Sifferman of Bergdorf, South Russia. She was born January 11, 1878, the
daughter of Christian and Katherina (Metzger) Sifferman. Six years later they
came to America, arriving in Lehr, in 1905.

Mr. Grabau worked on the railroad for 6 months after his arrival in Lehr. He
then settled on a homestead 6 miles northeast of Wishek. Later he purchased a
larger farm in the same vicinity. Here Mrs. Grabau died January 22, 1939. In
1942 Mr. Grabau moved to Wishek and is still a resident of this city. He is the
father of 5 children: Andreas, Jr., Lehr, N. D.; John, Wishek; Henry, Napoleon;
Mrs. Magdalena Schmidt, Oakland, Calif., and Friedrich, Wishek.

George Herr, Sr.

George Herr, Sr., was among the early pioneers of McIntosh County who struggled
through many hardships and helped establish the community. He was born in
Kassel, South Russia, on July 18, 1871. His father died when he was a small boy
and at the age of 13 he was adopted by Jacob Herr, Sr. He came to America in
1884 and went to Scotland, Dakota Territory. Here he worked for several years
and in 1887 he came to McIntosh County.

With financial aid from Jacob Herr and the money earned at harvest he purchased
a team of horses, a team of oxen, wagon and a plow. The following year he
homesteaded fifteen miles west of the present sight of Wishek, N. D., in the
Beaver Creek area.

In 1891 he married Christina Boschee. Rev. E. Matzke, minister of the Baptist
Church of which they were members, performed the ceremony. To this marriage
there were ten children born: Emanuel, farmer, retired because of ill health,
living in Wishek; Willie, farmer, Wishek; Edward G., mechanic in Wishek; George
G., farmer, Wishek; Gottfried, farmer, Wishek; Adam, farmer, Wishek; Christina
(Mrs. Adolf Kramer), Missoula, Mont., Blandina (Mrs. Samuel Stading), deceased;
Martha (Mrs. Jacob J. Bertsch, Ashley, N. D.; and Bertha (Mrs. Theo. Springer),
Hardin, Montana.

Their first house and barn were built under the same roof and were made of sod
and stone. Their first crop of but a few acres was a huge success, three
bushels of flax producing 152 bushels that sold for eighty cents a bushel.
Wheat, that same year, brought only fifty cents a bushel. Butter sold for five
cents per pound and eggs for four and five cents a dozen. Often times there was
no market for these products and the farmers would have to take them home again.

Churches and schools were not as yet established. Religious services were held
in homes and sometimes they were fortunate enough to have an itinerant pastor
to serve them. Mr. Herr took an active part and interest in affairs of the
church and community in which he lived.

His first wife died in 1923, and a couple of years later he married a second
time, taking for his wife, Karolina Bertsch. In August of 1933 his second wife
died and in November of 1938 he again married and took as his wife Friedericka
Siebert. They now reside in Eureka, S. D.

In the accompanying picture are shown four generations of one branch of the
Herr family. Lower right is George Herr, Sr., age 78; above right, his son
Emanuel, age 52; above left is Allen, the son of Emanuel, age 28, with one son
Bobby, who appears in lower right of the picture. Allen Herr and family reside
in Lodi, California. Bobby is six years old and was also born in Wishek, N. D.

August Breitbarth

On March 25, 1908, August Breitbarth arrived in Wishek on a freight train. The
following winter he returned to the place of his birth, Tinley Park, Ill., and
the next spring, 1909, he returned to Wishek. On June 14th of that year Mr.
Breitbarth was married to Alvina Busch in Chicago, Ill.

Mr. Breitbarth's father and brother had bought land for him 3-1/2 miles south
of Wishek, Section 33. He understood that it was all level but much to his
disappointment he found a big hill on it and innumerable rocks. The first year
of farming he broke up 70 acres and was hailed out completely without any
insurance. He rented a stone farm house and a sod barn from the Herr Brothers.
He bought a plow from Jake Hilcher and a drill and wagon from Gottlieb
Nickisch. Thus farming began for him and after the reverses of the first year,
there were fair crops until 1936. The drought brought farming to an end for him
and he moved his family to Monterey, Minn. where he lives now. He built the
first silo in the Wishek district and bought the first manure spreader in 1915.

August Breitbarth was born on April 16, 1885. His father's name is Christ
Breitbarth and his mother's name is Katherine, nee Arnold. Mr. and Mrs.
Breitbarth are the parents of five children: Alfred Breitbarth, of Alpha,
Minn.; Mrs. Luella Wolff, Monterey, Minn.; Mrs. Viola Bernhardt, Triumph, Minn.;
Mrs. LaVera Nordby, Odin, Minn.; and Estella of Monterey, Minnesota.

Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Ulmer, Jr.

Ludwig Ulmer, Jr., is one of the earliest settlers in McIntosh County who came
here in 1885. Mr. Ulmer's parents migrated from Germany to Russia in the
nineteenth century. Ludwig Ulmer and his wife Dorothy Diede brought forth a
son, Ludwig, Jr., on March 25, 1868, in Johannesthal, South Russia. He and his
parents came to America in the spring of 1885 and first settled in Scotland,
Dakota Territory. There they spent the winter and hearing of land being opened
for homesteads farther north, they left with ox team and located one mile from
Greenway, S. D., but Ludwig, Jr., came to McIntosh County and filed a homestead
11 miles southwest of the present sight of the city of Wishek.

Mr. Ludwig Ulmer, Jr., married Magdalena Gaier in 1885. To them five children
were born; two died in infancy. Living are two girls and William. Mrs. Ludwig
Ulmer died on December 23, 1937, and Mr. Ulmer died on April 11, 1946.

The only son of Ludwig, Jr., was William, who carried on the family name. Bill
was united in marriage with Annie Buchholz in 1914. One daughter, Clara, was
born to them, who is now Mrs. Edwin Speidel, of Bismarck, N. D. His wife died
on November 27, 1918. On February 15, 1920, he married Martha Knoll. To this
union there were 13 children born, one boy and one girl who died in infancy.
Living in the vicinity of Wishek are: Clarence, Bennie, Emil, Irene (Mrs.
Arthur Thurn), Gertrude, Wilbur, Norman, Frieda, John and Oscar.

The Ulmer's are members of the Baptist Church.

Mr. and Mrs. John Rieger

John Rieger was born September 13, 1859, at Gluecksdahl, South Russia. His
father's name was John Rieger and his mother's name was Eva Zisen Rieger. He
was married in South Russia on October, 1880, to Barbara Boschee who was born
February 12, 1858. Her father's name was John Boschee and her mother was
Barbara Heupel. Two boys and four girls remain. They are Katherina (Mrs. Henry
Klaudt), of Bismarck; Barbara (Mrs. Christ Dockter); Christina (Mrs. Christ
Walth); Emma (Mrs. Ed. Herr); Fredrick and Emanuel of Wishek.

When they came to this country in October, 1884, they brought nothing with them
but their clothing and bedding. They stopped at Tyndal, South Dakota, and
earned enough money to come to McIntosh County in 1885. First they stopped
north of Zeeland and then moved to a farm twelve miles west of Danzig. Because
it was late in the spring they were unable to plant a large crop. What they had
they sold for forty-five cents a bushel and that brought them enough money to
buy the necessities of life. Their equipment included a team of oxen, a cow, a
wagon and a plow.

They were active members of the Lutheran church. Mr. Rieger passed away on
August 17, 1922.

Mr. and Mrs. Karl K. Wacker

Karl K. Wacker was born October 10, 1876, at Yankton, S. Dak., to Karl and
Barbara Wacker, nee Niehart.

Mr. Wacker married Elizabeth Munsch on March 21, 1901, at Leola, S. Dak. Mrs.
Wacker is the daughter of Andrew and Louise Munsch, nee Berreth. She was born
in Russia, April 28, 1880.

The Wackers arrived in this community in 1905. Their family consisted of 12
children, 5 of whom died. The surviving children are: Andrew of Waukon, Iowa,
Arthur of Petaluma, Cal.; Enoch of Detroit, Mich.; Aaron of Dearborn, Mich.;
Laura Stroh of Milan, Mich.; Alvin of Milan, Mich.; Harold of Dearborn, Mich.

The Wackers are now making their home in Milan, Mich.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob W. Hofer

One of Wishek's first business men is J. W. Hofer. He was born May 17, 1882, at
Emery, S. Dak. He is the son of John D. and Anna (Gross) Hofer. Mr. Hofer spent
the early years of his life with his parents at Emery.

In October, 1901, Mr. Hofer came to Wishek from Eureka, S. Dak., arriving by
stagecoach. This same month the railroad was completed between Wishek and
Pollock, S. Dak.

The next two winters, Mr. Hofer taught school. Being more interested in a
business career than he was in teaching, he decided to become a druggist. After
the necessary schooling in Minneapolis and in Fargo, Mr. Hofer returned in 1905
and went into partnership with Dr. George Grant. The first drug store was the
building now occupied by the Farmers Hardware, the rear part of the building
was used by Dr. Grant for his office.

In 1910 the two partners erected a new 2-story building on the lot just north
of their store. In it they expanded their drug business and rented the second
floor for professional offices. The old building housed many business
enterprises, among them a restaurant, a general store, a furniture store, and a
hardware store operated by Mr. Hofer in connection with his drug business.

The partnership of Grant and Hofer was dissolved in 1918, and since that time
Mr. Hofer has continued to be one of Wishek's leading business men. In 1945,
Mr. Hofer sold the stock of his hardware store and the store is now operated
under the name of The Farmers Hardware Company.

On April 16, 1907, Mr. Hofer was united in marriage to Sarah Scott, the
daughter of Andrew A. and Jennie (Jenson) Scott. They have three sons and one
daughter: Grant, at Wishek; Mrs. Cinchona Mikes, St. Paul, Minnesota; Scott,
Franklin Park, Illinois; and Winfield, St. Paul, Minnesota. They have two
grandchildren.

Mr. and Mrs. Hofer have been active leaders in progressive community life. Mr.
Hofer has served as city mayor, city councilman, director of the Tri County
Fair Association, and has been an enthusiastic member of the Wishek Association
of Commerce. Mrs. Hofer is a leader in women's activities in Wishek. She is a
member of the Lutheran Ladies Aid, the Wishek Civic League, the City Library
Board, and the American Legion Auxiliary. They are members of the local
Lutheran Church.

Mr. and Mrs. John Henry Wishek and Nina Farley Wishek

John Henry Wishek, after whom the City of Wishek was named, was born of poor
parents on April 17, 1855, at Warren, Pennsylvania, the son of Charles Wishek,
who emigrated from Baden, Germany, in 1849, and Barbara Salathe, a native of
Lorraine. The family soon moved to Sharon Center, Ohio, where he spent his
early days. He worked his way through school, having learned the stonemason
trade and in 1878 was graduated by the University of Michigan law school,
receiving his LLB degree. After practicing his profession in Ohio for a number
of years he came to North Dakota in 1884, being in the first party to arrive in
Hoskins, Dakota Territory, that year. He assisted in the organization of the
county and was the first Clerk of Court and Register of Deeds, as well as
acting unofficially as States Attorney. He played a leading role in the
development of the county and especially in the development of the cities of
Ashley and Wishek.

He came to know intimately the officials of the Soo Line railroad and because
of this and his work in getting the right-of-way for the railroad, and his
position of leadership in the county, the new town of Wishek was so named in
his honor.

While Mr. Wishek maintained his residence at Ashley, he was from the very first
keenly interested in the development of the city of Wishek. He acquired the
townsite of Wishek and land adjoining the city as well as farm lands about it.
He had organized the first private bank in the county at Ashley, and in 1898
organized the First State Bank of Wishek. He engaged in the grain business in
Wishek for many years, practiced law in partnership with A. P. Guy. It was
natural that his activities caused him to spend much of his time in Wishek
where he was a familiar figure. Mr. Wishek donated the site of every church in
Wishek, as well as a portion of the city park. The family still maintain large
real estate holdings in and around Wishek as well as operating the grain
business established by their father, and other enterprises.

Mr. Wishek was married to Nina Farley in Aberdeen, South Dakota, on December
26, 1891. They have eight children, all of whom are living, as follows: Esther
Tange, Anna Benson, Jean Ofsthun, Carl A. Wishek, Homer C. Wishek, and Paul J.
Wishek live in California, while Max A. Wishek is a practicing attorney at
Ashley and John H. Wishek, Jr., is in business and lives in Wishek.

Nina Wishek came to McIntosh County with her father, Albert Farley, in the year
1887. Her father filed on a homestead just north of Ashley. Mrs. Wishek was a
leader in the Church and in civic affairs and was all through her life
interested in writing. She had published a book of poems some of which have
been used in national publications. She was always interested in history and
years ago began a tireless task of securing historical data of the county and
its settlers. This momentous undertaking finally resulted in a fully
illustrated history of McIntosh County with 437 pages, entitled "Along the
Trails of Yesterday," a story of McIntosh County and was published in 1941. The
book secured many favorable reviews and is considered one of the outstanding
histories of sectional North Dakota. Its value will increase with the passage
of time. Mrs. Wishek, now nearly 80 years of age, lives in Whittier, California.

John H. Wishek, Sr., was definitely an outstanding personality in McIntosh
County during his long and varied life. He was endowed with a forceful
character and possessed unlimited physical energy together with a keen and
penetrating mind. His advice was often sought by others and he was free to give
aid to all who sought it. He died at the age of 76 on January 13, 1932, and is
buried in the family plot in the Ashley cemetery.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred J. Sayler

Fred J. Sayler was born December 31, 1886, in Odessa, Russia. He is the son of
Jacob Sayler and Christina Sayler, nee Kautz. He was married to Christina Link,
September 6, 1908, at Zeeland, North Dakota. She was the daughter of Gottlieb
and Phillipina Link, Zeeland, North Dakota. To this union seven children were
born of whom the following survive: Edwin A., Los Angeles, California; Elmer E.,
Wishek, N. Dak.; Walter R., Wishek, N. Dak.; Arthur T., Wishek, N. Dak.; Roy
O., Inglewood, California; Erna I. (Klein), Yankton, S. Dak.

Fred J. Sayler came to the United States in 1899 and to this community in 1905,
locating at Wishek, N. Dak. The first time he saw Wishek was in 1903 when he
was on his way from his home at Artas, S. Dak., to work for the Soo Line at
Braddock, N. Dak. At that time the train left Wishek at 6:00 P.M. for Bismarck,
arriving in Braddock at 2:00 A.M. In 1905 he came to Wishek again with his
brother John and went to work for C. H. Suess, who was managing the North Star
Lumber Co. His salary was thirty dollars per month of which thirteen was paid
to Mrs. Jennie Hill for room and board.

The first people he met in Wishek were M. E. Pratt Postmaster; J. C. Nippolt,
Manager of the Hill Hotei, A. P. Guy, Attorney at Law; Gottlieb, Blacksmith,
Jacob Hofer, Country School Teacher; and E. H. Cook, Proprietor of a Pool Hall.

His first business transaction in Wishek was the sale of material to L. A.
Laurent, Contractor and Builder, who built the Wishek Public School, frame
structure, in 1908. One Saturday evening as Fred Sayler was burning weeds
behind the main shed of the North Star Lumber Co., a sudden wind blew the fire
against the building. The fire alarm was sounded. What was then the only Wishek
Fire Department--people with buckets and brooms--rushed to help. With much
effort and luck the fire was placed under control without much damage and thus
his home was also saved for he and his family occupied quarters in the front
part and upstairs of the lumber yard office.

Fred Sayler recalls when the first airplane came to Wishek. This was in 1919.
He was then President of the Wishek village board and was given the only ride.
It was a great thrill.

Fred Sayler was a charter member of the Congregational Church which was
organized in 1912. He was treasurer for twenty-four years. He was Chairman of
the County Welfare Board for several years. He was a member of the Wishek
School Board and served as Alderman a number of times. He also was a member of
the local Red Cross Board.

In 1945 he retired from the lumber business. Since that time he has supervised
the erection of several homes and business structures in Wishek. He has spent
several winters in California.

Mr. and Mrs. Martin Doering

Martin Doering was born October 6, 1875, in Mansburg, South Russia, son of
Gottlieb Doering and Karolina Hiely. In 1888, at the age of thirteen he
immigrated to this country. In July 23, 1904, he married Elizabeth Becker,
daughter of Henry Becker and Margaret Detscher, at Napoleon, N. Dak. In 1926,
they came to Wishek and made their home here. The Doerings have three children,
Herbert, Wishek, N. Dak.; Ruth (Mrs. Teddy Brandt), Wishek, N. Dak., and
Willard, Wapheton, N. Dak.

Mr. Doering's trade was carpentry work. He has built many things, including a
few houses and some barns.

Some of the business establishments he remembers seeing in 1926 was Pete
Ackerman's Store, Fred Bauman's Garage, Nickisch Blacksmith, and Treick's
Lumber Yard.

Mr. Doering had always been in good health, until the last four years. He had
to quit working and is now enjoying a rest.

Mr. and Mrs. Kunrad Kogler

Kunrad Kogler was born June 7, 1854, in Rohrbach, South Russia, son of John and
Katherine Kogler, nee Fleaman. He was married to Katharina Kost, December 8,
1881, at Worms, South Russia. Katharina Kost was the daughter of Ludwig and
Margaret Kost, nee Suess. To this union were born ten children of whom seven
survive, as follows: Katherine (Becker), Napoleon, N. Dak.; John, Wishek, N.
Dak.; Emma (Becker), Napoleon, N. Dak., Jake, Wishek, N. Dak; Christina
(Ulmer), Wishek, N. Dak.; Martha (Quatier), Wishek, N. Dak.; and Lydia,
Bismarck, N. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. Kogler came to the United States in 1885 and located at Artas,
South Dakota. In 1901 they moved to McIntosh County, purchasing two quarters of
land from John L. Justern, one-half mile west of Green Lake. On this farm Mr.
Kogler lived until his death on January 24, 1919.

Some of the first people Mr. Kogler had contact with in Wishek were Chris
Suess, Manager of the Lumber Yard; Doyle and Fischer, store; Volk and Klein,
store; A. P. Guy, attorney; John H. Wishek, and Charles Roehm.

After the demise of Mr. Kogler, Mrs. Kogler moved to Wishek, where she resided
until her death on May 30, 1941.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Wolff

Gottlieb Wolff, Wishek, N. Dak., was born October 4, 1884, in South Russia. His
father was Johannas Wolff and Fredricia Bender. Mr. Wolff married Christina
Pfeifle November 1, 1907, at Wishek, N. Dak. Mrs. Wolff was born April 28,
1885, at Tripp, S. Dak., the daughter of Peter and Kathrinia, nee Deischle,
Pfeifle. To this family six children were born, two died. The children are
Arron at Lodi, Calif.; Gideon at Montray, Minn.; Kathrine (Mrs. Ben Beck),
Eureka, S. Dak.; Amanda at San Francisco, Calif.

Mr. Wolff came to this country in 1885, and to this community in 1905. He
located here in 1907 five miles northeast of Wishek. Mrs. Wolff passed away May
18, 1918, and Mr. Wolff died November 28, 1927, at Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. Wolff retired in 1916, residing in Wishek where he worked with a road
construction crew under Jacob Herr. Later he started constructing roads for the
county. Being a very efficient manager his road building was very successful.
He also built the present Standard Service Station, which is still operating
under that name.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Weisser

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Weisser were born and raised in South Russia, as were many
other pioneers of this territory.

Mr. Weisser was born in Kliennerdorf on August 4, 1870, the son of Matties
Weisser and Karolina Hieb. His wife, a native of Kassel, was born November 24,
1871, the daughter of Ludwig Gunst and Elizabeth Frolich.

After their marriage on February 18, 1894, the young couple remained in their
native land until the year 1901 when they decided to journey to America in
quest of freedom that prompted so many to migrate during those years. Their
first destination in this country was Hosmer, S. D. They spent two years there.
In the spring of 1903 they came to this community and settled on a rented farm
seventeen miles south of Wishek. A year later in 1904, they filed on a
homestead located on section 30, township 132, range 73. There they remained
for eight years. In 1912, they sold the homestead and bought a farm fifteen
miles west and three miles south of Wishek where they lived for 25 years.

Interested in the welfare and development of the community, Mr. Weisser took an
active part in district school affairs and community projects.

Thirteen children were born to this union, eight of whom survive. They are
Katherina (Mrs. John Zeigler), Kulm, N. D.; John, Verona, N. D.; Christina
(Mrs. Ed. Sayler), Wishek, N. D.; Jacob, Zeeland, N. D.; Lydia (Mrs. Ed Weist),
Wishek; Emma (Mrs. Ed Eckman), Wishek; Hertha (Mrs. Reinhold Weist), Rupert,
Idaho; and Ed. of Wishek.

It goes without saying, that it was with faith and courage that these pioneers
met and overcame the hardships of privation which were the common lot of the
new settlers.

In 1937 Mr. and Mrs. Weisser retired and moved to Wishek, where they are still
enjoying good health and a well earned rest.

Affiliated with the Lutheran church, they have worked toward the interest and
welfare of it during their entire married life.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Kramer, Sr.

Mr. Jacob Kramer, Sr., was born February 7, 1861, in Kassel, South Russia, the
son of John F. and Christina (Hochhalter) Kramer. His boyhood and youth were
spent in Russia with his parents. On January 28, 1888, he was married to
Elizabeth Minderlen of Kassel. She was born May 2, 1868, the daughter of
Fredrich and Regina (Murschal) Minderlen.

Following the great movement of relatives and neighbors out of Russia, the
family came to America and settled nine miles southwest of Wishek in April,
1901. Here they lived for 20 years improving their land and rearing their
family of twelve children, nine of whom grew to man and womanhood.

Mr. and Mrs. Kramer were thankful to be American farmers. The edge of pioneer
hardships was worn off by the time they arrived here. Their real hardships were
endured in Russia before they were able to leave that country. They were very
happy here in America. The first time they saw Wishek was in 1902. It was a
friendly little town to them, consisting of a few small buildings, including a
grocery store, post office and livery stable. Most of their trading was done at
a country store owned by Mr. Hoffsteader. Some of the articles purchased in this
little farm store are still in use in the Kramer household today.

The religious life of the community, however, was never neglected. Since the
services were held morning and afternoon, dinner was served to all who
attended. From the very first meeting, Sunday School was not neglected. The
early pioneer minister of this Baptist faith was Rev. Christian Bischof. Later
the congregation erected a central meeting place in which to worship--the
Beaver Creek Baptist Church.

On October 20, 1920, Mr. and Mrs. Kramer retired from the farm into the home in
which Mrs. Kramer and her daughter, Magdalene, now live. Mr. Kramer passed away
March 6, 1931.

Children who grew to adulthood are: Adolph, Missoula, Mont.; August, American
Falls, Idaho; Jacob J., Wishek; Mrs. Albina Bettenhausen, Wishek; Mrs.
Christine Krein, American Falls, Idaho; Elizabeth, Los Angeles, Calif.; and
Magdalene, Wishek. Mrs. Emma Bettenhausen and Mrs. Pauline Bettenhausen, both
of Wishek, are deceased.

Mr. and Mrs. John Rudolf

John Rudolf, one of the prominent farmers of McIntosh County, came to America
in 1884, as a boy of nine, with his father, Ludwig Rudolf and his mother,
Katherine (Schnabel) Rudolf. Johann was born in Kassel, South Russia, in March
8, 1875.

Upon arriving in this country, the Rudolfs spent a year near Menno, South
Dakota, before they came to this community in 1885.

Mr. Rudolf filed on a fourth section of land and farmed on this place until
1913, when he sold it and bought a bigger farm consisting of 480 acres; as the
years passed he bought more land and before he retired he had owned 800 acres
of land.

Mr. Rudolf was married to Karolina Sprenger, daughter of Friedrich Sprenger and
Magdaline (Boschee) Sprenger. They were married November 16, 1897, in McIntosh
County. They had fourteen children, five having died. Those remaining are
Friedrick, Otto, and Adam at Fullerton, N. Dak.; Christina (Mrs. Andreas
Ulmer), Oakes, N. Dak.; August, Oakes; Magdalina at Wishek; Margaretha (Mrs.
Fred Walth), Wishek; Christ and Ferdinand at Wishek.

Mr. Rudolf took great pride in his district school affairs and community
projects. He served as a member of the school board for several years. He was a
faithful member of the Lutheran Church.

In the Spring of 1941, Mr. and Mrs. John Rudolf retired from farming and moved
to Wishek. On May 5, 1943, Mr. Rudolf died and was laid to rest in the St.
Luke's Lutheran cemetery, Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Daniel G. Nickisch

Mr. Daniel G. Nickisch was born October 1, 1881 in Turner County, South Dakota,
son of Daniel and Augusta Nickisch. He was married to Grace B. Sheridan on June
28, 1905, at Bismarck, North Dakota. To this union were born the following:
Harold G., Mound, Minnesota; Mildred (Swalve), San Francisco, California; Alice
(Krein), St. Louis Park, Minnesota; Ramona (Herr), Salem, Oregon; and Myma
(Kittson), Hammond, Indiana.

Daniel G. Nickisch came to Wishek in 1899, engaging in the blacksmith and
machinery business with his brother Gottlieb. He continued in this business
until 1905 when he purchased the Sheridan Hotel. The first people Mr. Nickisch
recalls in Wishek were the Bailey family, Pat and John J. Doyle, M. E. Pratt,
Joe Singer, Stephen Fischer, Volk, Klein, Palm, Bjornson brothers, Chris Suess,
Anton Helmer.

Our informant avers that the marriage of our subject was the first in Wishek of
two Wishekites. Mr. and Mrs. Nickisch now reside in St. Louis Park, Minnesota.

Mr. and Mrs. George Ackerman

Included among the early arrivals of pioneers in the present vicinity of Wishek
was George Ackerman. Mr. Ackerman was born in Rohrbach, South Russia, on
February 1, 1862. His parents were Frederich and Elizabeth Ackerman. In 1883 he
was married to Elizabeth Zoller in Rohrbach, South Russia.

Ten children blessed this union. They are Mrs. Elizabeth Jacob Klein, deceased;
Mrs. Adolph Hochhalter, Ashland, Wisconsin; Mrs. Wm. Sayler, deceased; Mrs.
Fred Klein, Fairyland, Calif.; Fred H. Ackerman, Wishek, N. D.; Mrs. Edward
Ackerman Wishek; Mrs. Fred Sayler, Wishek, and Mrs. Fred Gall, Blanchard, N. D.

In the spring of 1889 the George Ackerman family accompanied by the John
Ackerman and Israel Hildenbrand families arrived in this country. Their first
stop was at the Wiest home. They remained there for several days. From there
they journeyed to their respective homesteads.

The George Ackerman homestead, which was built of sod, was located 5 miles
northwest of Wishek. Of course at that time there were no building materials to
be bought so nearly everything had to be made by hand.

The Ackermans had their share of toil and hardships. Along with their neighbors
their courage and industry triumphed and they achieved a modest fortune and
were thus assured of a comparatively carefree old age.

They later moved from their farm to Wishek. Here they lived the last few years
in retirement. Mr. Ackerman died on May 16, 1936. His wife died four years
later on June 14, 1940, in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Adolf Stebner

Adolf Stebner, son of Andreas and Kathrina Doerr Stebner, was born on September
23, 1885, at Horske, South Russia. He grew to manhood in Russia, desirous of
more adventure and a better livelihood he came to America in June, 1909, and
located on a farm near Wishek. On October 25, 1910, he was married to Pauline
Sprenger, daughter of Johann and Kathrina, nee Mehlhof Sprenger. To this union
were born seven children, four of which died in infancy, and the living are
Gustav, Lehr, N. Dak.; Adolf, Lodi, Calif.; and August, Wishek, N. Dak.

The Stebner family moved to Idaho in 1913 and filed on a homestead, where they
lived for six years. Having a desire to come back to their first American
community, they came back to North Dakota and rented a farm near Hazelton.
Retiring from farming in 1923, Mr. Stebner came again to Wishek and entered the
plastering business, in which he is working at the present time.

Konrad Ernst

Konrad Ernst, a native of Worms, South Russia, was born March 1, 1870. His
parents were Mr. and Mrs. Adam Ernst, nee Salome Kieler. He was united in
marriage with Elizabeth Trautman in 1892, also of Worms.

Upon arriving by train at Lehr in March, 1904, they were met by Adam Nagel,
with whom they spent a few days. Later they homesteaded near Streeter, N. D.
The family lived under a wagon box until their sod house was ready to live in.

In 1911 the family moved to Wishek and settled on a farm south of Wishek. In
1928 they moved to Dupree, S. D., and two years later moved again to Venturia
where they spent the winter, and then made their home in Turtle, N. D. Mr.
Ernst died here a year later.

Ten children were born to this union, and they are as follows: Elizabeth (Mrs.
John Kogler), Wishek, N. D.; Jacob, Fargo, N. D.; Amelia (Mrs. Christ
Peterson), Menasha, Wisconsin; Christina Janke, Woodworth, N. D.; Henry,
Woodworth, N. D.; Fred, Appleton, Wisconsin; Adam, Appleton; Peter, Lodi,
California, and Edward, at Milwaukee, Wisconsin; John, Grafton, N. D.

Mrs. Konrad Ernst was later married to John Kautz of Artas, S. D. Mrs. Kautz is
now residing in Artas.

Mr. and Mrs. John Weber, Sr.

John Weber, Sr., was born January 21, 1867, in Giessen Oberhessen, Germany.
After growing to manhood and serving five years in the army in Germany, he
decided to come to the United States. On October 10, 1892, he arrived at
Eureka, South Dakota. There he worked two years for Mr. Pepper, who had a flour
mill. In 1894 Mr. Weber was married to Christine Aman. In 1895 Mr. and Mrs.
Weber came to McIntosh County, where they homesteaded four miles east of the
present site of where Wishek is now located. Their first house and barn was
made of sod. The only fuel the first winter was hay and straw. Their trading
towns were Eureka, South Dakota, and Kulm, North Dakota.

In 1898 when the railroad was built from the east, it came within twenty feet
of the house, so a new house had to be built farther away from the tracks. This
was built of sod also.

Seven children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Weber, five of whom survive. They are
John, farming on the old homestead; Emma (Baltzer), Napoleon, North Dakota; Ida
(Brown), Wishek, North Dakota; Helen (Burrack), Monticello, Iowa; Arthur,
farming near Lidgerwood, North Dakota.

Mr. Weber had always taken an active part in the community, county and state
affairs. He served for twenty years in various offices in the School District.
He served as representative to the Legislature in 1917 and 1919 and as Senator
in 1921 and 1923.

In 1918 Mr. and Mrs. Weber retired and moved to Wishek, where Mr. Weber died on
June 23, 1938. Mrs. Weber still lives in Wishek. Mr. and Mrs. are charter
members of the American Lutheran Church.

Mrs. C. C. Sheridan

Mrs. C. C. Sheridan was born November 6, 1850, daughter of William and
Christine Tepperman. She was married to William Sheridan in Minneapolis,
Minnesota. Two children were born to this union. They are Grace (Nickisch),
Minneapolis, Minnesota, and Clarence A., deceased.

In 1899 she built a small restaurant where the Pfeifle Garage is now located.
In 1900 Mrs. Sheridan constructed the Sheridan Hotel--second hotel in Wishek.
In 1905 she sold this hotel to her son-in-law, Mr. D. G. Nickisch. It was
almost impossible to obtain vegetables and meat to supply the restaurant
because there were no markets and the farmers raised only enough for their own
use. Between 1898 and 1899 the railroad between Kulm and Wishek was completed.
In order to provide food for the railroad help the construction foreman had
provisions sent in from Eastern Markets. Mail and fresh meat were delivered
from Eureka once a week by stage.

Mrs. Sheridan came to Wishek in 1898 and remained here until her death in
March, 1938.

Mr. and Mrs. P. H. Mueller

Mrs. Magdalena Mueller, daughter of Henry and Rose Aman, nee Leicht, was born
January 14, 1883, at Bergdorf, South Russia. In June, 1900, she was united in
marriage to Philip H. Mueller, son of Philip G. and Rose Mueller, nee Goebel,
at Eureka, South Dakota. In 1901 the Mueller family saw Wishek for the first
time and the following year on July 19, 1902, they moved to this community, and
located on a farm fifteen miles northeast of Wishek.

Eleven children came to bless the Mueller home, and were brought up in the
Evangelical faith as were Mr. and Mrs. Mueller. The children are Arthur, Kulm,
N. Dak.; Albert, Herbert, Theo, Robert, Thomas and Mrs. Rose Kocher, all at
Wishek, N. Dak.; Mrs. Ruth Schnabel, Napoleon, N. Dak.; Mrs. Freda Eggen,
Burnstad. N. Dak.; Mrs. Lydia Wolf, Mobridge, S. Dak.; and Lena, Moorhead,
Minnesota.

Mr. Mueller passed away March 18, 1944, and since his death Mrs. Mueller
resides in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. John G. Schmidt

Worthy of mention as pioneers of Wishek are Mr. and Mrs. John G. Schmidt. Mr.
Schmidt was the son of George and Clara Schmidt, nee Goldade, born January 28,
1882, at Kandel, South Russia.

Mr. Schmidt came to this community with his parents in the spring of 1889, and
located near Ipswich, South Dakota, where they remained for the summer months.
The father of Mr. Schmidt worked on a threshing machine to earn enough money to
file on a homestead. He started west on foot, arriving at Eureka, South Dakota.
He was informed that there was a lot of government land forty or fifty miles
further northwest, where he filed on a homestead. He then walked back to
Ipswich again, where he bought a pair of oxen for $60, an old wagon for $10,
and an old cow. He packed what little household goods they had on the wagon and
with Mother Schmidt and the five children they started for their new home. The
Schmidt's first home was a sod shanty with two rooms. The family lived in one
room and the oxen and cow in the other room.

On October 13, 1902, Mr. Schmidt was united in marriage to Margaretha Mastel,
daughter of Cassimer and Elizabeth Mastel, nee Neninger, at the St. Aloysious
Church near Hague, North Dakota. To this union were born nine children, Clara
(Holzer), Trail City, S. Dak.; Lawrence, Dickey, N. Dak.; John, Wishek, N.
Dak.; Sister M. Constance, St. Paul, Minnesota; Betty, Gladstone, N. Dak.;
Catherine (Werner), Spokane, Wash.; Mary (Meier), Wishek, N. Dak.; James,
Wishek, N. Dak.; and Leo, LaCrosse, Wisconsin.

In 1904 Mr. Schmidt filed on a homestead in McIntosh County, west of Wishek.
Like many other pioneers they were forced to gather buffalo bones, which were
sold at $4.50 a ton, so that a few groceries could be bought. The nearest
trading post was Eureka, South Dakota, which was about sixty miles from the
homestead, and would require about a week to make the round trip. It was only
their determination to make good in this country that kept them going and
helped them through those severe winters of the pioneer days.

Mr. Schmidt took great interest in church and school affairs of this community
and also served as a member of Legislature for one term.

On July 24 1943, the earthly record of John G. Schmidt was closed by death. His
death marked the passing of one of this community's respected citizens. Mrs.
Schmidt is at present making her home on the Schmidt homestead with her son
James and daughter Mary Meier.

Mr. and Mrs. John Junkert

As a young lad, John Junkert came with his parents to America in April, 1885.
He was the son of George and Margeretha Meyer Junkert, born on January 25,
1882, at Kassel, South Russia. The parents of Mr. Junkert located in this
community where he grew up to manhood and was united in marriage to Mary Hein,
born May 3, 1886, daughter of Christian and Magdaline Schwalm Hein, on December
21, 1905.

Five children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Junkert. They are Emil, Enderlin, N.
Dak.; John, Minneapolis, Minn.; Edwin, Wishek, N. Dak.; Mrs. Lenora Costin,
Coeur d'alene, Idaho; and Mrs. Florence Newgren, Fargo, N. Dak.

On September 21, 1911, Mr. Junkert began working on the Soo Line railroad as a
car man. Working hard and continuously for a period of thirty-six years, he
retired on November 29, 1947, from the above mentioned job. He is at the
present time enjoying his well earned rest.

The Junkert family joined the Congregational church when coming to Wishek, but
at the present time Mrs. Junkert is a member of the Assembly of God.

Friederich Vossler, Sr.

In May, 1886, Mr. and Mrs. Friederich Vossler came to their new home 17 miles
southwest of Wishek. Mr. Vossler had been born on September 29, 1841, in South
Russia. Mrs. Vossler, Friedricka, nee Adam, was born on March 10, 1853. The
first stopping place in this country was Scotland, South Dakota, where they
spent their first winter in the United States.

Coming to McIntosh County they too, established their home, building it of sod.
With other pioneers they gathered buffalo bones, the sale of which supplied
them with the means to purchase the necessities of life.

Going to Ipswich in late fall of one of those early years, Mr. Vossler and
several neighbors were caught in a snow storm, as they were returning to their
prairie homes. The snow became so deep that they could go no further with their
wagons. They found some trees and made snow boats, on which they loaded their
supplies and continued homeward. In Spring they returned to pick up their
wagons. After eight days' absence, they arrived at home, thus alleviating the
care and worry of their families.

Mr. Vossler was also one of the men instrumental in the organization of St.
Andrews Lutheran congregation and served as deacon for many years.

Mr. Vossler passed away on March 20, 1919. His wife followed him in death on
May 15, 1921.

Their children are: Christina (Mrs. Bader), deceased; Dorothea (Mrs.
Bollinger), Herried; Johannes, deceased; Friederich, Jr., Lodi, California;
Maria (Mrs. Bietz), Turtle Lake, N. Dak.; Edward, deceased; Mathilda (Mrs.
Pfau), deceased; Katherina (Mrs. Just), Mercer, N. Dak.; Jacob, Wishek;
Gottfried, deceased; and Christ L., of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Martel

Jacob Martel and his wife Margaret (Spitzer) Martel were natives of Kassel,
South Russia. Mr. Martel was born August 27, 1877, the son of Jacob and
Christina (Schnabel) Martel. Mrs. Martel was born March 20, 1872. They were
married in 1897 and made their first home in Russia. There their three oldest
children were born. Late in 1905 they left Russia and came to America to live.
They arrived in Wishek in February, 1906, and settled on a farm northwest of
Wishek. Here they resided until Mr. Martel's death in 1933. Mrs. Martel moved
to Wishek and resided here till her death in 1938.

Children born to them who grew to adulthood are Mrs. Mary Rueb, Wishek; Jacob,
Minneapolis, Minn.; Mrs. Paulina Boschee, Wishek; Mrs. Christina Boschee,
Wishek, N. D.; Mrs. Annie Rudolf, Napoleon, N. D.; Mrs. Elsa Stevahn, Elgin, N.
D.; and August, Elgin, North Dakota.

Philip Just

Philip Just was born on April 8, 1866, in Kassel, South Russia. His parents
were Christof Just and Barbara, nee Frey. He was married to Karolina Martel in
1889. She was the daughter of Jacob Martel and Christina, nee Schnabel. Her
birthday is September 5, 1867. They were the parents of nine children. The
following are still living: Gustav, Martha (Mrs. Peter Gall), Emma (Mrs. Edward
Vossler), Pauline (Mrs. Arthur Vossler), Philip P., all of Wishek, and Jacob of
Bismarck, N. Dak.

In 1894 the Just Family came to this country and spent some time at Eureka,
South Dakota. In 1905, they bought themselves a farm, 16-1/2 miles southwest of
Wishek. Here Mr. Just continued to farm until 1938 when he turned his farm over
to his son, Philip P.

Mrs. Just passed away December 2, 1926. Mr. Just remarried again on November 8,
1927, with Mrs. Christina Wohl, nee Dockter. His second wife is still living
with her children on the farm.

Mr. Just passed away on December 11 last year and is buried in the cemetery of
Friedens Lutheran Church, of which he was a member for 42 years.

John Weisser

The life history of Johann Weisser varies little from that of other pioneers in
the Wishek territory. Born November 23, 1878, in Kessel, South Russia, where he
grew to manhood and in 1903, was married to Christina Bungard, also a native of
Kessel, born in June 21, 1881.

Two years after his marriage he and his wife, in the spirit of adventure and
with the hope of advancing their own interests, departed for America. Their
destination upon their arrival in this country was in Ipswich, S. D., where the
young couple remained for one year. Then in the summer of 1906 they came to
this community, settling on a farm seven miles west of Burnstad, where some of
their first acquaintances were George Gall, Fred Ritter, Sr., Jacob Frank and
Christian Schock.

Seven children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Weisser, all of whom are living except
one who died in childhood. Those surviving are: Jacob, Lehr, N. D.; Martha
(Mrs. William Trautman), Tuttle, N. D.; Paulina (Mrs. T. Metzel), Napoleon, N.
D.; August, John, Jr., and Emma (Mrs. Gidion Eiseman), all of Burnstad, N. D.

Mr. and Mrs. Weisser are charter members of the American Lutheran Church and
have remained active for its welfare up to the present time.

In the fall of 1947, turning the management of their farm over to their son,
John, Jr., they retired and moved to Napoleon where they are still making their
home.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Bader

Many years ago in Alexandershalf, South Russia, Christian Bader was born. It
was August 9, 1836. When this young man grew up he married Jakabone Ruff on
October 22, 1858. She was born March 22. 1836. in South Russia, too. This young
couple reared a family of 20 children, 6 of whom died. They are Jacob,
Johannas, Christian, Anton, Kathrina, William, and Henry. After 14 years of
married bliss Mrs. Bader died at their home in South Russia on April 30, 1872.
Shortly afterwards Mr. Bader remarried to Johanna Heinbuck, nee Bader, in
Russia.

Because they found it difficult to rear their children properly in Russia they
emigrated to America and first settled at Scotland, S. D., in 1884. Later they
moved to a farm 15 miles southwest of Wishek. They lived in the regular sod
house. There weren't any schools either in their community or churches so they
gathered at one of their friends' homes for their church services.

Mr. Bader died in March of 1906. The only remaining child is Henry, who is
living here in Wishek at the present time.

Mr. and Mrs. Frederick Preszler

Mr. Frederick Preszler was born on March 27, 1864, at Kassel, South Russia, the
son of Martin and Elizabeth (Mehlhaff) Preszler.

In 1874, Mr. Preszler came to the United States, settling for a short time at
Yankton, S. D. On May 3, 1885, he moved to Ipswich, S. D. Here he built a little
shanty of boards. He grew restless soon and moved on to Menno, S. D.

On February 6, 1886, Mr. Preszler married Elizabeth Goering. Thirteen children
were born to this marriage. Four of the children died. Those remaining are
Frederick, Lehr; Margaret (Mrs. John Bietz), San Francisco; Henry, Washington;
Barbara (Mrs. Christ Straile), Hazelton; Mrs. Christ (Elizabeth) Herr, Wishek;
Helena (Mrs. William Hien), Ashley; Edward, Bismarck; Martha (Mrs. Walter
Levance), Detroit, Michigan, and Emelia (Mrs. Julius Herr) of New Salem, Oregon.

After marrying, Mr. Preszler went back to his homestead in Ipswich. This time
he was much more successful. On May 6, 1887, he located on a new homestead
about 1 mile south of Eureka. His possessions were few. During the next two
successive summers, Mr. Preszler found employment and with his meager earnings
built a small house and barn.

In 1905, Mr. Preszler purchased the general store from John Bietz in Eureka. In
1912, he went back to farming again. Here he specialized in raising purebred
livestock and owned a very fine and well equipped farm.

Losing his wife by death on March 24, 1910, Mr. Preszler remarried on December
20, 1910. His second wife was Mrs. Fred Hoff, formerly Margaret Guthmiller of
Eureka, S. D. Her children by a former marriage are Christina (Mrs. Fred
Guthmiller), of Hosmer, S. D.; Fred L. Hoff, Lodi, Calif.; Henry, Lydia (Mrs.
Fred Ammon), Margaret (Mrs. Martin Miller), Wishek; August and Lina (Mrs. Ed.
Herr), deceased. Mr. Preszler farmed with his family until 1917.

He purchased an interest in the hardware store in Wishek in 1917. He worked
here until 1923, when he retired. He still lives in Wishek enjoying the rest he
earned so well during his long, busy, and successful life.

Johan Kurle

Johan Kurle of Wishek, N. Dak., who was born June 17, 1850, in Bergdorf, South
Russia, son of Jacob and Eva, nee Laemmle Kurle. In 1876 he married Eva Maier
in Bergdorf, South Russia, who was born March 13, 1852, whose father was Jacob
Maier. Ten children were born to this family, five boys and five girls, four of
whom have passed away, two girls died in infancy in Russia, John at Mercer, N.
Dak., and Christian. The living are Jacob of Mercer, N. Dak; Karolina (Jacob)
Hagel, Magdalena (Carl) Sayler Bismarck, N. Dak.; Fred, Carson, N. D.; Eva (Mrs.
Fred) Haas, Elgin, N. D.; Edward of Galt, Calif. They entered America April 22,
1893, and came to this community May 4, 1894, locating 16 miles southwest of
Wishek. Mrs. Kurle passed away November 7, 1918, and Mr. Kurle September 29,
1929, at Wishek, N. Dak.

Some of the first acquaintances in Wishek were John J. Doyle, the Pratts, Mrs.
Jennie Hill. On the homestead, Ole Haukeness, Adam Berdahl, John L. Ostrem
after who Ostrem Post Office was named, George Herr, Andrew Onstadt, and Ludwig
Rudolph. The first business transaction was the sale of a load of wheat. It was
sold to John Doyle who was buying on a platform before his elevator was built.
S. Lowe & Co. were operating a store in a tent. Eureka, S. Dak., was the
nearest railroad station and the supplies often ran low so necessity became the
mother of invention. Roasted barley substituted for coffee and a porridge of
cornmeal or flour and milk was often the supper to go to bed on and sleep the
sleep of the just. An unusual amount of snow in the winter of 1896 starting in
October and lasting until April adding to the hardships and suffering of those
days. Probably even more drastic was the diphtheria epidemic of 1897, when
without any medical aid, death often was the only relief and entire families
were, in many instances, stricken by this dreaded disease. Crop failures were
not unusual. One year a bumper crop was in prospect but, before very little was
harvested, a disastrous hail storm wiped out everything he had hoped and waited
for so patiently. The first prairie fire also left its imprints on his memory.

Nick Welder

Nick Welder, living near Wishek, N. Dak., was born October 15, 1883, in South
Russia, son of George and Barbara, nee Roth. On November 26, 1905, Nick Welder
married Anna Marie Lacher, born January 15, 1885, at St. Anthony. Her parents
were Sabastian and Rosalias, nee Schweitzer. To this couple eleven children
were born, four boys and seven girls, who are Barbara, Adam, Joe, Eva,
Margaret, Ann and Theresia, all of Napoleon, N. Dak.; Sebastian, Marion and
Rose, of Bismarck, N. Dak., and George, of Burnstad, N. Dak.

Nick Welder came to America in 1888 and to this community in 1889, locating in
Logan County, Napoleon N. Dak. They saw Wishek for the first time in 189i in
the fall. Mrs. Welder passed away March 24, 1933, at Wishek, N. Dak. Mr.
Welder's first acquaintances were John J. Doyle, Steve Fisher, George Roehm,
Sr., and Leo Hunkele.

One of the first business transactions in Wishek was lumber for a house and
barn in 1905, from the Thompson Lumber Company.

Mr. Welder can remember when his father picked up buffalo bones and took them
to Dawson, N. Dak., taken there with oxen where they were sold for eight and
ten dollars a ton, with which they bought food and other necessities.

In 1905 Mr. Welder started farming and acquired a goodly amount of land, 880
acres to be exact. He took a great interest in school affairs and community
projects. He is a member of the Catholic church.

Jacob Hilscher

Jacob Hilscher, residing at 309 Lodi Avenue, Lodi, California, was born July
14, 1870, at Leipzig, South Russia, son of John and Wilhelmina, nee Schwartz,
Hilscher. Jacob Hilscher was married to Julianna Wolf in September, 1894, at
Leipzig, South Russia. She was the daughter of Jacob and Justina, nee Miller,
Wolf.

To this marriage nine children were born, five boys and four girls, namely:
Henry Hilscher, Ashley, N. Dak., Mathilda Kidroski, 511 Locust Street, Lodi,
Calif.; Theodore Hilscher, 72 Newton Street, San Francisco, Calif.; Marie
Sprenger, box 216, Ione, Calif., Ernest Hilscher, 316 East Walnut Street, Lodi,
Calif. Daniel, Martha, John and Emma are deceased.

The Jacob Hilscher family came to America in May, 1898, residing at Eureka, S.
Dak. In November, 1905, they came to Wishek, residing on a farm 8 miles
northwest of Wishek. They saw Wishek for the first time in 1904.

Some of the first people they knew after arriving in this community were: J. H.
Wishek, Sr., Daniel Nickisch, Sr., John Dewald, John J. Doyle and John Herr.

Mr. Hilscher purchased a livery stable, which was destroyed by fire. There were
the usual pioneer hardships. They lost a child who died of diphtheria. The
winters were severe with a great deal of snow.

Mr. and Mrs. Andreas Pfeifle

Andreas Pfeifle, Wishek, N. Dak., was born April 3, 1884, at Hutchinson, S.
Dak., son of Jacob and Christina, nee Denning Pfeifle. He was married to Marie
Stadel December 16, 1905, at Scotland, S. Dak., who was born March 5, 1881, the
daughter of Konrad and Dorathea, nee Denning Stadel. To this family four
children were born, one boy and three girls. The children are: Anette (Mrs.
Anton) Kolasch, Ellendale, N. Dak.; Laura (Mrs. John) Kemmet, Wishek, N. Dak.;
Helmuth, Andelene (Mrs. Julius) Bender, Guenthner Pfeifle, all of Ashley, N.
Dak. They came to this community in 1906, settling 15 miles south of Wishek.

Mr. Pfeifle took up a 160-acre homestead, starting his venture with one cow,
two chickens, one rooster and one pig. He borrowed his father's team of horses
and hand plow to till the soil. They lived in a frame house of boards 14x22
where they also kept their seed. Their barn was 14x14 covered with hay. His
first harvest was given to his father for his payment. Their means of travel
was by horse and wagon. In 1909 they built a larger barn 26x48 and in 1917
moved one mile south where they built a house. He helped work on the Soo Line
railroad when they built the tracks to this community.

Karl Wiest

Karl Wiest was born June 20, 1880, in South Russia, son of Heinrich Wiest, Sr.,
and Johanna Wiest, nee Hoffman. He was married June 17, 1899, to Dorothea
Boschee, daughter of Valentine Boschee and Barbara Boschee, nee Gehring. To
this union the following children were born: John K., Herried, S. Dak.; Ed. K.,
Wishek, N. Dak.; Theodore H., Venturia, N. Dak., Paulina (Fischer), Ashley, N.
Dak.; Reinhold A., Rupert, Idaho; Frieda (Krein), Clair City, S. Dak.; Clara,
Watertown, S. Dak.; Marie (Hieb), Rupert, Idaho; Karl J., Ashley, N. Dak.;
Rosina (Just), Zeeland, N. Dak.

Mr. Wiest came to this country in 1885, to Menno, S. Dak., and the following
year went to McIntosh County, N. Dak., where he homesteaded, erecting a sod
house, which was very common in the early years of our pioneers--the sod for
the house was plowed with oxen. His nearest town was Ipswich, S. Dak.,
Ellandale, N. Dak., and Eureka, S. Dak. On this vast prairie where the buffalo
had roamed by the thousands, naturally their bones were numerous and proved to
be valuable in providing a livelihood for the early settlers. The bones were
gathered and hauled to town by oxen, where they were traded for groceries,
which were not too plentiful. Food conservation is not a new subject. To
conserve sugar a table fork was used instead of a spoon. With very crude
machinery and oxen for power, farming was accomplished.

In 1889 he helped build the railroad grade into Wishek. In 1899 he helped build
one of the first grain elevators to be constructed in Wishek, N. Dak.

In 1925 Mr. Wiest was elected County Commissioner from his district and served
until 1929--one year as Chairman. During his term of office as county
commissioner, he was influential in the construction of the first county
highway.

On July 15, 1937, Mr. Wiest passed away in Venturia, N. Dak. On August 16,
1944, Mrs. Wiest passed away in Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. Heinrich Deyle

Heinrich Deyle of Wishek, N. Dak., who was born October 15, 1872, at Bergdorf,
South Russia, son of Emanuel and Margaretha, nee Jung, Deyle. Heinrich Deyle
married Barbara Hochhalter, February 13, 1898, at Kassel, South Russia, who was
born August 13, 1876. She was the daughter of Friedrich and Margartha, nee
Rueb, Hochhalter. To this union seven children were born, two boys and five
girls, two are deceased. The children are Barbara Boschee (August) Magdalina
Eisenbeis, (Jacob) Henry H. Deyle, all of Wishek, N. Dak.; Margaretha Rudolf
(August), Harden, Mont.; David Deyle, Billings, Mont.

Mr. Deyle came to America in 1891 and to this community in May, 1899. They took
up a homestead eight miles west of Wishek, N. Dak. They saw Wishek for the
first time in 1898. Mr. Deyle passed away October 31, 1927, at home.

They experienced the usual hardships of pioneers. Their first home was a tent,
later they built a sod house where they lived for a few years. The crops at
that time were poor, and labor was fifty cents a day.

The first people Mr. Deyle met upon his arrival here were: August Herr, John
Herr, Ludwich Buchler, John Rudolf, and Jacob Schauer.

Mr. and Mrs. Wilhelm Mittelstadt

Wilhelm Mittelstadt was a native of Germany, having been born in the town of
Dunergeroik on April 17, 1857. During his boyhood he migrated with his parents
to the Ukraine area of Southern Russia. On October 24 1878, he was united in
marriage with Miss Katharina Pleinus at Neudorf in South Russia. She was a
native of Neudorf, having been born there on July 1, 1859. Two children were
born to them, a daughter, Ernstina, and a son, William.

Early in December of 1905, Mr. Mittelstadt, with his wife and two children
arrived as immigrants in the Port of New York. They arrived in Wishek on
December 20, 1905. In 1907 he filed on a homestead located six miles north of
Ashley. However, he did not make proof on the land but in 1909, he relinquished
the homestead and returned to Wishek where he established his permanent home.

Mr. Mittelstadt was a harness maker by trade and immediately opened a harness
shop. This he continued to operate until his death on August 27, 1930.

Mrs. Mittelstadt survived her husband by seventeen years, making her home in
Wishek during most of that time. She passed away on January 6, 1947. The
daughter, now Mrs. Ernstina Groves, lives at 1919 McIllwraith, Muskegon, Mich.
The son, Wm. J. Mittelstadt, who in the '20's, operated a tire repair shop in
Wishek, now resides at 1489 Briarwood Ave., Columbus, Ohio.

Daniel Nickisch

Daniel Nickisch was born in Sachsen by Karlsruhe, Oberschleisen, Germany, March
4, 1853. In 1874 he married Augusta M. Wieczorek. Eight children were born to
this union; two dying in infancy. In 1880 he came to America and the following
year he sent for his wife and children. Mrs. Nickisch died in 1886. The
following year Mr. Nickisch married Anna Milde. After living in Davidson
County, South Dakota, for a number of years they moved to Eureka, South Dakota,
where Mr. Nickisch acted as immigration agent for the Northern Pacific railway.
In 1901 they moved to Wishek, filed on a homestead 5 miles northwest of Wishek
and lived there until 1907. During the time Mr. Nickisch lived in Wishek he was
engaged in the insurance and real estate business, and had also been elected
Justice of the Peace for a good many years.

By his first marriage Mr. Nickisch had the following children: Traugott
Nickisch, deceased; Gottlieb Nickisch, Wishek, North Dakota; Augusta Simon,
Milwaukee, Wisconsin; Daniel G. Nickisch, St. Louis Park, Minnesota; Anna
Becker, Redfield, South Dakota; and Carl Nickisch, deceased. Five children
blessed the second union. They are: Emma West, Bismarck, North Dakota; Otto
Nickisch, Miles City, Montana; Rose Wieczorek, Parkston, South Dakota; Paul
Nickisch, Redfield, South Dakota; and Gertrude Treick, Woodland, Washington.

Mr. Nickisch passed away March 10, 1945.

Mr. and Mrs. H. E. Timm

Henry Elmer Timm has been a resident of Wishek for the past forty-one years and
a statement of his connections and associations with business and civic affairs
in Wishek and in McIntosh County during those years indicates that he has been
one of the substantial men in the community, being a type of man who is never
lacking in some useful connection and relationship with his community.

Mr. Timm was born in Franklin County, Iowa, October 22, 1885, the son of Albert
C. and Mary (Nicklas) Timm. His father was a native of Wisconsin and his mother
a native of Pennsylvania. Mr. Timm was educated in the public schools of Ackley,
Iowa, graduating from high school in 1905 and for two years attended Cornell
College at Mt. Vernon, Iowa. He came to Wishek as principal of its schools for
the year 1907-1908. In 1908 he became bookkeeper for the firm of Herr Brothers
& Company, later homesteaded near Green Lake in McIntosh County, and in 1911
entered the employ of the First State Bank of Wishek.

He served as Assistant Cashier of said bank until 1919 when he was elected
Cashier, which position he held until 1935. He was also Secretary-Treasurer of
the Bankers Loan Company of Wishek and conducted a real estate and insurance
business in connection with the bank. Since quitting bank work, he has engaged
in the real estate and insurance business.

Mr. Timm was an official of the Village of Wishek continuously for eighteen
years, from 1912 till 1930, and when, in 1930, the Village was incorporated as a
City, he was elected as its first Mayor. In 1913 he assisted in the
organization of the Wishek Volunteer Fire Department and served as its first
Chief. He was also one of the organizers of the Wishek Association of Commerce
and served as a Director for ten years. He was also interested in the
organization of the Tri County Fair Association and served for years as its
President.

Mr. Timm is a Republican and in 1930 was elected state representative from the
Thirty-sixth Legislative District, serving in the 1931 Session of the North
Dakota Legislature. He is also prominent in the Independent Order of Odd
Fellows of North Dakota, is a Past Grand Master and Past Grand Secretary of
that Order, and is serving his twenty-fifth year as a state trustee of the Odd
Fellows Home at Devils Lake. He also holds membership in Linton Lodge No. 98,
A. F. & A. M., and is an elder in the Presbyterian Church.

Mrs. H. E. Timm, whose maiden name was Carolyn Gruye, was the daughter of
Philip and Bertha Gruye. She was born in New Ulm, Minnesota, on November 20,
1886. She grew to womanhood in Winthrop, Minnesota, attended the public schools
and graduated from Winthrop High School in 1903, then attended the State Normal
at Mankato, Minnesota. After five years of teaching in Minnesota schools, she
came to Wishek in 1909 and taught the intermediate grades in the Wishek schools
for two years.

Mr. and Mrs. Timm were married at Winthrop, Minn., on December 28, 1910. They
had two sons, Ralph Elmer, born October 28, 1911, who died in infancy, and
Elbert Clifford, born April 11, 1917, who is now an oil geologist with
Socony-Vacuum Oil Company in Louisiana. Mrs. Timm passed away on July 28, 1947.
Mr. Timm still makes his home in Wishek.

During her thirty-eight years of residence in Wishek, Mrs. Timm was active in
civic, fraternal and church organizations. She held membership in the Civic
League, the Legion Auxiliary and showed a deep interest in activities of the
Boy Scouts, the Girl Scouts and the Brownie Scouts. She was a member of the
Presbyterian Church and active in its Ladies Aid. She was a charter member of
Golden Rod Rebekah Lodge #90 when it was instituted in 1914 and served as the
first Noble Grand of said Lodge. She also held membership in the Order of
Eastern Star at Linton. No. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob C. Krein

Mr. Jacob C. Krein was a child of one of McIntosh County's earliest pioneers.

He was born in the village of Neudorf, South Russia, the fourth child of
Christian and Magdalina Krein on October 21, 1884. In 1886 he accompanied his
parents and two older sisters and an older brother to America. The family
became homesteaders on a tract of land eight miles west and north of the
present site of Wishek and it was here that Mr. Krein spent his boyhood and
early manhood.

On November 24, 1908, he was united in marriage to Lydia Ackermann, the
daughter of Johanes Ackermann of Wishek, another of Wishek community's earliest
pioneers. The couple settled on a farm northwest of Wishek.

In 1911 the couple moved to Wishek and took up residence in the home where Mr.
Krein lives at the present time.

The first business venture was a grocery store in 1911. In 1912 he owned and
operated a meat market. In 1913 his business interest was a coal, cream and
flour station which he owned for three years. Following that he founded with
his brother, Christ F. Krein, Wishek's first electric light and power company.
The first electric light and power were brought to Wishek through the efforts
of the two brothers. They had realized that electric lights were the coming
thing and dreamed of the time when Wishek could have this facility. This dream
became a reality in the spring of 1916 when the first poles were set and the
first wires strung, giving Wishek the distinction of having the first electric
light and power in the whole territory. The brothers erected their own building
to house the plant. It is the building now occupied by the Scharf Blacksmith
Shop.

In 1919 Fred Krein became a partner in Krein Electric and the three brothers
operated the plant together until 1920 when J. C. Krein sold his share to J. G.
Ritter.

The Electric Company operated under this new partnership until 1924 when the
business sold out to the Dakota Public Service Co.

In 1922 and 1923 he was agent for the Standard Oil Co. and operated the bulk
station and delivery truck in Wishek. In 1923 he became manager of the Farmers
Cream Association and continued in that capacity until 1946 when the
Association was dissolved, and the business became a partnership between Mr.
Krein and his son, Victor. The business is now operating under the name of
Krein & Co.

During his years in Wishek Mr. Jacob C. Krein has taken an active part in
Wishek community life. He is the father of three sons and three daughters, five
of which are residents of Wishek, Mrs. Ida Walth, Alvin, Mrs. Irene Duncan,
Mrs. Edna Meidinger, and Victor. Rev. Gideon Krein is pastor of the First
Presbyterian church in Superior, Wisconsin. In 1947 on November 5, the family
unity was broken by the death of the wife and mother of the home. One child,
Reinhold, died in infancy.

In 1913 he served as city assessor, and again in 1925 and '26. He was school
director from 1932 to 1938. During the years 1938 to 1942, as city Mayor he saw
the laying of many sidewalks in our city, the construction of the municipal
swimming pool, and the beginning and near completion of the erection of the
Wishek City Auditorium.

He became a member of the city library board when it was organized and is still
a member. Mr. Krein is affiliated with the Reformed church.

He has seen Wishek grow from its insignificant beginning to what it is today.
He can point with pride to his share in the city's growth.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Krein

True hardships are measured by a man's fight to keep his family fed and
clothed. Pioneers coming into a land of grass and disappearing buffalo
experienced all the bitter losses and deepest needs of human existence. One
pioneer family who helped establish the civilization of this region was Mr. and
Mrs. Christian Krein, who emigrated here from Europe.

Christian Krein and his wife Magdalena Schauer were born in the city of
Neudorf, South Russia, Christian in the year 1850 and Magdalena in 1855.

To a German living in a land under Russian ownership, political freedom enjoyed
by Americans seemed most precious. The year 1886 found Mr. and Mrs. Krein and
their four small children homesteaders in the northwestern part of McIntosh
County.
