The first home was a 2-room sod structure, in one part of which were kept their
oxen. Anyone living today in the age of machinery and good roads cannot realize
how much the oxen meant to the pioneers in carving homes and communities out of
the wilderness of grass. The nearest town, Ipswich, S. D., was 80 miles away,
and only the most urgent need could make the father take the week-long trip. He
often related to his children stories of his early privations, one of which
they still talk of. At one time in the middle of the winter the family ran out
of flour. As their meals consisted of bread and coffee, and wild rabbits when
they could be caught, flour was necessary to keep the family from starving. The
oxen could not wade through the deep snow to town, nor could the father leave
his wife alone with the small children long enough for him to go to town, so
pulling a small hand sled behind him he walked thirteen miles to his nearest
neighbor. To his surprise they insisted he take 100 pounds of flour back with
him. A deeply grateful family treasured that precious sack of flour through the
remaining months of winter. Gratitude comes from the simplest kindnesses.

The family home was one of great happiness and contentment. With the coming of
nearer towns hardships were lessened. Each new neighbor was heartily welcomed.
Seven children grew to adulthood in this pioneer home. Kathrina Ackermann,
Lodi, California; Christ Krein, Wishek; Christine Boschee, Zeeland; Jacob C.
Krein, Wishek; Fred Krein, deceased, of Sidney, Montana; John Krein, Trail
City, S. Dak., and Mrs. Anna Stabler, Billings, Montana.

There are 50 grandchildren, seven of which are Wishek residents. Mrs. Christian
Krein died in 1929 and Mr. Krein died at the home of his son, Jacob, in 1930.

Mr. Krein was a man respected and loved. Privations did not make him hard, but
kind. He is recalled by all who remember him, for his fine singing voice and
his sincere Christian faith of the Reformed church. His death marked the
passing of one of Wishek's pioneer patriarchs.

Theodore Donner, Sr.

Theodore Donner, Sr., Wishek, N. Dak., was born November 20, 1854, at Old
Danzig, Russia. His mother was Sussia Koth. In 1880 he married Anna Huebner at
Old Danzig, Russia. Mrs. Donner was born September 20, 1863, the daughter of
Andrew and Annie, nee Giedt, Huebner. Twelve children were born to this family,
six boys and six girls; seven passed away. The living are Theo. T. and Emanuel,
of Wishek, N. Dak.; Walter, of 1400 N. Madison, Saginaw, Mich.; Henry, of 1939
N. Albany Ave., Chicago; and Matilda (Mrs. E. F.) Kaps, 3020 Bellow Ave.,
Chicago, Ill.

They came to America in 1885 and settled in this community July 15, 1886,
locating eight miles south of Wishek, N. Dak. Mrs. Donner passed away at their
home in Wishek, May 13, 1927, and Mr. Donner died August 10, 1934, at the home
of his son, T. T. Donner. They saw and remember Wishek at its beginning.

Mr. Donner's father died when he was born and was adopted by an aunt, Mrs.
Micheal Berndt, with whom he lived until his marriage to Anna Huebner in
Russia. Mrs. Donner's father financed the couple for their voyage to America.
Upon their arrival at New York they came west and their next stop was with
relatives at Menno, S. Dak. In 1885 at Menno they built a covered wagon and
with team of oxen and a cow they moved further north, settling in a wild
prairie country, after more settlers came they called the station Danzig, built
a church and tilled the soil. Lawrence Donner financed father to this event
(eight miles south of Wishek) and built a home of sod. With much hard labor the
house was made livable by painting inside and plastering with clay on the
outside. The barn was built of sod, too. They lived in their covered wagon
until the house was completed. Traveling at that was slow.

Ipswich, S. Dak., was the trading center the first few years, an 80 mile
distance was made by oxen. Later Eureka, S. Dak., came into existence and
finally Wishek. Now we were enthusiastic, many times the trips to these towns
of Ellendale, Eureka and Wishek were made on foot.

After this tough time was over they bought a team of horses which proved to be
much faster than the oxen team. They worked hard and reared their family,
worked themselves up to a good standing, praised the new country, (Our America,
the home of the brave) and lived a happy and contented life; attended church
and proved themselves good citizens. They retired from their old homestead,
leaving the youngest son to carry on, while they built a home in Wishek where
they lived until claimed by death. They were members of the Baptist church.

Mr. and Mrs. Andreas Stevahn

Andreas Stevahn was born in South Russia on October 25, 1876. He was the son of
Andreas and Christina (Meidinger) Stevahn. On January 25, 1900, he was married
to Christina Ziegenhagel, born November 6, 1881, the daughter of George and
Carolina (Gursie) Zigenhagel.

Shortly after their marriage the young couple came to America. They came
directly to Wishek and were welcomed into the Adam Meidinger home. Here they
spent their first winter in America. Early spring found them erecting their own
two-room sod home and establishing their own farm fourteen miles southwest of
Wishek.

For forty years this couple lived and labored on their farm. Improvements and
conveniences which followed that first two-room sod home established this farm
as one of the finer ones in the county. No home thrives continuously. There are
always some hardships. This family like all others endured hail storms and
drouths. With Mr. and Mrs. Stevahn it was a joy to see the country they loved
become more thickly settled, to see better roads over which they could haul
their produce to market, and to see the newer conveniences in farm equipment.

They were the parents of nine children, eight of whom grew to adulthood. They
are Lydia Ketterling, Wishek; Andrew G., Sacramento, Calif.; Paul A., Bismarck;
Christina Bier, Wishek; Arthur, Stockton, Calif.; Ernest, Redding, Calif.;
Amanda Walth, Wishek; and Alvin, Wishek.

Mr. Stevahn was always interested in community affairs. He served as a deacon
of Frieden Lutheran Church for seventeen years. In that church he was also
Sunday School teacher for several years. He served as a member of the board of
directors of his local school district for five years.

In 1940 Mr. and Mrs. Stevahn and their two youngest children moved to Wishek to
make their home. On April 9, 1941, Mr. Stevahn passed away after only a short
illness. Mrs. Stevahn still resides in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. August W. Herr

August W. Herr was born October 15, 1880, at Hutchinson County, South Dakota.
He was the son of Jacob and Margaret Herr, nee Krein.

In 1887 his father migrated to McIntosh County. He became familiar with all
kinds of farm work and received his education in district schools.

In 1902 he homesteaded a quarter section of land in Logan county, about 13
miles northwest of Wishek. He lived there for three years and then sold his
relinquishment and purchased the Doyle elevator in Wishek, turning his
attention to the grain business. In 1905, in partnership with his father and
brother, Jacob purchased the mercantile business of Mike Klein and conducted
business under the firm name of Herr Brothers and Company for ten years, at the
end of which time it was incorporated as The Herr Mercantile Co. At this time
he began to give most of his attention to farming and buying and shipping of
livestock. He did most of his country buying on horseback.

In 1921 Mr. Herr bought a fine herd of registered Aberdeen Angus cows with
calves at side which he continued breeding until 1938 on his farm one-half mile
northeast of Wishek. He continued building his fine herd of Angus through
selection of the best heifers and purchasing of high grade bulls until 1934,
when he was forced to sell over half of his herd of 210 Angus because of the
severe drought. Then in 1938 he disposed of the entire herd. Mr. Herr is to be
commended for the many fine bulls he leased farmers to improve their herds.

In 1916 the present Wm. Hoff Hardware store was built by Mr. Herr and operated
as a hardware store and meat market. He operated this business for several
years, then traded it off for a half section of land near Ipswich, S. D.

In 1938 Mr. Herr, in partnership with his son, Clifton, opened a livestock
auction at the site of the old stockyards. Doing business as the Wishek
Livestock Sales Co. This enterprise continued doing business until 1941.

In July of 1905 Mr. Herr married Miss Lydia Pudwill, daughter of Paul Pudwill
and Katherine Pritzkau, Mrs. Herr's father homesteaded on the site which is in
the city of Wishek today. Mrs. Herr's father had the first cafe in Wishek and
she worked there as a waitress. She has been a lifelong member of the Wishek
Baptist Church, also of the Baptist Ladies Aid of which she has been an active
member. Mrs. Herr lives in Wishek with her son, Clifton, in the former Fred
Hoff residence.

To this family three children were born, namely: Agnes Colvin, Ludington,
Mich.; Margaret Dircks, Minneapolis, Minn., and Clifton, of Wishek.

Mr. Herr passed away February 5, 1941, and was laid to rest in the Wishek
cemetery.

Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Sayler

Friedrich Sayler was born May 13, 1860, in Johanesthal, South Russia. In 1883,
he was married to Barbara Genzle in South Russia. Looking for a better land,
Friedrich Sayler, his wife and their three children left Neuzats, South Russia,
and came to Minno, South Dakota, where they remained eight days. While here
they purchased two horses, two oxen, two cows, a wagon and a plow, which they
shipped to Eureka by train. The remainder of the trip was made by wagon to the
place where the farm is now located, six miles northwest of Wishek. They
brought lumber along for a roof for a sod house and also some kitchen
equipment. He was here four years when his wife died, leaving him with five
small children, the oldest being ten years of age and the youngest less than a
day old.

The winters were very long and cold, as fuel for the stove they used dried
manure, the stove itself was built of stone and mud. The crops were poor, all
the planting was done by hand and they plowed with a hand plow. Prairie fires
were not unusual and often destroyed feed for the cattle.

One day Friedrich and his son, Jacob, went seeding and a young colt followed
them. They tried chasing it home without much success. The dog began chasing
the colt and it ran over Mr. Sayler, stepping on his back. He had several
bruises, a broken rib, and he also received a blow on his head which left him
unconscious for two days.

One of Mr. Sayler's greatest joys was attending prayer meetings and revivals
which were held in the homes.

Mr. Sayler was again married to Christina Werner, daughter of David Wemer and
Philipina Nagle, at Napoleon, N. Dak. They had nine children, Henry, Wishek, N.
Dak.; Edward, Yankton, South Dakota; Fred Wishek, N. Dak.; Emma (Mrs. Fred G.
Ackerman), Wishek, N. Dak.; Martha (Mrs. Edward Ackerman), Wishek, N. Dak.;
Bertha (Mrs. John Rieger), Wishek, N. Dak.; Christ, Detroit Lakes, Minnesota;
Albert, deceased, and Ida (Mrs. Albert Klien), Napoleon, N. Dak. The children
from the preceding marriage are Barbara Foederer, Linton, N. Dak.; Katerina
Gall, Wishek, N. Dak., Jacob, deceased; William, Jamestown, N. Dak.; and Maggie
(Mrs. Christ Schnieder), Hebron, N. Dak.

Mr. and Mrs. George Rath

George Rath, a son of George and Eva Rath, nee Bender, was born September 8,
1866, in Bergdorf, South Russia. George Rath came to the United States with his
parents in October, 1889. The family settled near Long Lake, South Dakota. On
February 17, 1894, George Rath was united in marriage to Dorothea Hoffer,
daughter of Ludwig and Barbara Hoffer, nee Dockter. After his arrival in this
country in 1889 George Rath was determined to take advantage of the
opportunities that this country offered. At times it was necessary for him to
take any kind of work he could get, in order to make a living because crops did
not always mature. Mr. Rath carried the burden like all other pioneers. He
farmed with oxen and hand plow. That was the modern method of tilling the soil
in those days. After the beginning of Wishek Mr. and Mrs. George Rath decided
to find a permanent home farther north. They arrived in McIntosh County in
1901. They settled on a homestead six miles southwest of Wishek. To this union,
thirteen children were born from which four died while in their childhood. Nine
children reached maturity and are Ludwig Rath, Galt, California; John W. Rath,
Burnstad, N. Dak.; Barbara (Mrs. Reinhold Sayler), Lodi, California; Jacob
Rath, Wishek, N. Dak.; Emelia (Mrs. Adolph Sayler), Sacramento, California; Eva
(Mrs. Ferdinand Ketterling), Wishek, N. Dak.; Mary (Mrs. Andrew Munsch),
Sacramento, California; Ferdinand Rath, Wishek, N. Dak.; Edward Rath, Wishek,
N. D.

On the homestead they built a sod house and a barn. Since there were no
churches in the community at this time, religious services were held in the
farm home. George Rath broke the sod and sowed the land by hand. Hail storms
and prairie fires were a common hazard. Crops were hailed out quite often. The
market place was only six miles away. This made pioneer life a little less
discouraging.

This family struggled along as well as they could. After twelve years on the
homestead, George Rath purchased a farm two miles west of the homestead. George
Rath was brought up in the Lutheran faith. In 1914 this family helped organize
the St. Jacobus Lutheran Church, nine miles southwest of Wishek. Mr. Rath was
particularly interested in community activities. He was a prominent farmer in
raising good livestock and grain. Mr. and Mrs. George Rath lived on their farm
home until they passed away. Mrs. Rath preceded her husband in death. On August
13, 1938, Mrs. Rath passed away. She was laid to rest in the burial grounds of
the St. Jacobus Lutheran Church. George Rath passed away on January 19, 1939 He
was laid to rest beside his wife.

Christian Kramer

Christian Kramer was born July 2, 1880, in Kassel, South Russia, son of John
Kramer and Christina Hochhalter. He was married to Barbara Schnabel February
28, 1902, in South Russia, daughter of George Schnabel and Eva Stevahn. In
1905, they came to America and in 1906 they moved to Logan County on a farm
nine miles northwest of Wishek. In 1920, they moved on another farm five miles
northwest of Wishek.

Christian Kramer has seven children. They are Christina (Mrs. August Becker),
Lehr, N. Dak.; Fred, Minneapolis; George, Wishek; Andrew Wishek Emil, Wishek;
Eva (Mrs. Jacob Stock), Wishek, and Adam, Wishek.

In 1942, Christian Kramer and his wife moved to Wishek where they lived until
the time of their death. Mr. Christian Kramer passed away at Wishek on July 3,
1946. His wife, Barbara, passed away November 6, 1946, at Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Eissinger

Adam Eissinger's pioneer story is in common with that of other people in this
part of the country in that he is a native of South Russia, being born in
Johannestal, February 14, 1867. He came to this country with his parents, Mr.
and Mrs. Andreas Eissinger in 1887. He, his father, and his brother filed claim
on land as homestead about 2 miles northwest of Wishek. The first year they
broke 15 acres of land with oxen and hand plow. They also seeded by hand. It
took all spring to prepare the soil properly for seeding. His father built the
first lumber barn in this territory. They brought the lumber from Eureka with
oxen teams. His father planted trees, which are still standing northwest of the
Gott. Siewiert farm. That was 55 years ago.

Adam Eissinger was married to Christina Lutz on October, 1889, at Artas, S. D.
He and his bride came to their new homestead 2 miles north of Wishek. To this
union 11 children were born, 7 boys and 4 girls. They are Adolph, Mrs. Wm.
Sailer, Jacob, Karl, and Henry of Wishek, and Mrs. Sussana Hawkinson of
Aberdeen, S. D.; Mrs. Karl Keubler, Aberdeen, S. D.; Mrs. Charles Hildenbrand,
Burnstad; Edward, and Benjamin of Wishek, and Christ of Lauton, Oklahoma.

They built their first home of sod and found out 15 years later that their
house was not built on their own homestead. Later a house was built of clay
blocks. This was destroyed by fire in 1913; the same year a frame house was
built.

During the first several years Eureka was the nearest shopping center. One fall
Mr. Eissinger loaded a wagon load of wheat to take to Eureka the next morning.
During the night a blizzard came up and made it impossible to make the trip
until spring. This caught the family short of supplies. But somehow they
managed to live through the winter.

Once when Mr. Eissinger went to Kulm for a load of lumber a burr came off of a
wheel while turning around in the Kulm lumber yard. The wheel didn't come off
though until he was east of Lehr. He then had to walk the rest of the way home
holding the wheel in place. Three months later on another trip to Kulm the man
at the lumber yard gave him the burr which had been found in the lumber yard.

The Eissinger family farmed 700 acres, and at one time owned 33 horses and
worked as many as 24 hours a day. He bought his first horse in Eureka, and his
first cream separator from George Gackle of Kulm, N. D., in 1904. In those days
5 gallons of kerosene, 100 lbs. of sugar and 1 package of matches lasted a year.

When the railroad was built Mr. Eissinger offered the land free of charge, but
later he was paid 80 cents by the railroad company because they couldn't accept
it as a gift. The railroad crew camped west of his farm. Mrs. Jenny Hill cooked
for the crew and was well known by most people in Wishek.

Mrs. Eissinger died April 20, 1910, and left Mr. Eissinger with a group of
small children. In 1913 he married Mrs. Elizabeth Hildenbrand. She had 11
children, which made a combined family of 16 children.

Mr. Adam Eissinger died at home on his farm on November 30, 1934, after a long
illness. Mrs. Eissinger died January 27, 1948, at the home of her son in
Bismarck.

Mr. and Mrs. Johann Boschee

Among the immigrants who came to America on the crest of the tide, which swept
in from South Russia, was Johann Boschee.

Born in Kassel, South Russia, on December 21, 1862.

A young lad of 22 years, he left his native land with his parents, Johann
Boschee and Barbara Heupel Boschee for the land of promises, America. Arriving
here in the spring of 1884. A year later on April 5, 1885, he was united in
marriage with Katherina Herr, daughter of George Herr and Katherina Bamerberger
Herr, in Tripp, S. D., also a native of Russia, born July 1, 1886.

Shortly after their marriage they took up a homestead eight miles west of
Wishek. Being one of the first settlers to settle in this territory, they
endured the same hardships, had the same joys and possessed the same courage
which was manifest among all of the early settlers.

Years of crop failures, due to late planting and lack of rain, made it almost
impossible to carry on at times, so in order to make a living and build up their
homestead, they gathered up buffalo bones, which were hauled to Ipswich, S. D.,
and sold. This being the nearest source for business transactions and necessary
supplies. These bones being in great demand brought from ten to twelve dollars
per ton.

The new town, Wishek, did not come into being until 13 years after the Boschees
settled on their homestead in the year of 1898.

Nine children were born to this union, three of whom preceded their parents in
death, namely: Mrs. John Guthmiller and Madgalina of Wishek, August of Miles
City, Montana.

The surviving children are: Christ, employed as a butcher with the Herr Merc.
Co., Wishek; Mrs. Samuel Kempf, a housewife in Havre, Montana; Mrs. Jacob
Martel, a housewife living on a farm west of Wishek; Emil, a farmer east of
Wishek, Theodore, who owns and operates the City dray line in Wishek; Arthur, a
mechanic at Linton, N. D.

Mr. Boschee passed away in his farm home on January 10, 1933. In the fall of
the same year Mrs. Boschee retired from farm life and with her son, Arthur,
moved to the city of Wishek. She remained with her children until her death at
the home of her son, Ted, on August 30, 1946.

Mr. and Mrs. Michael G. Frank

Michael G. Frank, a pioneer resident of this community, was born January 12,
1888, in Worms, South Russia, son of Peter Frank, and Elizabeth Frank, where he
lived until April 19, 1902, at the age of fourteen he immigrated to Eureka,
South Dakota with his parents. In May, 1902, he moved to Logan county, on a
farm 10 miles northwest of Wishek. In 1908, he moved to Wishek and worked in J.
J. Doyle's Elevator for two years. On December 10, 1910, he married Carolina
Miller, daughter of Christian Miller and Margaretha Schwindt. In the same year
he bought and operated a livery stable for a period of three years.

In 1916, his wife died and left him with three small children. This same year
he married Elizabeth Miller, his first wife's sister. This marriage was blessed
by five children, Peter, Linton, N. Dak.; Christ, Valley, Nebraska; Henry,
Wishek, N. D.; Johnny, Wishek, N. Dak.; and Irene, Wishek, N. Dak. The three
children from the preceding marriage are Elizabeth (Mrs. Gil Albert), Edgeley,
N. Dak.; Lydia (Mrs. John Skaret), Clarkfield, Minnesota, and Martha (Mrs.
Clifford Volden), Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. Frank, in order to provide a decent living for his family has worked out
all his life, having also served as marshal and later as an assessor for this
community. At the present time he is employed at the Wm. F. Hoff Hardware Store.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Gaier

Christian Gaier was born in December 16, 1863, in Grossliebenthal, South
Russia, son of John Gaier and Barbara Betsch, where he grew to manhood. He came
to this country in October 20, 1885, and in 1886, he moved fifteen miles
southwest of Wishek. In December 25, 1893, he was married to Barbara Dollinger,
daughter of John Dollinger and Elizabeth Rieger, in McIntosh County. To them
were born six children, one having died in infancy. The children are Lydia
Bailey, Yakima, Washington; Rose Bailey, Sacramento, California; Bertha
Sholley, Tucson, Arizona; Ida Schock, Lodi, California; and Emily Seefried,
Lodi, California.

One day when Christian went to Ellendale to the market, a journey of eight
days, he encountered a little difficulty on the return trip. The snow was
exceedingly deep, all that could be seen in the wild expanse was the union of
the sky and the snow in the horizon. Suddenly, Christian, horse, wagon, and all
dropped down through the snow into Hoskins Lake. One horse drowned and the
supplies were lost but fortunately Billy Briggs came along and rescued
Christian.

Some of his pleasant memories were seeing the wilds cleared and the development
of the town, and also the building of his sod house.

Christian Gaier died January 16, 1934, at Wishek, N. Dak. Mrs. Gaier is living
at Lodi, California, at the present time.

Mr. and Mrs. Paul Pudwill

Paul Pudwill, one of the early pioneer settlers of the site of present Wishek,
was born January 19, 1857, at Alt Danzig in South Russia. He was the son of
Christoph and Katharina Pudwill. When Paul was still quite young, his father
died and he became the bread-winner of the family.

In 1881 he was united in marriage with Katherina Pritzkau, an uncle of the
bride performing the ceremony. The couple continued to live in Russia until
1886, at which time Mr. Pudwill decided to seek a new home in America. On
November 2, 1886, he, together with his wife, three small children and his
widowed mother landed as immigrants in the Port of New York. They proceeded by
train to Tyndall, So. Dak., where they remained over the winter of 1886-1887
with a brother-in-law. In the spring of 1887, the family moved by team into
what is now McIntosh County, and Mr. Pudwill filed on land in Section
10-132-71, which land is now a portion of the Wishek townsite. A sod house was
built and a farm established.

After proof was made on the land, Mr. Pudwill moved to land in Section
35-131-71 but he continued to farm both tracts until 1898. On June 20, 1898, he
sold the land in Section 10-132-71 to the Minnesota Loan & Trust Co. and that
Company arranged for the platting of a townsite and the founding of the town of
Wishek on a portion of that land the same fall. With the founding of the town,
Mr. Pudwill built one of the first buildings erected in the town, for use as a
restaurant. The building still stands on Wishek's main street, is owned by J.
W. Hofer and is now occupied by the Farmers Hardware Co. Mr. Pudwill soon
discontinued the restaurant business and returned to the farm, leasing the
building to Tony Biornson for use as Wishek's first drug store.

Mr. Pudwill was a sturdy and hard working individual. He lived through all the
hardships and adversities experienced by our early pioneer settlers. He early
recognized the business of cattle raising as a source of profit in an area
where range land was plentiful. He expanded his business of cattle raising and
increased his herds. In 1906, he moved to Section 20-31-70 on the north shore
of what is now known as Pudwill Lake near the town of Danzig, No. Dak., where
more range and hay land was available. Fortune smiled upon his efforts and he
increased his land and property holdings from year to year.

Death came to Mr. Pudwill very suddenly on July 20, 1918. At the time of his
death, he was the owner of 2,160 acres of land in McIntosh County, No. Dak.,
and 160 acres in the State of Texas. Mrs. Pudwill survived her husband by more
than twenty years and passed away only a few years ago.

Mr. and Mrs. Pudwill were the parents of thirteen children. Three of the
children, Anna (Mrs. T. T. Donner), Benjamin and Harold are now deceased. The
surviving children are: Lydia (Mrs. Aug. W.) Herr, William J., Mrs. Amma
Anderson, John J., Theodore, Sophia (Mrs. J. R.) Meidinger, Paul J., Jacob,
Kate (Mrs. R. G.) Mensing and David.

Mr. and Mrs. Martin Elmer Pratt

Martin Elmer Pratt, real pioneer of McIntosh County and one of the founders of
Wishek, was born May 1, 1863, in Canal Fulton, Ohio. At the age of sixteen, he
left home to shift for himself. He worked for a year in Kansas, then went to
Colorado where he worked for the Santa Fe railroad in 1880. The next year found
him working for the Mexico Central railroad as a lineman, helping build a
railroad in Old Mexico. Later he worked in California, then in the State of
Oregon. In the spring of 1885, he arrived in Jamestown, No. Dak. His uncle,
Martin Young, after whom Youngstown Post Office and Youngstown School District
was named, was at the time living on the east bank of Lake May, near the
present site of Wishek. He induced young Mr. Pratt to settle in McIntosh County.

On December 18, 1885, Mr. Pratt was married to Sallie Paxton by Probate Judge
Alanson Richards. They were the first couple to be married in McIntosh County.
The following spring, they moved to their homestead located two miles east and
one mile north of the present city of Wishek.

In the fall of 1893, Mr. Pratt was named postmaster of Youngstown Post Office,
conducting the same in his home. The family later moved to Kulm, but when the
railroad reached the present site of Wishek, Mr. Pratt came with it. In
September, 1898, he moved the first building, from Kulm, onto the present Wishek
townsite. That fall he built a combination Post Office and home for his family
on Lot 14 of Block 2, adjacent to the First State Bank building. Youngstown
Post Office was moved into Wishek and Mr. Pratt became Wishek's first
Postmaster, an office which he held until 1914. With his wife acting as
Assistant, he operated a dray line at the same time. After retiring from the
Post Office, he was employed as a grain buyer by the Doyle Grain Company for a
number of years.

Mr. Pratt was active in the early development of Wishek. He served as the
community's first school treasurer. He was also a member of the Village Board
of Trustees and acted as Village Marshal.

Mr. and Mrs. Pratt were the parents of seven children: Elmer, born 1886, died
1888; Henry, born 1887; Charles, born 1888 (recently deceased); Eva, born 1889,
deceased; Pearl, born 1890, died in infancy; Lillian, born 1894; Wishek, born
1900, died by fatal accident in 1910.

In the late '20's, Mr. and Mrs. Pratt moved to Yakima, Washington, where he
passed away in 1932 at the age of 69 years. Later Mrs. Pratt remarried. Death
came to her several years ago in her home in Washington.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Bauer

Among the early pioneers of McIntosh County, coming here in 1886, were
Christian Bauer and his wife, Katherina. Mr. Bauer was born in Alexanderhilf,
South Russia, October 31, 1855. He married Katherina Hemmerick in October,
1881. She was born in Alfreudensthal, South Russia, on September 4, 1858.

With his wife and two children he arrived in Scotland, South Dakota, in June,
1886. Knowing of a friend that lived farther northwest, he purchased two oxen,
a wagon, and a plow, and the family journeyed on to McIntosh County where they
located their friend, Christ Bader. They filed on a homestead 14 miles
southwest of the present site of Wishek. Since the spring season was over and
seeding could no longer be done, they decided first of all to build a house,
which was made of sod; a barn was also constructed to shelter the stock. Hay had
to be gathered to be used for fuel over the long severe winter months. In the
Spring of the following year a few acres were broken up in which flax was
planted. The first years on their homestead were lean ones, but every year
brought some progress and as the years went by they acquired more land and more
property.

The trips to town were usually long ones since there was no nearby towns until
1898. Mr. Bauer heard that there was a town northeast of their homestead, so
with his team he started across the prairie with no road to follow. After he
had driven about 14 miles he came to a very small town, which was being started
and he was told that this town was Wishek. After this they made it their
trading center.

Realizing the need for a church, they started building the St. John's Lutheran
Church in 1891, of which Mr. Bauer was a member.

Mr. and Mrs. Bauer are the parents of eight children, one of whom died in
infancy. They are Katherina (Mrs. Jacob Brosy), Wishek; Christian, deceased;
Henry, deceased; Christina (Mrs. Jacob Ottmar), Mott, N. Dak.; Pauline (Mrs.
Christof Gerr), Tuttle, N. Dak.; Phillipina (Mrs. Henry Rueb), Streeter, N.
Dak.; and John, Wishek, N. Dak.

On October 10, 1911, Mr. Bauer was called to the great beyond, and was laid to
rest in the St. John's cemetery. After the death his widow, with the help of
the family, took over the burden of farming for some years and then she made
her home with her son, John, until the time of her death on April 13, 1923.

Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Herr

The history of Wishek would be far from complete without mentioning Gottlieb
Herr and his family. Mr. Herr came to Wishek in its early stages and has lived
here, worked here, and is still in business with the Herr Mercantile Co. His
'service with a smile' is much appreciated by all. He has added much to the
welfare of the community and is a faithful member of the Wishek Baptist Church.
He has been a member of the church choir for 39 years and has seldom missed a
rehearsal nor the rendering by the choir at a Worship service. His wife
Katherine, daughter of Peter Pfeifle and Katherine Deuschle, is also a faithful
worker in her Church and is one of the two remaining charter members of the
Women's Missionary Society.

Mr. Herr was born in Tripp, S. D., in 1885 and saw Wishek for the first time in
1899 when he came in on a wagon with his father. His first purchase was a very
important one for him as a lad, for it was a bag of candy, and this purchase
was made from Mr. A. Klein, the storekeeper.

On December 22, 1907, Gottlieb Herr married Katherina Pfeifle and to this
marriage there were ten children born: six boys and four girls. The two oldest
boys, Isador and Oscar, and the youngest, Robert, died. Alvin Herr resides in
Los Angeles, Calif.; Harvey, in Portland, Ore.; Kenneth, in Wishek, N. D.;
Eleanor (Mrs. Gerald Bailey), in Wishek, N. D.; Priscilla (Mrs. John
Bartholomew), in San Luis Obispo, Calif.; Stella (Mrs. Alvin Boschee), in
Valejo Calif.; Evelyn (Mrs. Lerry Staten), Los Angeles, Calif.

Not soon to be forgotten are many incidents in the life of Mr. Herr. Among them
he recalls the 16 oxen on his father's farm and working with them. How could a
boy forget the time when he left his three-horse team hitched to a harrow, to
see whether his parents, who had returned from town, had some treats for him,
then to have his horses run away and break the implement? There was the first
cream separator on his father's farm and people came from miles around to see
it work. Their first house was a sod structure, 8 x 20 ft., which housed
horses, cows, chickens, pig, dog and family--and what a happy lot they were and
very grateful for a home of their own.

At 18 years of age Mr. Herr began as a helper in the Herr Mercantile Co. store
and in later years became a partner in the business.

Wishek, N. D., is "The" place to live for Mr. and Mrs. Herr, and they reside in
their present home at the east end of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred J. Just

Fred J. Just was born January 7, 1890, at Zeeland, North Dakota. He was the son
of George and Christina (Rudolf) Just. In 1914 he was married to Magdalena
Schwindt at Zeeland. She was born March 15, 1893 the daughter of Simon and
Christine (Weidmeir) Schwindt.

The young couple just after their marriage went to Montana. They lived two
years in Miles City and then farmed for three years near there. In 1919 they
moved back to Zeeland and lived there for two years. In 1920 they moved to the
farm they now occupy 3-1/2 miles north of Wishek. For 28 years they have been
building up a fine prosperous farm, establishing themselves with Wishek's most
progressive farmers. They have six children all in this community: Mrs. Ida
Ebel, Mrs. Eleanor Rath, Mrs. Esther Weisser, Alvin, Albert, and Stella. They
have three grandsons.

Mr. and Mrs. J. C. Nippolt

Among those who saw Wishek from its early beginnings and added to its growth
are Genevieve M. Hill and J. C. Nippolt. Miss Hill arrived in Wishek, N. D., in
1898. Mr. Nippolt in 1899 and they were married on the 24th day of December,
1899, in Aberdeen, S. D. To this union there was a son and a daughter born:
Irwin W., now residing in Long Beach, Calif., and Irene G. Nippolt Noble,
residing in San Gabriel, Calif.

Mr. Nippolt is the son of Wm. J. Nippolt. He was born in Stillwater County,
Minn., on the 14th of May, 1876. Mrs. Nippolt was born in old Fort Lincoln,
Dakota Territory, on August 20, 1877, and is the daughter of Wm. Hill. In 1894
she came with her mother to Eureka, S. D., where they operated a hotel. Mrs.
Elill mother of Genevieve, came to Wishek, N. D., at its early beginnings and
operated a rooming house until her hotel, The Wishek House, was built. It was
completed in the fall of 1898 and that fall her daughter came to Wishek for
permanent residence.

To see Wishek for the first time, Mr. Nippolt made a special detour on the 6th
of July, 1899, when returning from a 4th of July celebration at Eureka, S. D.
At this time he was herding cattle at Wescot Creek, Emmons County, near the
sight where Linton now stands. Perhaps, in all fairness, it might be said here
that not only the new Wishek attracted Mr. Nippolt to make the trip, but he was
not a total stranger among the people here at that time. Among them, by far not
the least, was Miss Genevieve Hill, who soon thereafter became his bride. Other
acquaintances were: John J. Doyle, Steve Fischer, Andrew Wollman, Dick Bailey,
Ed Lunn, and several others. On this first visit he met: Arthur Guy, Frank
Spurzem, Nordy Biornson, Mr. and Mrs. Elmer Pratt.

Hardships were not particularly felt in those days by Mr. Nippolt. Food was
plenty and work was to be had. "Although," he says, "most everything could be
classified as a hardship compared to present standards." Some of the present
generation may think it unusual to sleep in a tent and cook all meals on an
open fire for five months, rain or shine, and keep 700 head of cattle on proper
range. Mr. Nippolt writes, "I am now past 71 years of age and the most pleasant
memories I have are centered around my life spent in Wishek--believe it or not.
Everybody had a lot of fun. Some scraps loomed awfully high at the time, such
as political fights, baseball games, horse races and elections. My, my, how
serious they were at that time. When it snowed in for a week or so we had
dances and parties. The old I.O.O.F. was always on the job. Every Thursday
night there was a lunch or a dance or both. If any one got sick or had bad
luck, the real old spirit of neighborliness was always present. Such was my
experience in the early days of Wishek. May it live long and prosper!"

Mr. Nippolt also expressed his desire to be present for the Jubilee this
summer, but since his writing, he has passed to the Great Beyond. He passed away
on March 29, 1948. His wife preceded him on December 29, 1945. They have run
their course, may their souls rest well.

Mr. and Mrs. John Moore

John Moore, son of Robert Moore, was born in 1861 in Illinois. Jesse McKinley,
of Hokuh, Minn., became his wife in 1890. She was the daughter of Mr. and Mrs.
Donald McCallen. Three boys and one girl were born to this wedlock: Jim,
deceased; Niel, Wishek, N. D.; Elizabeth, also deceased; Don, Neenah, Wisconsin.

The westward urge brought Mr. Moore into Wishek in September, 1901, as a pump
repair man for the Soo Line railroad. Then in 1902 he filed on a homestead one
and a half miles east of Wishek and had his family come here from Hokuh, Minn.,
the same year.

Mr. Moore was a charter member of the Odd Fellows Lodge here in Wishek and
remained a member until death in 1947. First acquaintances were: the Dick Putly
family who operated a Livery Barn business; A. P. Guy, Attorney; J. J. Doyle, a
General Merchant. The first household necessities were purchased from Mr. J. J.
Doyle.

Being a man of varied ability there arose no special difficulties in supplying
the family larder. Farming, along with the continued work with the Soo Line
railroad gave the necessary means for a good livelihood. Hardship and reverses
of course were not entirely unknown to the Moore family. At an early age his
son Niel had the misfortune of becoming disabled--a misfortune that he has
carried with him bravely throughout the years. Mrs. John Moore died on February
17, 1946, at the age of 82 years, and Mr. Moore passed away in 1947, August
10th.

Naturally, pioneering in North Dakota would scarcely pass without a few
memorable experiences. Especially could a Dakota blizzard add zest to life on
the plains. In 1903, April the 24th, a blizzard hit this vicinity that froze
hundreds of cattle and put human life in jeopardy. At another occasion Mr.
Moore, as section foreman, turned butcher. The train ran into a herd of cattle
and massacred 35 head. Those must have been the "good old days" when the Soo
Line was still young and nimble and could put full steam ahead.

Mr. and Mrs. August Sukut

June 7, 1887, marked the milestone of a great experience in the lives of Mr.
and Mrs. August Sukut when their boat docked in the New York harbor. Here they
found the beginning of a new and vast land that offered much to those who were
ready to prove themselves worthy of its riches and blessings. Leaving New York,
Mr. and Mrs. Sukut began the seemingly endless journey to Bowdle, S. D. Here
they were met by his brother Wilhelm, who had come to this country three years
earlier, and was at this time settled in the Beaver Creek area. Thence began
the search for land upon which to build a home and make a livelihood. A covered
wagon, a team of oxen, and many days of driving, brought them to a place about
20 miles northeast of where Wishek now stands, on section 12-134-70, in Logan
County.

In spite of many reverses and hardships, this place offered a home and a good
living for a family of 13 children. The first house was built of virgin prairie
sod. The closest railroad was Ellendale, N. D. From five to six days were
required to make this trip with the streamlined 'Oxen-mobile.' Food was at a
premium. Buffalo bones were gathered and hauled into Ellendale and this
provided a little cash with which to purchase flour and a few necessities. Meat
was almost unknown for the first winter and great rejoicing was in that home
when on one occasion they were able to catch a wild rabbit. In 1889 a prairie
fire razed the country and destroyed their home. Penniless, homeless, and
courage sorely tried, but not defeated, Mr. Sukut went as a laborer and worked
for one dollar a day on the Martin Young Ranch. On Saturday night the trip home
was made on foot, so that he could visit his family. The luxury of a mail
service amounted to, not daily service, nor twice a week, but twice a year,
until Kulm was built. On one occasion Mr. Sukut carried a small bag of flour 30
miles for his family.

August Sukut, son of Wilhelm Sukut, was born June 17, 1862, in Paris,
Bassarabia, South Russia. In the year 1884 he wed Juliana Bogner, who was also
born in Bassarabia, South Russia, and to this union 9 boys and 4 girls were
born: Edward, of Napoleon N. D.; Daniel, Kidder, S. D., Marie (Mrs. Karl
Zimmerman), Napoleon, N. D.; August, who served in the first World War and was
killed in action on Armistice Day; Wilhelm, Ellendale, N. D.; Julius, Wishek N.
D.; Mileta (Mrs. George Scharf), Wishek, N. D. Gottlieb, of Lehr, N. D.; Emma
(Mrs. Arthur Bender) Accampo, Calif.; Aaron, Wishek, N. D.; Gideon, Aberdeen,
S. D.; and Esther (Mrs. Andrew Boschee), of Lehr, N. D.

Mr. August Sukut passed away on February 10 1929, and Mrs. Sukut passed away on
March 6, 1947. A young son, Ferdinand, died in 1897, at the age of 7 years. Not
only did the old homestead provide a home for the living, it is also the family
resting plot.

Mr. and Mrs. Henry Gall

One pioneer in Wishek who remembers very vividly life in Russia as well as in
the United States is Henry Gall. The eldest son of George and Elizabeth
(Trautman) Gall, he was born in Worms, South Russia, on July 21, 1887. Here he
lived with his parents until he was 12 years old.

In 1899 his family left their home in South Russia and came to America. The
first settlement was at Tyndale, S. D. Here they resided until 1906 when they
came to Wishek and settled 14 miles west of Wishek. Mr. Gall recalls that the
family moved to Wishek in January of that year, and that the weather was so
severe and the snow so deep the town was without train service for a whole
month. The train was stalled in huge snow drifts over at the Manango crossing.

On May 27, 1907, Henry Gall was married to Kathrina Sayler, the daughter of
Fred and Barbara (Gienzle) Sayler. The next year the young couple moved to
Hettinger, N. D., where they lived until September, 1909, when they returned to
Wishek and took a homestead on land five miles northwest of Wishek. On this
farm they brought up their six children. One son, Henry, Jr., still resides on
the original farm.

In October 1941, Mr. and Mrs. Gall moved to Wishek and reside here at the
present time. They have one son, Fred, living at Blanchard, N. D., and three
sons, Adolph, Henry, Jr., and Theophil, at Wishek. They have two daughters:
Mrs. Lydia Bettenhausen and Mrs. Martha Wieczorek, also of Wishek.

George Gall

One of Wishek community's early pioneers was George Gall. He was born August 3,
1863, at Worms, South Russia, the son of George and Julia (Hoffman) Gall. In
this village he grew to manhood and in 1886 married Elizabeth Trautman, who was
born November 16, 1866, in Worms, the daughter of Jacob and Christina (Milbire)
Trautman. In Russia several of their seventeen children were born.

Feeling the urge to follow many of their friends and relatives to America, they
left Russia in 1899 and settled at Tyndale, S. D. In 1906 the family moved to
Wishek and located on a farm fourteen miles northwest of town. Here they reared
their large family of children, twelve of which are still living. Mrs.
Elizabeth Ketterling, LaMoure, N. D.; Mrs. Christian Bahmiller, Burnstad, N.
D.; Philip Gall, Racine, Wisconsin, Gust Gall, Tacoma, Washington; Mrs. Paulina
Frank, Columbus, N. D.; Mrs. Anna Martel, Pollock, N. D.; and Mrs. Fredericka
Sayler, Mrs. Amelia Mueller, Mrs. Lydia Bader, Henry, Peter, and Ed of Wishek.

Early pioneers were constantly in fear of the Indians not far to the West.
Although they were not molested they were nevertheless ready to leave home at
the slightest alarm. A much greater danger was the autumn prairie fire. Many
times the fires swept uncontrolled across farm after farm, leaving behind only
the sod houses.

In 1937 Mr. and Mrs. Gall moved from their farm to Napoleon. Two years later
Mr. Gall died. Mrs. Gall continued to make Napoleon her home until her death in
1947.

Mr. and Mrs. Theodore T. Donner

Theodore T. Donner was born in the community of New Danzig, Russia, on
September 2, 1882. He was born the son of Theodore and Anna (Huebner) Donner.
In 1885 he accompanied his parents to America and settled with them in Danzig,
N. D. The first home was made of sod. Mr. Donner worked out for neighboring
farmers to help his father support the family.

The trading center in those days was Ellendale, four days travel away. It was
there the family hauled grain and other surplus products to exchange for
necessary food and clothing. The people were satisfied when they had enough
bread. They were thankful when they got their seed back. Mr. Donner's share of
his first crop was $70.00. This amounted to a whole year's income.

In 1906 he purchased a farm of his own and three years later he was married on
December 17 to Anna Pudwill, the daughter of Paul and Katherine (Pritzkau)
Pudwill. They were the parents of five children, the only son of which died in
infancy. Their four daughters are Mrs. Martha Beveriedge, Ashley, N. D., Mrs.
Florence Jameson, Minneapolis; Mrs. Viola Diede, Jamestown, N. D.; and Mrs.
Violet Kaseman, Wishek.

For several years Mr. Donner owned the garage in Danzig, renting it out while
he continued with his farming. He worked very hard to give his four girls a
good home. All four of them were graduates of the Wishek High School. Mr.
Donner has been actively interested in his community. He established one of the
finest farms in the county. He served as representative in the North Dakota
legislature in 1917.

Mrs. Donner died November 29, 1944, after a long illness. On April 14, 1946,
Mr. Donner was married to Mrs. Johanna Giedt of Danzig. The couple still reside
there but will become residents of Wishek as soon as their new home is
completed on the corner of Logan Street and Frazier Avenue.

Mr. and Mrs. John C. Schock

Mr. John Schock of Burnstad, was born October 24, 1889, in Russia. In 1903 he
came to America with his parents, Christ and Louisa (Gubart) Schock. February
9, 1911, he was married to Lydia Boschee, who was born September 11, 1892, the
daughter of Jacob and Katherine (Rudolf) Boschee.

After working for other farmers long enough to save capital for his own farm,
he purchased land three miles southwest of Wishek. Later he purchased the farm
on which he still lives, nine miles northwest of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Schock are the parents of nine children, eight of whom are living:
Gustave, Lodi, Calif.; Margaret, Great Falls, Montana; and Edward, Theodore,
August, Hilda, Emil, and Amanda, all of Burnstad. Emelia passed away at the age
of 2 years.

Mr. Schock endured the hardships common to all farmers during the past 40
years. He is farming a half section of land at present and is well pleased with
rural life. He is very proud for the opportunity of being an American farmer.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Gruebele

One of the early pioneers of Wishek is Christian Gruebele, son of Johann and
Anna Maria (Hummel) Gruebele, was born January 8, 1872, in Neuburg, South
Russia. At the age of seventeen he came to America with his brother and located
near Greenway, South Dakota.

He was united in marriage to Rosina Hinger, born March 11, 1875, daughter of
Adam and Carolina (Kemer) Hinger, on October 23, 1894. In the year of 1896 they
left the parental home of Mrs. Guebele and filed on a homestead twelve miles
southwest of Wishek, North Dakota.

The Gruebeles' first home was a sod house with home-made furniture. The winters
were very severe with a lot of snow; many times the sod houses were hidden in
the snow. It was nothing unusual for them to wake up in the morning after a
blizzard to find their doors blocked with snow, so it was generally necessary
for them to bring their snow shovels into the house in the evening, in order to
shovel themselves out in the morning.

Their nearest trading post was Eureka, South Dakota, which was thirty miles
from their farm home. Several years later, there was a store and post office
three miles west of their farm, known as the Hochstetter Store. Wishek was
established three years after they settled here. Mr. Gruebele helped build the
railroad south of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Gruebele took great interest in religious matters. They were
leaders in the Evangelical church, and for many years services were held in
their home. They were charter members of the Evangelical Eben Ezer Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Gruebele were the parents of ten children. One daughter died in
infancy, and Mrs. Emma Walz, oldest daughter, died June 12, 1944. The living
children are Mrs. Matilda Wetzel, Danzig, North Dakota; William, Wishek, North
Dakota; Christian, Wishek, North Dakota; Emil, Wishek, North Dakota; Mrs. Rosa
Bader, Lodi, California; Mrs. Magdalena Scharf, Wishek, North Dakota; Mrs. Lydia
Scharf, Wishek, North Dakota; and Mrs. Martha Levey, Lodi, California.

In 1936 they retired after forty years of successful farming, and went to Lodi,
California, where they lived until they were called to the Great Beyond. Mrs.
Gruebele passed away September 25, 1942, and after a short period of less than
two months Mr. Gruebele, too, was called to meet his Maker on November 2, 1942,
at Lodi, California. They were both laid to rest in the Evangelical Eben Ezer
cemetery southwest of Wishek, North Dakota.

Dr. George Grant

In writing this biography of Dr. George Grant, tribute is paid to one of the
most highly respected and beloved personalities in Wishek's history. For
thirty-four years he lived in and served the community as its "Family Doctor."
In his daily and professional life, he exemplified the virtues of the Good
Samaritan for his ministrations were alike to the rich and the poor, the high
and the low, the learned and the unlearned. He loved the children and they, in
turn, showed their deep affection for him.

No records are available as to the number of mothers whom he attended during
childbirth but such cases must number in the thousands. The first child whom he
brought into the world during his practice here was Olivia Miles, daughter of
Mr. and Mrs. R. C. Miles; the second was James Howard Bailey, son of Mr. and
Mrs. Wm. J. Bailey, Sr. He ministered at the bed-side of hundreds of our people
when they passed to the Great Beyond. Humanity is better and Wishek and the
world richer for his having lived and served.

Dr. Grant, a Canadian by birth, was born in Bothwell, Ontario, on June 2, 1873.
He was the son of Malcolm and Martha (Lather) Grant. His early education was
obtained in the local schools and at Chatham Collegiate Institute. Subsequently
he taught in Canadian schools for five years.

In 1900 he came to the United States and started the study of medicine. Two
years were spent in the University Medical College at Kansas City. He finished
his medical education at the Illinois Medical College in Chicago, receiving his
degree in 1904. His first year of practice was at Linton, No. Dak., but in 1905
he located permanently in Wishek. Soon after his arrival, he formed a
partnership with J. W. Hofer, known as the Wishek Drug Company. They purchased
the drug stock from Tony Bjornson and the building in which it was located from
Paul Pudwill. The firm operated the combination for a number of years, Dr.
Grant in the practice of medicine and Mr. Hofer in management of the store. In
1910 the firm erected the two-story, brick and concrete building that still
houses the Wishek Drug Company. The partnership was later dissolved, Dr. Grant
retiring from the firm.

The Doctor was Scotch in descent and ancestry and was a thrifty individual.
During his life, he accumulated considerable farm and town property. He served
as Vice President of the Security State Bank from the time of its organization
until his death in 1939.

On July 14, 1911, Dr. Grant married Miss Clara Belle Link of Pipestone,
Minnesota. Three children were born to them: Jeanette, now Mrs. Marion Griggs;
George, Jr., and a daughter, Marian, who died in childhood.

The Doctor was an honored member of both the North Dakota State Medical Society
and the American Medical Association. Fraternally, he maintained continuous
membership in Wishek Lodge No. 99, I.O.O.F., for thirty years. He held
membership in the Masonic Order at Edgeley, No. Dak., and belonged to the Elks
Lodge in Bismarck. He was a supporter of the Republican party.

Death claimed him on May 20, 1939, leaving a grief-stricken community to mourn
with his bereaved family.

Mrs. Grant continues to maintain a home in Wishek but spends the winters with
her children in the West.

Mr. and Mrs. John F. Spreuger

John F. Sprenger, son of Freidrick and Magdalena Sprenger, was born October 6,
1883, in Kassel, South Russia. He married Katharina Krein in 1905, daughter of
Jacob and Margaret Krein. She was born January 1, 1884.

Six children were born to them, Christana (Mrs. Emanuel Rieger), Wishek
Margaret (Mrs. G. G. Herr) Wishek; Anna (Mrs. Adolph Krein), Bismarck; Esther
(Mrs. Albert Woehl), Wishek; Wilma, at home; Theo. Sprenger, Hardin, Montana.

Mr. Sprenger came to the U. S. when he was two years old. His parents settled
12 miles southwest of the present City of Wishek. He first saw Wishek in the
summer of 1898. They made the trip in a wagon drawn by a team of mules. Then
Wishek consisted only of a few shacks. The grocery store was in a tent.

In 1905 Mr. Sprenger filed his homestead about five miles southwest of Wishek.
Here he lived two years, then moved to his father's place. In 1910 he bought a
half section of land and built a farm on it.

Mr. Sprenger served on the district school board and took an active part in the
work of the Reformed Church.

In 1943 the Sprengers moved to Wishek where they live now.

Mr. and Mrs. Christoph Kramer

Among the pioneers who saw this section of the country grow into a region of
five farms and prosperous towns was Mr. and Mrs. Christoph Kramer, who came to
this country from Kassel, South Russia, in 1884.

Christoph Kramer was born March 29, 1859, in Kassel, South Russia. The son of
Johann and Christina Kramer, nee Hochhalter. He married Magdaline Rueb February
1, 1882, the daughter of Jacob Rueb and wife Katherine, born Ketterling.

Thirteen children were born to this couple of whom eight survive their parents.
They are: Christine (Mrs. John Schell), Turtle Lake, N. Dak.; Caroline (Mrs.
Fred Vilhauer), Wishek; Henry Kramer, Bismarck, N. Dak.; Eva (Mrs. Carlo
Mortimicci, Bismarck; John Kramer, Minneapolis, Minn.; Rosina (Mrs. August
Martel), Wishek; Emanuel Kramer, Wishek; Anna (Mrs. William Wilson), Portland,
Oregon.

When the Kramers arrived in this country they located near what is now Tripp,
S. Dak., and homesteaded one quarter of land. In 1886 he sold this land for
$90.00 to his uncle, Henry Schnabel, and moved seventeen miles southwest of the
present city of Wishek. Here the Kramer's homesteaded two quarters of land, one
homesteaded the other a tree claim. The Kramers became citizens and continued
to farm this land for eighteen years. During the years of hardships they lost
five children. They saw Wishek built from the ground up. In 1904 they moved
seven and a half miles southwest of Wishek. Here they resided until they died.
The Kramers came to America almost penniless. Yet on Mr. Kramer's death he was
able to leave to his children 1,280 acres of land, two farms and considerable
personal property the results of hard labor and weathering the hardships of the
pioneer days.

Mrs. Kramer died two years later at his farm home September 21, 1925.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Vossler, Sr.

Christian Vossler, Sr., was born February 8, 1858, in Bessarabia, South Russia.
He was the son of Martin and Karolina Vossler, nee Brost. In the year 1885 he
married Christina Anhorn in South Russia. She was born July 27, 1866, the
daughter of Gottlieb Anhorn and wife Barbara, born Fredrick.

To this union nine children were born of whom two died. Ottelia (Mrs. Fred
Kurle), Carson; Gustave Vossler, Miles City, Montana, deceased; Emanuel
Vossler, Ellendale; Katharine (Mrs. Fred Gums), Jamestown; Barbara (Mrs. Gust
Just), Wishek; Ida (Mrs. Wm. Herr), Wishek; Edward Vossler, Mercer; Helen (Mrs.
Henry Martin), Forbes.

Mr. Vossler served in the Russian Army and hoping to find freedom and security
he brought his family to this country in 1894 and homesteaded, 16 miles
southwest of the site which later became Wishek. The early settlers endured
many hardships. The first winter here, they lived with Mr. Vossler's brother,
Fredrick. In the spring they built their own sod house and began to farm. They
had faith in this new land and by the help of God reared their family and lived
on the homestead until they died. Mr. Vossler died May 8, 1929. and Mrs.
Vossler died July 6, 1932.

Mr. and Mrs. Fredrich Thurn

Fredrich Thurn was born May 25, 1860, at Kassel, South Russia. His parents were
George Adam Thurn and Elizabeth, nee Schnadt. He was married in South Russia in
1883. A year later he and his wife Christina came to America. They came to this
community May, 1885, and located on S. 1, T. 130, R. 73.

Thirteen children were born unto them. They are: Christina Boshee, Peter Thurn,
Emma Just, all of Wishek, Fred F. Thurn and John F. Thurn of Venturia, N. Dak.;
Emil of Zeeland; Bertha Schnable, Lydia Dockter and Maria Wiest of Venturia;
Ottelia Gub, Selby, S. Dak., and Albina Haas of Zeeland.

Like most of the other immigrants they came here without money, hoping to find
security in this new, strange land. At first Ipswich, S. Dak., and Ellendale,
N. Dak., were their closest towns. Seventy miles was a long trip when made with
oxen. Mr. Thurn recollects the times when food supplies were low and they had
to travel a long distance to purchase them, and how often they were caught in a
raging blizzard and only the hospitality of other settlers saved their lives.

Mr. Thurn died January 25, 1925, and his wife died June 28, 1928.

Mr. and Mrs. John Woehl

John Woehl was born April 20, 1887, at Lichton Feld, South Russia. The son of
Jacob Woehl and Margaret Brenneise Woehl. In 1911 he married Elizabeth Ackerman
at Ashley, N. Dak. They are the parents of: Herman Woehl, Modesto, Calif.;
Martha Gainer, Stockton, Calif.; Benjamin Woehl, Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. Woehl was two years old when his parents came from Russia and settled near
Eureka two years later. In October of 1889 they located 16 miles southwest of
Wishek.

Mr. Woehl recalls he saw Wishek the first time in 1898, then it looked like a
large farm with lumber yard going up near the railroad station. Mr. Doyle and
Mr. Fischer were operating a General Merchandise Store.

The Woehls now reside in Lodi, Calif.

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Ketterling

Among the pioneers worthy of mention are Mr. and Mrs. Adam Ketterling, who came
to this country in 1885.

Adam Ketterling was born December 28, 1860, in Kassel, South Russia, the son of
Johann and Magdalena Heupel Ketterling. He married Eva Schaeufele February 20,
1884, in South Russia. They came to America in search of better political and
social conditions and arrived at Ipswich, S. Dak., in 1885. On June 18, 1885,
Mr. Ketterling filed on a 480-acre tract of land, Section 33, T. 131, R. 71,
about 18 miles southwest of the present city of Wishek.

To this union seven children were born, two died. The living are Eva (Mrs. Jacob
Perman), Zeeland, N. Dak.; Magdalena (Mrs. August Vilhauer), Wishek, N. Dak.;
Katherina (Mrs. Fred Maier), Golden Prairie, Sask., Canada; Emma (Mrs. Henry
Backhaus), Miltona, Minn.; Adam A. Ketterling, Wishek, N. Dak.

Mrs. Ketterling passed away March 25, 1897.

The early years were full of hardships. They bought a cow and a team of oxen.
It took them a long time to pay for these precious possessions. They picked
buffalo bones which they took to Ipswich, a distance of about seventy miles.
The round trip with oxen took about six days. The bones were traded for flour
and the bare necessities of life.

In 1898 Mr. Ketterling remarried. The second wife was Mrs. Louise Wiest, nee
Katherina Schnabel Rudolf. She had the following children:

Heinrich and John Wiest, Emelie ( Mrs. Wilhelm Mitzel), who all reside at
Sunnyside, Washington; Jacob Wiest, Zeeland, N. Dak.; Louise (Mrs. Jacob
Aipperspach), deceased, Zeeland, N. Dak.

The children of this second marriage are August, Ferdinand and Edwin, at
Wishek; Otto, at Zeeland; Reinhold and Julius, at Sunnyside, Washington; Hilda
(Mrs. Adam J. Ketterling), Grand Rapids; Ida (Mrs. Arthur Remmick), Dawson, N.
Dak.

In spite of countless hardships and disappointments Mr. Ketterling became a
well-to-do farmer. The Ketterlings were of Lutheran Faith. Mr. Ketterling died
October 20, 1935, at his home on the farm.

Mrs. Louise Ketterling died December 9, 1946.

Mr. and Mrs. George Martel

A hardy pioneer of Wishek was George Martel, born January 5, 1870, to Jacob and
Christina (Schnabel) Martel in Kassel, South Russia.

Mr. Martel was married on January 28, 1892, to Kathrine Heupel. To this union
were born 13 children, five of whom died. Those living today are Mrs. Jacob
(Christina) Raile; Jacob G.; August G.; Leopold; Paul G.; Mrs. Henry (Karolina)
Vossler; Mrs. Emmanuel (Paulina) Kramer, all of Wishek.

In early 1900, they moved out onto the open Mr. Martel heard of the land of
plenty and on May 5, 1899, he and his wife arrived in Eureka, S. D. On May 9th
they located near Wishek at the Philip Just home. Here they stayed for a period
of one year, while Mr. Martel worked for neighbors and relatives.

prairie, and building a house, barn, and chicken coop of stone and clay,
started farming on their own. Their furniture consisted of a stone and clay
stove, a table of old lumber and a few old beds. They stood at the table for
lack of chairs. The only fuel available was straw, which was given to them by
neighbors.

For three successive years their crop was lost because of the terrible
hail-storms. Mr. Martel was forced to go to Bismarck where he obtained work on
the railroad. In this way the family managed to survive.

In the summer of 1902 they dug a small cellar, covering it with twigs, straw,
and sod. This served as their storehouse until the hard teacher, experience,
forced them to abandon this by the following tragic, yet amusing, experience:
While the family was in church, on Sunday, their only cow walked over it,
falling in and eating five gallons of her own products--butter. The cow died
the next day. The butter had been saved to be sold, with which groceries were
to be bought. Thus, the family lost one of their major sources of income, and
were in harder straits than before.

The family were members of the Peace Lutheran church. Mr. Martel served as
sermon reader during the absence of its minister and aided greatly in its
advancement. He served for many years on the school board and was a staunch
advocate of education.

Mr. Martel died on November 2, 1916, at his home. Mrs. Martel continued
operating the farm until 1940, when she retired. She is now living with her
youngest son, Paul, on the farm.

Thus ends the history of a staunch and hardy pioneer couple.

Mr. and Mrs. Jakob P. Hoirup

Although most of the earlier settlers of our community came from South Russia,
Jakob Hoirup is an exception. Mr. Hoirup was born in Sommerstad, Nordschleswig,
Denmark, the son of Peter J. and Elizabeth (Gott) Hoirup, on January 4, 1864.
His wife, whom he married in Forest City, S. Dak., on December 24, 1896, is
Elizabeth, who was born on August 6, 1878, the daughter of Christ and Elizabeth
(Weinert) Layer. She came from Russia in 1889 with her parents.

Of the ten children born to this couple, seven are still living. They are:
Elizabeth (Mrs. Mickelson), Wishek; Peter J., on the home farm, Burnstad; Karl
N., Naches, Washington; Jakob P., Cumberland, Washington; Edyth (Mrs. Wm.
Humann), Cumberland, Washington; William C., Turner, Oregon; and Edna (Mrs.
Adrian Boyum), Wishek.

Of the early days, Mr. Hoirup tells us that he came to this country in May,
1881. For four years he lived in New York, where he was employed in a tannery.
In the spring of 1885 he came west to Mina, S. Dak. After several weeks stay,
he purchased a team of horses and a covered wagon. With these, he journeyed to
Hoskins in McIntosh County. The town site of Hoskins had been established by
Lilly and John Wishek, Sr., in the fall of 1884. The only buildings that he
recalls at Hoskins were the Land Office of Lilly and Wishek, a small grocery
store and a small bakery.

Mr. Wishek aided him in locating a homestead and Tree Claim four miles west of
Hoskins. Here he built a combination sod house and barn. He also managed to
break the five acres of land, as required by the Homestead Act. No jobs were to
be had in that first summer. Some of the settlers gathered buffalo bones and
hauled them to Ellendale, where they received $12.00 per ton. The following
spring Mr. Hoirup broke up 60 acres for other settlers at a price of $3.00 per
acre. He also hauled freight from Ellendale to Hoskins. Finally in 1887 he
bought himself a pair of oxen and began farming for himself.

Mr. Hoirup recalls that the first train came to Wishek in the fall of 1898. Mr.
Pratt was the first Postmaster and Gottlieb Nickisch operated the first
blacksmith shop. Pfeifle and Klein operated the only store when the Hoirup
family came to Wishek in 1900.

Because the drought had struck home, Hoirups moved further north from Hoskins.
This time they came up to Island Lake, in Logan County, nine miles north of
Wishek. Here they bought new land and started a horse and cattle ranch.

In 1921 Mr. and Mrs. Hoirup built a home in Wishek, so that their children
might have better schooling. For many years, they spent their time alternately
between their farm and their home in town.

Although they have retired, Mr. and Mrs. Hoirup still enjoy going out to their
farm to spend their summers.

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Reich

Among the retired farmers living in Wishek, we find the family of Adam Reich.
Mr. Reich was born in Kassel, South Russia, on December 16, 1885. His parents
were Adam and Katharina (Schatz) Reich. His wife, whom he married on February
13, 1913, is Katharine the daughter of Johann and Katherina (Goehringj Weber.
Of the thirteen children born to this couple, ten are still living. They are:
Katherina (Mrs. August Martel); Pauline (Mrs. Jacob Alexander); Lydia (Mrs.
Reinhard Werre); Maria (Mrs. Paul Martel); Adam, Jr.; Erna (Mrs. Melvin
Schwind); Edwin; Bertha; Alvina and Edward. All are living in the Wishek
vicinity.

The Reich farm is located some 13 miles west of Wishek. It is now in the hands
of his children.

Of the early years that he spent here, Mr. Reich mentions that the first people
he met with, when he came, were Fred Eckman, Sr., Jacob Herr, Sr., and Jacob
Schatz. His first business transaction involved the purchase of a wagon and
other machinery from Gottlieb Nickisch, and the purchase of 346 acres of land
from John J. Doyle.

Although hardships were plentiful, Mr. Reich and his family succeeded in
increasing their land holdings to the present 826 acres. Mr. Reich, too, has
been a member of the Friedensthal School District Board for several years. He
has also held membership in the Jacobus Lutheran Church, and now is a member of
St. Luke's congregation in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Andreas Meidinger

Andreas Meidinger, another of the pioneers, who found his home in the vicinity
of Wishek, was born on the 11th of November, 1883, in Kassel, South Russia. His
parents were Friedrich Meidinger and Katherina, nee Thurn. When only 11 months
old he was brought to the United States by his parents. Their first winter was
spent with relatives at Tripp, S. Dak. The following spring, 1885, the family
moved to McIntosh County. When he had reached the age of manhood, he took up a
homestead nine miles west of Wishek. This land was to be his home for the rest
of his life.

On February 20, 1906, he was united in marriage with Lydia Barreither. Her
birthday is December 25, 1885, and her birthplace was Scotland, S. Dak. She was
the daughter of Daniel and Margaretha (Heyne) Barreither. This marriage was
blessed with 16 children of whom 12 are still living.

Mr. Meidinger passed on to this reward very unexpectedly on November 15, 1934.
His wife passed on June 9, 1946.

The following children are still living: Julius, on the family farm; Ida (Mrs.
Leonard Howell), Snelling, California, Bertha (Mrs. Ferdinand Vossler), Wishek;
Frieda (Mrs. Ralph Sitter), Minneapolis, Minn.; Alma, Oakland, California;
Hulda (Mrs. Theodore Eckman) Wishek; Agnes (Mrs. Walter Meidinger); Venturia N.
Dak., Ella (Mrs. Wendelin Kuhn), Napoleon, N. Dak.; Irene (Mrs. Leroy Freidli),
Lodi, California; Oswald, Wishek; Gertrude (Mrs. Clifford Eissinger), Kansas
City, Mo.; and Lilly, Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Johann Vossler

Johann Vossler, one of Wishek's earlier settlers, was born of Fredrick and
Christina (Bietz) Vossler on November 22, 1867, in South Russia.

He entered the United States in 1885, going immediately to South Dakota.
However, not finding this region to his liking, he moved to McIntosh County on
May 6, 1886. The first people he became acquainted with were the Doyles, the
Singers, Gottlieb Nickisch, William Baily, Sr., and Konrath Herr.

He worked for Konrath Herr during 1886 and '87, receiving room and board and
the comparatively enormous sum of $75 per year. He worked as a laborer until
1891, when he married Caroline Boschee, daughter of John and Kathrine (Herr)
Boschee. Immediately, he filed a claim for a homestead, 16 miles northeast of
Zeeland. Moving there, he began working on his own.

To Johann Vossler and his wife, Katherine, were born 11 children, all living.
They are: Mrs. Jacob (Kathrine) Krein, of Vancouver, Washington; Jacob J.,
Henry, Lydia, Edward, Arthur and Ferdinand, all of Wishek; Adam Vossler, of
Ashley; Mrs. David (Bertha) Schaible, of Aberdeen, S. D.; Mrs. Ferdinand Krein,
of Wishek; and John J. Vossler, of Lehr, N. D.

Mr. Vossler recalls some amusing incidents in his life. Among these is the
following: "During the first few years," he says, "there was an abundance of
buffalo bones lying around on the prairies. It would have been a shame to leave
them there when good money, so scarce in those early days, could be had for
them. So, whenever he had a bit of time, he hitched his oxen to the wagon,
gathered the bones and hauled a load to Ipswich, S. D.

After living a long, happy and useful life, Mr. Vossler joined his wife, (who
had preceded him in death on May 7, 1929) in the Great Beyond on February 14,
1931. He died in the Bismarck Evangelical Hospital and was interred in the
Wishek cemetery. May they rest as happily and fully as they lived.

Mr. and Mrs. Fred R. Wolff

The year 1896 brought a young boy to the Wishek Community, who was to spend an
entire lifetime here. He was Fred R. Wolff, born on February 11, 1887, at
Eureka, S. Dak., the son of John and Fredricka (Bender) Wolff.

The first people that he knew were John Doyle, Charles Roehm and M. E. Pratt.
He was one of the people who was in the tent store which was destroyed by a
windstorm, during the first year of the town's existence.

In commenting on his first business transaction, Mr. Wolff tells us that he
purchased the first bicycle ever sold in Wishek. With it he made a trip to
Eureka, S. Dak., following cow trails most of the way, because there were few
roads to be found.

On November 11, 1909, he married Emelia Pfeifle, the daughter of Peter and
Katherina (Deischle) Pfeifle. They are the parents of five children: Adelia
(Mrs. H. H. Doering), Wishek; Wilma (Mrs. Andy Asch), Minneapolis, Minn.;
Erwin, deceased; Glenaris (Mrs. William Resch), Minneapolis; and Arlean (Mrs.
Jacob A. Just), Drake, N. Dak.

For a number of years Mr. Wolff was engaged in the General Merchandise
business. Later he worked for the Standard Oil Company as bulk agent. As such
he hauled oil to neighboring towns with horses and wagon, for the only bulk
station in the county was located here. From 1920 until 1940 he built highways
and county roads.

He was a member of the Wishek baseball team from 1907 to 1912. He was also one
of the organizers of the Fire Department, and still holds his membership. Mr.
Wolff has also served this community as a member of the School Board and City
Board.

Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Rudolf, Sr.

Ludwig Rudolf, Sr., was born in Kassel, South Russia, on February 11, 1840. He
was the son of Johann George and Christina (Burgart) Rudolf. His wife was
Katherina Schnabel, daughter of Ludwig and Katherina (Liedle) Schnabel. She was
born in October, 1845. The couple was married in 1862. They became the parents
of ten children. Of these six are still living.

The Rudolfs came to this country in October, 1884, and settled 17 miles
southwest of Wishek, on May 25, 1885. Both Mr. and Mrs. Rudolf are deceased.
Mrs. Rudolph passed to her eternal reward on August 10, 1894, and her husband
died on November 3, 1911.

The children who are still living are: Ludwig, Wishek; Louisa (Mrs. Adam
Ketterling), Zeeland, N. Dak.; Katherina (Mrs. Jacob Boschee), Wishek; George,
Wishek; August, Wishek; and Fred, at Shepherd, Montana.

Fred N. Gillis

Fred Norton Gillis, an active citizen and prominent business man during the
first half of Wishek's history, was a native of Ohio, having been born at
Kinsman on February 13, 1882. He was the son of John and Hattie Norton Gillis.
When he was three years of age, his parents moved to Topeka, Kansas, and he
completed his grade and high school courses in the schools of that city.
Entering the Kansas State College at Manhattan, in 1899, he graduated with a
Bachelor of Science degree in 1903. During his school years he was an
outstanding athlete, showing promise in baseball, football and tennis. At one
time he held the Missouri Valley Tennis championship.

For a year after his graduation, Mr. Gillis was employed on a U. S. government
survey in the Bad Lands of North Dakota. In August, 1904, he came to Wishek as
Assistant Cashier of the First State Bank. When F. D. McCartney resigned in
1905, Mr. Gillis succeeded him as cashier, which position he held until 1919.
In that year, he was elected Vice President of the bank and continued as such
until the time of his death.

On December 24, 1908, he married Miss Nellie Bittner, a graduate nurse of
Rochester, Minn. Seven years later, a daughter, Roberta, was born to them. Mr.
Gillis was a business man of more than ordinary ability. His counsel and advice
was sought by many citizens of the Wishek community. He was interested and
active in civic and municipal affairs, serving three years as Chairman of the
Village Board of Trustees. During his term of office, the first issue of
Village Bonds were voted, the present City Hall was built, street and sidewalk
grades were established, cross-walks laid and franchise for Wishek's first
electric power plant was granted. He excelled in many lines of endeavor. For
several years after his arrival in Wishek, he was an unbeatable pitcher on
Wishek's baseball team. As a hunter and marksman, he had few equals.

In 1919, he left with his family for California to regain his broken health. He
returned to Wishek and to his work in the bank in 1922 and carried on until
January, 1926. Wishek lost a highly respected and valuable citizen, when death
overtook him on April 14, 1926.

Mr. Gillis was prominent in fraternal circles, holding Masonic membership in the
Lodge at Edgeley and membership in the Elks at Bismarck. He was among the first
class of initiates taken into Wishek Lodge No. 99, I.O.O.F., at the time of its
institution, was a Past Grand and past representative to its Grand Lodge. In
politics, he was a staunch supporter of the Republican party.

Mrs. Gillis now resides with her daughter and son-in-law, Mr. and Mrs. E. Frank
Bach, in Greeley, Colorado.

Mr. and Mrs. Bernard Kaseman

Coming to the United States in 1889 was Bernard Kaseman, already in middle age.
His life is typical of the life of many of our early pioneers.

Mr. Kaseman was the son of Ludwig and Anna (Scherley) Kaseman. Born on August
12, 1855, in Grosliebensthal, South Russia. He grew to manhood there. When
Bernard was 21, his father died and from then on, he had to rely on his own
abilities.

His belongings at that time amounted to 20 rubles. He worked for neighbors for
three years. In 1879, he married Anna Kiel. Two children were born to this
union. They died in infancy. His wife followed them in February, 1883.

In June, 1883, he married Paulina Hepper, daughter of John and Margaretha
(Diale) Hepper. To this union were born 14 children, six of whom died. Those
living are Jacob, John, Bernard, Gottlieb, Edward and Albert Kaseman, all of
Wishek; Mrs. John (Paulina) Thurn, of Venturia; and Mrs. Fred (Karolina)
Oberlander, of New England, N. D.

In 1889. Mr. Kaseman heard of America and the opportunities that it offered.
Gathering his possessions, he embarked for the United States via ship. They
landed in New York and went by train to Armour, S. D., where Mrs. Kaseman's
parents were living. They stayed there for three weeks, then they went on to
Eureka by train, and from there to the Gottlieb Bertsch farm with a team.

On June 10, 1889, Mr. Kaseman staked his claim to 160 acres of land in McIntosh
County. They built a house and barn of sod and stone. Their stove was built of
the same material. They bought two oxen, two cows, a plow and a wagon. It was
too late to seed that year, so Mr. Kaseman broke 11 acres for the next year's
crop.

Mr. Kaseman recalls that prayer meetings were held at the homes of the people,
and out on the open prairies. In 1895, Mr. Kaseman led a movement to build a
church. Crude though it was, built of stone and clay, it still meant more to
them than many a great cathedral does to people in communities where churches
are common. It required two years to build the church, and it was in use until
1945, when it was replaced by a more modern structure.

In the early years, the Kaseman's were often tempted to go back to Russia, but
they persevered. They prospered, and by the time they retired, owned 2,403
acres of land.

In 1924, Mr. Kaseman retired, bought a home in Wishek and lived there until his
death on February 20, 1940.

Mrs. Kaseman still lives at the age of 85 years. She resides, at present, with
her son, B. B. Kaseman.

Such are the pioneers who founded our county. Enduring hardship, disaster and
death in order to make a free, progressive and independent community. May we
carry on the work which they so valiantly began, and may the spirit which they
created live in all Americans to the end of time.

Mr. and Mrs. Christian Stock
