Mr. Christ Stock was a late comer to Wishek. He was born to Christian and
Dorathea Stock on January 10, 1875, in South Russia.

He was married to Barbara Unruh, born to Mr. and Mrs. George J. Unruh on October
28, 1876, in South Russia. They were married at Gluckstal, South Russia. To
this union were born 13 children, eight of whom died. The remaining children
are: Jacob, Mrs. Arnold (Magdalena) Brown, John and Christ, Jr., all of Wishek;
and Mrs. Philip (Rosa) Bender of Fargo. The first time Mr. Stock saw Wishek was
on October 28, 1908, while driving his grandfather's cattle to Lehr. The first
people he knew here were the Herr Brothers, Dan and Gottlieb Nickisch and Peter
Ackerman.

Having come to Eureka, S. D., in 1905, Mr. Stock lived on a farm there for four
years. Then he moved to a farm seven miles north of Lehr where he lived for
nine years. He then sold his farm, buying another, 3-1/2 miles northeast of
Wishek.

Mr. Stock, during the course of his long life, saw many hardships, but the good
in his life counter-balanced the bad. He eventually became a wealthy farmer
through hard work and good management.

When Mr. Stock retired in 1942, he owned 800 acres of land and a fully equipped
farm. He moved to Wishek and has commenced to enjoy his well-earned rest.

John Fetzer

One of the later pioneers of Wishek was John Fetzer, born February 3, 1883. He
is the son of Henry and Katharina (Greubele) Fetzer, originally of Neuberg,
South Russia.

Mr. Fetzer came to America in May of 1900. He went to Eureka, S. D. In 1902, he
worked for Christ Gruebele of Wishek, seeing this town then for the first time.

In 1907, he filed claim to a homestead located 11 miles south of Wishek, N. D.
On November 10, he was married to Rosina Perman, daughter of Jacob and
Katharina (Hoenges) Perman. To this union were born eight children, one of whom
died. Those living are Mrs. George (Lydia) Schilling, Venturia; Mrs. Henry
(Martha) Roth, of Forbes, N. D.; Mrs. Harold (Anna) Schmidt, of Stockton,
Calif.; Mrs. Adam (Ida) Schilling, of Venturia, N. D.; Mrs. Benny (Agnes)
Martz, of Lodi, Calif.; Wilhelm Fetzer, of Venturia, N. D.: and Arthur Fetzer,
now at Wiesbaden, Germany.

He was very successful in his occupation, and owned 400 acres of land and a
well-built, well equipped farm home. He was active in community projects and
stood firmly for better education.

In 1931, Mrs. Fetzer died and on November 7, 1933, Mr. Fetzer married a second
time to Mrs. William Delzer.

In 1936, Mr. Fetzer moved to California, coming back to Venturia after about
six months. He lived there for about four years, then moved back to Wishek
where he bought a home.

Mr. Fetzer's second wife bore him a son, Clarence, who is now attending school
at Wishek.

Mr. Fetzer is a staunch supporter of the Wishek Evangelical church.

Gott. Schock

In August of 1855 Gott. Schock was born in Russia, where he grew to manhood and
married Fredricka Eisenbeis (born August 24, 1856) while he lived there. Mr.
and Mrs. Schock came to this country in 1895 looking for a new home and place
of livelihood. In 1908 they settled in this community, and for some time the
Schock family lived on a farm six miles south of Wishek. After a few years they
found a new opportunity and moved to Wishek, here Mr. Schock owned and operated
a flour mill. The home was one block north of the Wishek Public Schools.

To this union seven children were born, three boys and four girls. The
surviving children are: John of Ashley, N. D.; Mrs. Barbara Maule of Longlake,
S. D.; Mrs. Rosina Ritter of Wishek, and Christ of 1224 Pleasant Avenue of
Lodi, California.

Mr. and Mrs. Peter Schmalz

It was in 1909 that Peter Schmalz first came to this community. He was born
June 18, 1891: the son of Sebastian and Magdeline (nee Tuchscher) Schmalz. The
first acquaintances of Peter Schmalz during his first Wishek trip, in
September, 1910, was Peter and George Ackerman.

In November 12, 1912, Peter was married at Hagne, N. D., to Kathryn Heilman
(born in 1892), the daughter of Anton and Ludwina Koller Heilman.

To this devout Catholic family eight children were born; six boys and two
girls. Of these children, four young boys passed away. The two girls have
answered the call of the Church and became nuns. They are: Magdeline S. M.
Petronia of St. Paul, Minn., and Mary S. M. Leucina of Mankato, Minn. The two
sons are Adam Schmalz of Tappen, N. D., and Sebastian Schmalz of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Christ Ketterling

Christ Ketterling was born in Kassel, South Russia, on December 6, 1884, and at
the early age of one year he came with his father, George Ketterling, and his
mother, Anna Maria (nee Merkel) to McIntosh County. Here Christ Ketterling grew
to young manhood. It was at the age of 15 (1899) he was in Wishek for the first
time. He was helping a neighbor haul lumber for a new barn which he was
building.

With hard work, careful management, and courage the Ketterling family became
one of the successful farmers of this community. When Christ became of age the
father's farm became his, and he continued to farm successfully.

On the 21st day of December, 1909, Christ Ketterling was united in marriage to
Rosina Schwindt, daughter of Simon and Christina (Whitmaier) Schwindt. To this
happy union four boys and four girls were born. The children, namely, are: Mrs.
John Bechtel (Ida); Mrs. Dan Rudolf (Pauline); Mrs. Henry Gall, Jr. (Hertha),
all of Wishek; Otto, Ferdinand, Gottfried, Mrs. Matt Rau (Edna), all of
Minneapolis, Minn., and Paul of Sacramento, Calif.

With all the work of running a fine farm, and continually improving on it, and
equipping it with the latest equipment, Mr. Ketterling found time to be
interested and active in school and community projects. He was a member of the
American Lutheran Church.

Mr. Ketterling died on January 29, 1932.

Mrs. Ketterling married the second time to Christ Opp on July 4, 1937.

Mr. Opp's children are: Mrs. Jacob Stadel (Maria) of Dawson, N. D., and Mrs.
Adam Ghner, of Marsh, Montana.

Mr. and Mrs. Edward Herr

Born on April 14, 1894, in McIntosh County about seven miles southwest of
Wishek, to Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Herr, Sr., was none other than the youngest of
the family, Edward. Also born in McIntosh County about eighteen miles southwest
of Wishek on December 30, 1906, to Mr. and Mrs. Freidrich Meidinger was their
youngest daughter Rosina.

Edward Herr and Rosina Meidinger were united in marriage on January 4, 1934. To
this union was born one child, a son named Roger Edward. From a previous
marriage with Magdalena Hoff, daughter of Fredrich Hoff and Margaretha Hoff
(nee Guthmiller) Eureka, S. D., three children were born: one son on February
27, 1916, Stanley Marvin, who preceded his mother in death at the age of
twenty-one days; two daughters, Phyllis Marcella, born February 20, 1917, now
Mrs. Walter J. Doyle and Genevieve Margaretha, born May 25, 1919, now Mrs.
Marvin Brandt, both of Wishek, N. D. Their mother died suddenly following an
operation on December 9, 1932, at the Midway Hospital, St. Paul, Minn., and was
laid to rest beside her son in the Wishek Cemetery.

Mr. Herr spent his boyhood on the farm seven miles southwest of Wishek until
1898, then moved with his parents to the farm nine miles south of Wishek. There
he attended school mostly under the able teacher, Mrs. W. S. Gordon. During the
summer he became a cowboy herding the dairy cows and sheep. In the fall of 1907
he again moved with his parents to Wishek where he attended school under Mr.
Timm, Miss Clara Link, and in the spring of 1910 he graduated from the eighth
grade under Mr. Jennings. During this same period he performed the duties of
delivery boy for the Herr Bros. store and on April 5, 1913, he was advanced to
the position of bookkeeper. Under Mr. Herr's leadership the firm of Herr Bros.
& Co. was dissolved on December 31, 1915. A new organization was formed and on
April 5, 1916, a charter was issued to do business under the firm style and
name "Herr Mercantile Co." Mr. Herr was elected its general manager which office
he has administered for thirty-two years.

Mr. Herr's present holdings consist of stock in the Herr Mercantile Co.,
interest in the Herr Investment Co., and the farm eight miles south of Wishek,
formerly owned by his father. Mr. Herr always took great interest in civic
enterprises. Among the many activities in this connection he has held the
following offices: Secretary of the local American Red Cross Chapter, School
Clerk 1923-1930, Chairman of the McIntosh County Welfare Board 1935-1939,
Chairman of the McIntosh County Selective Service Board 1940 to 1944, and
treasurer of Wishek Association of Commerce since its organization.

Mr. and Mrs. Herr are members of the Wishek Baptist Church. Both are active in
its different organizations. Mr. Herr has been its treasurer since June 1, 1925.

Some of the memories in the early pioneer days are as follows: Seeing Wishek in
its beginning with just two tents for business establishments. Seeing what they
called "Wolf's Jagd." About this same time a report was circulated that a lion
had escaped from a circus at Bismarck and was seen by some one roaming in our
immediate vicinity. The report must have been false as the animal never did
show up. In 1901 seeing a furious three-day blizzard in the latter part of
April which took a heavy toll among the sheep and cattle.

A few of the more pleasant early memories were the large barrel of cider which
all stores had in the back end or cellar. This was always a most welcome treat
at lunch time. He remembers the coming of ice cream in 1905 and pop in 1906.

Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Staebler

Joseph Staebler was born in South Russia on the 28th of April, 1862. His
parents were Ludwig and Elizabeth (Mittleider) Staebler. Mr. Staebler was
united in marriage with Margaret Deyle in Russia on May 16, 1889. Margaret was
born May 27, 1870, and was the daughter of Emanuel and Margaret (Jund) Deyle.
On October 15, 1890, the Joseph Staebler family came to America and settled for
a while near Eureka, S. Dak. In 1906 the family moved to their new homestead
about 10 miles northwest of Wishek. Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Staebler were the
parents of five children. They are: Mrs. Fred Schock (Katherina) of Seattle,
Wash.; Mrs. Fred Ritter (Margaret) of Burnstad; John of Billings, Mont.; Mrs.
Henry Hochhalter (Magdelina) of Wishek; and Joseph, deceased, of Ashley, N.
Dak. The family continued to farm until 1916 when they moved to the City of
Wishek. Mr. Staebler died September 7, 1929. Several years later Mrs. Staebler
married for the second time to John Herr of Kulm, and they continue living
there now.

Mr. and Mrs. Christ J. Herr

On March 22, 1882, a baby boy arrived at the home of Jacob and Margaret Herr.
The young fellow was named Christian. He later found out that his parents had
come from Russia in 1876 to make their home and find their happiness in the
United States.

At quite an early age, little C. J. received his first train ride. His parents
decided to leave their present site near Tripp, South Dakota, for one that
looked more promising to them. The father tried to drive the cattle, oxen and
other livestock, but soon found that the cattle could not make the journey. The
stock and household goods were loaded into a box car and the trip was made as
far as Bowdle, South Dakota, by train. From here the stock was driven to a place
west of Wishek, where in due time a house was built and the soil tilled. This
trip could have been in the year 1887, but this is not definite.

On this farm Christ and his brothers received their training as farmers. Here
Christ experienced what it meant to herd cattle. Shoes were strange things to
him. They hurt! and anyway shoes were meant for Sunday. Here Christ learned to
handle both oxen and horses. One day when he was out plowing with the oxen, he
became more interested in birds than plowing. In a short time he was skipping
over the field, trying to bring down a bird with his long whip. When he
returned to work his team was gone. Later he found the team cooling itself in
the nearby creek. Christ had to get his father to help get the oxen from the
place.

Christ was no stranger to the horse-power and the threshing machine. This
experience meant much to him in later years. Here Christ learned to fear the
strange smoke in the sky and the glow on the horizon--signs of terrifying
prairie fires.

The father, dissatisfied with his present place, soon moved to a new location
nine miles south of Wishek. It was here that Christ learned to know his God, and
that life consisted of more than material things. It was also here that he
learned to know and love a happy, carefree, neighbor girl. And soon William and
Barbara Koth lost their daughter, Lydia, to him. On February 7, 1904, they were
married, and homesteaded one mile south of his father's place.

In partnership with John Koth, Christ owned the first steam engine in the
community.

Among the memories of farm life are the high snow banks, and the severe
blizzards. One year the farmers had to raise their pumps as much as ten feet,
so as to be able to water their stock, and the cattle walked directly from the
tops of snow-banks to the roofs of barns. Steps had to be made from the door to
the top of snowbanks to get in and out of the house.

In the fall of 1904 Adina Amelia arrived and during a snow storm in 1906 Ernest
William was born. In 1908 a second daughter was born. But her sojourn in life
was short as in a few short weeks she passed away.

In 1910 Christ and his wife decided to move to Wishek. Here Christ, in
partnership with others, bailed hay during the winter and farmed during the
summer. He and his brother Jacob purchased a section of land near Elgin, North
Dakota, to raise flax. This venture was a costly one, as it was a complete
failure.

In 1911 Clarence Christian was born. The little four-room house was getting a
little crowded so a new larger modem house was built. This new home was the
site of many a "home-roasted sun-flower seed fest" as friends gathered to visit
and spend the evenings. Into this home, in 1916, Vernon Walter was born.

Christ tried his hand in the furniture business for three months, but in 1914
sold his share in the venture, and went to work for his father in the Herr
Mercantile establishment. Upon the death of his father, Christ was elected as
President of the Corporation, which office he held until his retirement in 1943.
Christ was manager of the Dry Goods and Ready-to-wear Departments, and had
charge of a large portion of the firm's buying. He was a Director for 29 years.

In 1920 another tragedy hit the family. The mother suddenly passed away.

In the fall of 1920 Christ was married to Elizabeth Preszler. Elizabeth took
over the household duties and the place of a mother. She was an active member
of the Baptist Church. She served both as an assistant teacher and as teacher
of the Ladies Class in Sunday School for several years. She was Secretary of
the Baptist Aid for about six years and Treasurer for several years.

Christ was active in church work--serving as Deacon for over 25 years. He
worked to establish a park in Wishek, and helped to dig holes for the first
trees planted in the park. He was active in getting the City form of Government
for Wishek, and served as Alderman for two terms. He was an active member of
the Association of Commerce, and served as Director for several terms.

Today the married couple is alone again. Three of the children having
married--Adina, living on a farm near Lynd, Minnesota, and Vernon and Ernest
residing in Wishek; Clarence is in La Grange, Ill., and Vivian is attending the
University of Minnesota. The happy couple, looking back over the eventful years
of their life, are thankful that in a meager way, they too have contributed to
the growth of Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Nies

Among the pioneers who came as a young family to McIntosh County were Mr. and
Mrs. Ludwig Nies. Mr. Nies was born January 24, 1860, in South Russia. His
father was Christian Nies. In 1885 Mr. Nies was united in marriage to Margaret
Walth (born December 6, 1862) in South Russia. Margaret was the daughter of Mr.
Gottfried and Mrs. (nee Schmidt) Walth. The young family came to this community
ten miles southwest of Wishek on May 1, 1905. Here they homesteaded on one
quarter of land. The family worked very hard on their farm all their early
life, and experienced the joys and sorrows common to all early settlers. They
were life members of the Lutheran Church. To this couple nine children were
born. Five of these children have passed away. The surviving children are: John
of McLaughlin, S. Dak.; Mrs. Jacob Bauer (Christina) of Aberdeen, S. Dak.; and
Jacob and Ludwig L. of Wishek. In 1944. being unable to take care of themselves
satisfactorily, they deemed it best to spend the remaining years in the
Lutheran Old People's home at Eureka, S. Dak. On April 5, 1947, Mrs. Nies
passed away, and Mr. Nies continues to make his home at the Old People's Home
in Eureka.

Mr. and Mrs. William G. Bailey. Sr.

William G. Bailey, born in Corning, Iowa, on September 30, 1879, came to Wishek
in the fall of 1898 and worked with his father in the Livery and Feed Stable.
Recollections of the early days in Wishek call to mind such incidents as
driving surveyors for the Soo Line; plowing the firebreak along the railroad
right-of-way from Wishek to Pollock, So. Dak.; driving salesmen with their
heavy trunks of merchandise for a week at a time, making not more than two or
three towns; and driving the doctor in all kinds of weather (often on these
trips they would have to stay up all night and wait for daylight to make their
way back over the trails.

William filed a homestead southwest of Wishek and on October 15, 1903, was
married to Naomi Petrehn of New Albin, Iowa. They made their home on this
homestead until 1912 when they moved into town where Mr. Bailey operated a
Livery Barn for several years. He was then employed as a grain buyer until he
moved to Fargo where he is employed by the Central Selling Agency at West Fargo.

The eight children (including two sets of twins) born to Mr. and Mrs. Bailey
are: James H. Bailey, Bismarck, N. D.; Edythe Bailey, Rapid City, S. D.;
William G. Bailey, Jr., Wishek, N. D.; Gerald C. Bailey, Wishek, N. D.;
Geraldine Bailey, Aberdeen, S. D., Amy Bailey Scott, Vancouver, Wash.; Donald
Bailey, Aberdeen, S. D.; and Delores Bailey Mattson, Roscoe, S. D.

Mr. and Mrs. Richard James Bailey

Richard James Bailey (born October 8, 1856) and Charlotte Gregory (born
December 29, 1862) were married in Coming, Iowa, and it was here that three
sons and a daughter were born--William G., Bennett H., Edith and Roy G. (the
latter two passed away in childhood). They then moved to Sioux City, Iowa,
where Ashburton Gregory was born. After living in Sioux City for several years
Mr. Bailey, with his family, moved to Eureka, So. Dak., where he was agent for
Standard Oil Company and also, assisted by his son William, freighted to Fort
Yates for Standing Rock Agency. Richard James, Jr., was born in Eureka.

Hearing of a new town being started in North Dakota, Mr. Bailey came to Wishek
during the late summer of 1898 and built a Livery & Feed Stable. Six weeks
later, his son William, drove a caravan of horses and hayracks from Harvey,
North Dakota, to Wishek and joined his father in the Livery & Feed business.
During the winter, Mr. Bailey and William boarded with Mrs. Hill, who was
running the first hotel in Wishek. The following spring, Mr. Bailey moved his
wife and three sons to Wishek and they made their home on a homestead on the
site of the farm now owned by Emanuel Koth.

Mr. Bailey was active in community affairs, having been a member of the school
board for several years and an active fireman. He later was proprietor of a
meat market and then in the cattle business until his death on December 10,
1931. Mrs. Bailey preceded him in death on March 4, 1928.

Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Heyne

The late Mr. Jacob Heyne was born March 1, 1876, at Neiderdorf, South Russia.
Like other young men he came to America in 1893, and after four years he
settled sixteen miles north of Zeeland, N. Dak. Here he was married, in 1897,
to Charolette Wacker.

This union was blessed with nine children, five boys and four girls: Jacob J.
Heyne of Linton, N. Dak; Edward, Ephraim, Albert and August, all of Wishek, N.
Dak., Mrs. Christina Hieb of Hague, N. Dak.; Mrs. Hertha Huber of Zeeland, N.
Dak., Mrs. Emma Huber of Walker, S. Dak.; and Mrs. Pauline Meidinger of Rupert,
Idaho. Both Mr. and Mrs. Heyne continued to live in their family home near
Zeeland until the time of their deaths. Mrs. Heyne passed away March 21. 1920.
and Mr. Heyne on October 20, 1941.

Mr. and Mrs. John Schaeffer

John Schaeffer, son of Christof Schaeffer and Rosina Schilling, was born in
South Russia August 3, 1878, and grew to young manhood there. In 1903 he
married Margerta Nies, the daughter of Christ Nies and Roseina Huck. To this
happy union nine children were born, and the surviving children are: Mrs. Peter
Knolt (Rose) of Lehr, N. Dak.; Mrs. Donald Roudolf (Frieda), John and Arthur of
Jamestown, N. Dak.; Rinehold, Mrs. Philip Wanner (Emma), Mrs. Andrew Eszler
(Viola) of Wishek, N. Dak., and Mrs. Rinehold Schaible (Martha) of Long Lake,
N. Dak.

The young couple left Russia in 1907 as many others decided to make their new
home in America. They arrived in Java, S. Dak., June 19, 1907. Not long after
their arrival Mr. Schaffer came to Wishek. Here he met Jacob Herr, Sr., and for
a short time Mr. Schaffer worked for Mr. Herr on the Herr farm. With much hard
work and sacrifices in 1908 the Schaeffers were able to establish their own
home on a farm seven miles southeast of Wishek.

Prior to Mrs. Schaeffer's passing away in 1944, the Schaeffer family moved to
the city of Wishek. Mr. Schaeffer, a member of the Evangelical church, is still
living in Wishek.

Christ Bader, Jr.

Over eighty years before this writing there lived in Russia the family of
Christ and Jacobine Bader. To this union the son, Christ Bader, Jr., was born on
September 4, 1864. At the age of twenty (1844) he came to America and arrived
at Tripp, S. Dak. Then as many others he wished to establish his home on a
farm, and hearing of the new town (Wishek) growing up in McIntosh County he
went there. In 1885 Mr. Bader came here and located on a farm 16-1/2 miles
southwest of Wishek. In 1880 Mr. Bader took for his wife Katie, daughter of
Fred Hochhalter and Margaret Rueb. Katie was born on November 22, 1869. There
were 12 children, two died. The others are as follows: Mrs. Phil Rieger
(Lousia) of Eureka, S. Dak., Mrs. Rinhold Buchhalz (Pauline) of Lehr, N. Dak.;
August and Henry C. of Wishek; John of California; Albert of Halie, S. Dak.;
Mrs. David Faulkner (Kate) of Java, S. Dak.; Maggie of Redfield, S. Dak.;
Christ of Eureka, S. Dak.; and Mrs. Ed. Reiger (Emma) of Aberdeen, S. Dak.

In the early days the Christ Bader family suffered hardships similar to those
of other pioneers. The blizzards and sweeping prairie fires were sources of
terror. Many hard days of toil were spent to keep the family from suffering.
Both Mr. and Mrs. Bader continued to live on their family farm until the time
of their deaths. Mrs. Bader passed away in 1920 and Mr. Bader in 1928.

        These are photos of local townspeople who were to be included in the
        biography section of this book, but no eulogies were written for them.

Johanes Herman

Johanes Herman, Wishek, N. D., was born the 17th of October in 1870 in Beresina
Bessarabia, South Russia. His father was Mr. Gottlieb Herman and his mother was
the former Reginia Maier. Mr. Herman married Christina Grams, February 8, 1896,
in Romania. Mrs. Herman was born March 31, 1873, at Beresina Besarabia, South
Russia. Eight children were born to this family, four boys and four girls, one
of which died. The children are Reinholt, Arca, Idaho; Christian, Pollock, S.
D.; Marie (Mrs. Andy Schlenker), McLaughlin, S. D.; John, Stockton, Calif.;
Ricka, Kathrina and Susan (Mrs. J. E. Meeker), all of Los Angeles, Calif.

Mr. Herman came to America in 1897 and to this community in 1907.

Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Guy McClelland

Thomas Guy McClelland was born February 6, 1873, at Elgin, Illinois. He was the
son of Wilkin and Sophia McClelland, nee Forrester. He was married December 1,
1897, to Ida Orluske at Jackson, Minnesota. Ida Orluske was the daughter of
John and Elizabeth Orluske, nee Sworth. To this union were born three children,
who are: Myrtice Lela (Scott), Wishek, North Dakota; Althea Margaret, Wishek,
North Dakota; and Bernice Corine (Sayler), Wishek, North Dakota.

Mr. McClelland came to McIntosh County on June 1, 1904, locating fifteen miles
north of Zeeland, North Dakota. The first time Mr. McClelland saw Wishek there
were only a few unpainted shacks. Mr. McClelland taught school nine terms. He
was influential in organizing the first school board in his district, which was
known as the Bishof community. Our informant avers that Mr. McClelland owned the
first steam engine and threshing machine in the Bishof community, operating it
for eight years. Mr. McClelland also served as postmaster for the Bishof
community. Mr. McClelland also served his community in the capacity of a
veterinarian and sometimes as a medic.

The hardships endured by this family were numerous which was common to all the
early settlers. In 1910 and 1911 a severe drought occurred, and the community
also suffered a great loss in their cattle herds, due to Blackleg, a cattle
disease not under control at that time. In the fall of 1913 Mr. McClelland
suffered the loss of his barn, by fire, also sheep, chickens, grain and a top
buggy which was missed very much. Our informant states of some unusual
experiences encountered by our subject; getting lost on the prairie in the
night and especially in the winter while teaching school.

Mr. McClelland passed away November 11, 1947, and Mrs. McClelland is making her
home with her daughters, Bernice and Myrtice.

John Ketterling

The lure of the land across the sea caused the Ketterlings to leave their
native land and come to America. John Ketterling, the son of Philip and Barbara
Ketterling, nee Pietz, was born January 5, 1871, at Kassel, South Russia. On
January 6, 1892, Dorathea Haug, daughter of Jacob and Karolina Haug, nee Lehr,
became the bride of John Ketterling. Two years later the young couple set sail
for America and located at Hosmer, South Dakota. There they lived for a period
of six years.

In 1901 they came to this community and filed on a homestead, eighteen miles
southwest of Wishek. He started his farming with a drag-harrow and a team of
oxen for which he paid $125. Even though Mr. Ketterling had to face many
hardships he was a successful farmer and at the time of his retiring in 1935
had acquired a lot of land.

Twelve children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Ketterling. They are: Jacob, Rupert,
Idaho; Phillip, Lodi, California; Peter, Lamoure, North Dakota; Arthur, Wishek,
North Dakota; Martha (Gabel), Lodi California; Emil, Fredonia, North Dakota;
Emma (Zimmerman), Lehr, North Dakota; Albert, Streeter, North Dakota; Adam,
Grand Rapids, North Dakota; Rubin, Zeeland, North Dakota; and Alvin,
Fullerton, North Dakota. One child died in infancy.

Mr. and Mrs. Ketterling reside at Wishek.

John V. Boschee

John V. Boschee was among the early pioneers of this community. He came to
America in October, 1884, with his parents and settled at Menno, South Dakota.
Mr. Boschee was the son of Valentin and Philippina Boschee, nee Meyer, born
August 25, 1883, at Kassel, South Russia.

In the Spring of 1885 the father of Mr. Boschee loaded all of their belongings,
including oxen and wagon, on the train and came as far as Ipswich, South
Dakota, the end of the railroad, from there on they came by covered wagon. They
filed on a homestead eighteen miles southwest of Wishek. Upon arrival at their
new home, the first thing for them to do was to break up sod to build their
house. Due to lack of money they were forced to gather buffalo bones, and haul
them with oxen teams to town, which was about eighty miles from the Boschee
farm. Many times a trip to town would take a week. There the bones were traded
for flour, sugar and a few other needed items. The winters were very severe,
many times the barns were covered with snow that the doors had to be shoveled
out. The hay and straw oftentimes had to be carried into the barns with bushel
baskets for the oxen and cows. In 1897 the first lumber barn was built, which
is still part of the barn of the Boschee farm.

On December 8, 1904, Mr. Boschee was united in marriage to Christina Thurn,
daughter of Fredrich and Christina Thurn, nee Schnadt. To this union were born
eleven children, nine of whom are living and are: Theobald, Wishek, North
Dakota; Amanda (Fetzer), Venturia; Edna (Woehl), Wishek, North Dakota; Rosina
(Ackerman), Artas, South Dakota; Martha (Martel), Wishek, North Dakota; Leaman,
Wishek, North Dakota, Ida (Bauer), Galt, California; Alvin, Hillsview, South
Dakota, and Rubin, Wishek, North Dakota.

In 1945 Mr. Boschee retired from his farming, selling his belongings at an
auction and resides now in Wishek.

Mr. and Mrs. Boschee are members and faithful workers of the Evangelical United
Brethern Church.

Mr. and Mrs. Henry Mindt

Mr. Henry Mindt was born February 16, 1878, in Worms, South Russia, son of
Jacob Mindt and Christina Weber.  He came to this country in 1884, and in 1907
he moved eight miles west and three miles north of Wishek. Mr. Mindt filed on
one-fourth section of land and went into the cattle buying business for
twenty-seven years. In 1908 Mr. Mindt moved to Wishek and built himself a home.

Mr. Mindt was married to Rose Werre, daughter of Gottfred Werre and Kay
Witmaier, at Eureka, South Dakota, December 16, 1903. To this marriage was born
nine children, two having died. Those living are Arthur, St. Paul, Minnesota;
Lena Schultz, Sacramento, California; John, Wishek; Ervin, Wimbledon, N. Dak.;
Alvin, Lodi, California; Ruben, Sacramento, California; and Roy, Wishek, N. Dak.

Mr. Mindt started as a mail carrier in 1886, from Ashley to Eureka, S. Dak., on
a daily route. He held this position for one year. Later he had the route from
Ashley to Ostrom for one year. He also had a mail route from Ashley to the
Giedt postoffice and to the Youngstown postoffice. He recalls having taken Mr.
John H. Wishek back and forth at times, and also the difficulty in traveling,
due to the bad roads and snow.

Mr. Mindt has retired from active life and is at present enjoying his well
earned rest.

                Pioneers

A dwindling few, they stand today,
Between the years to come
And our inheritance.
Their arms are tired, their feet are slow.
Their height is slackened
With the load of years.
But their remembrance goes alertly back
To when they stood, in youth,
Upon our mighty prairie land
With its appalling breadth of grass.

Where are the pioneers?
Does this small group
Hold the well-springs of our progress?
Ah, yes, it does.
For they have had the will to leave life's ease
And reach out to its bitter cold.
They have cursed and blessed
The rains, and suns, and storms.
They have seen so many springs
Break through the winter's snows
On the trackless prairie sea.
They have sowed and they have garnered,
They have hoarded and have saved.
They have spent their lives that we might live.
'Tis true that they have known defeats and loss:

But too, they've known the nobleness
Of carving home and progress--stark and clean
From out the elements of history.

Where are the pioneers?
Some with labors done
Are resting now beside the way,
And others tired and old
Are waiting to join them soon.
Will their spirit die with the last one's passing?
Ah, no, it permeates and breathes
Through every comfort of today.
In the tone of every church bell on a Sabbath morn
In every flag that waves above a schoolroom door.
In the twinkling lights of every town
In the roads that lead across the plains.

Let us hold that spirit close to our ideals,
We who still must pioneer.
Let us write that spirit deep into our souls
That we may carry into years unborn
Their courage and their faith divine.
The Heart that knows its glory can span the years
To quicken in a kindred heart
The fire that beacon lights
Our pathway up to God.

                        Wishek Locals
        (From the McIntosh Republican of November 11, 1898)

What's the matter with this town? It's all right.

Nearly everybody is now on their permanent location on the townsite.

Our efficient depot agent is now comfortably located in his new quarters.

The people are anxiously awaiting the appointment of a postmaster for this
place.

If we have correctly understood Dame Rumor a church edifice will be erected in
the near future. (It materialized in 1906).

Tony Bjornson, the proprietor of the city drug store, was down from Kulm
between trains Sunday.

The City Livery and Feed Stable, under the able management of R. J. and Will
Bailey, is enjoying a prosperous patronage.

Our county seat was represented last Sunday by Dr. F. W. Maercklein, Prof. L.
A. Weatherby, Treasurer Lamotte Miles J. H. Wishek and M. Lutgen.

A casual remark overheard, it was made by an experienced traveling man: "No new
town ever started in the northwest with better prospects and a more
enterprising lot of business men than Wishek." (This is still true today).

Judging from the size, material, and workman6hip of the newly erected
residences ye scribe has to conclude that not a few have pinned their faith in
the new town and have taken steps to make their permanent home here.

A stranger might possibly think that we are trying to put on airs when he looks
at the bank, hotel, and other buildings. No, that is not the case. The business
requires it and let it be noticed right now that this fall will hardly show the
first chapter of our city's development.

Rumor has it that a large department store will be among the new acquisitions
of this town early next spring.

The thing this town needs and it is expected it will have it before many months
is a well stocked hardware store.

It is expected that a school will be started here soon.

Lake May would be a fine skating place just now, but we are still too busy to
indulge in that pastime.

The reports about the new town of Wishek on the Soo extension evidences that
it's going to be an important trading point for Emmons and McIntosh county
farmers.

The depots for the new towns along the Soo extension are built in Minneapolis,
shipped out in sections, and put together on the spot. The one for Wishek is
nearly completed.

The "Windy City" Wishek is located in the center of the coming cattle region of
North Dakota and the town has ample stockyard and shipping facilities.

Wishek & Guy are prepared to put their time and money toward developing the
cattle business in this region.

The First State Bank building is a credit to the town and the bank should
receive the patronage of every citizen in the county.

                (NOTES FROM A. P. GUY)

In 1898 Wishek gave Eureka stiff competition for the honor of the world's
primary grain market Farmers came from as far as the Strasburg and Hague
territories. There were times when teams were lined up three abreast for a
distance of three blocks, waiting until late into the night to be relieved of
their loads. The McMillan elevator was the first elevator in Wishek although
John Doyle was one of the first, with a smaller granary and a place up in the
town.

For several years after 1898 the mail came to Wishek on the stage from Eureka.
Supplies were also brought from there. The stage driver had a box fitted at the
back of his rig, in which he put ice and carried fresh meats to Wishek for the
hotels and other customers.

It was in 1898 that the diphtheria epidemic wreaked such ravages Dr. Fred
Maercklein went through the country vaccinating for it. The people did not
believe in quarantine, and there were many deaths among the children. (It is
recorded that over 300 children died during the epidemic)

No cemetery was needed at Wishek for three years. The only doctor was Dr.
Maercklein, who came over from Ashley when called. The first man to die was
Andrew Pfeifle. The second was Wrestler Gambler, whose neck was broken in a
match. The third was a blindpigger who died with delirium tremens. He needed no
embalming, said Mr. Guy.

Record it for the grandson of your son___
A city is not builded in a day;
Our little town cannot complete her soul,
Till countless generations pass away.

                Wishek Public Schools

The Village of Wishek was founded in 1898 and was located in the central part
of the Youngstown school district.

There was school in the Youngstown school district prior to the founding of
Wishek at the Elmer Pratt homestead. This was closed when the Pratts moved to
Wishek in 1898. The first teachers in this Youngstown school were Anna Jacobs
and J. E. Monroe.

The first school in the Village of Wishek was held in a building north of the
First State Bank and post office known as the Palm building. Mr. R. C. Miles
taught here from April 18 to June 12, 1899, two months for $35.00, followed by
Mr. Weatherby in the fall of 1899, who taught for a term of six months.

In 1900 the district became owner of the present plot of ground known as Block
7 of the townsite of Wishek. This land was purchased from the Minnesota Loan
and Trust Co. for $100.00. In the same year a one-room school house was built
on the center of the block. Mr. Miles again became the first teacher, followed
by Mr. Friedrickson who taught for two terms. In 1903, the one-room school was
remodeled and enlarged into a two-room building which now is the Catholic
Church. Teachers in the two-room building were Mr. Donahue, Fanny Jacobs, Clara
Link, Miss Kelly, Mrs. Hattie M. Wilson and H. E. Timm.

In 1907 the two-room building could no longer house the pupils and so plans
were made for a larger building. Early in 1908 a new six-room two-story frame
building with full basement was erected by contractor Albert Laurant at a cost
of $9,750.00. Clara Link (later Mrs. George Grant), Sophie Laugeson, and
Harriet Davies were teachers from 1908 to l909. In 1909 and 1910 G. L. Jennings
and others taught. In this year the following pupils graduated from the eighth
grade, James Moore, Gladys L. Best, Thomas Kuiper and Edward Herr.

Everyone remembers the Class of 1911. Think back. Don't you recall that it held
some special distinction? Of course, now you remember--it was the first class
to graduate as a group from the eighth grade here in the City of Wishek. The
students that graduated were very proud of their Baccalaureate and Commencement
exercises. They had now publicly entered this great big world and were now on
their own. They were all very anxious to try their new wings out. The students
who took part in this new venture or experience were, left to right: Frank
Roehm, Fred Roehm, G. L. Jennings, Supt., John Ackerman, and Amos Bond. Second
row, Amelia Roeszler, Anna Kuiper and Frieda Stuke.

        CLASS OF 1911

The growth of the town as well as Youngstown district in general progressed and
by 1915, three country schools were established and were known as Schools No.
1, 2, and 3. At this time there were 207 pupils enrolled in the town school and
18 in the country schools. In 1916 and 1917 the number on the roll was 310 and
again the school had outgrown its quarters. Some of the grades had to be taught
in the new city hall and other buildings. The patrons of the school, realizing
that the building and its facilities were entirely inadequate, soon petitioned
and favorably voted upon the matter of a High School building.

In 1919 it became a reality as the two-story brick building was completed with
eight rooms on the first and second floors and more rooms and auditorium in its
full basement. This building was erected at a cost of $48,000.00 by a
contracting firm, Dalstrom and Lindbers.

        Old School House Still in Use

Although there were high school subjects offered in the 9th and 10th grades as
far back as 1911 there was no full four-year course offered until 1919 when the
new building was completed. At this time athletics took a firm hold and we soon
had a very good boys' basketball team, followed in 1927 by a regular eleven man
football team. This was later changed to six man. 1924 marked the beginning of
girls' basketball which continued for about twelve years. F. R. Durenberger was
the first superintendent in the High School Building. He held that office for
ten years. During his second year, 1920, the enrollment in the grades reached
the all-time high of 339 pupils. The high school enrollment was still low with
only 21 pupils, making a total enrollment of 360.

In 1921 the first class graduated from High School were Irene Nippolt, Florence
Link and Edith Eaton.

The High School progressed and continued with a graduating class each year,
increasing in number until 1942 when the largest class graduated, 35 in number.

In 1925 after a state inspection was made and certain requirements were met,
the standard of the high school was raised from third to second class and in
the early thirties, the standard was advanced to a first class high school.
Since 1945 it is listed as a fully accredited High School.

In 1926 the school year opened with the addition of a commercial department.
New equipment, including typewriters, were added and installed.

In 1927 by petition followed by a special election the country school system
known as Schools No. 1, 2, and 3 were consolidated with the city schools. The
school houses and other buildings as well as equipment were sold. One of the
buildings is now used by the Assembly of God as a Church. At this time three
school bus bodies were purchased at a cost of $1,696.65. Route No. 1, 22 miles,
26 pupils; Route 2 34 miles, 20 pupils, and Route 3, 22 miles, 9 pupils were
established. Following the drought years in 1938 the bus transportation system
was declared a luxury and discontinued.

In 1933 the High School students started to publish a paper known as the
Panorama, which is still being maintained.

On July 1, 1936, a Smith-Hughes Agricultural Department was added. New
equipment was purchased and installed in the basement of the two-story frame
building and later with the completion of the new auditorium it was moved into
the basement room of the two-story brick building, formerly the auditorium.
Teachers in this department were Roman Pung, Richard Armstrong and V. D. Rice.

In 1938 the school was reorganized into the Junior High system.

The new stone and concrete auditorium with additional rooms on the second story
for Home Ec. department was built in 1942 with the city of Wishek as sponsor of
the project under P.W.A., the city having leased the site from the school
district. The cost of the construction was about $140,000.00.

In the upper rooms of the new auditorium we have a well established Home Ec.
department fully equipped with electric stoves and refrigerators, five sewing
machines and all other necessary equipment. We can truthfully say we have one
of the best equipped and nicest appearing departments in the state.

The present school plant is composed of the two-story frame building, the
two-story brick building and the stone auditorium building.

The following superintendents have served the school: Geo. Boschma, 1914-15; B.
L. Larson, 1916-1918; F. R. Durenberger, 1919-1928; V. E. Erickson, 1929; C.
T. Yestness, 1930-1931; O. C. Meyer, 1932-1941 and Ivar Knapp, 1942 to the
present date. Both Mr. Durenberger and Mr. Meyer served ten years. Miss
Quatier, one of the present high school teachers, holds the record of twelve
years of teaching service. Emil Breitbarth, however, holds the record of the
longest continuous service, having served sixteen years as janitor of the
school.

        WISHEK HIGH SCHOOL

While the all-time high in grade school enrollment was reached in 1920 the high
school reached its all-time high in 1939, with an enrollment of 164. The
present enrollment in grades 1 to 6 is 154; Jr. High (7 to 8) has 58 and grades
9 to 12 have 148 or a total enrolled in all departments of 360. This is the
same total we had in 1920.

Wishek is proud of its school system. It has few equals in any town or city of
like size in North Dakota.

        BEN EISSINGER School Director

                Commissioners

Fred Wolf

Mr. Fred Wolf, Commissioner of First District in McIntosh county, N. Dak., was
born and raised in McIntosh county. Mr. Wolf lives on a farm southwest of
Fredonia. He is the son of Fred Wolf, Sr. Mr. Wolf has one daughter who resides
on the farm with her parents.

John Salzer

Mr. John Salzer is Commissioner of the Second District in McIntosh county, N.
Dak. Mr. Salzer came to this county 31 years ago. At present is farming 500
acres. Mr. Salzer is the son of Gottlieb Salzer. John has seven children, four
boys and three girls.

A. M. Nolz

Mr. A. M. Nolz, Commissioner of Third District in McIntosh county, N. Dak. Mr.
Nolz came to this country in 1921 and is the son of an old pioneer family. He
has served as commissioner for one term. He does farming in a big way and at
present is working 2,300 acres. Mr. Nolz has eleven children, eight boys and
three girls.

                Wishek Volunteer Fire Department

Any fire that started in the early days of Wishek had little chance of being
extinguished. There were no trained men nor any fire fighting equipment. Water
was carried to a fire in buckets by men, women, and children. This was
laughingly nicknamed the Bucket Brigade. There were several fires which burned
down entire buildings. The first big fire Wishek suffered was in February,
1909, when the following burned: Herr Bros. Store, Palm Meat Market, Pratt
living quarters, and the Post Office, situated on lots 14, 15 and 16, Block 2.

The need of a fire fighting system grew with the size of the town. On December
16, 1913, a group of citizens met to organize. It was named THE WISHEK
VOLUNTEER FIRE DEPARTMENT. Officers were elected by ballot and the first chief
was H. E. Timm. The succeeding chiefs up to the present time served in the
order named: R. J. Bailey, Ed Mattison, John Dockter, A. F. Hoff, Fred E.
Ackermann, John Kundert, Otto Brandt, Fred Herr, Almond Mindt, John W.
Ackerman, A. G. Bailey, Jacob G. Ritter, and Emanuel Fercho. The last two named
served five years each. The oldest active member in point of service is Fred R.
Wolff. Space does not permit the names of all the firemen who served throughout
the years with risk to themselves, no pay whatever and sometimes little
gratitude. Of these men it can truly be said, they are Public Spirited.

It is interesting to note the first equipment the new department requested
(1/5/1914) of the Wishek village was a suitable fire alarm, and a bell was
furnished. See picture #1 for first equipment. Picture #2 shows "Chemical
engine" added in 1925. Picture #3 shows the up-to-date fire truck acquired in
1948.

A memorable fire call came from Napoleon in February, 1914. It was promptly
answered. A special Soo Line train consisting of an engine and two box cars,
loaded with the hand drawn chemicals and about fifteen men were rushed to the
Napoleon fire. It was kept under control and watched throughout the night.
Early next morning the special train returned to Wishek. Shortly afterward the
department received a three page resolution of thanks and a silver megaphone
with the following inscription: "Presented to WISHEK VOLUNTEER FIRE DEPARTMENT
by the citizens of Napoleon in appreciation of aid rendered February 7, 1914."
(This megaphone is on display in the city fire hall).

The second big fire occurred in the Fall of 1916. A row of six frame buildings
on lots seven to twelve inclusive in block one were destroyed. The men in the
Fire Department fought hard and did well with the equipment at hand.

Since its organization the department has saved much property, homes, and
business places. The men who volunteer for this service earn the good will of
the community.

        WISHEKS FIRST FIRE FIGHTING EQUIPMENT

Ever mindful of the slogan "forewarned is forearmed," these firefighting
machines illustrate how our city's fathers have endeavoured to safeguard the
lives, homes and businesses of their people down through the years.

                Wishek Association of Commerce

"TO PROMOTE THE CIVIC, ECONOMIC AND SOCIAL WELFARE OF THE PEOPLE OF WISHEK
AND VICINITY."

Past years have seen several business men's organizations come and go in the
city of Wishek. The organization that finally developed as an aftermath of all
the others and which has stood the test of time is THE ASSOCIATION OF COMMERCE.
This organization has been the driving and aggressive force in Wishek for the
past twenty-five years. Records show that the organizational meeting was held
in the City Hall on September 25, 1923. Organized primarily to promote the
civic and business interests of the city, the group soon expanded its
activities to matters effecting the community's entire trade territory. The
ASSOCIATION'S work has become so varied and involved that it would be difficult
to pick out any one particular project. However, the one outstanding project of
which the community is especially proud is Wishek's Park.

In 1928 the business men of Wishek gathered on the south end of town to dig
holes for the planting of trees which had been ordered from the Valley City
Nursery. Later that year the Northern Power & Light Company loaned the group a
hole digger without charge. At the time the city did not have title to the
tract on which the park was being started, but in 1929, Mr. John H. Wishek,
Sr., donated a block of land and provided the city with a deed.

The early years of park development were not easy ones. To Mr. James T. Lowers
fell the work of nursing the small saplings through their first years. Frost,
wind and sleet took a heavy toll among the beautiful trees. During the drought
years in the '30's pumping facilities and new tree stock had to be provided.
Each spring found more trees dead from winter-kill. Each year the dead trees
were replaced.

The Association park committee soon realized that cultivation was necessary to
insure a successful tree growth, and in 1929 hired Mr. John Pfahl, for the
small sum of $20.00 to plow and cultivate the tree area of the park. In 1930 it
became necessary to hire men for digging ditches and laying pipe through which
the trees could be watered. Late in 1930 the Association received a grant of
$150.00 from the city for park improvements and with this small sum the ground
work for our present park was laid. Time and hard work brought about
improvements which were easily noticeable and these improvements aroused the
enthusiasm of other citizens who had previously shown little or no interest.

During the drought years of the '30's, progress in park enlargement and
development continued. The years of 1935 and 1936 saw the following
improvements: a well-equipped children's playground, three clay-surfaced tennis
courts and a well lighted outdoor skating rink. WPA provided the funds and the
work was done by local men. The National Youth Administration (NYA) planted 500
trees furnished by the Association. A city grant of $50.00 was invested in
additional playground equipment. In 1937, as a WPA project, a substantial
shelter house was constructed with brick donated by the Soo Line railroad.
Outdoor fireplaces were also built.

In 1938, the Association focused the spotlight on the possibility of a new
swimming pool for the city to be located in the park. A $6,000.00 bond issue was
floated and with the help of WPA engineers a modern swimming pool was planned.
Construction of the pool was started in 1939, WPA furnishing the labor.
Completed in 1940, the pool was dedicated in June of that year. Two tennis
courts were also cement surfaced.

The park trees were badly damaged by a sleet storm in 1942 and many had to be
replaced. An early frost in 1943 killed many more, requiring replacement.

Much credit for the success of the Wishek Park goes to the Association's park
committee, many of whom have served on the committee since its inception. The
members have had the welfare of the youth of Wishek always in mind. Adequate
help has always been provided to chaperon the children and at night, proper
police protection has been provided.

        DR. T. C. RITTER

Wishek and its Association of Commerce are truly proud of the park which is the
beauty spot of our City.

Roads and Highways

The Association's road committee has performed an outstanding job for the
community of Wishek. As early as 1929, the committee realized that active work
was necessary, if Wishek was to get its share of the business from its trade
territory. Meetings were held with the County Commissioners and requests were
made for graveled feeder roads to the south and west and for improvement of
the Green Lake road.

Highway No. 13, which passes through the city, also received attention. Under
heavy traffic, dust from this highway became unbearable and in 1934 the state
highway department was induced to pave it through the city, even though funds
for this work had been allocated and restricted to county seat towns only. Four
miles of the same highway were also paved and No. 13 was also graveled to the
Emmons County line. No. 13 from Wishek to Fredonia had been almost impassable
for years. Under steady pressure from the committee, the state department
reshaped and graveled the full 28 miles in 1932.

The Association road committee has been a clearing house for road problems.
They have listened to the complaints of farmers and town folk alike, and have
presented these problems to the County Commissioners, always with a definite
plan of improvement in mind. In 1945, a complete county highway development
plan was laid before the commissioners for the trade area southwest of Wishek.
Work on roads as planned was started and in 1946 much of the program was
completed and the work of gravel surfacing started.

Scouts

The Association has never lost interest in the welfare of the community's
youth. Realizing that the youth of today are the citizens of tomorrow, the
Association, in 1931, assumed the sponsorship of Wishek Troop No. 81, Boy
Scouts of America. Same had previously been sponsored by the Presbyterian
Church. Through the years, the Association has provided good councilmen,
efficient leaders and funds when funds were needed. Scout headquarters have also
been provided.

The interest which the city has shown in its scouts has been well worth while,
and the scouts in turn have been ever ready to share in community activities,
giving aid in clean-up drives, school policing, first aid and life-saving work
at the swimming pool. The "father and son" banquet program was also sponsored,
with much of the ground work and planning laid out by the scouts themselves. In
past years, Wishek has had some fine Scout troops. They have attended
Jam-bo-rees, entered into the scout contests and have returned home with high
honors.

Agriculture

The Association's agricultural committee fully realized the necessity for close
relationship between the farmer and the townsman, if Wishek was to grow and
prosper. As early as 1929, it put on a drive for better sires, better breeds of
livestock, and better strains of grain. It also advocated and encouraged the
planting of sweet clover and alfalfa as soil building crops. It is reported
that 400 acres of these legume varieties were seeded in the county. The
Association committee worked in close cooperation with the Extension Department
of the Soo Line and in 1929 a pure bred sheep train exhibit was brought to
Wishek. An experimental corn plot was also planted. In 1932 a train with better
grain exhibits was brought in by the Soo Line, which railroad also sponsored a
farm life film which was well attended.

        WISHEK'S SWIMMING POOL

During the severe drought and heavy grasshopper infestation of the '30's, the
agricultural committee contacted state and federal authorities and requested
help in grasshopper control. Poison was provided from federal funds and
thousands of acres were covered.

        Early Threshing Near Wishek

        WPA Project, Wishek, 1933-1935

        WISHEK'S BAND

Farmers filed complaints that the Wishek station had a higher freight rate than
stations in surrounding towns. The Association committee brought pressure to
bear to bring the rates in line.

Spotlight Chronology

Improvements and developments in which the Association of Commerce took a
definite and active interest or sponsorship:

1927--Wishek's first Sauerkraut Day--continued successfully for a number of
years.

1928--Wishek Cemetery Association reorganized, Cemetery re-surveyed and a new
addition added, a gravel road built within the cemetery and a shelter room
provided. One Rural Free Delivery route established and petitions filed for two
additional routes.

1929--McIntosh County Athletic Day sponsored, in which all schools of the
county participated.

1931--Wishek's Bazaar Tuesday, continued for two years.

1933--Better sidewalks projects, constructed under WPA.

1935--Wishek's City Park, extensive expansion and development.

1937--Auto parking changed and more parking space provided.

1938--Pancake Day sponsored--enormous crowds in attendance. Auditorium Bond
issue sponsored--Drive made for its passage.

1940--Auditorium constructed.

1942--Auditorium Equipment--Drive sponsored for Purchase of same.

1944--Water and Sewerage System--Drive to pass the bond issue.

1945--Turkey Day sponsored--very successful. Relief committees set up for the
handling of Red Cross and Infantile Paralysis drives. Water and Sewer Bond
issue--Drive made for its passage.

Athletic Field--park expansion; Association advanced $1,000.00 for its purchase
from the Wishek Investment Company.

The Wishek Star; established in October, 1945, through the efforts of the
Association, which sponsored a contest in the schools to select a name for the
paper. Edwin T. Brost became the editor.

1947--Wishek's Airport--Association raised over $5,000.00 by private donation,
160 acres of land was purchased and deeded to City. Wishek's Home Christmas
Lighting contest--sponsored by Association and prizes given. 1948--National
Guard Armory--As a home for Wishek's National Guard Company, its erection is
assured. Site has already been chosen.

The Wishek Association of Commerce now has 185 members. They are business and
professional men and other interested citizens. A board of ten directors are
chosen by the membership annually and upon them falls the responsibility of
guiding and directing the affairs of the Association. The Association has been
a vital force in the expansion and development of Wishek for twenty-five years.
